BAILLIÈRE ET FILS 19, RUE HAUTEFEUILLE, PRÈS DU BOULEVARD SAINT-GERMAIN 1893 All rights reserved ANCIEN LIRECTEUR DES ASILES PUBLICS DE CADILLAC ET DE BORDEAUX TÉMOIGNAGE D'ESTIME ET DE RECONNAISSANCE ERNEST BERGER. There aren't many books on practical market gardening; at least those that can be consulted with advantage, those written by men in the trade, who have put their hands to the grindstone, as the saying goes. The good ones are expensive; the others are incomplete, too old or written especially for certain regions. It was to fill this gap, and to offer a book that could be consulted with profit just about everywhere in France, that we wrote Vegetable Plants and Market Gardening. This work, conceived on a new plan, can be consulted by the amateur as well as the gardener: each will find information of interest. We have emphasized only the practical side of cultivation, what it is necessary to know in order to do well. After giving some general ideas on how to create and set up a low-cost market garden, we go on to describe each plant: 1° Origin; 2° Cultivation in the open ground and early crops on beds and under frames, appropriate to different climates; 3° Description of the best varieties to grow; 4° Seeds, practical means of harvesting and preserving them, and their germination period; 5° Diseases and pests, and the best ways of destroying them; 6° Uses and economic and nutritional properties of plants. The last part includes a calendar of sowing and planting for the twelve months of the year. The book is illustrated with 64 figures, thanks in large part to the kindness of Messrs Vilmorin et Cie, which show the appearance of the species described and make it easier to recognize them. All the plants, even almost all the varieties we mention, have been cultivated, tested and studied by us with the greatest care. We can therefore affirm that by following exactly our cultivation instructions, which are the fruit of serious and reliable observations, good results will be obtained. October 1892. E. BERGER THE MARKET GARDEN PART ONE ESTABLISHING A MARKET GARDEN CHAPTER ONE THE SOIL I. CHOICE OF SOIL. - When you want to create a market garden, you need to choose good soil, of such a type that its composition lends itself to the cultivation of the various vegetable plants and activates their vegetation. Three main types of soil, taken separately or in combination, meet these conditions. 1° Free soils. - These are composed of: one-tenth carbonate of lime, one-tenth humus, three-fifths alumina or clay. This is the most fertile type of soil, and is generally suitable for growing all vegetable plants. It is easy to work with, and can do without soil improvers; it accepts all fertilizers, which it keeps decomposing for a long time, and from which it gradually benefits; it is also used as a base for composts intended for temperate greenhouse plants: BERGER, Plantes potagères. finally, it is used in mixtures with potting soil intended for seedlings or early plantings. In general, this type of soil is found at low altitudes. 2° Marsh soils - Composed of small quantities of sand, peat and humus, and very similar in fertility to free soil, these soils become much more fertile when amended with limestone or lean sand, which gives them porosity and makes them lighter and therefore easier to work. In their natural state, these types of soil are poorly vegetated and water remains stagnant for part of the year. In this case, when the subsoil is not too bad, the earth removed from the ditches is thrown back onto the other parts of the soil to raise it up; this work should preferably be done during drought, so as not to be hindered by water. This type of soil is particularly suitable for summer: because of the moisture in which the plant roots are located, they grow vigorously and magnificent results are obtained. In winter, it becomes too cold, and some plants suffer. 3° Siliceous-clay soils. - Their base is sand mixed with clay and limestone. They are easy to work, and most vegetable plants thrive in them; however, in hot weather, they can be a little scorching. There are many other intermediate soils. They are generally referred to by names that recall the two dominant parts. For example: Clay-limestone - composed mainly of clay and lime. Silico-calcareous. - Composed mainly of sand and lime. Many of these soils become excellent after a few years of cultivation, provided they have not been starved of fertilizer. In general, all soils that are not too compact are good for market gardening; sand, which is absolutely pure and very thin, can, with hard work and manuring, improve remarkably and become a first-rate soil for this type of cultivation. As the subsoil influences the quality of the top layer, its nature and depth must be taken into account. If the subsoil is compact and tenacious, and retains water, the plant layer will be constantly damp during heavy rains, and the plants planted there will suffer from this overabundance of water. If, on the other hand, the subsoil is porous and absorbent, the top layer will always be healthy. It is possible (though rare) for the top layer to be thick enough for the subsoil to exert no influence on it. It is therefore important to carefully study and choose your soil as upper and lower layers; those that are light, soft and deep are the best. II. exposure, orientation. - The choice of exposure is no less important, because of the major role it plays in plant growth. The best exposures are gentle slopes facing south, east or west, but always sheltered from the north as much as possible. In slightly steeply sloping soils, we prefer those dominated by clay. These soils retain a fair amount of moisture, as we have said, when they are on the plains, but here, because of their sloping surface, they quickly lose this excess of moisture through drainage and almost always remain healthy and warm, with the sun's rays acting more intensely than on the plains, whereas light soils, placed in the same conditions at the time of great heat, become scorching and even sterile; these same soils, placed on the plains or on gentle slopes, are excellent and completely fertile. To sum up, strong soils should be chosen as far as possible on slopes, and light soils on plains; however, these various conditions are often difficult to achieve because of the layout of the location or the means at our disposal, but we will get as close as possible. As important as it is not to have a soil that is too wet, it is equally important to choose a site that is supplied with the water essential for watering during hot spells, because without water in summer in dry soil, market gardening is impossible. CHAPTER II SOIL PREPARATION I. RUNNING. - Raking is an operation which consists in stirring up all or part of the plant layer, depending on its depth, so that the top soil takes the place of the soil below. This work should be carried out in autumn and during part of winter, in dry or cold weather. Avoid doing it in rainy weather, as compacted soil dries with great difficulty and has a poor effect on plant roots. Not all soils require deep ripping: in some low, damp soils, a good spade is all that's needed; a natural meadow, which has been worked for a long time, usually provides excellent soil that is very suitable for vegetable plants, and here deep ripping would be of no use. The spade is used for this purpose. Start by removing the turf in thin slabs, arranging them in square piles and placing them in beds, with the surface underneath; if the soil in which you are working lacks limestone, powdered quicklime or, failing that, well-crushed marl, or even plaster, is added; fermentation soon takes place, and after a year spent in this state, the whole thing forms a kind of potting soil that is spread in equal parts over cultivated squares. Such is the method used by certain growers in central France, which is worth noting and imitating. Once the turf has been removed, we start breaking up the soil, taking care to turn the top parts underneath. When deep trenching is required, the ground is divided into squares, so as to proceed in an orderly fashion, and to cut only those parts where the work can be completed. After this layout, a trench or gauge is opened, varying from 70 to 80 centimetres and even 1 metre in width. The soil from this first trench is transported to the site where the work is to be completed. The depth varies according to the nature of the soil and the thickness of the plant layer, at Om, 50 or Om, 60 at the most; this is quite sufficient for growing vegetables. Once the first trench has been opened, a second of the same width is dug, and the soil from this one is used to fill in the first. This process continues until the work is completed, and the last trench is filled in with the soil from the first one, which was transported there at the beginning. The tools most commonly used to carry out these major jobs are the German shovel, the Piedmont or Correze pickaxe, and the beche. Sometimes, a forked hoe is also used. If the land is very large, it may not be possible to break it all in the first year. In this case, the broken part will be reserved for tap-rooted plants such as salsify, carrots, etc., and the other part, which will only receive a good ploughing with a spade, will be reserved for tracing-rooted plants such as peas, beans, etc..... As the soil is broken up, a layer of half-consumed manure is spread over the broken-up area. This is spread regularly, and successive rains and frosts soak up the salts, which infiltrate the new soil and fertilize it. When this layer of manure is more or less stripped of its principles, a month or six weeks later, a new layer is spread and buried. In this way, the soil can be used for all kinds of crops from the very first year. As you break up the soil, you remove any stones, tuffs or pebbles likely to hinder plant growth; all these materials are buried in the alleys in place of the good soil, which is then transported to the squares. By this exchange, we put everything in its place, taking advantage of everything; the pebbles or stones will strengthen the soil in the alleys, and the good soil removed will benefit the jar- din by increasing the vegetation layer. II. TRACÉ. - Once the ground has been levelled and its composition evened out, we finish levelling it and lay out the paths. As for the width and number of these, we must keep in mind the principle that, since cultivation is the goal, we must divert as little land as possible, taking rigorous account of the necessities or conveniences of farming; from this point of view, we will lay out the necessary paths. The layout and direction of the paths will take into account the nature of the site, the position of the water supply, the ease with which it can be distributed, the location of watering wells, manure pits, obstacles, ditches or watering holes, etc.When the layout of the land allows a regular layout to be made, we begin by dividing it up by laying out the main path, which should be a good 1.50 m wide, in the direction of the slope of the land; we then lay out another transverse path of roughly the same width; after which, we divide the remaining land into squares, varying from 10, 15 or 20 m long by the same width, separated by 1 m paths. These alleys are lined with useful plants such as sorrel, thyme, strawberry and savory. On the other hand, manure pits should be placed at a distance from the house, because of the unhealthy fumes they give off, and the unpleasant sight of them should be concealed by planting green trees such as thuya, ligustrum, laurel-tin, etc. around the pits. Although the proximity of fruit trees is generally harmful to vegetable crops, if the trench is deep enough, two rows of apple or pear trees can be planted along the main paths. These trees, which should be raised to a certain height above ground level to reduce their impact on crops, should be trained and pruned every year, choosing varieties of average vigor that set fruit quickly. HI. AMENDMENTS. - Amending a plot of land means providing it with the elements it lacks, and introducing the improving substances needed to correct its nature and enable it to produce the plants you intend to grow there. It's important to carry out such work only after careful study of the soil, because it means recomposing the soil; giving it what it lacks, and in such a proportion that the result of the amendment is an improvement rather than the sterility that could occur if it were done badly; and finally, it means giving consistency to soil that is too light, and porosity to strong soil. Compact, clayey soils can be successfully amended by adding a reasonable proportion of lean sand. However, in some soils that are too clayey, the sand is unable to combine with the other elements, and tends constantly to penetrate the plant layer, arriving in the subsoil without having acted. In this case, crushed terracotta debris, broken oyster shells, coal ash, etc. are used to advantage. It has also been suggested that the surface crust of the topsoil should be calcined: under the effect of the heat of the fire, clay cracks, hardens and, as a result, produces the same effect as gravel. This is a good and practical procedure in certain regions where other types of soil improver are unavailable and would become very expensive. Light soils, which generally become very hot in summer, should be amended with compact materials, but 1 See D. Bois, Le petit Jardin, Paris, 1889 (Bibliothèque des connaissances utiles). Clay that is too compact will only produce a mediocre amendment, and clayey marls are preferable. Marsh soil, excavated or cleared soil, river mud and cistern sludge produce excellent effects on these soils, as they not only serve as an amendment, but also as fertilizer. The various types of marl that can be used on almost any type of soil "except limestone" produce good soil improvers if you know how to make the most of them, but you mustn't overuse them, as their role is not that of fertilizer, and too much of them would be harmful. Marl is usually applied to the soil in autumn. It is deposited in heaps; the frosts and rains of winter begin to disintegrate it, and in spring it is spread out and mixed with the soil. In market gardening, this type of amendment is rarely used, as it is time-consuming and sometimes costly. IV. CHAULAGE. - In certain limestone-free soils, liming is an excellent operation that considerably increases the value and fertility of the soil. Not only is lime a good soil improver, but it also destroys insects and weeds, and promotes the decomposition of plant and animal debris in the soil. All soils, regardless of their composition, adapt more or less well to lime amendments, which is why we advise you to carry out a few partial tests before working on large areas. We use stone lime just out of the kiln, which is laid out in small heaps, 5 to 7 meters apart, covered with a layer of earth about 25 centimeters thick. They remain in this state for two weeks 1. and, when melting is complete, we take advantage of a fine day to spread it and then bury it immediately, mixing it well with the soil; liming must be renewed every five years. V. DRAINAGE. - Soils that are too wet are unsuitable for all crops, and it is essential to rid them of this excess moisture. This is achieved by drainage, which consists in draining water through underground conduits. To this end, ditches are dug about 80 centimetres or 1 metre deep, 60 or 80 centimetres wide, and spaced 5 to 15 metres apart or more, depending on the degree of moisture in the soil. In these ditches, a series of clay, concrete or cement pipes are laid end-to-end, carefully covering the joints with shards of brick or tile, coarse gravel or broken stone, to prevent soil from penetrating; the pipes should be secured as they are laid. In certain localities where stone is readily available, the pipes can be replaced by gutter-shaped conduits, built by laying out the rubble, or by placing rough stone in the bottom of the ditches. As many cavities as possible are left, then covered with gravel, clinker, pebbles, crushed stone and so on. Pine wood is preferable to any other species, as it rots very slowly in the ground. This latter method is widely used in the south-west, mainly in the Landes region. VI. SANITATION. - Sanitation or external drainage consists of draining water into the open air, through wide ditches that are dug more or less deeply, depending on the humidity of the soil; these ditches are directed in the direction of the slope of the land. When water is plentiful, the first ditches are cut with cross ditches to speed up drainage. In all cases, a collecting ditch will receive the whole, discharging the water into the lowest parts and, preferably, into a stream. CHAPTER III LABOURING LAND Ploughing means stirring up and turning over the topsoil, loosening it by dividing its parts, in order to make it suitable for growing new crops. By turning over the soil, we bring to the surface the lower parts impregnated with the salts that have precipitated there as a result of the infiltration of water through fertilizers. Ploughing is generally carried out with a spade or fork, and in some regions, with a hoe. Today, spade ploughing is the only method used by market gardeners in major centers (Paris, Lyon, Marseille, etc.). In strong soils, the fork is sometimes of great use, even indispensable for getting the work done, as it digs in more easily. For this purpose, very strong forks, specially manufactured for this purpose, are used. When ploughing land for immediate planting or sowing, the clods are broken up and crushed as much as possible, so that the roots of the plants can easily penetrate and adapt. Ploughing should be more or less deep, depending on the plants to be cultivated. Plants with taproots, which penetrate perpendicularly into the soil to achieve normal development, require slightly deeper ploughing. Plants with tracer roots, on the other hand, require shallow ploughing. As a general rule, however, wherever possible, good ploughing should be carried out with a full spade. Clay-limestone soils are difficult to work in summer, because of their extreme hardness, and because they then form a compact, tenacious mass. In such soils, when irrigation is possible, this hardness is remedied by running water through the soil, leaving it to stand for a few hours; two days later, when the soil is dry again, ploughing begins; but if irrigation is not possible, and the spade is unable to divide the parts, the soil is first broken up with a hoe; then ploughed with a spade, breaking up the clods. In winter, strong soils require little ploughing; once the harvest has been removed, rough spading is carried out, retaining as many clods as possible. In summer, these soils need to be ploughed frequently, so that the heat can penetrate them more easily and remove the moisture they contain. This procedure is hardly applicable to vegetable growing, as you would never choose a strong soil for commercial cultivation. Light, sandy soils that are dry by nature retain their looseness for a long time, and require less ploughing than heavy soils. It is mainly in winter that deep ploughing will be beneficial, as rain and frost will correct the lack of moisture by impregnating the soil. In summer, it's best to choose a rainy or dark day to carry out the frequent plowing, hoeing and weeding required by this type of soil. In excessively wet soils, where water remains in winter, it is customary, as soon as heavy rains approach, to spade the free soil into large furrows or ados, and let it spend the winter in this state, until it is possible to penetrate it and bury the fertilizers. When a plot of land of a certain size needs to be spaded, the most expeditious means of reducing labor costs should be sought. First, divide the land into as many planks as you deem necessary, and then, at even number, open a trench with a good spade iron, and place the earth with the wheelbarrow on the end of the plank where you will finish. The surface of the soil is then turned over and levelled. Fertilizers are buried more or less deeply, depending on the type of plant you intend to put in them. Before reaching the end of this first plank, we move to the opposite side of the second, in order to break it in while keeping the surface uniform and the gauge equal; this means is the most appreciated and the most expeditious. In a market garden, cultivation is so intensive that it is impossible to determine the time of ploughing, which is carried out at all times, as a bed is emptied: ploughing, smoking and planting are carried out immediately. In a well-managed vegetable garden, you should never see any free squares. This advice applies above all to middle-class and public-establishment cultivation. CHAPTER IV SECONDARY WORK I. STRAIGHTENING, LAYOUT OF BEDS. - After ploughing, if the edges of the squares are not marked by a border, they are drawn with a chalk line, then the soil is divided into planks 1m, 25 to 1m, 40 and more wide, depending on the type of plants to be sown. The division paths are marked out by stretching the chalk line out straight, holding it between the two feet as you walk, and spacing them out more or less according to the width you wish to give the paths. When planting vegetables, which require a certain amount of space between plants, first draw the two outer lines, using the iron tracer or a simple piece of tapered wood, which serve as a guide for drawing the central line, without having to replace the string. With a little practice, it's easy to draw straight lines: for example, on a board where you want to draw seven lines, you'll only need to draw the cord for the middle line and the one on each side, i.e. for three lines only. This is the same way for ray sowing, except that the lines are drawn with the narrow end of the hoe, so that they are a little deeper. For transplanting certain plants in summer, and even seedlings, it is customary in dry soils to form beds with large edges; to do this, the soil is raked back into the paths and along the edges; in this way, the water used for watering is of greater benefit to the plants, as it does not spill onto the paths. II. HEDGING. - Whatever the method of sowing or the type of planting, soil preparation is of paramount importance, firstly for success and secondly for the eye. After the beds have been laid, if the surface of the ground is not suitable for sowing, it is raked or harrowed. This is done by walking a fork back and forth across the ground, breaking up clods, loosening the surface and bringing up stones. After sowing, the soil is harrowed again, this time to distribute the seeds and bring them into contact with the soil, covering them as it were; in light soil, this operation is sufficient to cover the seeds by a small volume. Around Paris, and in the neighbouring départements, this system is widely used, and is very rapid. III. WEEDING. - Weeding consists in pulling out weeds from young seedlings or plantations, and making them disappear: for seedlings, this work should be done when they are still young, so as not to shake them too much and lift them up. At the same time, if necessary, they should be thinned out. To be done properly, thinning requires a certain amount of practice, to be able to distinguish without trial and error which plants need to be removed. Women are generally better at this work than men; they operate with greater dexterity. In summer, when the soil is dry, water at least one hour before starting to weed, to give the plants and the soil surface time to dry out. Weeding should not be carried out during the hottest periods, as young seedlings will suffer and have difficulty recovering. As far as possible, choose dark or not too hot days, and if you can only work in the evening, especially if the area to be weeded is not very large, this will be preferable, as the night firms up the plants, and the coolness makes them stand upright. Whatever the time of year, unless it is raining, before leaving the work, give the weeded areas a good watering. IV. HOEING. - Hoeing is a superficial ploughing operation whose aim is to loosen the surface of the soil around the plants, making it more permeable to atmospheric influences and at the same time preventing weeds from growing. This work is carried out with a tool known as a hoe, and can be done in any season. The soil is disturbed to a depth of between 8 and 12 centimetres, depending on the type of plant being hoeed. Avoid hoeing when the soil is too wet, and wait until the morning dew has fallen; experience has shown that hoeing is most profitable when the plants are completely dry. As soon as the soil begins to harden, hoe again, as the more the soil is loosened around the plants, the better they will thrive. V. PLUMBING. - Leading is designed to bring the seeds into contact with the soil: it is done after harrowing with a fork and after raking, as far as possible, in dry weather and mainly in light soils; in strong soils, it would produce poor results. This work consists of treading the soil, either with the feet and walking at a slow pace, or with a stick, the back of a digging shovel, or even, in certain regions, with a small hand roller. This operation becomes necessary in soils that are too light, and ensures success by encouraging germination. CHAPTER VE FERTILIZERS AND FUNURES S4 Fertilized soil would be rapidly exhausted if it were not given back the fertilizing principles and salts it has lost through successive cultivation. This is the only way to maintain and perpetuate soil fertility. I. CHEMICAL FERTILIZERS. - There are a large number of chemically compounded fertilizers available today, but they have never given very good results and are therefore rarely used in market gardening. II. MANURES AND NATURAL FERTILIZERS. - The fertilizers best known and appreciated by gardeners are rotten or consumed manures; horse, donkey or mule manure is the most popular, and consequently the most widely used. In their fresh state, these manures give off a gentle warmth that lasts quite a long time, and are used in winter to make layers for planting and sowing early crops. When decomposition is complete, the resulting potting soil forms the base for crops or beds. In a vegetable garden, nothing should be wasted; all plant detritus from vegetable dressing, grasses, sweepings, in short anything likely to decompose through fermentation, should be piled up; on its own, this detritus would only form a fertilizer, not of zero effect, but of too slow efficiency; that's why it's preferable to combine it with other animal manures which, while improving it, also activate its fermentation. Not all soils are suited to the same fertilizers, so they should be distributed with discretion, according to the nature and needs of the soil. Horned cattle manure, which is greasy and unctuous, is suited to light, warm soils; by fertilizing them, it gives them the tenacity that binds their parts together and preserves a certain moisture. Pig manure, which is very cold, is also suitable for these same soils. Half-rotted manure from horses, mules and donkeys is suitable for cold, damp soils, lightening them by dividing the parts. This type of fertilizer is the most widely used, and is generally suitable for all soils, provided that decomposition is allowed to take place according to the nature of the soil for which it is intended. Rabbit and sheep manure are very hot and activate vegetation enormously. Pigeon and hen droppings, otherwise known as colombine, are the most powerful fertilizers known; they should only be used with caution, as they are so hot that they burn plant roots; soaking them in water for twelve to fifteen hours produces a liquid manure that is excellent for activating the vegetation of vegetable plants, especially cucurbits. Fecal matter is a very energetic fertilizer that always gives good results, but must be used with care. Used alone, they would burn the plants; mixed with manures, they produce very rapid fermentation, and consequently a highly stimulating fertilizer that all plants, especially cabbages, can handle. Whatever type of manure you use, we advise you not to use it straight from the stable; you need to pile it up for a few days, to allow fermentation and even decomposition to begin. Winter, autumn and the beginning of spring are the most favorable seasons for fertilizing land; however, when cultivating intensively, we fertilize in all seasons when the soil is ready, and with each new crop, so as not to exhaust the soil. There are exceptions to this rule, however, as root vegetables such as carrots and salifis do not adapt, or adapt poorly, to recent fertilization. During our excursions across France, we noticed that in certain regions, notably Vienne, Charentes and part of Gironde, farmers did not know how to collect and dispose of their fertilizers: as the manure leaves the stable, it is piled unevenly in a corner, outside or in the open air, exposed to sun, rain and anything else that dries it out and destroys its soluble and volatile principles. In this way, fermentation takes place only very unevenly, and the result is a poor fertilizer that lacks any stimulant, and fertilizes the soil only imperfectly; growers who treat their fertilizers in this way never get good results, and there's no reason to be surprised. To get good fertilizer, you have to do things differently. You dig pits of varying depths, depending on the needs of the farm, from 50 centimetres to 1 metre and more, 2 to 3 metres wide and 4 or 5 metres long; the bottom of the pits is sloped; in the lowest part, a hole of a certain width is reserved to receive the liquid manure, which is removed as the hole fills up, to be distributed over the various squares under cultivation. During hot spells, fertilizers in fermentation are often watered with this liquid manure, to speed up decomposition. When you can build masonry manure pits, coated with cement on the inside, you should not neglect to do so, as you will gain by preventing liquid fertilizers from escaping through infiltration. Alternatively, and less expensively, you can coat the bottom of the pits and even the walls with clay, into which you add a little hydraulic lime. Leached wood ashes, soot and plaster, although not considered proper fertilizers, can be used as good stimulants, influencing vegetation by stimulating plant organs to draw more nourishment from the earth and the atmosphere. They can be spread on plants as they begin to develop, in dry, warm weather; plaster is particularly good for crucifers, turnips, cabbage, etc. Poultry feather is a very powerful fertilizer that is not as widespread as it should be. Market gardeners in the south-west of France, particularly around Bordeaux, use it extensively. It gives very good results on all vegetable plants. Its effect is immediate, so to speak, but short-lived: about two months, or seventy days, are enough for this fertilizer to give all it can. It can be used at any time of year, and the results are always excellent, though preferably when vegetation is active in spring and summer. Leafy vegetables, i.e. those whose aerial parts can be eaten, such as lettuce, cabbage, spinach, sorrel, etc., seem to do better with it than root vegetables. Gardeners use this fertilizer in several ways. In the case of seedlings, the soil is hoeed, the manure placed in the gauge and a layer of feathers spread over it, so that it is about 5 centimetres above the soil. For transplants, cabbages, tomatoes, etc., they put a handful of manure on each plant, opening a hole with a hoe or by hand. In regions where planting is done with a hoe, the feather should be put in while forming the rays. As far as possible, use this fertilizer only when it is fresh, i.e. before it has undergone any kind of fermentation. III. LIQUID FERTILIZERS. - All manure juices make excellent fertilizers; they activate vegetation and should be used in the morning or evening, especially in hot weather. Care should be taken to ensure that the leaves of the plants to be watered are not too saturated. Fresh fecal matter, diluted ten times its volume, is excellent for activating the vegetation of all vegetable plants; if you have only this fertilizer at your disposal, you're sure to get magnificent results. Never use it unless the plants have fully recovered. IV. MULCH. - Mulch is short manure, rather dry than fatty, which is used to cover cultivated beds throughout the summer, to intercept the sun's drying rays and prevent the soil from being beaten up by watering. Seedlings benefit from being covered with a light mulch: as well as conserving moisture, it prevents the seeds from being disturbed by watering as they germinate; it is spread as evenly as possible, dividing the molecules well. It is particularly suitable for dry, hot soils. V. POT. - Potting soil is the result of the complete decomposition of manure, grasses, leaves, etc., reduced to a friable state. Potting soil made from layers of horse manure is considered the best. It is used to cover fine-seeded seedlings in the open ground, to protect them and ensure their emergence; it is also used to fill new layers for early crops, and to cover seedlings grown in pots. It is always a good idea to have a sufficient quantity of this type of potting soil at hand. CHAPTER VI WAYS OF ACTIVATING PLANT VEGETATION I. LAYING. - Layers are a powerful and indispensable means of activating the vegetation of certain vegetable plants. Since a large proportion of them cannot be sown in the open ground, which is not yet sufficiently heated to cause the seeds to germinate quickly, it is through layers that many of our cultivated plants begin their first development in winter and then complete their vegetation in the open ground, when the heat is sufficient. Any organic material that can generate heat when fermented can be used to make layers. Horse manure is the best choice for long-lasting warmth; working horse manure is preferable, as the straw is better broken down and impregnated than in luxury horse manure. These manures give very strong heat, but their action can be moderated by mixing them with annealed manures, hornbeam, oak or lime leaves, grass or moss. The heat of the layers depends on the materials used in their construction, and also on the thickness they are given; the thicker the layer, the stronger and longer-lasting the heat. It is therefore important to know the degree of heat required by the plants you want to grow, in order to place them in the conditions that will best promote their development. In market gardening, three types of layers are used. 1° Warm layers. - Hot beds are erected from December to March; their dimensions vary according to the length and width of the boxes to be placed on them; their thickness is proportionate to the heat required by the plants: it varies between 40 and 70 centimetres. Before starting to build the layer, the manure is mixed, if necessary, then the four sides are marked out with ropes and stakes. The manure is then taken in bunches and laid flat, going backwards; the impregnated parts are mixed with the dried parts. The impregnated parts are mixed with the dried parts and spread evenly over all the points to form a first even bed; each forkful is pressed down firmly with the back of the tool; any overhanging straw is brought in to form a straight, solid edge. Once this first bed has been spread out, it is trodden on, watered if the manure is too dry, then a second bed is placed, then a third, until the desired height is reached. The surface should be as flat as possible, with a few forks of manure in the hollows. If the bed is to be used for bells, it is edged; if, on the other hand, it is to be used for chests, these are placed as soon as the last bed is in place, and a small layer of manure is spread inside, tamped down and loaded with potting soil. The frames are then put in place, and covered with straw mats to help the bed get its first light, which takes six or eight days. Many growers around Paris and in the north of France build their beds all at once, which is quicker, but it takes a great deal of practice to ensure uniformity, and the results are no better than those obtained using the method just described. 2° Warm coats. - Warm layers are made in the same way as the previous ones; they are made from annealed or previously used manure, with a little fresh manure to give a stimulating effect; leaves, herbs, etc. are also added. All these ingredients must be well mixed and sufficiently moistened. These beds are very useful in January, February and March, for sowing or transplanting celery, tomatoes, chillies, etc. When a warm layer has been used up and has produced just about all the effect expected, it can still be used, as the manure has not yet been used up, so it can be reworked by mixing it with the same care as the first time; a little new manure can be added and an excellent warm layer can be formed. 3° Muted layers. - They are made in the same way as the others, and are very useful for transplanting seedlings made on warm layers, and for planting certain types of plants. Pits or holes for melons, which are made in the spring in the open ground, are merely deaf layers. Based on our experience, we believe that when you have good soil at your disposal, you should not neglect to mix it with the composts intended for cultivation in layers, after having sieved it thoroughly; this mixture gives consistency, prevents the soil from drying out too quickly, and plants develop much better in it than in pure potting soils. Layers are generally placed in a healthy location with good exposure, facing south, but in such a way that they can be reached as much as possible in winter by the sun's rays from east to west. II. ReCHaups. - In winter, when a layer begins to cool, it needs to be revived; this is done with the help of rechaups, i.e. by piling up good fresh manure or leaves in the paths and around the trunks, which are firmly tamped; they are often reshuffled, each time adding to them to fill the voids produced by settling. III. AccoT. - The accot differs from the rechaud in that, instead of using new manure, we use leaves, old manure, straw or fern. The aim is to prevent the cold from penetrating the inside of the trunk, so it's not necessary to build them up very thickly; 15 to 20 centimetres is enough. Pile them up around the trunks to their full height, keep the outer edge straight, and place E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 2 a frame of slats held in place by good stakes firmly driven into the ground. An accot lasts a whole winter, if you take care to reload as the soil settles. IV. EDGE. - Layers that are not to receive coffers should be edged, to prevent the soil placed on them from collapsing. There are several ways of edging a layer, either with strong straw pads, or with long, slightly twisted manures, which are secured with long wooden or wire pegs. Some growers border with a large board that they place sideways on the edge of the bed; the soil is then brought down to the foot and firmly packed, the board is supported by strong wire or wooden pegs; this is a good, inexpensive way. V. Apos. - Ados are flowerbeds, sloping to the south, that are successively established in a square or along a wall. Once the site has been chosen, the procedure is as follows: once the soil has been well tilled, lines are drawn at varying distances according to the width to be given to the ados, from 1.50 to 2 meters, then with the spade, the earth is removed from the front to reload the back. The back of the spade is used to beat the top of the glacis to hold the soil in place, or even better, to hold it in place with planks, which is preferable. The slope to be given to the teens is 15 centimetres per metre, then the rake is passed over the whole, and a certain thickness of compost is spread. This kind of work is excellent, as it produces vegetation earlier than flat cultivation, and always guarantees good results; it's especially during winter and spring that ados are needed. Only in the vicinity of Paris, in the north and east of France, are teas successfully used; in the Midi, they would only give good results in winter, at the time of the coldest weather. CHAPTER VII POTTING PLANT MULTIPLICATION Propagation by seed is the most natural method of reproduction, and also the one that gives the best results. Almost all plants can be propagated by sowing, except for a few genera which do not produce fertile seeds, such as tarragon and chives. As the choice of good seeds is one of the main conditions for success, the grower should harvest most of the seeds needed for his operation himself, and carefully select and raise all his seedbeds so that they are kept clean. All similar varieties should be kept away from each other, to prevent the mixing of fertilizing dust. Seeds should be harvested only when they are fully ripe and not completely dry; those of some genera, such as lamb's lettuce and sorrel, should be picked before they are fully ripe. Drying is completed by spreading them out on canvas. Certain types, such as spinach and salsify, which only ripen their seeds successively, should be visited every day; as soon as they start to turn yellow, they should be picked to complete their drying in semi-shade. Plants such as carrots, celery, parsley and cabbage, whose seeds, though ripe and even dry, are difficult to detach from their receptacle, should be tied up in bundles and hung in a well-ventilated attic, where the seeds will mature and dry out. Many seeds, such as sorrel and parsnips, only retain their germinative qualities for a short time, and only sprout in the first year. To avoid disappointment, you should choose seeds from the last harvest. Other seeds, on the other hand - beans, broad beans, peas, etc., all papilionaceous plants in general - retain their germinative power for a long time; their longevity is even considerably increased when stored in their silique or pod. In short, all seeds retain their ability to germinate to a greater or lesser extent, depending on the degree of permeability of their envelope, their consistency and their chemical composition. All these considerations must be taken into account by the grower, who must be familiar with the different types of seeds, know how long they germinate, and know how long it takes each plant to reach full development. This knowledge can be acquired quickly, with a little attention and, above all, practice. I. SOWING IN THE GROUND. - Not all seeds can be sown under the same conditions, and sowing must be modified according to the nature of the soil, the climate, and the size or tenacity of the seeds. The finer the seeds, the less they should be buried in the ground; some types even germinate without needing to be covered: raiponce, purslane, etc. All you need to do is lightly plumb the soil, spread a good mulch and water often if the weather is hot. Medium-sized seeds such as spinach, carrots and radishes should be sufficiently covered, while beans, peas and broad beans should be covered more thoroughly. Any seed should be covered with twice its volume. Light, loose soils are generally suitable for all kinds of seeds at almost any time, because their porosity makes them more suitable than others for germination and subsequent emergence. In strong soils, many seeds would not germinate, or would only germinate with difficulty, if they were not covered with loose soil or light loam. For certain seedlings sown in hot weather, if the soil in which they are to be planted is dry, it is a good idea to soak the seed in water for a few hours, to hasten germination. Beans and radishes lend themselves well to this system. We would point out, however, that many procedures have been advocated, none of which has produced good results. Let nature take care of itself, and we'll be better off. It is preferable to sow a little thickly, as it is easy to thin out afterwards, whereas if we do the opposite, it is not always easy to resow or transplant. II. broadcast sowing. - Broadcast sowing is the most commonly used method; most of our vegetable plants are perfectly suited to it. When this type of sowing is done in a plot of land divided into beds, only half is sown at a time. You take a handful of seeds with your right hand, and, with a rapid series of wrist movements in a semicircle, you give the seed free passage by squeezing or spreading your fingers more or less. You follow it with your eyes as it falls, to distribute it evenly. 2. Do the same on the other half of the board, avoiding spreading the seed over the part already sown, and in the path. On large plots, broadcast sowing is done differently. You take a handful of seeds and, by moving your arm back and forth in a slight semicircle, spread them over the soil; in large-scale cultivation, this is how it's done. When broadcasting fine seed, many gardeners mix it with fine sand, wood ash or potting soil to distribute it more evenly. A good sower does not need to resort to this expedient (except for raiponce), and we can affirm that we have always sown our seeds as they were presented. III. SOWING IN RAYS OR ROWS. - Once the soil has been loosened and evened out, trace out the necessary number of parallel rows, spaced out according to the development required by the plants you wish to sow. The depth of these rows varies between 4 and 6 centimetres. The seed is placed at the bottom of the comb, with the hand raised 15 centimetres above it; the wrist is moved back and forth slightly in the direction of the combs. The seed is then covered by folding back the edges with the rake. When sowing in rows and beds, it is customary not to fold over the seed, but to leave the outer edges of the two end spokes to form a bead, so that this bead, serving as a frame for the bed, arrests its shape and retains water for watering. In strong soils, it is sometimes difficult to cover ray seedlings well, so potting soils or sifted earth are used, which are distributed as evenly as possible in the ray; straw, if necessary, and water. To draw the lines for the seedlings, use the narrow end of the hoe, a pointed piece of wood, a tracing tool, etc. Some growers in the North, when sowing fine seeds in rows, use their feet to draw two rays at a time, spacing them more or less according to the distance between each ray. This procedure is very quick, but can only be used on light, recently ploughed land. IV. SOWING IN CLUMPS OR BUNCHES. - To sow in clumps, deep rays can be traced as for row sowing, and sown at the desired distance and in a chessboard pattern; they are covered by folding back one of the edges of the ray; the hoe, planter or hand can also be used if the soil is light. The most common method, however, is to dig holes with the hoe at equal intervals, more or less deep, depending on what you want to sow; you cover by rejecting some of the soil, and at the first hoeing, when the plants are a little larger, you finish levelling the soil. This method is particularly suitable for planting potatoes or any other large seed. In some central and western regions, beans are sown in this way, as a counter-plant to other crops. V. DIFFERENT WAYS OF COVERING SEEDLINGS. - There are several ways to cover field seedlings. In many fields, well-applied harrowing with a fork is sufficient for most small-volume seeds. A rake is also used, with the tines pressed lightly and quickly into the soil, then passed over the surface to smooth it out. This is the system that predominates in France and gives good results in light soils. After sowing, lightly harrow the soil with a fork, and plow if necessary; then, using a full or forked hoe, remove a certain amount of soil from the paths, throwing it right and left onto each half of the bed; distribute it as evenly as possible, pass the rake over it, then mulch if necessary. When sowing fine seeds in compacted soils, fine potting soils or sieved soils are used to cover them, bearing in mind that the seeds need to be buried to a greater or lesser extent depending on their volume. It will be objected that this procedure is not practical over large areas; this is true, but we are addressing amateurs, who often have vegetable gardens in poor soil. A market gardener, who makes a living of it, will not choose a clay soil to cultivate it. Gardeners in the north of France use a lot of potting soil, and most of their crops are grown in terra-cotta; this is a good method that can be applied to all soils. In the Midi, terreautage is not widely practiced; it is hardly used at all: gardeners say they are wasting their time; this is a big mistake, because in the opposite case, they would obtain better results, despite the fact that the mild climate favors cultivation. VI. SOWING ON BEDS. - Many of our vegetable plants, which provide us with their produce and ripen their seeds in a relatively short time, would yield little or nothing if we waited until the soil was sufficiently warm to sow them. By means of early sowing on warm beds, we are able to raise plants of a certain strength; we plant them when the soil is sufficiently warmed, a time which coincides with that when, without the help of beds, we would only be able to sow. Layer sowing differs little from full-earth sowing; it is done in rays or broadcast; lightly press down with the hand or bat, cover with light loam and water with a fine apple. Above all, it's important not to sow before the seedbed has had its first light. Despite all precautions, seedlings often wither; to avoid this, give them as much air as possible, in proportion to the strength of the young plants; keep the soil moderately moist, and shade delicate plants to avoid sunburn. CHAPTER VIII PLANTING - TRANSPLANTING I. TRANSPLANTING FROM THE GROUND TO THE NURSERY. - Transplanting into a nursery is very advantageous for many vegetable plants that cannot be sown in situ, such as cabbages, celery, etc.; it favors root emission and the plant's outward development; by transplanting, we obtain well-branched, perfectly formed individuals, whose recovery is assured when set in place. The plants to be transplanted should be chosen in their infancy, when the first two leaves are beginning to develop; if they are too old, they will not cope well with successive transplanting and will become less attractive. The soil in which transplanting is to take place must be recently ploughed and loosened. If transplanting takes place during hot weather, mulch lightly and only operate in the evening; if the heat is very intense, shade during the three or four days following transplanting. This work, while not difficult, does require care and attention. First of all, it must be carried out promptly, ensuring that the young plant is in perfect contact with the soil, without too much settling, and that the hole is properly filled underneath. Care must also be taken to ensure that the roots are not in any way turned back, as this would have a detrimental effect on recovery. II. PERMANENT PLANTING. - When a plant is sufficiently developed, whether it has been transplanted in the nursery or left on the bed, it must be planted permanently; this operation is carried out in the same way as above, with the difference that the distance between plants is calculated according to their own development. Plants are generally planted in a staggered pattern, and watered immediately after planting. In some dry, hard summer soils, a superficial wetting is done before transplanting, which prevents the soil from clogging the holes, and helps to speed up the work. When planting peppers, eggplants and melons in layers, keep the roots as clod-free as possible; dig the holes beforehand with a spade or hoe, and use a hoe to do the planting. Subjects planted in this way suffer little from this change. Planting with the hoe is preferable to any other method, but as it is less expeditious than with the planter, it is more commonly used. For summer planting, a dark or rainy day should be chosen; otherwise, planting should be done in the evening; never during the day, as the sun's rays would damage the plants and delay their recovery. III. DEPLANTATION. - Instead of pulling them out with force, at the risk of breaking almost all the rootlets, which slows down recovery, if the soil is a little hard, use a trowel, spade, hoe or fork; plunge the tool deep into the soil, lifting it up in large clods, then remove the young plants without breaking the roots. If the soil is too dry, water a few hours before uprooting. As far as possible, only pull a few plants at a time, to prevent them from wilting. IV. PLANT PREPARATION. - Many young plants, such as strawberries, leeks, chicory, celery, etc., need to be prepared before being put out to pasture, by cutting off about half the leaves and some of the roots. Chicory and escarole seem to grow more slowly in spring, if you take the precaution of sufficiently cutting the pivot. This preparation is useful, as it promotes balance between the above-ground and below-ground parts, i.e. between the stem and the root. As a general rule, all plants to be transplanted should have their pivots cut to improve their future performance. Parsley, for example, which has hitherto been regarded as a plant to be sown in situ, produces magnificent results when transplanted with the poppy cut off first. V. LAYERED TRANSPLANTING. - Transplanting in layers differs significantly from transplanting in the ground. Tomatoes, peppers and eggplants, which are sown early and very thickly over a relatively small area, must be transplanted when the first leaves begin to appear; the bed must be suitably prepared and filled with good potting soil; once transplanting is complete, the plants are watered and covered with frames, which are kept closed for a few days to facilitate straightening. Air is then given, ensuring that the young plants do not wither, and that there is no overabundance of humidity; shade is applied if necessary. CHAPTER IX WATERING I. WATERING SYSTEM. - Watering being the active agent of market gardening, having water nearby and in abundance is an important advantage, especially in soils prone to drought, especially as it is during hot weather that vegetables are most valuable; the most advantageous means of obtaining water should therefore be studied. In certain soils where water is found at average depths, it is customary to dig wells, as far as possible in the highest part; a pump is installed, and then, at each corner of the square, iron-rimmed barrels are buried, 25 or 30 centimeters above ground level; oil or petroleum barrels are generally used. In the south-west and south of France, cement sump pits are used, either made on site or ready-made by certain manufacturers. Water is channeled through underground pipes or cement, wood, cast-iron or zinc slabs, passing from one barrel to the next, all the way to the far end of the garden. Although this method is not very costly, it does require continuous and arduous work for the worker in charge of operating the pump. It is preferable to set up a merry-go-round that can be driven by a horse for the duration of the watering operation. Today, there are few market gardens that are not equipped with a merry-go-round and even larger or smaller collecting basins, depending on the surface area to be watered. With these, watering is quick and easy. If they are high enough, you can use a lance, a watering can and even drip irrigation. II. DIFFERENT QUALITIES OF WATER. - All water does not have the same qualities; it is more or less suitable for watering, depending on the substances it contains, and the substances that make up the soil it passes through. Well water, if dug in limestone soils, contains carbonate of lime; it is considered the least good for watering, due to the salts it holds in solution, which form a crust on the surface that prevents plants from enjoying atmospheric influences, and hinders vegetation. To be beneficial, all well water must be exposed to the air for a few hours before use. Water that is brought to the surface by pumps, or any other means that stirs it sufficiently, can be used immediately, because its passage through the various pipes warms it up to some extent. Running water from streams, rivers, channels, etc., is the best for watering; it is all the better if its point of origin is further away, and especially if it is beaten by the waterwheels of factories. These waters, which are aerated and warm, are generally suitable for watering all vegetable plants. Rainwater is recognized as the best and purest, and can be used for a variety of watering purposes, provided it has not been left to stagnate for too long. The stagnant waters of ponds, cisterns, fish ponds, etc., with little or no renewal, become, because of the innumerable substances that putrefy in them, veritable liquid fertilizers that must be used with circumspection, otherwise the plants watered will perish: because of the fatty matter they contain, they prevent the oxygen in the air from penetrating the roots of the plants. III. EpOquE OF WATERING. - It is hardly possible to determine the right time and circumstances for watering each type of plant. The nature of the soil, altitude, exposure, atmospheric disturbances, etc., are all causes which make it impossible to specify the right time. Watering in the open ground begins in April and does not end until September. Watering from April to the end of May and in autumn should be carried out mainly in the morning, or even during the day. At this time of year, when the nights are still cool, evening watering would keep the plants at a temperature that is too cold in relation to the heat of the day, and would slow down their growth. On the contrary, from the end of May until September, watering should preferably be done in the evening or early morning, never during the day, because of the high heat. If it's morning, the plants will benefit from watering all day long. If you water in the evening, evaporation or absorption will no longer take place as it does under the drying rays of the sun, and the roots will be able to draw assimilable principles from the water they have been given; the opposite happens if you water during very hot weather: evaporation takes place so quickly that it cannot benefit the plants. IV. DIFFERENT WAYS OF WATERING. - Not all plants need to be watered in the same way. Some should only be watered with great care, especially young seedlings or newly planted transplants. They are watered with the apple, the holes in which are more or less fine, depending on the strength of the plants. Often, too much watering will bed them down and mix them together, making it difficult for them to stand up again. Seedlings planted in beds or terra-cotta flowerbeds should be watered with apples with fine holes, as over-watering scatters the soil, burying some seeds too deeply and uncovering others, so that emergence is uneven and defective. When watering carrot, salsify and turnip seedlings in the open ground, the surface of the soil can sometimes become so hard during hot spells that one watering is not enough to moisten it all the way down to the roots. In this case, a light drenching is used to moisten the surface of the soil and prepare it for a second watering. If, despite this, the soil is not sufficiently soaked, water a third, even a fourth time, if necessary. In dry summer soils, water the surface before planting, then water each plant immediately and give it a good general soaking. For plants that are far away and already strong, water them with the spout, taking care not to uproot them, pour the water gently and close to the base, and to prevent it spreading too much, make a small funnel-shaped basin around each subject. Watering with a hose is a good idea, as it provides plenty of water. However, in certain calcareous soils, the soil compacts and hardens quickly, and frequent hoeing is needed to loosen the surface. V. IRRIGATION. - Irrigation or imbibition is an excellent way of stimulating vegetation during the hot summer months, but it is not applicable to all soils. Most gardens in southern France, Spain and Portugal are irrigated. The land is divided into narrow plots, or furrows, separated by a gutter, and water is successively brought to each of them. When one plot seems sufficiently watered, it is diverted to the second, and so on. This is an excellent method that always gives excellent results, so we must not neglect to apply it whenever possible. In a market garden, the various waterings must be carried out with discernment and proportioned in the same way, so that each plant is watered at the right time. Watering should be more or less frequent, depending on the intensity of the heat, the soil and the size of the plant. CHAPTER X CROP ROTATION AND COUNTER-PLANTING I. ASSOLEMENTS. - In market gardening, rotation is one of the first keys to success; it always produces beautiful, magnificent produce. Assolating a plot of land means alternating crops and fertilizers, so that the same type of plant only comes back every three or four years in the same soil, because a plant placed several years in a row in the same square, even if the land is well tilled and smoked, cannot produce a good harvest if the type is exhausting. In this case, what happens is this: the first year, the harvest is perfect; the second, there will be a decrease in the product; the third, the subjects will be puny, stunted, woody, degenerate and bastardized. So it's important to alternate crops, with exhausting plants following non-exhausting ones. The crop rotation that seems to give the best results, and which we have always practiced, is the three-year rotation, with a layer in the first year. The squares in part 1 are given copious manuring: vegetables requiring good manuring are planted there; the other part is also fertilized with decomposed compost; the third part, which was copiously fertilized the previous year, receives only liquid fertilizers, liquid manure, sewage, etc. The second part is fertilized with a mixture of fertilizers and compost. Fertilization distributed in this way is not very expensive, and gives just as good results as fertilizing with large quantities of fertilizers that are poorly distributed. Proper crop rotation reduces the use of fertilizers without reducing the soil's fertility; on the contrary, it improves it. Not all plants require the same amount of fertilizer, and each kind of plant draws its own principles from the soil, so that what is not beneficial to one kind of plant is beneficial to another. Taprooted plants do not do well with recent fertilization, but if they are planted after crops that received copious fertilization the previous year (leeks), they do well and grow vigorously; despite this, mulching and topdressing must not be neglected. We will exclude from this category all poor soils recently under cultivation, as they do not yet have the fertility required to be sown, and it is only after a certain number of years of cultivation, with annual manuring and good fertilizers, that we can begin crop rotation. II. CROP INTENSITY, COUNTER-PLANTATION. - Crop intensity depends on crop rotation; it's thanks to this principle and to well-understood counter-planting that some market gardeners harvest an enormous quantity of produce from a relatively small area of land. Here are a few examples of intensive cultivation, or rather counter-planting. Let's assume a well-exposed bed. In the second half of January, you prepare the soil well, then sow white-tipped radishes, or others, and cover them carefully; you then plant lettuces, raised under a frame: you can even plant green romaine; you interplant a few early marjolin or early rose potatoes; you harvest first the radishes, then the lettuces, then the potatoes. - Towards the end of February, cauliflowers are counter-planted in the potatoes. Once the potatoes have been harvested, the cabbages are worked well and sown with radishes, chicory, spinach, sorrel, etc., harrowed with a fork. Under no circumstances should you counter-plant a plant that could harm the one you are growing. In a square of cauliflower or other summer species, sown very early on a good exposure or on a bed, and usually planted around March 20th, alternate with romaine or spring lettuces; before planting cabbages and romaine, radishes can be sown; radishes are harvested first, then romaine, then cabbages. As the cabbage harvest begins, we plough the open ground well, then sow spinach or chervil, even purslane. When the cabbage harvest is completely finished, chicory, escarole or leeks are planted in its place. You can also remove every third row of romaine or chicory from a first-season plot, plow it under and plant tomatoes. Once the romaine has been harvested, draw two rows on either side of the tomatoes, sow them with spinach or radishes, or plant summer lettuces, reserving a small path for watering. As the tomato harvest draws to a close, we spade the intervals, then plant long leeks, after sowing radishes. At the end of winter, before the leeks are fully developed, as they are always needed for blanching or for sale, we first pull up every third or fourth row and transplant bacalan cabbage. It's easy to vary crops and make the most of them, depending on the gardener's intelligence. But as good and advantageous as this process is when it is well directed, it becomes onerous if it is not well understood, as counter-planting varies greatly and undergoes numerous modifications, depending on the nature of the land and its altitudes; it is especially in light, fertile soils that counter-planting can be varied ad infinitum. We have noted some good fertile soils, notably in the Charente and Gironde regions, where cultivation is still routine. Most of these soils are in the hands of winegrowers or bad growers, who due to their lack of method, their bias to work only according to their own ideas, and their disregard for all good practices, are making a hard living where, with less routine, more intelligent and more scientifically sound cultivation, they could find relative ease. But what is certain is that, in this trade as in any other, you can't know if you haven't learned, and you only become skilled through years of persevering, sustained work, preceded by well-understood teaching. The best apprenticeship is practice under the watchful eye of capable parents or teachers. Brought up in gardens, in the midst of cultivation, you acquire a taste for it, you inherit intelligent traditions, and, broken in early to current practice, you find yourself in a position to advance it later, for market gardening is an art, a science that can only be acquired through persevering effort, intelligent study and consummate experience. III. PREJUDICE, ROUTINE. - Many farmers are unfortunately too prone to prejudice and routine, a tradition that in some regions is handed down from generation to generation, and always faithfully observed. For example, many market gardeners claim that, for certain seedlings to be successful, they must be sown on the day of or the day after a certain festival, follow the course of the moon, soak the seeds in rainwater or old wine, sow on an old moon so that the plants don't bolt, etc. All this is a mistake, an error, a mistake! Mistake, mistake, all these are prejudices, above which any sensible man must know how to rise. The lunar star has no influence whatsoever on success; what is required is that the soil be suitably prepared, then sow in good conditions, and you will always succeed. 8 The description we are about to give of each type of plant is the fruit of serious and reliable observations; almost all the varieties we mention have been cultivated, experimented and studied by us. We have adopted an alphabetical description, which is better than botanical classification by family, which can only be understood by those already familiar with the science. In addition to the origin of the plants, we describe their cultivation in the open ground, in its various forms and adapted to different climates; the early cultivation of the most common plants; how to harvest the seeds, their germination period; the diseases and animals harmful to each plant, and the means of destroying them; the uses and economic and nutritional properties of the plants. ASSINTHE Artemisia Absinthium L. Compositae. Native, perennial; multiplies from seed in spring or from splinters or clumps. Used as a seasoning and vermifuge. CULTIVATED GARLIC OR ORDINARY GARLIC (fig. 1). Allium sativum L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Linnaeus first gave Sicily as the homeland of Allium sativum, but later gave no origin. Several authors agree in denying Linné's assertions, but none of them gives a definite homeland for garlic. De Candollo claims that garlic was found spontaneously in the Kirghi desert of Soongarie in temperate western Asia. It has been cultivated for over 2,000 years. The English call it garlic, the Germans Knoblauch, the Basques baratchouria, the Bretons quinen, the Arabs thoum, the Chinese suan, the Spanish aïo, the Italian aglio, etc. CULTIVATION. - Cultivated garlic is one of the hardiest vegetable plants. FIG. 1: Common garlic. It can be grown in all types of soil and climate, with generally satisfactory success. The soils that seem best suited to its cultivation are lightly sandy open and marsh soils. Recent manuring with fresh manure seems to be contrary to its good cultivation, and arranges the bulbs to be greased in parts, i.e. instead of forming a rounded head at the top, the upper part of the cloves spreads out, bursting the film that covers them and no longer presenting any regularity in shape. Garlic of this nature, although losing none of its qualities, is depreciated by consumers. Garlic grown for a long time in the same soil gradually diminishes in volume, producing only walnut-sized bulbs with a dozen or so cloves. From time to time, garlic needs to be planted in different types of soil, and the seed renewed. The best time to plant garlic is from October 20 to November 20; you can continue until March, but the last plantings only produce round bulbs, sometimes quite large, which are highly appreciated on the markets, but not very profitable for the gardener. To get an early crop of green garlic, called aillet, you can start planting the cloves in September, and very thickly, 8 centimeters along the row and 15 centimeters between the rows; plant deep to get plenty of white. For good garlic planting, the soil must first be well ploughed with a spade. Smoke the soil with a well-consumed fertilizer, then mark out 1.10-metre-wide planks, dividing them into five rays; make a path 40 centimetres wide, then plant, taking the clove between thumb and forefinger and pushing it 3 or 4 centimetres perpendicularly into the soil, then raking it over to finish covering it. In some regions, notably in the West, garlic is planted with a flat or forked hoe, known in the country as a pic. The cloves are placed a few centimetres above the bottom of the ray; they are pushed in slightly to hold them in place; as the second ray is formed, the first is covered, and so on. In the seventh, no pods are used to form the pathway, but York sprouts, escarole, lettuce, etc. are planted. Before planting, all the small pods are set aside and planted separately, either for household use or for green sales; they are then placed at a short distance from each other and planted a little deeper to ensure plenty of white; they can even be buttered. When the plants have reached a height of 10 or 12 centimetres, the first light hoeing is carried out; a month later, a second, slightly deeper hoeing is carried out; then, around mid-April or in the first days of May, a third hoeing is carried out, but deep enough to plant lettuces, romaine and cabbages between each row of garlic. You can also sow beans, radishes, turnips, etc., or any other plants whose roots don't go deep into the soil. When the tops begin to change color, it is customary in some parts of the south-west to tie them in knots, as this is said to make the heads grow larger. This operation, which is nothing more than an old routine, produces no effect, and in no way increases the size of the bulbs. In our western and south-western regions, this is usually done in the second half of June and the first few days of July. Choose a nice, warm day. In light soils, weeding can be carried out fairly easily by hand, by pulling on the tops; but in strong, compact soils, we use the hoe or a small hoe. The bulbs are spread out as evenly as possible in the alleys or along the edges of the squares; they are arranged in equal rows; they are left like this for a few days, then turned over in the opposite direction. After this reshuffling, one day is enough to finish ripening the bulbs, at which point they need to be brought in. This work should be done in the morning, as the cool of the night has moistened the leaves and there is less risk of breaking them. Garlic is spread out in an attic or under a shed; you can even make uneven bunches, tying them two by two and hanging them in this state. As they ripen and dry, they are cleaned, counted and woven into bunches for sale on bad weather days. VARIETIES. - There are two perfectly distinct varieties of ordinary garlic. Early pink or black garlic. - The cloves are broad, thick, slightly elongated, obtuse and well arched; the skin enveloping the flesh is pale pink, sometimes grayish; this variety is more resistant to humidity than the following one, and is suitable for marshes where it reaches large dimensions. It is the most widely planted, productive and highly appreciated variety. Late white garlic. - A very valuable variety because of its slow growth rate.

can be stored for twelve to thirteen months. The pods are elongated, pointed, slightly curved and rarely very large; the tops are more elongated and spindly than in the previous variety, and the leaves more numerous; this is an excellent species. SEEDS. - Ordinary garlic has never produced seeds in our climate. DISEASES, PESTS. - Few diseases attack garlic. Sometimes, however, due to fertilizers and the nature of the soil, some heads rot and are affected by Rhizoctonia alluï. There's nothing you can do about this disease, which is due to the development of a parasitic fungus that starts at the base of the cloves, between the skins, in the form of numerous small white filaments, which then decompose them, giving off a putrid odor. Sometimes the leaves are affected by uredo or rust, but this disease is harmless. No animal attacks garlic, apart from slugs and snails which, when very young, will devour a few plants, but rarely. USES. - In cooking, garlic is used to enhance the flavor of dishes, making it an energetic condiment. In the south of France, people eat it raw with bread, drizzling it with oil, and consume a great deal of it. In the North, on the other hand, they use it sparingly. It is preserved in vinegar, like gherkins. Crushed and mixed with oil, egg yolk, salt and pepper, it makes up the aïoli of the Marseillais and is used to mask cold meats. Daily use of raw garlic, because of its pungent, acrid flavor, can cause stomach illness. Its highly viscous juice is used as a lut to glue pieces of broken porcelain. SPANISH GARLIC, ROCAMBOLE Allium Scorodoprasum Lin. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - It is not known exactly where the plant grows spontaneously: it is found around Paris, according to Cosson and Germain; in eastern France, according to Grenier and Godron; in the Alpes-Maritimes, according to Messrs Burnat and Ledebourt. It also grows in Russia, from Finland to the Crimea. It is not spontaneous in Spain, as its name might suggest. CULTURE. - Rocambole is a perennial plant, which differs from common garlic in that its bulbs become larger and its stems rise each year, bearing at their tips a number of small bulblets in place of seeds; for propagation, the use of cloves is preferable and quicker. Cultivation of this variety is the same as that of common garlic, with the difference that it is preferable to plant in January or the first fortnight of February, either for bulbils or cloves. If you don't want to harvest bulblets, pinch off the stems as soon as they appear. SEEDS. - Purplish flowers change into bulblets, which take the place of seeds. When the stems supporting them start to yellow, it's time to collect them. They are spread out in a well-ventilated, dry place, and when they appear sufficiently ripe, they are collected in boxes; they keep for a year. DISEASES, PESTS. - This variety suffers from the same diseases as ordinary garlic; insects have little or no effect. USES. - Although less pungent than common garlic, its uses are the same. ORIENTAL GARLIC Allium Ampeloprasum L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Oriental garlic is considered a variety of wild leek. In fact, this plant bears many similarities to the wild leek: same growth habit, fuller in all its parts, same more pronounced flavor with a slight garlic taste. This flavor is somewhat repugnant, which is probably why it is rarely cultivated. CULTIVATION. - Oriental garlic can be propagated either by sowing its seeds in January and February in good exposure: when the young plants have reached a suitable size, they are planted and treated like leeks; or by planting its cloves in autumn, in November, and treating them like common garlic. To keep the heads looking good, it's best to pull them out only every two years. SEEDS. - This variety goes to seed every year, and good seed is obtained from two-year-old plants. It keeps well for three years, but we recommend sowing current-year seed if possible. DISEASES, PESTS. - Like leeks, Oriental garlic is sometimes affected by a worm that gnaws at the heart. There is only one remedy for this: cut off the stems below the insect's ravages, and bury the cut parts far away. Rust or uredo sometimes affects it, but this disease is not dangerous. USES. - The cloves can be used like common garlic; the green tops or stems, like leeks. ALKEKENGE, COMESTIBLE PEQUER Physalis philadelphica Lamk; Physalis chenopodiifolia Lam; Physalis atriplicifolia Jacq; Physalis violacea Carr; Phy- salis edulis Sims. Solanaceae. ORIGIN. - According to M. Bois, it was reintroduced in 1873 or 1874 by M. Balarce, then Minister Plenipotentiary of the Argentine Confederation in Paris, who sent seeds to the Société d'Acclimatation under the name of petite tomate du Mexique. This plant was studied by M. Bossin, who gave it the name Physalis edulis, already applied to another species. In 1882, M. Carrière found it, without any indication of origin, in the garden of the Société d'Horticulture d'Étampes, and named it Physalis violacea. GROWING. - Sow in March, under a warm frame, in layers. The seed is lightly covered and sprouts in a few days. Once the seedlings have developed their first leaves, transplant them to a warm bed, to help them branch out and grow hair. In May, we pull them up with their rootballs and set them at 0.60 in all directions, preferably in recently ploughed and copiously smoked soil. When the plants have grown sufficiently, a stake is placed at each foot, in order to attach and support the numerous branches, which can reach a height of 1 metre or more. A heavy mulch is laid around the plants, and they are watered copiously throughout the summer, hoeing deeply and often, especially at the beginning.
