When the stems have reached the desired development, they are pinched off. large quantity of small, smooth, round fruits (fig. 2), greenish at first, then light yellow when ripe. These fruits, covered by the calyx, which is purplish next to the stalk, must be picked as soon4. as they come into contact with it, otherwise the calyx will burst at the top and, due to the weakness of the stalk, the fruit will become heavy when it reaches this size, detach easily and fall at the slightest shock. It is therefore important to look after them every day, so that they can be picked fresh and in good condition. SEEDS. - To harvest the seeds, select a few beautiful, ripe fruits, place them in a warm place and, when they begin to rot, take out the seeds, wash them and dry them in the shade. They keep for eight years. We know from experience that, when this plant has been cultivated in a garden, it is rare to lose the species; there are always a few fruits that fall and whose seeds rise the following year. DISEASES, PESTS. - We know of no particular diseases that attack this plant. Slugs and snails devour it when it is young. USES. - In France, the various varieties of cockerel are not much appreciated; only in the Midi region are they eaten raw. In Spain, Germany and Switzerland, however, they can be found on the best tables. Pickled in vinegar, like gherkins, they can replace them; in fact, this is the main use we had for them in the public establishment where we ran the cultivation for a few years; the patients were very fond of these fruits, and most of them preferred them to other fruits pickled in vinegar. You can also make a very good jam by choosing absolutely ripe fruit and adding sugar of equal weight, lemon, orange peel, vanilla, etc.; reduce to three-quarters. ALKEKENGE OFFICINAL, COQUERET OFFICINAL Physalis officinalis Mœnch. Native perennial plant, found spontaneously in most of France. We found it in abundance, especially in the vineyards around Cognac (Charente); in some places, the soil was covered with it. The farmers of Charente do not like this plant and destroy it without mercy; they call it coqurou. USES. - We have known several connoisseurs who cultivate the officinal cockle for the beauty of its fruits, which are cherry-red when ripe; these fruits, which are eaten in certain regions, are generally known by the names of "cerise de Juif", "cerise de Mahon", "cerise d'hiver", "mirabelle de Corse", "coccigrolle", "baguenaude", "herbe à cloque", "physalide", etc., etc... But this species is most useful as a medicinal plant. The leaves, flowers and roots are aperitive; the fruits are diuretic. In some parts of France, the fruit is used to color butter red. ANETH A nethum graveolens L. Umbelliferae. Annual plant of southern Europe. Sow outdoors in spring. Seeds are used to flavor pickles and other preserves; leaves are used like fennel. ANGELICA OFFICINALE (fig. 3). Angelica Archangelica Lin. Archangelica officinalis Hoffmann. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Native to the Alps, where it grows at low altitudes, particularly in damp locations. FIG. 3 - Angelica officinalis. CULTIVATION - A perennial, or rather a triennial in our cultivation, angelica thrives best in deep, cool soil, fertilized with good, strong fertilizers. Sow from the second half of March to July, in nurseries, in racks. Cover the seed sufficiently with loose soil or compost. Water copiously when necessary, thin out, hoe and weed. In autumn or the following spring, plant them in rows 10 cm apart in all directions. The tips of the leaves are trimmed back slightly, without damaging the roots. Water immediately after planting; a few days later, hoe deeply, as much as possible after a good rain. You can start harvesting leaves as early as the second year. In the third year, the plant goes to seed, and continues to do so every year. For this reason, it is customary to destroy plantations that have reached this age; it is claimed, with good reason, that the leaves develop much less when the plant goes to seed. A plantation of angelica can last ten years or more, and we know of some that have outlived this age, and which nevertheless, with care, produce very fine products. Angelica is grown mainly around Niort (Deux-Sèvres) and near Clermont-Ferrand, in Auvergne, as a large export crop; it is also grown extensively in Bohemia. In Auvergne, growers usually plant 15,000 plants per hectare, and harvest 11 to 12,000 kilos of fresh stems, plus the roots, which can be dried, and even the leaves, which keep well once dried. It's a very profitable product, provided you have good, deep, moist soil that can be submerged if necessary. VARIETY. - Angelica has not produced any sub-varieties as such. The following varieties form distinct races that are grown in Europe only in botanical gardens. Angelica atro-purpurea Lin. - From America, where it replaces our Angelica officinalis, especially in the United States, where it is highly esteemed. Angelica lucida Lin. - Also from America, where it is much sought-after, especially in Canada. Same properties as Angelica officinalis. SEEDS. - The plant does not go to seed until the third year. As they detach easily from their receptacle, it's a good idea to collect the umbels as they turn yellow, and lay them out on cloths in the shade to dry. They keep for one to two years. DISEASES, PESTS. - There are few diseases that attack angelica; sometimes uredo or rust claims a few victims. Snails and slugs attack plants when they are young, but they don't seem to be too fond of them. Aphids, on the other hand, are more terrible, attacking the flowering stems as they are born and leaving them only when they die, i.e. when they are completely dry. The only way to combat them is to spray them often with nicotine reduced to 3 or 4 degrees. USES. - The highly aromatic angelica seed is used by confectioners to make highly prized liqueurs. Stalks and stems preserved in sugar make highly sought-after preserves. We even make a spirituous liqueur known as angelica ratafia. Here's how it's made: fresh angelica stems and bitter almonds are macerated in brandy, mixed with sugar syrup and filtered after a few hours' rest. It is said that Lapps eat the leaves, root and seeds. The stems are used to make an excellent cream, which is prepared as follows: 250 grams of fresh stems are cut into small pieces and infused for six weeks in 3 liters of brandy with 4 grams of cinnamon, 2 cloves, 6 grams of nutmeg and 1 kilogram of sugar melted in three-quarters of a liter of water. This liqueur is excellent for the stomach, and stimulates digestion. Stems also make very good jam: cut to length, throw into almost boiling water, remove from heat and leave to infuse for an hour, then remove the epidermis, i.e. the first skin. Then boil them until they are white and tender, let them drain, and throw them into a boiling sugar syrup two or three times. Leave to cool, then boil the syrup again the next day, add the stems and leave for twelve hours, remove and dry. We obtain 1 kilogram of candied stems with 185,500 of fresh stems. These candied stems are used extensively in pastry-making, and especially in making gingerbread. As for real angelica liqueur, here's the formula: Angelica seeds... ... ... ... . 30 grams Fresh angelica stems ... ... ... . 30 - Bitter almonds, blanched and crushed ... . 50 - White sugar ... ... ... . Alcohol 600... . Water ... ... ... ... . 1kg,500 ... . 6 liters ... . 1 liter. The root, which is the most active part, is used in many pharmaceutical preparations, notably in angelica wine. ANISE Pimpinella Anisum L. Umbelliferae. Oriental annual. Sow in spring in a well-placed spot. Seeds are used as a vermifuge and as a condiment in making liqueurs, sweets, etc. ARMOISE Artemisia vulgaris L. Compositae. Native perennial. Sows in spring in nurseries. It can also be propagated by separating clumps. Leaves are sometimes used as a condiment or decoction. GARDEN ARROW Atriplex hortensis L. Chenopodea. ORIGIN. - Annual, native to northern Europe and Siberia. Cultivated for less than two thousand years, it has become naturalized in Europe and in some parts of France. It grows abundantly in gardens and fields, mainly in topsoil, rubble and embankments, where it sometimes reaches considerable growth. In France, it is known by several names, the best known being: arrode, arronse, belle-dame, bonne-dame, érode, follette, irible, prude-femme, glorieuse, rouble, armol, etc. E. BERGER, Vegetable plants. CULTURE. - Garden arroche is not difficult to grow in the right soil, and succeeds everywhere, provided fertilizer is plentiful and the soil is sufficiently loosened. It is best sown in place, from March to August, in shallow rays 30 to 40 centimetres apart; sow lightly and cover lightly; and, if sowing during the heat of summer, mulch and water abundantly. When the young plant has developed its first three or four leaves, thin out and transplant if necessary, then give the first light hoeing; this operation should be repeated often, as should weeding. As arrowroot sets seed quickly, it's a good idea to sow often, at least every twenty days, so as not to run out. This plant freezes easily, and the first frosts destroy it; green arrowroot is the most resistant. VARIETIES. - There are four main varieties of edible arrowroot: Green arrowroot (Atriplex hortensis). - Typical species with dark-green, thick, fat, rounded foliage and strong, angular stems; very vigorous plant, not widely cultivated; the blond variety is preferable. Arroche blonde (Atriplex hortensis lutea). - The best and most commonly cultivated, and generally much appreciated; it's just a variety of the previous one; yellow-green leaves, very fleshy and fat. Red arrowroot (Atriplex rubra). - Stem and leaves are a beautiful carmine red. This color disappears with cooking; however, to remove it, it is a good idea to boil it in a first water. Less prized than the blond variety. Dark red arrowroot (Atriplex ruberrima). - Stems and leaves are a beautiful bright carmine red. This variety is rarely grown in the kitchen garden. It's best thought of as an ornamental plant, producing a very attractive effect when grouped in clumps. When cultivated for seed, these varieties should be kept far apart, as cross-fertilization is rapid and colors fade. SEEDS. - First-season seedlings ripen their seeds within the year. These seeds detach from the plant with extreme ease, and the slightest wind blows them away. To avoid this inconvenience, the uprights should be collected before the seeds are fully ripe. Once dry, the seeds are detached by beating them with a small flexible stick, or better still, by passing the stems between the two hands. They keep well for five or six years. Each gram contains around 250 seeds. DISEASES. ANIMAL PESTS. - Green and sometimes blond arrowroot sometimes suffer from a disease similar to powdery mildew, which develops on rose leaves. Affected leaves are stopped in their growth, curled, curled up, twisted, and the plant becomes stunted and no longer grows. This disease seems to develop on suffering plants, but is fortunately quite rare. Affected plants should be uprooted as soon as possible, and disposed of or buried. There is perhaps no vegetable plant, especially when young, that slugs and snails are more fond of, even devouring the root. There are even soils where it is hardly possible to grow it. These animals must be destroyed by all known means. USES. - Arrowroot is used in cooking to make herb soups. It is used in the same way as spinach, and many people prefer it to spinach. It is mixed with sorrel to correct its acidity. ARTICHAUT Cynara Scolymus Lin. Compounds. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant. According to De Candolle, it comes from the Cardoon (Cynara Cardunculus) found in southern Europe, northern Africa, the Canary Islands and Madeira. Moreover, he says, the artichoke has not been found outside gardens, and as the Mediterranean region, home of all Cynaras, has been thoroughly explored, it can be asserted that it exists nowhere spontaneously. CULTIVATION. - Artichokes thrive best in deep, well-smoked, slightly damp soil, and liquid fertilizers are ideal. The latter is less common and less practical than the former, as the plants that emerge from it rarely possess the qualities of the varieties from which they came: degeneration occurs easily; however, in a sowing of a certain size, there are plants equivalent to the seed-bearing type. This means of propagation is only useful for obtaining new varieties, or for countries where eyelets cannot be transported. Sow in February and March in pots, under frames, on a warm bed; or in April or May, in the field. Small holes 20 to 30 centimetres wide are prepared, filled with half-rotted manure and packed well. Cover with good loose soil, then sow five or six seeds per hole. Water immediately to facilitate germination, and when the young plants are strong enough, thin out, leaving three in each hole, hoe FIG.74. - Artichoke. and water. Seedlings, sown in pots, are planted under the same conditions. Whether sown in the field, in pots or in nurseries, many plants begin to bear fruit in August or September; you can then make a judicious choice of the best varieties by pulling out all the inferior ones; it is only in the second year, however, that it is easier to judge the varieties you have obtained; those you keep will then be multiplied by eyeletting. 4. Carnation is usually carried out towards the end of March, April or May, depending on the climate, after the old plants have been dethatched, i.e. cleared of the clods of earth or straw that surrounded them to protect them from frost. All the smallest carnations are removed, leaving only three or four of the finest for production. The best carnations that are removed and destined for new plantings must be carefully detached from the stump. Before planting, the cut is cooled a little with a knife, and the tips of the leaves are trimmed. The carnation is planted immediately, and a certain number of plants are set aside in the nursery to replace the missing ones in July and August. An old routine practiced by many market gardeners is to replant in September the base of stems that have ceased to produce, in other words, the old cores. They are planted in situ, or better still in a nursery, to be replanted the following spring. In this way, you can obtain vigorous plants that are sure to grow again, but care must be taken when detaching the stems to retain a good portion of the stump. This is a good procedure to imitate. Whatever the propagation method, the land to be planted must be recently ploughed deeply, and smoked copiously. Plant with a chalk line or forked hoe at 90 centimetres or 1 metre in all directions, and set two or three eyelets together at 10 centimetres in the shape of a triangle. Firmly pack the soil around the roots and water throughout the summer, weeding and hoeing frequently. In a market garden, artichokes are rarely planted alone; cabbages, romaine and even white onions are intercropped. When the old plants have yielded their first crop in the second half of July, cut off the stems and remove the dry leaves. Then water copiously, or irrigate if possible. This way, you'll still have a half-harvest in autumn. After this last harvest, the stems that have produced are also removed and, in November, they are worked into a strong cone of earth around each tuft, compacted well with the back of the spade. Some authors recommend surrounding them with straw or manure, an excellent system but impractical for large quantities. This method is only practical for the north, west, east and part of central France; the south-west from Bor- deaux and the south don't need this precaution, artichokes rarely freeze in these regions, although the winter of 1890-91 destroyed some plantations. In the last few days of March or the beginning of April, if the weather looks like it's going to be fine, we start, smoke and plow with a spade to bury the manure well. We then proceed with carnationing as described above. For an artichoke plantation to produce good yields, it must be renewed every four years, or five at the most. This operation must be carried out partially, so that production is staggered. This method, so well understood, is practiced by gardeners in the north of France, but not in the south-west, where, in some regions, artichoke plantations are kept for ten, twelve or even more years. So what happens? the produce becomes increasingly inferior in volume and, as a result, depreciated and less sold on our markets. To increase fruit size, we recommend splitting the stem in four immediately below the receptacle and inserting two pieces of wood or reed crosswise. This system is used by some growers in Perpignan, who obtain enormous artichokes in this way. VARIETIES. - At least forty varieties of artichoke are known, taking their names from the localities where they are most commonly grown. Among the best are: Artichaut gros vert de Laon. - Very large, fleshy, fertile, half-aged, scales spread at the top. This is the species most commonly grown around Paris. *Artichoke from Brittany. - Very large, excessively fleshy, white, very broad scales, very vigorous and productive plant, worthy of recommendation. Artichoke from Niort. - Very vigorous, very productive, seemingly derived from the previous variety, of which it has all the characteristics, widely grown in Deux-Sèvres, Poitou and part of Anjou. Artichaut vert de Provence. - Very early, medium-sized, very tender, not very fleshy, excellent for eating with pepper. The plant is more sensitive to cold than the preceding varieties; grown extensively in the Midi and Bordeaux regions, among the vines. Long purple artichoke or gray artichoke. - A fairly vigorous plant producing elongated, loose apples with loosely packed scales. Very early, this is the variety that is grown in large quantities in the Pyrénées-Orientales region and is shipped almost year-round to Paris. Purple artichoke. - Very early, small-headed, widespread in Charentes and Spain, very productive, excellent for eating raw. Artichoke from Macau (Gironde). - In our opinion, this is just a variety of the Camus de Bretagne, highly esteemed on the Bordeaux markets. Fairly large green, very tender, with tight, wide scales. The plant is vigorous and fairly cold-resistant. In the Médoc, at Macau, it is grown mainly among the vines. Saint Laud oblong artichoke. - Very large apples. Artichoke from Brittany. - Round heads, purple at first, then taking on a coppery hue, dwarf plant, fairly large, pointed scales. Roscoff artichoke. - Sweet Genoa artichoke. - Purple artichoke from Tuscany. - Widely cultivated around Florence and in Sicily. SEEDS. - To harvest the seed, let the first fruit rise, i.e. the one in the center, which is always more beautiful and better nourished than the others that follow. It should not be picked until it is ripe and almost dry. Seeds keep for six years. USES. - By blanching the base of the petioles in the same way as cardoons, they can be eaten in the same way as cardoons, as the flavor is much the same. When the friuts have reached half their size, they can be covered with a thick canvas, straw or moss bag, to keep them in the dark. After a few days, the fruit turns a bleached white and can be used for a variety of culinary purposes, with an exquisite taste. The receptacle is eaten either cooked or raw. The dried flowers, commonly known as hay or thistle, are used to curdle milk. As the plant is rich in tannin, it is used by buttermakers, who prefer to use the flower heads. Dyers sometimes use the leaves in a decoction. The leaves dye bismuth-treated wool golden yellow. Burnt stems yield a lot of potash. ASPARAGUS Asparagus officinalis Lin. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to southern Europe. Grows spontaneously in many parts of France, notably on the Rhône and Loire islands. Asparagus has been cultivated since time immemorial, and the Greeks considered it a delicacy. The Romans sought it out with a kind of passion, especially the Ravenna variety, three of which, according to Pliny, were enough to weigh a pound. It was La Quintinie, head gardener of the Versailles kitchen garden under Louis XIV, who devised a way of growing asparagus. CULTIVATION - Asparagus thrives best in sandy soils with a significant clay content, on a healthy, permeable subsoil free of stagnant moisture. Asparagus is propagated by sowing the seed from February 15 to April, either broadcast, in beds, or better still in rays, spaced Om,40 apart. The seeds are covered with Om,04 or Om,05 of loose earth or light loam, and usually emerge within fifteen to twenty days; a few weeks later, the young plants are thinned out. Hoe and weed several times a year, and water during hot spells. Young asparagus should be used for planting after a year's sowing, as it is at this age that they will suffer the least from transplanting, and will therefore be the most successful (fig. 5). Two-year-old claws are still very good. After this period, I would not recommend planting, as older asparagus claws do not recover well, and you have to come back several times to get a complete plantation. Care must be taken when pulling out the claws, so as not to damage the roots too much; to do this, use a fork or a forked hoe, but don't be afraid to open up a good trench. In many regions, growers have the bad habit of planting too deep, with the result that the produce obtained is always inferior in size and quantity. This is because this plant, like any other, thrives and succeeds best in soil that is sufficiently smoky and, above all, warmed by the sun; and it's not by digging pits 50 or 60 centimetres deep that these conditions are met. So what happens? The soil in which the claws are to grow, being new and never disturbed or amended, receives the sun's rays only indirectly or, to put it another way, never at all; the result is poor vegetation. Here's M. Vilmorin's opinion on the subject! Generally speaking, we can say that the most important point, and the one on which success mainly depends, is not to remove the asparagus claw from the influence of heat, and to place it at the same time in an environment where it can find the food it needs in abundance. The claw must therefore be planted at a shallow depth, and covered with a fairly thick layer of soil only during the bedding season, when it is absolutely necessary to obtain asparagus of sufficient length". There are no special rules for planting asparagus; it is done according to the layout of the land, either in isolated rows between the vine rows, as is often done in the Gironde region; in rows along the walls, or in squares, or in beds. As this last method is recognized as the most practical, and the most widely adopted, we will describe it below. In autumn or early winter, the land to be planted is deeply ploughed. 1 Vilmorin, Les Plantes potagères. When it's time to plant, i.e. in February and March, and not in November as we've sometimes seen, we start by marking the spot where each bed is to be planted. The 1-meter planks, intended to receive the claws, are dug 15 to 20 centimeters deep, no more; the soil is thrown back into the 1.50-meter space left free for the path. After working the bottom of the pits, spread a 5-centimeter layer of well-consumed manure or potting soil, dried river mud or any other fertilizer; tamp it down by walking on it, cover with 5 centimeters of loose soil and level it off. Then draw two lines 25 centimetres from the edges, or 50 centimetres apart, and plant the claws 90 centimetres along the line, in a chessboard pattern. It's important, when placing them, to form a small pyramid of soil under each one, to give the roots a chance to sink in and seat the claw. The young roots are compacted with the hand to make them adhere to the soil, then covered with 4 or 5 centimetres of loose soil; the top is levelled, as are the intervals of the beds used for planting romaine, cabbage, radishes, turnips, etc., but the time needed for them to develop must be calculated, so that the intervals are free at the time when the beds need to be loaded or unloaded, fertilizers distributed and so on. Other care for the claws in this first year consists of frequent weeding and hoeing. In November or December, the stems are cut to 13 or 15 centimetres above ground level, then the soil is removed to a few centimetres above the level of the claw; a small layer of manure is spread on E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 3 each bed with a small layer of fat manure or good potting soil, and leave to overwinter in this state. In March of the following year, a good ploughing is carefully carried out, so as not to damage the roots; a little fertilizer is added, and the whole is buried in the soil; a small thickness of earth is added. The plantation spends the summer in this state, receiving the same care as the first year: weeding, hoeing, mulching, if useful; during hot spells, we water any plants that appear to be suffering. However, the heat hardly tires them at all in the first year. In the third year, the same operations are carried out, and in the spring, the plants are mounded more firmly; about 25 centimetres of soil is spread over the whole bed; the surface is levelled, as well as the bottom of the gaps. You can then start to produce a good small harvest. But it's not until the fourth year that a general harvest can be made. Thereafter, the same care is taken with cultivation; each year, more ridging is done to obtain longer asparagus. As for fertilizing, once the soil has been sufficiently amended, fertilizing should only be carried out every two years; in this case, in the year when fertilizing is not carried out, watering should be done with liquid manure; this is a very good operation which activates vegetation enormously. Planting between vines, as is often done in the South-West, particularly in the Gironde region around Bazas, is done in the same way, with the only difference being that there is only one row of claws per joualle; other care is exactly the same. A well-managed asparagus plantation can last from fourteen to sixteen years, producing magnificent products. However, when we pull up old plantations, despite the best we can do, we must be careful not to plant them again; this would be a bad operation. It's when the shoots start to appear above ground that it's time to pick them. This operation must be carried out with care, so as not to damage the new shoots. A special tool in the shape of a gouge is used, which is slid as perpendicularly as possible alongside the asparagus to be cut; once it has reached a certain depth, it is cut off. Many growers claim that it's better to remove the asparagus by hand or with a wooden spatula, and to split it at the base. This is how many good growers in Argenteuil operate. FORCED CULTIVATION. - Asparagus can be forced from October to February, in the open ground or on warm beds. In the open ground, forcing can only give good results if the planting has been done with this intention. The claws should be much closer together, so that no ground is lost; 30 or 40 centimetres in all is sufficient. Two or three rows are planted per board, depending on the width of the trunks to be placed on them. You don't start forcing until the fourth year; to do this, you dig 50-centimeter-wide, 40-centimeter-deep ditches on each side of the board, which you fill with good, well-packed horse manure from the stable; as you dig, you throw some of the earth back onto the board, place the chests with their panels, and form a good stove around them, which you often have to reshuffle and add new manure each time. Planks that have been forced the first year cannot be forced the second; this would compromise the harvest and ruin the claws. The same bed should therefore only be forced every two years. Forced cultivation on a warm bed is easier, gives better results, and is quicker and less expensive. But you need to have plenty of claws at your disposal, which is easy if you sow a few rows every year. Three- to five-year-old seedlings are the best for this type of cultivation. Old clumps that you want to destroy still give a good harvest. At the same time as we have indicated, we prepare good layers 40 to 60 centimetres thick. Place the boxes on top, add a little manure on the inside, tamping it down with your feet, then cover with 5 or 7 centimetres of a good mixture, half vegetable garden soil, half potting soil. Cover with a frame, and leave in this state until the layer has burned down; one week is enough. The claws are then placed upright, leaning against each other, with the collar at the same height. The ends of roots that are too long are trimmed back a little, and the soil is slid in with a small wooden palette; then water lightly, and cover with 5 or 6 centimetres of compost or more, depending on whether you want long, white asparagus. Some growers cover with moss, wood bran, even sand, etc. Eight to twelve days after these operations, the shoots begin to appear; they are cut as they develop. Care should be taken with the layers, putting heaters on them when they cool, and covering them with mats during the day, depending on whether you want green or white asparagus. VARIETIES. - Several varieties are grown, taking their names from the localities where they are widely cultivated, but in reality there are only two main breeds: green asparagus and violet asparagus. All those known as Ulm, Besançon, Polish, Marchienne, Ghent, Vendôme, Dutch, Bazas, Argenteuil, etc. asparagus are descended from these two races; all these varieties are improved by cultivation, and certainly have a special and distinctive merit. But it's the same with this plant as with many others: one variety produces magnificent products in certain localities, and very poor ones in others; moreover, all plants from seed are subject to sometimes singular variations. The growers of Argenteuil, who have been cultivating this plant for a long time, have succeeded by successive selections in improving the purple asparagus in a remarkable way. The resulting varieties have long been recognized as the best. SEEDS. - To harvest asparagus seeds, we choose the best shoots, when fertilization has taken place and the ovaries containing the seeds are beginning to appear; we put a stake on them, so that the wind doesn't break them; then, when autumn comes, after the first frosts, we pick up the ovaries, which have taken on a beautiful minium red color; we crush them in water, the seeds precipitate to the bottom, and the skins swim to the surface; it's therefore easy to get rid of them. The seeds are then spread out on cloths or boards, and exposed to the sun for a while, or better still in a well-ventilated place, in a draught where they dry out completely. They are then packed in small canvas or paper bags. They keep well for two years, but it's always best to sow the previous year's seed. DISEASES, NUISSIBLE ANIMALS. - We know of no disease that attacks asparagus. Sometimes, however, asparagus claws rot in autumn; this is due to the plant's poor constitution or to overly damp soil. In damp autumn soils, we recommend not covering with manure at this time, to avoid this disease. Slugs and snails are very fond of young asparagus shoots, gnawing at them and sometimes digging holes in them. These animals must therefore be destroyed by all known means. Geoffroy's Crioceris rubra is the most terrible enemy of asparagus. It's a small red insect, speckled with small gray and white spots, known as the asparagus tiger. The beetle lays two eggs, one in spring and the other in June and July. After the female has been fertilized, she wanders around the stems, looking for a place to lay her eggs. Each egg emerges from the body, coated with a material that sticks to it. After a fortnight, these eggs hatch into a multitude of greenish, dirty larvae, which devour the softer parts of the asparagus. It is in the larval stage that the locust does most of its damage, mainly because of the large numbers involved. When you notice that a patch of asparagus has been invaded by this insect, you must try to destroy it. To do this, take an airtight container into which a little oil has been poured; place the container under the plant, shake the stalks lightly, and all the insects fall in; the oil prevents them from escaping, so they are crushed. White grubs are also a terrible enemy of asparagus in certain soils. Destroying it is difficult, as there is only one way to do it, and that is to crush it. All the means that have been advocated, such as lime ash, tobacco juice, sulfur or other ingredients, have never given good results. Moles do not destroy asparagus, but they do wreak havoc, digging underground galleries that often expose their claws. They can be easily destroyed by placing wooden traps in freshly dug galleries. USES. - In France, white asparagus is eaten just as it appears. In Portugal and Italy, asparagus is eaten only after it has been left to green up, i.e. when it is a dozen centimetres above the ground. Asparagus root is one of the five aperitive roots, often used as a diuretic. The root's active ingredient is asparagine. Young asparagus shoots, known as tips, provide two medicines: syrup and extract. In Provence, the stalks are used to filter wine coming out of the vat. By fermentation and distillation, asparagus berries yield a very pure alcohol that makes excellent table liqueur. AUBERGINE or MÉLONGÈNE Solanum Melongena Lin. Solanum esculentum Lun. Solanaceae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to India, known locally as Bringèle, Marignan, Mayenne, Béringène, Bréhème, Mélanzanne, Œuf végétal, Pon- deuse and in Gascon dialect as Biédaze or Viédaze, etc. CULTIVATION. - The eggplant is propagated from seed, which is sown on a bed from January to the end of March. Cover the seed with about a centimeter of light, well-sieved potting soil; water and cover with panels; you can even leave them in the dark while germination takes place, by placing straw mats over the panels. Germination usually takes eighty or ninety hours. From this point onwards, the mats are removed, the plants are shaded in case of sunburn, and air is gradually added to prevent the plant from over-extending itself. Around four weeks after sowing, the seedlings will have sufficiently developed their second leaf. They are transplanted onto a warm bed prepared in advance and filled with 10 centimetres of a good mix of potting soil and loam. Place them 4 or 5 centimetres apart. As soon as they've been transplanted, water them and keep them smothered for two or three days to help them take root. Air is then given whenever the temperature permits, and around April 20 the panels can be removed, at least in the South-West; in colder regions, it's better to wait until May, but plenty of air must be given. Eggplants do very well, preferably in warm locations. In the vegetable garden, choose the best exposed squares for planting. In a well-sheltered bed, along a wall, it succeeds perfectly, but needs copious watering in summer, otherwise only small fruits are obtained. Eggplants can only be planted in the first half of May. Before then, as the soil is not sufficiently warmed, the young plants suffer and remain without any vegetation. There are several ways to set up the plants. Some market gardeners prepare the ground in advance, sowing radishes, lettuces or other vegetables, and when it's time to plant the eggplants, they plant among the other crops. These plants are harvested before they can harm the eggplants. The best way to plant eggplants is to spade and smoke the soil, which is then divided into plots 1.10 m wide. Mark out three radii on each one, observing a path 40 to 50 centimetres wide, then plant in a chessboard pattern 40 to 45 centimetres along the line, with a hoe, keeping a good clod at each foot, or with a strong planter. Water immediately and, a few days later, spread a good mulch over all the beds. Other care consists of weeding, hoeing and, above all, copious watering during the summer; liquid fertilizers are particularly suitable for this plant. We cut back the lowest branches, leaving three or four of the best placed, which should be pinched back when they have two or three flowers each. This is the best way to prune them, as experience has shown. VARIETIES. - We know several varieties: Early dwarf purple eggplant. - Low-growing plant, rarely exceeding 30 to 35 centimetres in height, well-branched from the base; medium-sized fruit of a beautiful purple-black color, very early and of excellent quality. Very early purple eggplant. - A new variety, derived from the previous one, of which it has more or5. less the same characteristics; fruits are smaller and more plentiful; particularly suitable for early cultivation. Long purple eggplant (fig. 6). - Excellent variety FIG. 6 - Long violet eggplant. fairly early, producing beautiful, superb fruit, very productive, the most popular on the market. Round purple eggplant - Very old variety producing large, elongated fruit; late, but very productive. Péhin black eggplant. - Round, dark purple, almost black, as well as part of the stem; semi-early, very productive and of good quality. Eggplant monstrueuse de New-York. - Improved variety of the old round one, producing enormous fruit; late and of mediocre quality. There are several other varieties of eggplant, but these should be considered more as collector's items than as varieties to be cultivated for profit. Among the best are Eggplant panachée de la Guadeloupe. - Esteemed by Creoles; White Aubergine (Solanum ovigerum), or egg plant. - More of an ornamental plant, it is said to be very unhealthy. Yellow eggplant; green eggplant, etc. SEEDS. - Sometimes, when the year is rainy in autumn, eggplant has difficulty ripening its fruit. So, when you want to harvest the seed, choose the best fruit early and let it ripen until the first frost. Then pick it, and when it starts to rot, take out the seed, wash it and dry it in the shade. It keeps well for four years. According to Vilmorin, one gram contains around 250 seeds. pests, harmful animals. - Sometimes, a few plants wither and desiccate almost suddenly: this is blanc or meunier, a cryptogamic disease, well known to market gardeners, which attacks the roots and destroys the plant; no effective remedy has been found. Sometimes, uredo attacks the leaves and fruit and, without destroying the plantation, does great harm to the plant by stopping its development. Well-dosed Bordeaux mixture is an excellent remedy. Slugs and snails are very fond of young fruit, which they partly devour by forming bizarre arabesques on the skin, which they seem to prefer to the flesh. They must be hunted down regularly to destroy them. USES. - Eggplant is a laxative and not very nourishing food. It should only be used when the fruit is perfectly ripe, otherwise it may cause discomfort through its pungency, due to the large amount of solanine it contains. In southern countries, eggplants are cooked on the grill over a low heat, after being cut into slices. They are then gradually soaked in oil or fresh butter, with a little salt and pepper, garlic and a few fine herbs. It can also be baked in the oven between two courses, making it an excellent dish. In our part of the world, it's eaten pan-fried like porcini mushrooms, with the same aftertaste, as a stew, in a white sauce, and so on. A few hours before cooking, we recommend sprinkling it with salt and pepper to draw out its water. It can also be dried: cut into thin slices, remove the skin and the spongy part. Throw it into boiling water for a few minutes, then remove it and lay it out on racks or linen. Dry in the sun or in an oven, and when the pieces are completely dry, store them in a healthy place. To use, fry in lukewarm water. Young fruit can be pickled in vinegar, like gherkins. AURONE Artemisia Abrotanum L. Compositae. Perennial, frutescent. Reproduces from seeds sown in April-May or from cuttings, which grow well in spring; for this, care must be taken to bring in old plants in autumn; the plant is not resistant to northern winters. Used like wormwood. BASELLE Basella. Chenopodea. ORIGIN. - East Indies. In America, where it is widely cultivated, it is known as Brèdes d'Angole. In France, it is known by several names: Epinard du Malabar, Epinard d'Amérique, Epinard de Chine, Epinard des Indes. CULTIVATION. - Baselle is not widely cultivated; this is a mistake, as it is easy to grow and can be very useful in summer, when the spinach it so advantageously replaces so easily goes to seed. In its native country, baselle is a biennial; but in our region, it's as an annual that it should be treated; perhaps in the Midi, on the shores of the Mediterranean, it could be considered as in its country of origin, i.e. biennial. Very easy to grow, Baselle thrives in any good, warm, well-smoked soil. Sow in a bed in March and transplant in May, always choosing the best exposure. It can also be sown after May 15 in rows 40 cm apart. Watering should be copious and regular. As the stems of this plant are somewhat voluble, for best production, they should be twigged with branches that are well branched at the base. For seedlings planted in rows, if they are too thick, thin them out to leave a space of at least Om, 10 between each plant. VARIETIES. - There are several varieties, almost all edible; the best are : Red Baselle and White Baselle, the most popular. Very broad-leaved Chinese Baselle (Basella cordifolia Lamk.), not widely cultivated. SEEDS. - Placed in a well-exposed spot, Baselle produces an abundance of good seeds. As they ripen only successively, they should be picked as they appear to be good; as soon as they are picked, they should be laid out in the sun for a few hours, then dried and ripened in the shade. They keep well for five years; 30 grams contain 1010 seeds; a liter weighs 460 grams. pests and animals. - Snails and slugs are very fond of young leaves; when seedlings start to emerge, sprinkle them with soot, ashes or powdered lime to prevent them from being eaten. Flea beetles sometimes attack baselle, especially in dry years. USES. - The leaves, which are thick and fleshy, are used in the same way as spinach; they have a finer, less bland taste, and are even said to be easily digestible and healthier than spinach leaves. In India, the fruits of the red baselle are used to dye Indian cloth, as in Senegal, but this dye is not very strong. BASILIC Ocimum Basilicum Labiatae. ORIGIN. - India, in dry, arid places. CULTIVATION. - Basil is not an essential plant, but it should have a place in the vegetable garden. Sow in February or March on warm or hot beds, or in April or May, in the open ground. You can also sow in well-drained pots or crates, which can be moved at will; emergence generally takes place in a short time, from forty to sixty hours, depending on the warmth of the bed or atmosphere. When the young plants have two leaves, they are transplanted onto another layer, watered and covered with panels to facilitate recovery. Once this has taken place, air is gradually added, and after a few days, the panels are removed. In May, we plant them in the open ground in a good warm exposure, in beds or, better still, in borders at Om,15 or Om,20 in all directions. During hot spells, water sufficiently and, to keep stems fresh and tender, cut them often, especially to prevent them from flowering. VARIETIES. - There are several varieties of basil; the best for the vegetable garden are : Lettuce-leaf basil. - Very good plant, with broad, thick leaves; very fragrant. Basilie grand violet. - A highly recommendable variety; dark purple leaves and stem, less broad than the previous one; very fragrant. Basilie grand vert. - Typical species, with dark green leaves and stems; fragrant, very hardy. In the second category, we'd like to mention: Basilie frisé. - Aniseed basil. - Fine purple basil. - Stocky varieties, forming tight, compact clumps, suitable for pot cultivation. In the south-west, gardeners grow few others. Basil tree (Ocimum Basilicum). - A plant with a very pleasant fragrance, growing to a height of 50 or 60 centimeters, a very good variety for growing in pots. SEEDS. - A few plants are left to grow, and when the ears are ripe and turning from green to red, they are cut and spread out on a cloth which is exposed to the sun for a few hours. The ripe seeds detach easily and fall onto the cloth. They are collected and dried in the shade; 30 grams contain at least 24,000 seeds; a liter weighs 530 grams; germination lasts eight years. DISEASES, PESTS. - We know of no disease as such that attacks basil. Snails and slugs are very fond of it, especially when the plants are newborn. To prevent them from being eaten, sprinkle with soot, ash, quicklime or sulfur. USES. - Basil is used as a condiment and to flavor sauces, all kinds of dishes and especially peas. It is also used to smoke hams and perfume hot baths. In medicine, it is used in infusions like tea. It is also used in vulnerary alcohol. BETTERAVE Beta vulgaris Lin. Beta rapa Moq.-Tan. Chenopodea. ORIGIN. - Biennial plant believed to be derived from chard or pear. (See Poirée.) Moquin-Tandon has divided this genus into three main varieties: 1° Beta maritima, the type species, which grows in the Canary Islands, on the Mediterranean and Ocean shores; 2° Beta cycla, the cultivated chard pear. 3° Beta rapa, the fleshy-rooted beet varieties. CULTIVATION. - Strong, free soils, well fertilized with good consumable fertilizers, are particularly suitable for growing this plant; adding a notable proportion of faecal matter or liquid manure to these fertilizers will produce magnificent vegetation. Beet is sown from March to May, in rows, nurseries or in the field. Nursery sowing is generally the most common in gardens. The narrow end of the hoe is used to trace small rays, 5 to 6 centimetres deep, 40 centimetres apart, 4 per bed. After sowing, the rake is passed over the whole area to cover the seeds. If the soil is too strong, cover with sifted earth or potting soil to encourage emergence. For in-place sowing, draw rays at the same depth, but 60 centimetres apart. The seed is covered with the rake, and when the seedlings have four or five leaves, they are thinned out, hoeed and even watered if possible during hot spells. Some growers use ploughs or hoes to make large furrows and sow or transplant beet on top. This method, which is generally used in agriculture, seems to give good results; we have seen some remarkable crops grown in this way. The best way to grow beets in gardens is to sow them in nurseries and then transplant them in place, in beds of four rows, 40 centimetres apart; a 60-centimetre pathway is left. On the line, we plant at 30 or 35 centimetres, depending on the variety. For transplanting, take advantage of a dark, rainy day and water each plant immediately after planting. When removing nursery-raised plants for transplanting elsewhere, give them a good wetting beforehand, so that they pull well. Before planting, trim the tips of the leaves and the pivot, if it is too long, and plant with a planter, taking care to pack the soil well around each plant. About a month after planting, give the first hoeing, which should be repeated two or three times during the course of vegetation. Water a lot if you want to obtain good yields. As soon as the beets start to grow, gardeners who grow for sale will start taking them to market, just as the amateur will start eating them. To make the best use of the land, sow radishes or lettuce among the beets. Beet to be kept over winter should be harvested in the second half of October. Choose dry weather and put them under cover in a cellar or other place where frost cannot penetrate. In regions where beet is grown for cattle, most growers don't have the space to house them indoors, so they choose a good, sheltered spot outside. They drive a good stake into the ground, then place the beets around it in a pyramid shape, the higher the pile, the wider the base. Once set up, the whole is covered with straw, heather, hay, etc., which is held in place by slats or straw braids attached to the top of the stake, and at the base to small stakes driven into the ground. Beets keep very well in this state until spring. VARIETIES. - Red beet, round. - Early, small, with rounded, half-flattened root, very dark, very good flesh. Red, black, flat Egyptian beet. - Very early, round root, irregularly flattened, entirely out of the ground, medium size, excellent quality. We have grown it extensively for a long time, and have always been satisfied with it. Bassano flat red beet. - A little rounder than the previous one, and more regular in all its parts, very dark and good flesh, medium earliness, fairly large. Dark red Whythe beet. - Elongated, medium size, very good quality, very sweet flesh; keeps very well. Red toad beet. - Very old variety, easy to distinguish from other species by its rough, thick, toad-like skin; very good, sweet, tender, keeps well, excellent baked. Dwarf red beet from Del. - New variety, round, pointed, fairly sweet, small, not very productive. Beet jaune de Castelnaudary. - Very old long variety, small, very sweet, excellent, but of average yield; highly esteemed in the South-West where it is widely grown. Long red or large red beet. - Late bloomer, the most voluminous of the varieties, with red flesh, but of mediocre quality, very well suited to large-scale cultivation. Eclipse" beet. - New variety with very sweet dark-red flesh, early but small. Reine des Noires" beet. - New variety, ovoid shape, dark red, sweet flesh, excellent quality; dark red foliage, very ornamental, medium size. Covent-Garden red beet. - Smooth root, slightly elongated, dark red, sweet flesh, very dark foliage, medium size. Strasbourg piriform beet. - Red salad beet from Treviso. - Early red beet from Dewcing. - These are new varieties. The last is very early, but small. We also grow a few yellow-fleshed varieties, but they don't have the same flavor as the red-fleshed varieties, so are superior in every way. In any case, yellow beets are not very popular. Big long yellow beet (fig. 7). - Very sweet, but tough, becoming quite large. Round yellow beet. - Very sweet, better than the previous one, more tender, more productive. SEEDS. - To harvest beet seed, in autumn, we pull up the most beautiful plants that characterize the species, store them separately in sand, and in spring, when frosts are no longer a threat, we plant them at a short distance from each other; we water immediately to settle the soil around them. When the uprights start to grow a little taller, they should be supported by stakes, or horizontally-laid laths, to which each stem should be attached. Care should be taken during vegetation to remove any shoots that develop late. In July and August, when the stems start to turn yellow, cut them back as the seeds ripen. Ripe seeds can even be collected from the stalk without cutting it off. Dry in the shade, attic or shed. They keep well for four or five years. Each gram contains 50 capsules or seeds, some of which have several embryos. The seed harvested in the North is superior to that harvested in a climate where the temperature is irregular and too high. DISEASES, PESTS. - Only slugs and snails destroy beet as it begins to grow; they can be preserved by sprinkling with ash or quicklime. USES FIG. 7 - Long beet. USES. - Beet is very healthy and refreshing. It is eaten in salads, after being boiled or baked. The leaf can be used in the same way as the Poirée. With sweet wine, it makes good jams. The young shoots that the roots throw into the cellar in winter are eaten as salad. BOURRACHE Borrago officinalis Lin. Borraginaceae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to Mediterranean regions, more medicinal than vegetable; borage should nevertheless have a place in the vegetable garden. CULTIVATION. - It is not very difficult to grow in the right soil, and succeeds everywhere. Its hardiness is even legendary, as it is sometimes found growing vigorously on walls. Sow in August-September or February-March. Autumn sowings are preferable, in that the plants, already strong in spring, acquire greater vigor and flower more. When the young seedlings have two or three leaves, transplant them at a distance of 40 cm, as you would a lettuce or chicory. Water immediately after planting, and weed and hoe when necessary. For this plant, choose the worst spots in the garden. However, if planted in good, well-smoked soil with sufficient exposure, it will produce better results. VARIETY. - There are three varieties of this breed: the first, with blue flowers, is the most common; the second, with pink flowers, and the third, with white flowers. SEEDS. - The seeds, which are very fine, fall as soon as they are ripe; so, to collect them, we must seize the moment when the ovaries appear ripe. They are picked and spread out on a cloth to dry out the seed. It is rare, once borage has been cultivated in a field, for it not to reseed itself in the second year. We once saw a borage bed that had been turned into a temporary walkway after the soil had been turned over. Three years later, the land was put back into cultivation, the whole area was spaded, and an innumerable number of borage plants sprouted shortly afterwards. The seeds keep well for eight years. USES. - Borage flowers, mixed with nasturtium flowers, are used to garnish salads. The chopped leaves, seasoned with vinegar, are eaten as an appetizer. In England and Italy, the flowers and leaves are cooked or fried. The flowers give a green color suitable for painting. BUNIAS D'ORIENT Bunias orientalis Lin. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to western Asia (Russia) and naturalized in northern France. CULTIVATION. - This little-known plant is grown like wild chicory. It resembles wild horseradish and belongs to the same family. It needs good, deep, well-smoked, humus-rich soil, which will produce well-branched stems over a metre high. Once the soil has been ploughed, sow the seed in the autumn or, better still, in spring, either in rows or in rows, covering the seed lightly. Thin out if the seed is too thick, then weed, hoe and water during the hottest periods. It's a good idea to renew plantings every four years, if you want to have good vegetation. Young plants that have been thinned out can be transplanted, but you need to choose dark, rainy weather, as they suffer from transplanting during hot spells. Transplanted plants become much more beautiful than those left in place. SEEDS. - After a year's care, bunias go to seed the following spring. When the uprights start to turn yellow, pick them up and lay them out on cloths in a shady, well-ventilated spot. Seeds keep well for three years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Snails and slugs devour them when the plants are still young. The most damaging insect is the flea beetle, the terrible enemy of crucifers, which perforates the leaves with a host of tiny holes, stopping the sap flow. USES. - In field crops, it is used as green fodder. In vegetable growing, however, the young, tender shoots are eaten in white sauce, or boiled in water and eaten in vinegar; these young shoots, blanched like a capuchin beard, are delicious. CAPRIER Capparis spincsa L., Capparis sativa Pers. Capparidae. ORIGIN. - A thorny shrub from southern France, growing in Provence and throughout the orange-growing region. GROWING. - In northern France, caper cultivation becomes difficult and unprofitable, as the shrub freezes easily and needs to be carefully covered to survive the winter. Capers thrive best in dry places, on rubble, demolitions and old walls. If you have a retaining wall, or an embankment to the south, plant it in it, with the roots in the wall or embankment. The stems are brought out through a hole dug for the purpose. Every year, at the onset of winter, cut off the stems and fill the hole with straw or litter, moss, etc., to prevent frost from penetrating to the roots. It can also be grown in back-to-back greenhouses, tarpaulins, frames, and in pots or crates buried in summer at the foot of a well-exposed wall, where the pots or crates are sunk in tannin; in winter, they are moved into a greenhouse or orangery. Capers can be propagated by seed, marcottes or cuttings. For our variety, layering is preferable in that it produces a fast-growing plant. Propagation by seed is slower, as seedlings do not always clearly characterize the type, and many only flower after a few years. The thornless variety, Capparis inermis, propagates readily from seed. Sow in March on a warm bed, in pots or cups filled with a mixture of light, sandy loam: at least two years of greenhouse cultivation are needed before it can be grown as described above. SPECIES, VARIETIES. - The genus Capparis comprises several species; the main ones are : Egyptian caper (Capparis Ægyptia Lamk.). - A species highly esteemed by the Egyptians, who use it as a seasoning. The flower buds, preserved in vinegar, have the same properties as those of Capparis spinosa; they are antiscorbutic and stimulating. E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 6 and the young shoots. Mount Sinai Caper (Capparis Sinaica). - The flower buds of this variety are very large. They are candied in vinegar; same property. Câprier des Antilles (Capparis Breynia Lin ), long-fruited caper or devil's bean. - This variety is little cultivated in Europe. Capparis ferruginea Lin, Capparis amygdalina Lamk, Capparis octandra Jacq, bois caca, partly because of the color of its wood. - West Indian variety. We still grow: 1° Capparis mithridatica Forsk, from Egypt; 2° Capparis Dahi Forsk, from Egypt; 3° Capparis ovata, M. v. B. from Barbary. SEEDS. - Caper seeds do not ripen in northern France, unless the plants are kept in greenhouses. But in Provence, where the plant is widely cultivated for industry, they are easy to obtain. They keep well for three years. DISEASES AND PESTS. - We know of no disease or insect that attacks this plant. USES. - What is commercially available under the name of capers are flower buds picked before they bloom; they are all the more appreciated for their smaller size, and are used as a stimulating appetizer. The aqueous or alcoholic infusion of the stems serves as an excellent chemical reagent, for discovering acids and alkalis. With acids, it gives a fiery red color; with alkalis, it gives a very beautiful green color. Tropœolum majus L. Tropéolées. ORIGIN. - From Peru, where it was brought in 1684. Perennial in its native country and in our greenhouses, but annual in our outdoor cultivations. GROWING. - Nasturtium thrives in good, cool, well-fertilized soil; during hot spells, it needs copious watering to develop vigorously. It can be propagated from seed, sown in pots on beds in March, or from cuttings smothered in cloches, taken in August and overwintered. You can also sow in place, in April or May. As this plant is an obvious climber, to ensure sufficient growth, it is best to place it near a trellis, where it can be twined up with numerous branches, so that the tendrils can cling to it. Watering should be copious and regular throughout the summer, as the more water it receives, the better it will grow. VARIETIES. - Several dwarf varieties, some with dark flowers, are derived from the tall nasturtium; same cultivation and same uses. SEEDS. - A few of the most vigorous plants should be staked, so that the first flowers to appear are preserved, as it is these flowers that give the best seeds. They should be collected as they ripen, otherwise they fall off easily. Then dry them in the shade. They keep well for five years; one gram contains 7 or 8. DISEASES, PESTS. - In some iron-deficient soils, nasturtiums become chlorotic and grow poorly; in this case, simply add a little good layered compost mixed with silica sand to each plant, and they will soon regain their normal vigor. Slugs and snails are very fond of them when the plants are young; to keep them away, simply sprinkle them with the ingredients indicated above. USES. - The flower buds before they bloom, and the still-green seeds, can be prepared in vinegar for the same uses as capers. Place in vinegar, salt, pepper and bay leaves; boil, skim and throw in the well-cleaned seeds; remove three or four minutes later, drain and cool, and seal in jars with new vinegar; in this way, they keep for a long time and are very good. The flowers are used to decorate salads and roasts. The plant's juice dyes wool a solid yellow, but the color is not pleasant. LITTLE CAPUCIN Tropœolum minus. Tro péolées. ORIGIN. - Peru; also known as India cress. CULTIVATION. - Same as above. VARIETIES. - This variety should not be confused with the other dwarf species derived from Tropœolum majus. It forms a separate race which has produced its own varieties; it is frankly dwarf. SEEDS. - Same care as above. Many seeds fall to the ground before they are ripe; despite this, they are very good and should be collected. DISEASES, PESTS. - Same as nasturtium grande. USES. - Same as above. Tropœolum tuberosum. Tropoeolae. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to South America; little cultivated and therefore little known. CULTIVATION. - Tubers, which are the size of a hen's egg, are planted in April in the open ground, in as loose, light, well-exposed soil as possible, 40 to 60 centimetres apart in all directions, in rows, in a chessboard pattern. Several deep hoeings are given during vegetation, until the numerous stems have completely invaded the soil. Once the first frosts have destroyed these stems, in November, we can start digging. Tubers, as long as they are buried in the ground, are not affected by frosts, so you can only pull them as and when you need to. USES. - M. Vilmorin says: "Cooked in water like carrots and potatoes, tuberous nasturtium roots are watery and have a rather unpleasant, albeit fragrant, taste. In Bolivia, where the plant is widely cultivated, the tubers are frozen after cooking. In this state, they are considered a delicacy and highly sought-after. Elsewhere, they are exposed to the open air in canvas bags and eaten half-dried. So it's hardly surprising that fresh tubers don't seem very good to us, since even in the country of origin, they are only eaten prepared. For our part, we have tried this tuber, cooking it in 6. several ways; we have never found it to be excellent. CARDON Cynara Cardunculus Lin. Cynara sylvestris Lamk. Composites. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to southern Europe. De Candolle says that cardoon is indigenous to Madeira, the Canaries, the mountains of Morocco near Moga- dor, the south and east of the Iberian peninsula, the south of France, Italy and Greece, and the islands of the Mediterranean Sea as far as Cyprus. A few years ago, it was moved to Montevideo, where it has multiplied to such an extent that it occupies immense plains and invades the Uruguay and Plata countryside, as well as part of the Buenos Aires region, to the point of hindering communications. It is also becoming a nuisance in Chile. CULTIVATION. - Cardoon can be propagated from seed, sown from April 15 to June, in warm beds under a frame, or in the open air in May. You could sow earlier on warm beds, but most of the plants will bolt, making them unsuitable for cultivation. Sow in pots or nurseries. If you have sown in a nursery under a frame, you can plant them when the third leaf begins to develop in pits prepared as described below. For in-place sowing, each pit is planted with three plants arranged in a triangle. Water immediately after planting. Potted seedlings are planted in the same way, leaving only three of the best plants. For seedlings planted in situ, dig 40-centimetre-square pits, 1.20 m apart. These are filled with good, well-decomposed manure, which is well trodden down. This manure is then covered with a few centimetres of loose soil, then four or six seeds are sown per hole, and pressed in with the finger. Sprinkle lightly with the apple, and all that's left to do is monitor emergence. When the plants are strong enough, we thin them out, leaving only three feet per hole. After this, lightly hoe and water. Experience has shown that it is preferable to raise young plants in nurseries, and then transplant them into the field; success is just as good, and sometimes better; but care must be taken when planting to cut the pivot at each plant. As these plants don't invade the entire space reserved for them within a few months, lettuces are counter-planted, and radishes, spinach, pumice, etc. are seeded. During the summer, the plants are hoeed several times and watered copiously, especially during the first hot spells. In October, we start to bleach them, and successively throughout the winter. First, select the strongest plants, tie the leaves together without compressing them too much, then surround them with straw and tie them in place. About twenty days after this operation, the cardoons are sufficiently white (fig. 8), and can be eaten; left longer, they would rot. Cardoons are sensitive to hard frosts; as winter approaches, they should be surrounded by earth, new manure or fern, which should be stirred from time to time to prevent rotting. In this way, they keep perfectly well. They can also be uprooted and placed in a gauge along a well-exposed wall; they are covered every evening with mulch- FIG. 8. Bleached cardoon. sounds, but care must be taken to clean them often, otherwise they will rot quickly. Alternatively, after trimming the tips of the leaves, place them upright in chests, a short distance apart; dry sand is slipped into the gaps. In this way, they whiten in a short time, with the advantage that they do not rot. The sand could be replaced by dry leaf, in which case they would blanch more quickly. VARIETIES. - Several varieties are grown: Cardons de Tours. - This is the most esteemed variety, with thick, full ribs that blanch well without deteriorating too much; but it has many thorns. In spite of this, it is cultivated and much appreciated. Spanish cardoon. - A very vigorous variety, with broad, half-full, thornless ribs, widely grown in southern France and Spain. Cardon plein inerme. - Another very vigorous variety, and despite its name, the ribs are less full than those of the preceding varieties; very ample in all its parts, whitens well and quickly. Cardon Puvis. - Another very vigorous variety with no spines, very broad, half-full ribs, ample leaves, good quality. Red-ribbed cardoon. - Not very old and widely cultivated variety, differs from Spanish cardoon only in its red-tinted, half-full ribs. SEEDS. - Cardoon only goes to seed in the second year after sowing; if it goes to seed before then, it has been sown too early, and the seeds it produces are worthless. To harvest good seed, you need to select a few of the best plants as seedbeds. It can last fifteen years or more; on the contrary, the older it is, the better the seeds will be. These seedbeds should be treated in the same way as artichokes, i.e. they should be deadheaded each winter, and eyeballed in spring; only one fruit should be left on each stem, the middle one. In August, when they are ripe, they are cut, gathered in bundles, hung in a dry place away from rodents, and, during the winter evenings, the seed is cleaned, which keeps well for seven years. One gram contains 25 to 30 grams; the weight per liter is 630 grams. DISEASES, PESTS. - Cardoon is subject to the same diseases as artichokes (see p. 69). USES. - The blanched ribs are eaten after boiling in water to remove their bitterness; they are then prepared with a white sauce, or with roasts of any kind, which is always a delicious dish. The flower, generally known as Cardonnette or Chardonnette, is frequently used in the countryside to curdle milk, and is said to be an excellent substitute for rennet. Cardoon is appetizing and nourishing, but also very warming. The Romans ate the receptacle that bears the flowers, and the Italians also eat it under the name of Girello. Modern farmers cultivate cardoon for the fleshy part of the leaves. (De Candolle.) CARROT Daucus Carota Lin. Daucus vulgaris Neck. Caucalis Carota Roth. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Native biennial plant also found in temperate western Asia, cultivated for over two thousand years. The typical species is found throughout France, particularly in Poitou, Périgord and Angoumois, in uncultivated fields, in high, dry meadows and along rural roadsides. The carrot is second only to the potato as a cultivated vegetable. Its continual use in cooking means that considerable quantities are consumed. EARLY CROPS. - Although carrots can be grown in all types of soil, they prefer light, moist, soft, healthy soil, and above all, soil that has been sufficiently improved with good fertilizers. Carrots are sown in warm layers from December to February, in layers about 40 to 50 centimetres thick, built up with new manure and leaves. Fill them with 12 to 15 centimetres of a good mixture of potting soil and well-sieved vegetable garden soil. When the layer has set fire to, pack the potting soil well, then sow the seed, covering it with a centimetre or so of the same mixture. Water immediately, and put up the panels. Leave in this state, without letting in air, until emergence, which takes place in eight or ten days. From then on, you need to make sure you give the plants some air when the sun comes out, to prevent the young plants from withering away. Stoves are maintained to keep the heat more or less even. We thin out the plants as the need arises; however, we can leave them a little thick, because as soon as the carrots are big enough to be eaten, we start to remove them, always thinning out. In January, during the hottest hours of the day, we give them plenty of air when they start to get strong. In light, sandy soils, you can sow in the open ground in October or November among radishes, and cover with litter or straw mats, to produce new carrots in the early spring, which sell very well. But it's hardly possible to do this in strong, compact soils saturated with moisture. When you have a large number of frames and boxes at your disposal, you can place them on the beds, and then care for them like other seedlings. You can also sow on layers prepared as described above, at a good exposure, along a wall; but in the open air, without boxes or frames, these layers must be well bordered, sufficiently packed and covered with wind-breaking mats or litter. Produce grown in this way can be harvested early and well before that grown in the ground. This process and the previous one are what can be called hasty cultivation. FULL-GROUND CULTIVATION. - Carrots can be sown in the ground from February to September, all year round in the warm, light soils of the Midi and part of the South-West. But the best time to sow, which offers almost certain success, is spring, from March 20 to May 15. April is the month when growers generally sow carrots. Summer sowing is generally very successful from July 15 to September; in strong soils, sowing at this time often gives better results than spring sowing. For success to be good, the soil must at all times be suitably prepared and fertilized with good, well-consumed fertilizers, as fresh fertilizers, because of the ammonia and nitrogen principles they release, force vegetation too much and cause the produce to fork, i.e. instead of having beautiful, well-shaped, round and conical carrots, most divide into several roots of equal size, produce of this nature is little esteemed. There are several ways to sow carrots, either in rows or in beds. The latter is the best and most common method adopted by gardeners. When the soil is ready, lay out beds 1.20 to 1.30 meters wide. Proceed as described in the article on training (p. 14). Sow in fine weather. The soil is taken from the paths, thrown to the right and left, then raked to level and break up the clods, which are brought back to the paths. Mulch if necessary. The subsoil is sufficiently moist for capillary action to bring moisture to the surface. In certain soils where the soil is difficult to divide, we would do a poor job if we operated in this way; we simply pass the fork back and forth and across (we harrow, in other words), which partly covers the seeds, we pass the rake to bring the stones to the sides, to form a rim, and divide the paths. We've been doing this for a long time, and it's always worked out well for us. For sowing in rows, we draw four rays per 1.20 m bed, 4 or 5 centimetres deep; we cover the seed lightly with the rake, taking care to make the loosest soil fall into it; if the soil is too strong, we cover with soil or potting mix passed through the rake. In general, spring seedlings can do without mulch; but the same cannot be said of summer seedlings, which must be mulched without exception, unless you are working in damp soil, or better still in land where you will be watering often in the morning and evening. At this time of year, water evaporates so quickly that it is of no benefit to the plants if you don't water them thoroughly. Carrots need to develop quickly, so they need constant humidity, especially when young. Seeds usually germinate in twelve or eighteen days, depending on the time of sowing and the warmth of the soil. When they are 7 to 10 centimetres high, they are thinned out and weeded, a task that requires great care and attention. It's generally left to the women, who do it very well. If the soil is dry, the seedling is wetted a few hours before the operation, so that the weeding can be done easily; after the weeding, the seedling is watered again to facilitate the raising of the plants that have been shaken by the operation. As a general rule, carrots should not be thick if they are to produce good results. Three months after sowing, you can start harvesting, especially if you have sown early varieties. E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. Although carrots do not fear the cold, they can be damaged by frost, so they need to be covered. In November, cut off the tops and fill each bed with 10 centimetres of soil taken from the paths, levelling the surface. You can also cover with heather mats and leaves. Soil is preferable, however, in that you don't have to worry about it afterwards. We pull them out as and when we need them for the household or for sale. In the latter case, when they are destined for the market, as the tops are lacking, they are replaced by a heather stem, so that they can be tied and bundled. VARIETIES. - For early seedlings, early varieties are preferred: Early short carrot from Holland. - Introduced to our crops in 1800, it is one of the best varieties to grow. Very short carrot with frame (fig. 9). - Variety of the preceding one, very early, but yields little, suitable only for early crops. Half-length carrot, Nantaise or heartless. - A very good variety, becoming very large and of excellent quality. Carotte demi-longue d'Eysines, known in Bordeaux as the carrot à jus. - An excellent variety, derived from the old demi-longue, improved by the market gardeners of Eysines near Bordeaux. Becomes very large. Carotte demi-longue du Luc. - Very good variety, fairly early, productive and good storage life. Carotte demi-longue pointue. - An old variety, grown almost everywhere, very good for marshland cultivation; becomes very large. Half-long, obtuse carrot. - Very good, productive variety, excellent for large-scale cultivation, becoming enormous. FIG. 9: Red carrot FIG. 10: Very short, half-long red carrot from Chantenay. Carrot demi-longue de Carentan. - A very good variety with small, few leaves, rather small than large, almost cylindrical in shape, good for early cultivation, heartless. Carotte demi-courte obtuse de Guérande. - Very good species for cultivation in rich soils, becomes very large, very good quality. Chantenay half-long carrot (fig. 10). - New variety, very productive, good quality, wider collar than the Nantaise, from which it probably originates. Danvers half-long carrot. - Very good variety, productive and fairly early, new. Carotte courte à forcer parisienne. - New, very small variety, suitable for forced cultivation, very early, very good quality. James intermediate half-long carrot. - A new variety with a pointed shape, very good and productive. Long-rooted varieties are less cultivated than half-lengths; a few, however, are just as good for large-scale cultivation; but, in general, they are a little later, and less appreciated on the markets Carotte rouge longue de Saint-Valéry. - Very good variety, pointed shape, excellent flesh, fine, smooth, very red skin, intermediate between half-long and long carrots, excessively productive. Pale red Flanders carrot. - A variety highly esteemed and cultivated in the Nord region, around Lille, Roubaix, etc.; not very long, it becomes enormous and consequently produces a lot, the root is bright orange in color, pointed in shape; very good for large-scale cultivation. Long red Altringham carrot. - English variety, very good quality red flesh, can reach 40 to 50 cm in length. Long red carrot - Old and good variety, very productive and of good quality, it is one of the best long varieties to grow for the kitchen garden. Brunswick long red carrot. - Sub-variety of the previous one, highly esteemed in Germany, keeps very well and is of good quality. Heartless long red carrot. - Variety with very red flesh, obtuse at the lower end, sweet, excellent, fine taste. Long red carrot with green collar. - Very hardy variety, becomes very large, pale orange in color, excellent and very nutritious, suitable for large-scale cultivation. On

We also grow yellow- and white-fleshed varieties, but only in large-scale cultivation. They are good for animals, but not popular. Among the best are: Long yellow carrot. - Short yellow carrot. - White carrot with green collar. - White carrot with green collar from Orthe. - Vosges white carrot. - Carotte blanche de Breteuil. This variety is becoming huge. SEEDS. - To harvest carrot seed, most growers select the best plants in autumn and transplant them separately. In June-July, the umbels begin to ripen. The most advanced are picked first, and so on as they mature. Grain merchants don't operate this way: they leave the carrots in place, and remove only the degenerate or bastardized subjects. Seeds harvested in this way are just as good as those harvested in the previous way, but they also yield many more seeds. They keep well for four years; 30 grams contain 24,300 seeds, and a liter weighs 360 grams. DISEASES, PESTS. - Few diseases attack carrots; only uredo, in rainy years, partially destroys the leaves. On the other hand, loaches, slugs and snails are very fond of them. When the plants are young, they devour them. They must therefore be destroyed by any means necessary. USES. - Carrots are a good, healthy food; they are used in stews, casseroles, sauces, fried, etc. All doctors recommend them. All doctors recommend it. The old seeds are used to make liqueurs. Applied to animal feed, the carrot is one of the most useful plants; it is eagerly sought after by all kinds of livestock, and is particularly suitable for horses. Sliced and mixed with chopped straw, it makes an excellent feed for sheep. Carrots are also an excellent feed for cows, but they should be given sparingly and mixed with other substances: eaten alone and in excessive quantities, they would be rather harmful.
