Carrots contain 9 to 10% of a sugar that has been reported to crystallize. It also contains phosphates, alkaline salts and a volatile oil that gives it its exciting properties and odor. In northern France and Belgium, carrots are used to make a syrup known as poiré. The flower is the basis of the liqueur known as Venus oil. Root juice is also used to artificially color butter. The flowers dye paper dark red; the dirty red decoction dyes wool, silk, cotton and linen lilac after boiling for a few minutes; blue red with the addition of sulfuric acid, and blue gray with alum. CELERY Apium graveolens Lin. Sium Apium Roth. Seseli graveolens Led. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Native biennial plant. The typical species is found in many parts of France, notably Boulogne, growing in damp places. Wild celery has an extensive habitat. It can be found from Sweden to Algeria, Egypt and Abyssinia, and in Asia from the Caucasus to Baluchistan and the mountains of English India. It has been cultivated for over two thousand years (De Candolle). The typical species is known by several synonyms: ache, ache d'eau, ache des marais, épraux, etc. CULTIVATION. - Cultivated celery thrives best in cool, well-amended soils with good, energetic fertilizers; marshy soils are generally very good for growing this plant. The first celery plants are sown on warm beds in January and February. When the young plants are strong enough to withstand transplanting, they are transplanted to another bed or even to another earthen bed with a good southern exposure. These plants, especially those raised under panels, are good for setting out towards the end of May. Three months later, you can start harvesting them. In April or the first fortnight of May, depending on the region, seedlings are sown in the ground, but care must always be taken to choose a good, well-exposed spot. Once the soil has been well ploughed, the necessary beds are laid out, carefully raked to ensure a perfectly flat surface, sown and lightly covered with compost, then spread a little long manure evenly over the entire sown area. This manure is used to soften the impact of watering on the seeds, and to protect the young plants from excessive heat. Most of the time, these kinds of open-ground seedlings don't do well, either because the soil is stony or compact, or because slugs and snails devour them. It's much better to sow in thick layers, and when the plants are strong enough, transplant them into the open ground in a nursery. Shade them for a few days, until they are ready to take root; these young plants, if well cared for, soon develop rapidly, and in this way, you always have beautiful, well-fed plants to put in place. It's towards the end of May and in the course of June that we put them into final position. There are several ways of doing this, depending on the region: in some countries, plants are planted flat, while in others they are planted in pits. Pit planting is the most widely adopted. When the soil is well ploughed with a spade, pits are dug 45 centimetres deep if two rows are to be planted, or 30 centimetres deep if only one row is to be planted, 25 to 30 centimetres deep and 1.30 to 1.50 centimetres apart. The bottom of the pits is worked a little, then 10 centimetres of well-consumed manure is spread in the bottom, which is compacted by trampling on it several times. This manure is covered with 12 centimetres of soil taken from the edges and raked down; the bottom is levelled, then one or two lines are drawn and the celery is planted with a planter, after cutting off the tips of the leaves and trimming the roots. In between, plant rows of lettuces, radishes and spinach. For flat cultivation, which in some soils gives just as good results as pit cultivation, there are two ways to proceed: The first is to spade the soil, spread the necessary manure over it, then cultivate with a flat or forked hoe. Then plant one or two rows of celery and four rows of romaine or chicory, etc. The second method consists in digging up the soil and burying the manure, then drawing the necessary lines and transplanting with a planter. This method is quicker and gives just as good results as the first. Whether you do it one way or the other, remember that celery needs a lot of water to grow quickly, as well as good fertilizers. Hoe and mulch during hot weather. STUFFING. - Butting is an operation designed to whiten the base of the petioles, making them tender and edible. For this operation to be successful, butting must be done at least three times. The first ridging should be light, followed by a second one about three weeks later, and a third and final one a few days later. The soil must be well levelled out between each plant, so that there are no gaps, and care must be taken to ensure that not too much soil gets into the eye: to avoid this, celery plants are tied up, or held by a worker. Ridging should only be carried out when the plants are well dried out and the weather is dry. In some localities, market gardeners plant celery in beds like chicory, and bed them with annealed manure, leaf or litter; others pull up the stalks and place them in a trench side by side, and bed them with soil; still others place them in sand. Any method works, as long as you succeed. Celery is not particularly sensitive to frost, but high humidity is detrimental. VARIETY. - The best varieties of full-grown celery are: 7. Large celery. - Very vigorous plant, keeps well, very full ribs, good quality. Céteri gros violet de Tours. - Very vigorous variety, full ribs, good quality, excessively productive. Céteri plein blanc court à grosses côtes. - Very good variety, very wide full ribs, upright foliage, less ample in all its parts than the previous one, very pro- ductive, due to the width of its ribs. Full white celery. - A good, vigorous, full-ribbed variety, reaching 40 to 50 cm in height. Early short white celery. - Early variety, good for early cultivation. Céleri plein blanc frisé. - As above, good for early crops, but not very productive. Céleri plein blanc chemin or golden celery. - Excellent variety, highly recommended, the ribs are very full and of a golden yellow that makes them always appear blanched, and, because of this, this celery can do without ridging, provided the top and bottom are attached. We particularly recommend its cultivation. Céteri Pascal, full white (fig. 11). - New variety, semi-short, with wide, fleshy, tender ribs, good winter storage life. Light yellow ribs, very good, good storage life. SEEDS. - There are two ways of harvesting celery seed: the first is to plant the plants separately, with the same care as for ordinary cultivation, during the summer, but without ridging. Care must be taken to remove any plants that are not typical of the species. You can also, at the time of uprooting, select the most beautiful plants and transplant them immediately, at 30 or 40 centimetres; if you are too afraid of frost, you can cover them with straw mats or leaves; in spring, you can smoke them well, or water them with liquid manure. Towards the end of July, in August or September, depending on the region, the umbels ripen successively. They are picked as they ripen, FIG. 11 - Céleri Pascal, full white. and dried by laying them out on cloths in the shade; to detach them from their receptacle, they are struck with a small stick, or rubbed between the hands. They keep well for eight years. One gram contains 2,500 seeds. MAlaDiEs, NUISiBlE ANIMALS. - Celery is often attacked by uredo or rust. So, in the Charentes region of France, celeriac growers call it "brumé". The best way to destroy this disease, which only attacks the leaves, is to treat celery with Bordeaux mixture, two or three times, in the same proportions as for vines. This procedure has given us good results. Centipedes and white grubs are very fond of celery, and in areas where these animals thrive, it is not uncommon to find several plants completely lost, and fortunately the damage is only noticed once it has been done. To keep them at bay, we recommend placing a handful of crushed oyster shells at the foot of each celery plant. USES. - Celery plays an important role in the culinary arts, and is used in stews, casseroles, pâtés and other dishes. It is eaten cooked under meat, or on its own, fried or with white sauce. In many places, it is eaten raw as a salad, but in this state it is very indigestible. The root and leaves contain significant quantities of mannite. The offshoots and residues are eaten by cattle. CELERI-RAVE Apium Rapa L. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - A biennial plant in our cultivations, derived from full celery, improved by cultivation which has developed the root instead of the leaves. Celery root is nowhere found spontaneously. CULTIVATION. - Celery root is sown in March or April in beds in the open air, or better still, covered with panels. When the young plants are strong enough, transplant them to the nursery in the same way as for full-grown celery. In light, well-fertilized soils, seedlings can be sown in well-exposed beds; taking care not to sow too thickly, transplanting can be avoided. However, this operation should not be neglected whenever possible, as it promotes the plant's development. In June, set them out in good, well-ploughed soil, thoroughly fertilized with good, widely-used fertilizers. They are laid out in beds 1.25 m wide, divided into five rows at equal distances. Leave a path 40 to 50 cm wide between each bed, and plant 30 cm into the row, preferably in a checkerboard pattern; water immediately after planting, and continue throughout the summer. FIO. 12 - Celeriac. Before planting, the root tips and leaf tops are lightly trimmed, hoeing is carried out several times during the summer, and the lower leaves, which are yellowing and spreading on the ground, are often removed. Towards the end of August, each plant is lightly dethatched, even removing the few roots that may be in the way, and left in this state; about a month later, the soil is put back in place, with more soil added; this operation, which is practiced in Alsace, is designed to encourage root development, as it is mainly in August and September that roots form. Harvesting can begin in October and continue until March. In southern regions, they survive the winter without suffering, but in departments with harsh winters, it's a good idea to butter them up, cover them with straw mats and even tuck them in and bury them in sand. VEGETABLES. - Erfurth celeriac. - Very old variety, widely cultivated, early, the root is fleshy, becomes very large, and is of good quality. Céleri-rave gros lisse de Paris. - A very good variety to grow, probably derived from the ordinary celeriac, with very large roots, the largest of its kind, good quality. Ordinary celeriac. - A very old variety, quite productive, but no longer cultivated today in favor of earlier varieties. Small-leaf celeriac. - Very early variety, derived from the Erfurth celeriac, few roots, small rootstock. Giant Prague celeriac. - Very good new variety, with very large, clean root, short foliage and relatively few roots. Céleri-rave de Paris amélioré. - A new, improved variety, producing enormous turnips. Celeriac with variegated leaves. - New yellow variegated breed, round, fairly regular celeriac (curious variety to grow). SEEDS. - To harvest celeriac seed, proceed in much the same way as for full celery. Seeds keep well for five or six years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Same diseases as full-grown celery, although it seems to us that uredo rages less vigorously on this breed. Insects are the same. USES. - Celery root can be served with white sauce or fat; it also goes well with roasts, and is sometimes used in stews to replace parsnips. CERFEUIL Scandix Cerefolium Lin. Anthriscus Cerefolium Hoff. Cerefolium sativum Haller. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, long considered spontaneous in France. De Candolle says: "... In western and southern Europe, it appears to be adventive, more or less naturalized; but in southeastern Russia and temperate western Asia, it appears to be spontaneous. Greek authors did not mention it; the first ancient mention is in Columella and Pliny, who call it Cerefolium. The species had probably been introduced into the Greco-Roman world since Theophrastus. CULTIVATION. - Chervil is very hardy, adapting well to any soil; when well smoked, it succeeds in certain shady exposures, where it would be impossible to grow anything else. Sow in shallow rows 25 cm apart, from February to October. Seedlings sown from February to July go to seed immediately and produce few leaves; to avoid running out, sow every two weeks. During hot spells, water copiously and choose a shady spot. Seedlings sown from August to October don't come up until April the following year, after having produced abundantly over winter and part of spring. At this time, they can be sown among spinach, carrots, salsify, cabbage, lamb's lettuce and so on. Weed them as and when needed, before they harm other plants. VARIETIES. - Common chervil. - Most commonly grown for markets, with deeply cut, serrated leaves, winged in two or three rows, borne on a long stalk; semi-cylindrical, canaliculated on top and covered with very fine hairs. Curly chervil or double chervil with curly leaflets. - A variety that reproduces fairly well from seed; a few plants always revert to the type species, but a selection of rootstocks produces good results. This is an elegant variety, preferable to common chervil for decorating dishes; it has the same flavor and fragrance: cultivation is the same, but it seems a little less hardy. SEEDS. - Spring sowings grow quickly, but the seed is not very good; it is better to harvest it from autumn sowings, which provide a greater quantity; ripening takes place in June and July. The stems should be picked before the seeds are fully ripe; they dry out in the shade. These seeds keep well for two years. DISEASES, PESTS. - In silica-deficient soils, chervil does not grow well, if not well smoked, and takes on a yellow, stunted hue, which leads us to believe that this plant likes soils where iron is found in large quantities. It is rarely attacked by uredo. In most areas, young seedlings, especially those sown in spring, fall prey to slugs and snails, which are very greedy: the damage is greater in wet weather. This can be remedied by throwing soot or powdered quicklime on them early in the morning, or even better, by sprinkling them with milk of lime. White grubs and mites sometimes eat away at the roots, destroying part of the plant. USES. - Chervil is used as a seasoning in stews and sauces, and as a salad garnish; it is also used to dress cold meats. Doctors have often observed poisoning caused by hemlock, a wild plant that resembles the common chervil in every way, but is much taller, less green and has a fetid odor when crumpled, which distinguishes it from chervil. To avoid these mistakes, M. Vilmorin recommends growing curly chervil, which cannot be confused with chervil. TUBEROUS CHERFEUIL Chœrophyllum bulbosum L. Umbelliferae ORIGIN. - A biennial plant, originating in the same regions as common chervil; it is a very old species that is still little cultivated. This is a mistake, as it is only through cultivation that we will succeed in improving it, and if today the products are not very profitable, they could be very profitable. CULTIVATION. - After stratifying the seed in fine sand kept fresh since November, sow very thickly in February and March, covering sparingly. Choose a good sunny exposure, which is absolutely essential for this plant to ripen its bulbs. You can sow in autumn, but as germination only occurs in spring, it's best to stratify. Some time after seed emergence, and when the young seedlings are sufficiently developed, we give a first light hoeing, which must be repeated often, to keep the plants in a perfect state of cleanliness; we water copiously during hot spells. It's time to harvest the tubers when the foliage begins to yellow and dry out. Pull them out and put them under cover in a healthy place. They are best eaten fresh. SEEDS. - Tubers intended for seed production should be planted in March, 10 cm apart in all directions. When the stems begin to rise, they are staked to prevent them from being uprooted by the wind. Harvesting of the umbels begins in July; the seeds that ripen first are the best, and keep well for a year. DISEASES, PESTS. - This variety is subject to the same diseases as common chervil, and is eaten by the same insects. USES. - The flavor of this tuber is very similar to that of chestnuts. It is prepared like potatoes, and used in sauces like salsify; the flesh is very starchy and sweet, with a very pronounced, pleasant aromatic taste. It's a very delicate food, much sought-after in Asia. After soaking for a few hours in water, this tuber is very refreshing and aids digestion. Myrrhis odorata Scop. Chærophyllum aromaticum L. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Native perennial; grows in mountainous regions of southern Europe, mainly in the Alps, forming clumps 80 to 90 centimeters high; very hardy. CULTIVATION. - This plant is not very difficult to grow in the right soil, and succeeds just about anywhere. The seed is sown as soon as it matures in a nursery, or stratified in fresh sand, like tuberous chervil, for sowing in March. Sow very thickly, as this plant needs to be transplanted to succeed. Once the young plants have developed their first five or six leaves, transplant them into place, spacing them 50 to 60 centimetres apart, weeding and hoeing several times; they can be kept this way for several years. SEEDS. - In the second year of planting, the plants go to seed. We select the most beautiful stems and remove all the others. These umbels ripen in July. As the seed does not retain its germinative capacity for even one year, it should be sown as soon as it matures. DISEASES, PESTS. - Few diseases attack this plant, and, because of its rather strong odor, snails are not very fond of it. USES. - Its foliage is used like common chervil. In Spain, where it is generally grown, it is preferred. In France, it is little known and little cultivated. The plant turns yellow. COMESTIBLE MUSHROOM Agaricus campestris Lin. Agaricus alutarius Pers. Amanita edulis Lamk. ORIGIN. - Absolutely unknown. CULTIVATION. - In recent years, the mushroom has been widely cultivated. Not so long ago, the mushroom was grown only in and around Paris. The catacombs of Paris are particularly well-suited to this type of cultivation; in France, there are other quarries where success is just as certain: Chancelade, near Périgueux, Langoiran, in the Gironde, Mouthiers, Saint-Même, Bompart, Giget, near Angoulême, in the Charente. The latter have not yet been used for mushroom cultivation. It's astonishing that no industrialist or market-gardener has tried. For our part, we have no doubts about success. Although the mushroom is best cultivated in quarries where the temperature is more or less regular, it can nevertheless be grown successfully in cellars, caves, caverns or any dark place, and can also be grown in the shade outdoors from March to October. Wherever you choose to establish a mushroom bed, the key to success is to choose good horse or mule manure, well impregnated with urine, from strong, vigorous, well-fed animals that work hard and receive little bedding. Luxury horses, or those fed on green fodder or bran, produce poor manure, unsuitable for this crop. When leaving the stable, place the manure in a heap 70 to 80 centimetres wide and 50 centimetres high, close to where you intend to place the millstones; when heaping the manure, divide it well and discard any large straw. Tamp it down and water lightly, preferably on the inside, to prevent it from heating up. Eight or ten days after this second operation, the manure should be ready for grinding, brown in color, soft to the touch and creamy. The millstones are given a width of 60 to 70 centimeters, and a height of about the same at the top. They are then mounted in the shape of a humpback or slope, decreasing steadily until the desired height is reached. Once set up, the sides are combed with the spread-tine rake or fork, and the sides and top are beaten with the back of the shovel or the beater. If you're working in the open air, cover with a layer of litter or straw to prevent the surface from drying out too quickly; indoors, this precaution is unnecessary. Grindstones can be mounted in a variety of ways, either on square or half-square planks that can be leaned against each other and moved at will, or in tubs, half-barrels, wooden crates, etc., all of which make little difference to production. However, the two-slope grindstone generally gives the best results. You can see that the millstones have acquired the necessary degree of heat, i.e. 18 to 20 degrees centigrade, by inserting an ordinary thermometer, or even a stake thermometer. Most growers of this type use round pieces of wood, which they push into the millstone: a few hours later, they remove them and, taking them by the handful, check the heat; it takes a great deal of skill to get it right. Then the grindstones are larded, i.e. the white is introduced into them and divided into pebbles the width and thickness of the hand. With the right hand, the patties are introduced into the grinding wheel, while the manure is lifted with the left. The lardons are laid out in rows, 20 centimetres apart. The first row is placed 15 to 20 centimetres from the ground. Once the lardons are in place, the manure is put back in as before, pressing lightly with the hand. After seven or eight days, you come back to check on the operation. If the operation has been successful, you can tell by the small white filaments that are beginning to invade the manure; if any are missing, replace them, then cover the wheel with a small layer of about 3 centimetres of potting soil or kitchen garden soil, mixed in equal parts with demolition soil containing platras. The mixture is spread over all parts of the grinding stone, then tapped with the back of a hollow shovel to hold it in place; a fine apple is used to water very lightly and cover with bedding, if the grinding stone is placed outside, to prevent heavy rains from disturbing the soil; care is taken to ensure that the surface does not dry out. Fifteen or twenty days after the operation, depending on the temperature, the mushrooms begin to appear; as they are picked, the hole left by the mushroom must be filled in; a little earth is poured in. Every two days at least, you have to come and pick them. If you know how to maintain it, it can last for two or three months. Part of this maintenance consists in watering it from time to time with liquid manure, with plenty of water and a little saltpetre or guano added. If this solution can be heated to 25 or 30 degrees, success will be better, but in this case you need to water sparingly and little at a time. There are several other ways to grow. Although we haven't tried them, we feel we should reproduce them here. Dr. Labordette's method consists in replacing manure with lime sulfate or plaster stone, which is well compacted and to which is added a sufficient quantity of potassium nitrate; mushrooms weighing 600 grams obtained by this method are quoted. The ancients cultivated the mushroom, but by certain procedures that seem dubious and bizarre to us. A fig tree stump covered with manure was often sprinkled with a mixture of wine and water, or a black poplar stump, or the soil was frequently sprinkled with water in which laurel berries had been boiled. The Chinese, in order to obtain several species that are good to eat, place pieces of rotten bark and wood from poplar, elm, chestnut, mulberry and other species in good soil and in a suitable exposure; after a few weeks spent in this state, the mushrooms appear. Coffee grounds, when rotted and placed in a damp place under suitable conditions, give rise to an agaric that is highly prized in Naples. In Italy, blocks of a stone called Pietra fungaja are sold. It consists of hardened earth, mixed with black ramifications, and has been recognized as the mycelium of a species of boletus, Boletus tuberaster, which is much sought-after in Naples and other countries. These stones, placed in a cellar and watered, produce a harvest of mushrooms overnight. These blocks sell at a high price and are exported as far north as France, but they degenerate and, in most of France, a greenhouse would be indispensable for this type of cultivation. Here's another method: You need: 1° a wheelbarrow of cow dung without straw; 2° a jar of mushroom spawn, saltpetre or nitre; 3° a wooden box without a lid, one metre long and 50 centimetres wide. The dung is left to dry in the shade for two weeks, then piled into the box in a heap less wide and less long than the box, leaving about 15 centimetres all around. The dung is spread in layers by hand, mixed with good fine earth and sprinkled with a little water in which the saltpetre has been sprayed. The finished wheel will be about 10 or 12 centimeters high, and will contain all the saltpetre; it should be firmly packed. The white is then spread out, without breaking it up too much, and covered with dung, followed by 3 centimetres of earth. Tamp it all down, place it in the dark in a cellar, under a staircase, anywhere, cover it with a straw liner and you're done. Six weeks later, the layer is covered with mushrooms, which continue to grow for about a year, after which it's demolished and a new one is made with the white that has invaded the dung. When the layer is too dry, water lightly, but excess moisture is very bad. USES. - Every year, several families perish as victims of carelessness or good faith. Age, temperament, the degree of development of the mushrooms, the environment in which they have grown (the method of preparation, the length of time they have been eaten, the quantity ingested, etc.), all influence their properties. Cases have been reported of people experiencing symptoms of poisoning after eating small quantities of mushrooms that others had eaten in abundance without experiencing anything. In most cases, fear caused all the harm. Some species, eaten even in considerable quantities, cause only discomfort, heaviness and swelling; others produce weakness, stupor and temporary delirium; but unfortunately, too many are subtle poisons. Nausea, vomiting, collapse, stupor, convulsions and death are the symptoms of mushroom poisoning. As soon as these symptoms appear, a doctor should be called, but until he or she arrives, a vomiting agent or, better still, a vomit-purgative should be administered as a matter of urgency; if the appropriate help has not been given in time, or if the accidents only manifest themselves a few hours after ingestion, a purgative should be used, for example a potion made with castor oil and nerprum or peach blossom syrup, enemas made with cassia, senna and magnesia sulphate, or failing that, a strong decoction of tobacco. Vinegar, ether or salt water should be avoided, as they would spread the poison throughout the economy! 1 See Gautier, Les Champignons considérés dans leurs rapports avec la médecine, l'hygiène publique et privée, l'agriculture et l'industrie, 1884, 1 vol. gr. in-8 with color plates. E. BERGER, Vegetable plants. Mushrooms can be prepared in a number of ways: the best and most widespread is Bordeaux-style, at least for ceps. After the mushrooms have been washed and cleaned in various waters, they are drained and wiped dry with a white cloth, browned on the grill to dry out their water, then plunged into a frying pan full of boiling olive oil, butter or fat (oil is preferable). Then add the necessary condiments: garlic, pepper, salt, parsley, etc. This is a highly sought-after dish, and all mushrooms can be prepared in this way. Mushrooms are preserved by laying them out for a few hours in an oven, or in the sun to dry. Dried, these mushrooms are used to flavour sauces, salmis and stews; they can also be preserved in salted water, in vinegar for flavouring, or in olive oil. Cultivated mushrooms are widely used as food in London, Vienna, St. Petersburg and especially Paris. CHENIL Pulses. ORIGIN. - Indigenous and annual, it grows in uncultivated fields, old abandoned artificial meadows and, preferably, in stony, arid soils. CULTIVATION. - Cultivation is extremely simple. Sow in place from the end of March to May, preferably in a border, in a sunny spot. Weed and hoe several times during the summer, and water slightly during hot spells. VARIETIES. - Four different types: Large caterpillar (Scor piurus vermiculata L.). - Small caterpillar (Scorpiurus muricata L.). - Striped caterpillar (Scorpiurus sulcata L.). - Hairy caterpillar (Scorpiurus subvillosa L.). USES. - The fruits, which resemble certain caterpillars, are put in salads to provide surprises for people who don't know them. CHERVIS or GIROLLES Sium Sisarum Lin. Umbelliferes. ORIGIN. - A perennial plant native to Upper Asia, De Candolle says it grows spontaneously in Altaic Siberia and Northern Persia. It is doubtful, he says, that it was discovered in China or the Himalayas, and it is also doubtful that the ancient Greeks or Romans knew this plant. Littré derives chervis from the Spanish chirivia, but it is more likely that the latter derives from the French. One of its varieties has been cultivated in China since time immemorial, under the name Ninzy. It enjoys a great reputation there, and is said to revive the vital forces. Introduced to our shores around 1548, chervis soon spread to gardens and fields. Its roots were served as a delicacy. Linnaeus reports that in his day, chervis was grown in most gardens; later, it was dethroned by the potato, and since 1869, it has been rehabilitated. CULTIVATION. - Chervis is propagated by sowing seeds in March and April, or in August, September and October, either in the field or in a nursery. When the seedlings are strong enough, they can be planted in well-tilled, cool soil, with copious watering and frequent hoeing throughout the summer. You can also propagate chervis by splitting off the stem and keeping part of the crown, treating them like plants from seed. The resulting plants are less attractive than those grown from seed. The roots are not affected by frost. They should therefore be pulled out only as needed. SEEDS. - Chervis goes to seed in the first year, but the seed is worthless, and is only good in the second year. It is ripe in August-September. Cut the stems, or simply the umbels, lay them in the sun for two days, beat them and winnow them. They keep well for three years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Slugs and snails are quite fond of young chervis plants. Keep a close eye on them and destroy them. USES. - The root is eaten in the same way as salsify, fried or cooked in hot water; it can also be used to make very good purées. It is said to be very healthy. The flesh is white, firm yet tender, sweet, very floury, but sometimes a little fibrous. The flavor is mild, very sweet, fragrant, with a hint of celery. According to Mr. Sacc, it is the richest in nutrients of all food roots, and is so easy to digest that it was once recommended as one of the most powerful analeptics. It can provide starch, sugar and alcohol. The leaves are highly prized by cattle. CURLED CHICORICE or ENDIVE Cichorium endivia crispa L. Compositae. ORIGIN. - Annual and biennial. This spontaneous plant grows throughout the region centered on the Mediterranean, from Madeira, Morocco and Algeria to Palestine, the Caucasus and Turkestan. It is especially common on the Mediterranean islands and in Greece. On the western side, in Spain and Madeira for example, it is likely to have become naturalized through cultivation, as it grows in fields and along roadsides. There is no positive evidence in ancient texts of the use of this plant by the Greeks and Romans, but it is probable that they used it as they did several other chicories. The vulgar names give no indication, as they may have been applied to both species of Cichorium. They are not very varied, and lead us to presume a culture that has emerged from the Greco-Roman environment; one Hindu name is cited, Kasni and Tamul Koschi, but no Sanskrit name, which indicates a late extension of the culture to the East (De Candolle). EARLY CROPS. - The first curly chico. rees are sown from mid-December to March, on warm beds. The seed should emerge in twenty-four or thirty-six hours at most, so that the plant grows more slowly. If the seed germinates slowly, the chicory will rise immediately before developing. Once emerged, if sunburn occurs, give the panels some air to prevent the young plants from withering. When they have seven or eight leaves, transplant them permanently onto another 40-centimeter-thick layer, previously filled with 12 to 15 centimeters of a mixture of half potting soil, half sifted 3. garden soil. Plant at 15 to 20 centimetres in all directions, lightly water each plant and cover with a frame. Once the chicories have recovered, which takes two days, you can start to give them a little air, weather permitting, because in addition to the loss of growth caused by lack of air, chicories grown under frames are subject to a fungal disease, Peronospora gan- gliformis, whose development is favored by the concentration of air. Chicory can also be grown under cloches. Once a bed has been established, it is edged and loaded with the same mixture as described above. Place the cloches in position and plant five chicory plants under each one. Cover with straw mats in cold weather and keep warm. Radishes can be sown in the spaces left between each bell. OUTDOOR CULTIVATION. - Curly endive can be grown in any soil, but does best in slightly damp, well-fertilized soils. In dry soils, it requires a lot of watering. Sow outdoors from April to July. For the first sowings, choose a good exposure, and for summer sowings, a half-shaded exposure, if possible. Once the plants have developed, and are strong rather than weak, trim off half the leaves and the root tips, mainly the pivot, and place them in ploughed, smoked soil. If you use a hoe, the distance to be observed between each plant is 25 centimetres in all directions; cover the plants up to about half their total length, and water. Each bed usually consists of five or ten rays. In many parts of France, chicory is transplanted in 1.20-meter beds, divided into five or six rows. They are then planted with a planter, at 20 or 25 centimeters depending on the variety. For us, here's the way we've always done it: after the soil has been well ploughed and fertilized, we mark out with our feet the divisions of the beds, which should be 1.25 m wide; we sow radishes or lettuce to be cut, we harrow this sowing well, then we mark out six lines and plant the chicory. Radishes or lettuce are best harvested and removed before harming the chicory; after complete removal of these plants, a good hoeing is given, repeated at intervals of a few days, and copious watering is given if the soil is dry. Some authors recommend mulching the beds before planting; this is an excellent method that gives very good results and should not be neglected, especially in dry soils. However, no matter how practical, this method can only be applied to small areas of land: if large-scale growers were to mulch, the operation would become costly; we recommend keeping the soil surface constantly moist and sufficiently loosened. When chicories have reached the right stage of development, they are blanched. There are several ways of blanching chicory: gardeners in Bordeaux tie them with straw or rushes several times, always starting at the base; a few days later, the top is tied; chicory tied in this way whitens better than if it were tied all at once. In the Charente region, as in many other places, chicory is tied in a single operation; once all the leaves have been brought back vertically, a tie is passed over them and attached a few centimetres above about half the length of the leaves. This procedure is more expeditious than the previous one, in the sense that you don't have to come back to it. Other growers bleach them in the ground; they open a slanting trench, in the direction of the chessboard planting, bring all the lettuce leaves in, as if to bind them, and lay them in the trench more or less horizontally; they uproot a little, but never entirely; they are held in this state with the left hand, and the tool, held with the right hand, brings the earth over them: The soil from the second trench fills in the first, and so on; by this means, they blanch very well in eight or ten days, and are very tender. You can pull them out entirely and place them elsewhere in a trench, but they rot more easily. They can also be covered with mats, leaves, litter, etc., just as they can be uprooted and, after being thoroughly cleaned, placed side by side in the sand in a cellar or storeroom. Whichever method is chosen, the lettuces must be well wiped dry, otherwise the concen- tration of moisture in the heart will lead to fermentation and rotting of the outer leaves. Chicory can also be preserved well into winter, either by covering it during the frosts with heather, fern, straw, etc., which is removed during the day and put back in place in the evening, or with windbreaks, straw mats, wooden panels, or chests, so as to cover a few planks; this method of preservation only applies to northern regions where the temperature drops very low. In the Midi, this precaution becomes unnecessary, as plants do not freeze. VARIETIES. - Summer or Italian fine chicory. - Early variety, medium size, very old, leaves in tight rosettes, cut in the upper half. Suitable for first-season and early-season seedlings, it doesn't keep well in the late season and rots quickly. It is preferred over fine summer chicory (race d'Anjou), with tighter, more finely cut leaves and a larger, more rounded rosette. Chicorée frisée de Rouen (fig. 13). - An excellent variety, forming large, very full rosettes that blanch easily almost by themselves, provided they are planted close to FIO. 13 - Curly chicory from Rouen is grown in large quantities around Paris, as well as in the North and South of France; in Bordeaux, it is much appreciated at markets. Well suited to late crops, it is highly recommended. Chicorée frisée béglaise. - Excellent variety, derived from Rouen curly chicory and improved by market gardeners in Bègles, near Bordeaux; very full rosette, reaching 35 to 40 centimeters, whitens very well, not very sensitive to frost, highly recommendable in all respects. Chicorée de Louviers. - Another variety apparently derived from the fine Rouen chicory; very full, tight, rounded rosette, not very large, whitens well and keeps well into the late season. Chicorée mousse. - Variety forming a small rosette, leaves finely cut, curled, frizzy, tight, crisp, narrow white ribs; produces little, and must be planted fairly close together; particularly suitable for cultivation under panels or cloches. Chicorée frisée de Ruffec. - A variety highly esteemed in the West, with very ample rosettes, thick, tender, fleshy ribs, and a full heart that whitens well and in a short time; perfectly suited to summer and fall cultivation; we know of no other variety that is more resistant to cold, says M. Vilmorin; highly recommendable. Chicorée frisée de Meaux. - Very hardy variety, voluminous, 50 to 60 centimeters in diameter, very large ribs, tinted pink in their lower parts, long, erect leaves, loosely packed heart; gives a considerable yield, suitable for institutions for the insane or hospitals, does well in autumn. Picpus curly chicory. - Another very good variety for outdoor cultivation, somewhat similar in appearance to Chicorée de Meaux, from which it probably originated; tight rosette, very full heart, very curly, finely shredded leaves; does very well in dry soils. Chicorée frisée impériale. - White variety, wide rosettes, tall and full, finely-cut, evenly-curled leaves, upright, broad ribs; almost self-blanching, good for summer; in autumn, it is the first to suffer from uredo. Curly chicory always white. - Low yield, sparsely packed rosette, narrow ribs, well-cut, lacy, pink-washed leaves, pale yellow in color, giving it an etiolated appearance. Chicorée bâtarde de Bordeaux. - Intermediate breed between escarole and chicory, highly esteemed in the Southwest, very vigorous; rosette 50 to 60 cm in diameter or more, long, broad, erect, lobed leaves, loosely packed heart, whitens well when tied several times, good for growing in large establishments. Performs well in autumn, fears disease. Curly chicory, large pancalier. - A very good, new variety with a fairly full heart and broad, upright curly leaves that turn white without being tied. Chicorée frisée fine de Guillande. - New variety sent to us in 1886 by MM. Dupanloup et Cie, grain merchants in Paris; like the previous one, it is good for winter cultivation. Winter chicory queen. - A new variety with broad, curly, shredded leaves, it does well in winter in the Southwest. Winter curly chicory. - Breed very similar to fine Guillande chicory; stores well in winter; does not grow very large, but has a full heart. SEEDS. - To harvest chicory seed, sow in February or March, in warm soil, or even in the open ground in good exposure. When the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted in June. Select the most beautiful, well-stocked plants from the heart, and uproot the others. By August-September, the seeds are ripe. The stems are pulled out when they begin to yellow and dry out. They are laid out in the sun for a few days, shaded if possible, after which they are threshed. As the seed is difficult to detach from its receptacle, they should be soaked several hours before threshing, or even the day before. They keep well for nine or ten years. One gram contains around 600 grams. DISEASES, PESTS. - Every year, we have had to fight uredo, the same disease found on full-grown celery, generally known as rust. This parasite usually appears towards the end of August or the first fortnight of September, and almost always after alternating periods of rain and very hot sun. A few days later, all the leaves are invaded by powdery red spots, followed by rot, and chicory is done for. All that remains of the leaves is the toughest part of the midrib. This fungus spreads very quickly, so as soon as a few plants are found to be affected, they are uprooted and bleached underground. We tried several remedies: the one that gave us the best results was the following: 2 kilograms of fat lime diluted in 10 liters of water, add 500 grams of well-pulverized soot, mix everything together and brush each diseased foot. Repeating this treatment several times will eliminate the disease. The same mixture works well on celery. Peronos pora glangliformis or meunier rarely attacks open-ground plantations. Only under-frame crops are sometimes affected, but this parasite acts less intensely on chicory than on lettuce, which it destroys entirely. In soils where there are many white grubs or Turkish worms, it is not uncommon to see several chicories whose roots have been devoured by this insect. Unfortunately, we don't notice its ravages until the damage is done; but it seems that we've just discovered the remedy for this scourge; this remedy, called Botrytis tenella, is a cryptogam that attacks the worm and destroys it completely in a few days. All you have to do is put a few contaminated worms in the soil, and all those present will quickly be affected. The fungus is said to grow very well in boiled potato mashed potato. We even sell tubes in various doses, ready-made for a certain area of land. According to the authors, the remedy is infallible and always effective. When they are numerous in a plot of land, mites or fumaroles, known as bars in the Bordeaux region, wreak havoc by cutting the root between two plots of land and digging numerous galleries. They must therefore be destroyed. The roots of some chicories, particularly escarole, are also sometimes host to innumerable greyish aphids, which suck the root and halt the plant's development. Several remedies have been tried, but they were more damaging to the plant than the plague itself. Water the base of the plant with soot water, mixed with a little fat lime; this is the only harmless remedy for the plant that has given us good results. Phenic acid strongly mixed with water has been mentioned, but this remedy imparts a bad odor to plants. USES. - After being blanched, chicory is mainly used as a salad. It also goes very well with all kinds of meat, especially mutton and pork, in white sauces made with milk or butter; it is also an excellent seasoning for roast chicken. E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 8 Leaves are aperitif, tonic and refreshing. SCAROLE CHICORY Cichorium endivia latifolia Lin. Compositae. ORIGIN. - Same origin as curly endive, of which it is only a broad-leafed variety. CULTIVATION. - Scarole is grown in much the same way as chicory. It is sown from June to September, to be eaten in autumn and part of winter. It makes an excellent salad, tender and tasty; it is less sensitive to frost than chicory: some varieties survive the winter very well without suffering too much. Blanched underground, it is excellent and very tender. VARIETIES. - Autumn green round chicory. - Excellent early variety, well-filled, tightly packed heart, almost headless, wide rosettes, thick whole leaves, toothed, wavy at the edges, wide, thick, white, crunchy and very tender ribs. It is fairly hardy, withstanding cold temperatures of 4 or 6 degrees. It is one of our best varieties, albeit an old one. Chicory from Limay. - More voluminous than the previous variety, with ample, waffled leaves, broad, thick ribs and a fairly full, but not very tight, heart. Well-bleached, it flatters the eye, but is tougher than the previous variety. In good, moist soil, it produces a lot, becoming very large. Not yet widespread. Blonde chicory with lettuce leaves. - Rosette slightly wider than round escarole, very sensitive to humidity in the late season, broad, upright leaves, slightly wavy and waffled, a little leathery, heart not very tight. This variety needs to be planted close together, so that it can bleach on its own. Less hardy than the previous two, very sensitive to uredo. Turkish endive. - This variety, improperly known as béglaise escarole, is widespread throughout the West and South-West. It's one of our main winter salads, as it doesn't freeze easily in these regions. The leaves are almost as broad as they are long, with numerous long teeth along the edges. The inner leaves, folded inwards, form a kind of apple, sometimes very hard; blanched, they are tender. Sowing in September provides good planting in October-November. You can even plant later, harvesting in March-April, at the same time as winter lettuces. SEEDS. - As escarole can survive the winter without suffering too much, in the south-western departments and part of western France, it is planted very late in November with a view to harvesting the seed; these plants come up in the spring and the harvest is very abundant. In other regions, where the cold is more rigorous, we sow very early on the bed, as for chicory. MAlADIES, PEST ANIMALS. - Uredo invades round green escarole and blond leaf lettuce very easily, especially if cold rains persist for a few days. Same treatment as for chicory. USES. - Same uses as curly endive. WILD CHICORY Cichorium Intybus Lin. Compositae. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to France and Europe, as well as northern Africa and western Asia, cultivated for nearly two thousand years, known as peasant's hair, écoubette. GROWING. - Wild chicory is very hardy, thriving in almost any well-amended soil. However, it thrives best in deep soils, where its long roots are at ease. It can be sown from March to July; April and May sowings are the best, and are the ones to choose especially, as the plants do not bolt. In gardens, it can be planted in borders and flowerbeds, sown in nurseries or in the field in shallow rays, about 3 centimetres wide and 20 to 25 centimetres apart. When the young plants have four or five well-developed leaves, they are thinned out if sown in place, and transplanted if sown in nurseries. Transplanted plants are always more beautiful than those sown in situ. The first light hoeing is carried out as soon as the soil hardens on the surface. Repeat the operation several times during the year, watering whenever necessary. Nursery plants are transplanted at 10 or 12 centimetres from the line, and if the soil is good, you'll get remarkably beautiful plants that produce a lot of leaves. Despite its bitter taste and roughness, wild chicory is much sought-after as a salad; it should be picked young to make it more tender and less bitter. There are several ways to grow capuchin beard in winter, a highly prized salad in Paris, northern France and even abroad. Here are a few of the most practical. We start blanching in the second half of November. To this end, a small layer of good horse or donkey manure is prepared in a dark cellar or storeroom. We pull up the chicories, and bundle them into bundles of about twenty feet each, holding the collars at exactly the same FIG. 14. - Bleached Capuchin beard. height. Securely tied with wicker, they are placed upright on the bed, side by side, the edges are formed with manure, and as the bundles are placed, the gaps are filled with the same manure or with potting soil. When you've finished placing the bunches, spread a few shovelfuls of potting soil over them, then water. After fifteen or twenty days, the chicory is ready for harvesting, and if you take care to cut the leaves flush with the collar without damaging it, it grows a second time; in Paris, it is usually sold with the root (fig. 14). When vegetation is exhausted, the bed can be reshuffled, a little new manure added, and other chicory planted again. If you don't have a cellar (which is more practical for gardeners), you can work under a frame. Once a good layer has been made, the boxes are placed, and the chicory planted as we have indicated. The panels are then placed and covered with straw mats and leaves, to provide the maximum shade necessary for wilting. Here's a method we think is worth quoting1. In the second half of June, sow wild chicory in rays 30 centimetres apart, with enough light in each ray for the plants to gain sufficient strength; during vegetation, water and hoe as much as necessary. For blanching in the cellar, I use either of the following two systems: either a frame made of four planks, 1 meter long and 1.50 or 2 meters high, which you lean upright against the wall in a dark place in the cellar, or one or more barrels (even barrels that have contained cement) which you drill with several rows of holes 2 to 3 centimeters in diameter and 7 or 8 centimeters apart. In November, a quantity of roots proportionate to consumption needs is pulled up. The root tips and leaves are cut off 2 or 3 centimetres1 Manuel pratique des jardins et des champs, by M. Daurel (Moniteur d'horticulture de Paris). from the collar. If using a frame, close the front of the frame, starting at the bottom, with a ceiling batten. Fill with soil up to the top of this lath, then place the roots horizontally on this layer of soil, with the collar in front and 4 or 5 centimetres apart; place a second lath on top of the first, leaving a sufficient gap between them to allow the shoots to pass through, cover with a second layer of soil on which roots are placed; continue in this way up to the top of the frame. If barrels are used, a layer of soil is also placed at the bottom, up to the level of the first row of holes, which are then filled with roots, with the collar slightly protruding, so that the settling of the soil doesn't drag them inside; soil and roots continue to be placed right up to the top of the barrel, which is, of course, ripped open. The barrels, like the frames, should be placed in the darkest corner of the cellar. In January, new frames or barrels can be set up, providing abundant salad over the winter. Although this procedure is said to give good results, we believe that success can be achieved by operating in the following way. Start by placing a thin layer of new manure mixed with annealed manure in a dark cellar, arrange it in a circle and trample it down hard, then place bunches of chicory in a pyramid shape: as you place the bunches, fill in the gaps with manure, which you must pound well with your hand. The layers are placed one on top of the other, with a slight gap between them. Once the pyramid is set up, you finish by throwing in a few shovelfuls of earth, then watering the whole thing. In this way, you can make heaps of any height and size; it's a good process that we recommend to amateurs. In some regions, notably around Paris, growers don't pull up chicory. In winter, they cover with heather, straw, manure or soil taken from paths, to a depth of 4 or 6 centimeters. When the chicory has grown sufficiently, we cut it between two plots of land, as we would an asparagus. VARIETIES. - Wild bitter chicory from Paris. - Variety closest to the type and probably the source of all other varieties: long, hairy, strongly toothed leaves, with acute lobes, sail- lant ribs, green or red. Placed in suitable soil, it produces many leaves, which can be cut several times during the summer, and are eaten either green or dry by cattle. It is the most esteemed and the best for making capuchin beard. Improved wild chicory. - Differs greatly from ordinary wild chicory in its broad leaves, much like a cone-shaped escarole; it is dulled and blistered, forming a small apple that is sometimes compact, fairly hard and tender; it is hardy and produces a lot. This variety was bred by M. Jacquin aîné; until now, it was thought to be a cross with escarole chicory. M. Vilmorin? claims the opposite, and we share his opinion. It's a highly recommendable variety which, in certain cases, can be blanched like chicory, and from which you can obtain ex-cellent nasturtium beard. 1 Vilmorin, Les, Plantes potagères. Improved variegated wild chicory. - Derived from the previous variety, it has the same characteristics, with the only difference being that the leaves are flagellated and spotted with reddish-brown. This color is brighter due to the plant's darker development, making it a very pretty salad when blanched. Improved curly wild chicory. - A variety that appears to be a cross between improved wild chicory and curly chicory; the leaves are lacy, cut, almost hairless. Do not sow these varieties before May, as they bolt. Among the large-rooted varieties, we'd like to mention: Chicorée à grosse racine de Brunsvick. - A variety with cut, almost spreading, long leaves and a large, long root. It is grown extensively in Germany and Belgium to make chicory coffee. Magdeburg large-root chicory. - A variety with whole, erect leaves, with few or no teeth, and very large, long, smooth roots. It is widely grown in northern France for the preparation of chicory coffee. Brussels or Withloof chicory. - A variety derived from Magdeburg chicory, widely grown in Belgium, where it is one of the main winter salads. We've grown it for a long time, and it has always given us magnificent results, so we have no hesitation in recommending its cultivation. It is grown in the same way as Paris bitter chicory: at the start of winter, it is uprooted and cleaned, with any inferior or undersized plants set aside. The leaves are cut off 3 or 4 millimeters above the collar; any shoots that have grown up around the9. collar are removed, and the root tips are cut off, reducing them to roughly equal length. Place them in a trench 40 or 50 centimetres deep, into which 20 centimetres of loose soil or compost has already been placed. They are planted in this potting soil 8 centimetres apart, with the collar at exactly the same height, and 15 or 20 centimetres below the top level of the trench, which must be filled with the mixture mentioned above. If you want them to start growing, spread 40 centimetres or more of good horse manure on the surface, depending on the temperature; three weeks later, you can start harvesting: to do this, remove the manure, dig up the root, and cut off the blanched apple, keeping a small portion of the neck. Roots replanted after this operation, and treated like capuchin beard, still grow and develop several excellent stems. Withloof makes an excellent, tender and appetizing salad. The regularity of its growth, which forms a small, tightly-packed head, makes it more appreciated than capuchin beard. In the public establishment where we ran the market garden for nearly a decade, we operated in a very simple way for blanching our withloof, and here's how: The chicory plants were pulled out of the ground as needed, prepared and placed side by side on a warm, fairly thick layer of fresh manure, mixed with a few leaves. The plants were buried up to the collar, either in sand, light loam, tannin, wood bran, etc... Once placed, they were watered, then covered with 10 or 15 centimetres of dry leaves or moss. The panels were placed on the trunk, and covered with double or triple mats to prevent daylight penetration; eight days later, harvesting began. It's a very quick process, and suitable for all crops. Here's how Mr. Poiret Délan! proceeds. In August, I cut the leaves 3 or 4 centimetres above the crown: these leaves can be eaten in salads, or fed to rabbits. When the time comes to take advantage of them, which is from November to March, I pull up my chicory, clean the roots, leaving them only 20 centimetres long; I cut the leaves about 5 centimetres from the collar, then place these roots upright under a bell, one against the other, in potting soil, on a pile of leaves. Even better is a mixture of manure and leaves which, through light fermentation, produces a temperature of 10 to 15 degrees centigrade, which activates the work. To make it easier for the plants to wither, the cloches are covered with manure or leaves. Under these conditions, this particular state of etiolation is achieved in twelve or fifteen days. Care must be taken during hard frosts, so as not to run out of plants suitable for consumption. The work should be repeated every eight or fifteen days. We think this method is excellent, and particularly suitable for amateurs who don't eat much of it, because if you work as Mr. Délan indicates, you'll always have good fresh produce at your disposal. SEEDS. - Although perennial, wild chicory goes to seed in its second year. This seed is ripe in August 1 Rerue horticole. September; the stalks are cut off and exposed to the sun; when they are dry enough to detach the seed from the receptacle, they are left outside overnight, to be threshed the next day, or they are wetted, and two or three hours later, they are beaten with a stick, and rubbed well between the two hands. The seed is then easily detached; it is winnowed and cleaned. One gram contains 700 seeds; it keeps well for eight years. DISEASES, PESTS. - This plant suffers from the same diseases as curly endive, and is eaten by the same insects. USES. - The large-rooted varieties are used to make chicory coffee. This type of production seems to have originated in Holland, and was introduced into Belgium towards the beginning of this century. The roots, after being boiled in various waters, can be eaten like salsify. In Sweden, the poor make bread from the root. Wild chicory is said to be excellent as animal fodder. Rabbits in particular are very fond of it, and it is a very good food for them. It can be cut several times a year, and last for eight or ten years. The whole plant turns a faint yellow. CULTIVATED CABBAGE Brassica oleracea L. Cruciferae. ORIGIN. - The typical species, with a tall, twiggy stem and glaucous, lobed, fleshy leaves, grows spontaneously on the seashore in southern England and Ireland, on the islands of Jersey and Guernsey, on the island of Laland in Denmark, on the island of Heligoland; in France, on the coasts of Normandy and Charente-Inférieure, on the northern Mediterranean coast near Nice, in Genoa and Lucca. Cultivated from time immemorial, the ancients applied it to all kinds of ailments and used it as an exclusive remedy. CULTIVATION. - Cabbage is very hardy, thriving in all types of heavily fertilized soil, and can be found just about anywhere, at a wide range of altitudes. However, it seems to do best in slightly strong, damp, slightly chalky soils. The best fertilizers for cabbage are manures mixed with fecal matter, sludge and city refuse, which should be used before decomposition. Watering each plant several times with liquid manure will produce magnificent results, even in poor-quality soils, but care must be taken when using this fertilizer, otherwise the results will not be what you expect. As a general rule, and with the right choice of varieties, cabbages can be sown almost all year round, in the open ground, with the exception of December and January, when it would be better to sow on a bed and then transplant to a good exposure. But the best seedlings, those which give the best results, and which are the most widely used in the South-West, are spring seedlings for the curly kite breed, and autumn seedlings for the cabbage breed - a rule adopted throughout France. Cabbage seedlings are usually planted in beds 1.20 m wide. They are broadcast a little thickly if you intend to transplant them, and less thickly if you intend to leave them on the bed. Lightly cover them with a fork and then with a rake, or spread 1/2 centimeter of soil taken from paths or from the ground over the beds. If sowing in hot weather, spread a light mulch over the beds and water often. When the plants are strong enough, and the first two or three leaves begin to appear, they are transplanted into beds, in rows 8 centimetres apart in all directions. Water as you go along. As far as possible, this transplanting work should be done when the weather is dark or in the evening, when the sun is less bright. It's a very good operation, which is almost essential for this type of plant, at least if you want to obtain beautiful, well-branched, strong plants with lots of hair. When the operation is carried out in very hot weather, it is urgent to shade the plants with windbreaks to facilitate recovery. The ground where the transplanting is to take place should be well ploughed and smoked beforehand, and the surface loosened with a rake. In many parts of France, such as Angoumois, Saintonge, Lot, Vienne, etc., market gardeners plant cabbages permanently with a forked or flat hoe; they form large rays and plant in alternate rays, so that the cabbages are 60 or 70 centimetres apart in all directions and in a chessboard pattern. Scarole, romaine, lettuce or any other vegetable is counter-planted in the false rays and between each cabbage, and removed before harming the cabbages. Smoke when planting, after first spreading the manure on the soil, and when making the comb, slide it into the bottom with one side of the tool. Consumed fertilizers should be used whenever possible. Old market gardeners claim that by doing this, the cabbages become much more beautiful. It's usually the women who place the cabbages. Those who are used to this kind of work are very quick. As soon as the cabbage is in place, they quickly pull a handful of loose soil over the roots, which they take from the bottom of the shelf, to hold the plant in place. A good racker can, without too much fatigue, give work to three people. As soon as the cabbages are covered, they are watered with the spout. Sometimes, in summer, when it's hot, we water a little on the soil, once it's been placed on the roots, but this doesn't prevent us from watering a lot once the cabbage has been buried. Planting with a planter is much quicker, and gives equally good results. The soil is well fertilized and ploughed, then radii are traced at 60 centimetres or more, depending on the species, then planted and watered, taking care not to pound the soil too much when doing the work. This is the way we've always done it, and we've never complained. Four or five weeks after planting, when the plants are well established, we give them a first deep hoeing, which must be repeated often. In areas where irrigation is possible, they should not be spared water, as they will grow all the better for the warmth and humidity they receive. DISEASES, PESTS. - Most cabbage seedlings, whatever the variety, and we could even say crucifers in general, have to suffer, especially in summer, from the bites of a small insect called the ground flea, because of its resemblance to the flea, but whose name is altise or tiquet (Altica oleracea). This little creature, which seems to prefer the young cotyledons and early leaves, pierces them through with an infinite number of tiny holes, stopping vegetation in its tracks; most affected plants succumb. There is no effective way of destroying them. We have tried several remedies, but have only succeeded in keeping them away for a while; the one that has given us the best results is this: Macerate soot in water, in which soda crystals have been dissolved; add a little plaster or lime to fix the product on the leaves, and brush with this mixture. You can also boil aromatic plants such as thyme, lavender, hyssop, wormwood, laurel, etc., mix this decoction with tobacco juice, or better still, boil the whole together, and sprinkle with this product. Another method: Throw soot, wood ash or lime on the young plants. When a cabbage seedling has suffered from drought, and vegetation has been slowed down, it is attacked at the base of the plant by a small white worm, which produces a nodule in which it is enclosed; there are sometimes quite a few on the same plant. We recommend popping them off with a fingernail or pruning-hook, and removing the insect. However, there's not much to fear from this worm, as almost all large cabbages, and kites in particular, have greater or lesser quantities, depending on the type of soil in which they are planted. Another insect that causes a lot of damage in certain years is the caterpillar. The best way to destroy them is to hunt for their eggs before they hatch; at the end of winter and summer, small butterflies of the piéride genus, with yellow wings spotted with black, flutter over the plantation and deposit their eggs, in hundreds, on the underside of the leaves; the eggs must be crushed immediately after they have been laid. USES. - Cabbage is a popular vegetable and is frequently used, especially by country dwellers. In northern Europe, it is the staple of the diet. It appears on the table of the rich and the poor alike. It is eaten cooked either lean or fatty; the classic cabbage soup is accompanied by the petit-salé of the Auvergnats; it goes very well with pigeon, goose or turkey quarters, or even partridge; it is delicious with sausages. Boiled cauliflower can be eaten au gratin, seasoned with butter, just as Brussels sprouts are delicious fried in butter. Young heads of any variety, boiled in salted water, can be eaten in vinaigrette like asparagus. Cabbage is a very healthy and restorative food, especially when prepared with meat. Robust, strong stomachs digest it easily and it does them a lot of good; but as healthy as it is for them, it is not very suitable for delicate people; it can produce serious indigestion and cause serious stomach illness. It is also forbidden to convalescents and the elderly. It is claimed that cabbage has laxative properties, and that people who use it frequently rarely need a purgative. It has been wrongly claimed, I believe, that cabbage imparts an unpleasant flavor to the milk and butter of cows fed on it. VARIETIES. - Now that we've covered the basics of general cultivation, let's move on to a description of the best varieties, highlighting the type of cultivation specific to each one. Botanists have divided cabbages into five classes: 1° Head cabbage; 2° Savoy cabbage; 3° Green cabbage; 4° Kohlrabi; 5° Cauliflower. HEAD cabbages Brassica capitata Hort. Cabbages in this class generally have a very large stalk, swollen in the middle; the leaves are fairly broad and smooth; the veins are prominent and numerous, with the median vein always well-developed and extending to the top of the leaf blade; glaucous in color, with a metallic sheen in some varieties (York oxheart). CULTIVATION. - Can be sown all year round, but the best time is July, August and September. They are planted as soon as the seedling is good. In ordinary winters, when the temperature doesn't drop above 8 degrees centigrade, they resist perfectly, and when the sun comes out, they develop rapidly. They can even be left in the nursery and not planted until spring; they do just as well, but are less early. Varieties such as Schweinfurt, Quintal, Bonneuil, Joanet, etc., have been very successful for us, sown in spring, and have produced magnificent products in autumn. For summer sowings, the varieties known as Bacalan gros, Bacalan hâtif and Bonneuil are those preferred by market gardeners. VARIETIES. - Varieties in this class are, in order of earliness: Chou d'Yorh petit and gros. - Two distinct varieties, rather small than large. Sugarloaf cabbage. - Slightly larger than the previous one. Beef heart cabbage small (fig. 15). - Medium-sized, very good and tender. Heart of beef cabbage large. - Similar to sugarloaf cabbage. Chou Bacalan gros. - The best and most esteemed of this class, very large, tightly packed, white and rustic. Bacalan hátif is a smaller sub-variety, a few days harsher. Chou de TourFIG. 15 - Heart of beef cabbage. laville - Very similar in shape to the above, but dwarfed and with blistered folded leaves. Lingreville cabbage. - Short-stalked, with large, wavy, blistered leaves and a pointed, very white head; not very productive. Joanet or Nantais cabbage. - Very good short-stalked variety; very hard, flattened, very white head; successful, sown in autumn. Brunswick or Tabouret cabbage. - Highly esteemed variety in Angoumois and Saintonge, where it is widely grown; very short stem; large, broad apples, very flattened at the top; highly recommended variety; does very well in fall and spring. Schweinfurth cabbage. - A very good variety, highly esteemed everywhere; very large, loosely packed apples, best sown in spring; the highest-yielding variety. Particularly suitable for large establishments, hospitals, asylums, etc. It is grown in large quantities in the departments of Vienne, Haute-Vienne and Loire-Inférieure, which ship them in batches to the Paris markets. Chou quintal. - Variety closely resembling Schweinfurth cabbage, but flatter; very large, firm head; a little late, but very high yielding; most widely grown in Germany for making sauerkraut, Pomeranian conical cabbage. - Late, high stem, cone-shaped, very tight and fairly large head, very productive, does well sown in spring; widely grown in Germany. Winigstadt pointed cabbage. - Similar in shape to oxheart cabbage; tight-knit, medium-sized head, late; does well in southwestern France, sown in autumn like Bacalan cabbage. Vaugirard cabbage. - Very hardy, withstands winter well without suffering too much; highly esteemed at Paris markets. Growers around Paris sow it late, in June, so that it does not begin to peel until after the frosts. Dax cabbage. - A highly esteemed variety grown extensively in the Landes and Pyrenees regions; similar in habit to the Milan cabbage of virtues; round, loosely packed, medium-sized head. Semi-tardy. Habas cabbage. - An excellent variety, widely grown in the Agenais region. It resembles the previous variety in shape; the head is larger, tighter and forms more quickly. Several other varieties of cabbage are also grown, which appear excellent and produce magnificent products in their country of origin, but which give very poor results elsewhere. We mention the following varieties as having been cultivated: Chou de Fumel or female cabbage. - Produces very fine products in Hérault and Haute-Garonne. Chou rouge gros, chou rouge petit, chou vert glacé d'Amérique, chou marbré de Bourgogne, chou rouge petit d'Utrecht, chou rouge conique, chou rouge foncé hâtif d'Erfurth, chou très hâtif d'Étampes. Among the new varieties introduced to the market in recent years, we should mention: Express cabbage, non-pareil cabbage, early Rennes cabbage, Christmas cabbage, Amager cabbage. MILAN CROW Brassica oleracea bullata. Race with more or less blistered, wrinkled or curled, intense green leaves (fig. 16); prominent midrib, acuminate in the upper part, rarely obtuse; the secondary veins are strongly apparent in some varieties and extend to the upper part of the leaf blade. The apples generally have less consistency than cabbages and are smaller, better tasting and more tender, especially when the first frosts have taken away the green taste. This breed of cabbage is more highly esteemed than cabbages, as they are less musky. CULTIVATION. - April and May are the best months to sow savoy cabbages. They are broadcast in beds. Mulch and water lightly to maintain constant soil moisture. When the plant is strong enough, it is thinned out and transplanted into a nursery. These cabbages are good for planting from June to August. 16 - Savoy cabbage. Early varieties can be sown as early as March for final planting in May and June. In summer, however, these varieties need plenty of water or cool soil to reach full growth. VARIETIES. - We will list them in order of earliness: Very early Savoy cabbage. - Very good, early variety, still little known, medium-sized head. Highly recommended. Small, early Ulm Savoy cabbage. - Variety with a small head, fairly high stem, strongly blistered leaves; very early, but not very productive; suitable for first-season planting. Very early Savoy cabbage from Paris. - Early variety, widely grown around Paris; medium-sized, firm head; glossy, dark-green leaves; very good for the first winter season (fig. 16). Early short savoy cabbage. - One of our best winter varieties. Short stalk, broad, finely blistered, dark-green leaves, firm, depressed head. This cabbage is very hardy and has good flavor. It is highly regarded at the market. Ordinary Milan cabbage. - An old variety that still has its merit; tall stalk, glaucous-green, slightly broad, blistered leaves, medium-sized, fairly firm head; highly esteemed in southwestern and western France, not very fussy about soil quality; excellent taste. Chou de Milan gros des vertus, also known as chou de Milan gros frisé d'Allemagne or chou de Paris - Very old variety, grown all over France; considerable consumption; tall stalk, large leaves, blistered at the top, dark glaucous green; fairly tight, flattened head. A very good winter breed. Milan cabbage, Pontoise late winter. - A tall variety with broadly blistered, glaucous, dark-green leaves and a large, very hard, late-ripening head. Generally formed in winter, it is the successor to Milan cabbage. This variety is said to be the type of the old Milan cabbage that Parisian market gardeners have improved. It's an excellent cabbage that we have no hesitation in recommending. Norwegian savoy cabbage. - Very late variety, fairly tall stem, erect, slightly blistered leaves, purplish in color, medium-sized, loosely packed head, forming very late. In winter, it withstands the most intense cold. Several other varieties are still grown, but are of little value outside their country of origin: Long-headed Savoy cabbage. - The elongated shape of the head is reminiscent of sugarloaf cabbage; it does very well in the late season. Early Savoy cabbage from Limay. - Very early and firm, very small head. Cabbage pancalier, small Joulin hâtif. - Very small head; very early. Pancalier cabbage from Tours. - Very early, but not very productive. Victoria Savoy cabbage. - Quite a good species, doing well in good, moist soil. Cape Savoy. - Very high stalk, tight head, degenerates easily, so that in a seedling there are only a few plants that pump well; fears frosts. Golden Savoy cabbage. - Short-stalked variety, medium-sized head, elongated, not very tight, yellow in color; prominent veins, very tender after the frosts have passed over it. New varieties include: Early Savoy cabbage from Aubervillers. - Early Agen cabbage. CROUX DE BRUXELLES - CHOUX ROSETTES Brussels sprouts are thought to have originated from Savoy cabbage. Its leaves, especially in the dwarf Brussels sprout, are very similar to those of the curly Savoy; they are blistered, dark green and form a small apple at the end of the stem. CULTIVATION. - Sow from March to June, in nursery beds, broadcast. The first seedlings, planted in June, will begin to produce towards the end of summer and throughout the winter. May sowings produce a little later, but the difference is so great that it is preferable to sow only once in the first half of April, and plant as soon as the seedling is good. E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 10 Cultivation is the same as for Savoy cabbage, except that the plants need to be spaced further apart so that they can pass easily between the rows and pick up the small apples; 50 to 60 centimeters is enough for the ordinary variety, and 40 to 50 centimeters for the dwarf variety. This cabbage thrives in good, moist soil, but should not be fertilized with strong fertilizers that force vegetation and cause the apples to grease, or to put it another way, prevent them from peeling. In order to encourage apple development, some growers have advised pinching the Brussels sprout once it has reached a certain height. We experimented with this procedure several times, pinching back every other row. The pinched row did not sprout earlier than the other, so we conclude that pinching reduces the yield without advancing development, so it's better not to pinch at all. Brussels sprouts are fairly cold-hardy, making them an excellent winter vegetable, very delicate and very fine, provided the apples are picked when they are firm; the smaller they are, the better they are appreciated. Brussels sprouts are not cultivated enough in France; this is a mistake, because a plot of land planted with this vegetable yields a lot. We have made comparisons, and have always found an advantage in its favor. In the establishment where we ran the cultivation for several years, we grew it in large quantities, and certainly, the production was good, and not only did we use the shoots as a dish, but the leaves were given to rabbits: in spring, when the plant was about to come up, all the heads that were a little plump, made tender by the frosts, were used for soup or certain dishes; they were very good. VARIETIES. - Ordinary Brussels sprouts (fig. 17). - The best variety, very productive, excellent quality; stems 70 cm to 1 m high, slender; spoon-shaped leaves, strongly petiolate and slightly blistered, slightly purplish. Numerous, widely spaced, very firm, small shoots, much appreciated in the markets. Dwarf Brussels sprouts. - Tall variety, 50 to 60 cm, with thick stem, broader, more blistered leaves than the previous one, larger shoots, closer together, less firm; perhaps a little earlier, but less productive. Sellier half-dwarf Brussels sprout. - This variety was sent to us in 1885 by MM. Dupanloup et Cie. We tried it, and the results were not as satisfactory as they had promised.
