It is a rather dwarf variety, but very vigorous, with fairly large, loosely packed shoots; needs to be improved. Brussels sprouts no longer ultra. - We have received this variety from Bordeaux, and it has given us very good results, but we don't think it can be made into a separate variety; it resembles the ordinary Brussels sprout in every way, but is perhaps not as tall. Half-dwarf Brussels sprout from La Halle. - A new variety not exceeding 65 centimeters, hardy and very productive. CHOU A GROSSES COTES ORDINAIRE This breed of cabbage, which botanists have classified as a Milan cabbage, is of little interest. It is grown very little in France, but a lot in England; it has the advantage of not being affected by even the coldest weather, which, on the contrary, is necessary for its qualities. CULTIVATION. - It is sown at the same time as Savoy cabbage, and cultivation is the same. It is mainly in autumn, under the influence of rain, that this variety develops most vigorously. The stem is short, the leaves are close together, very thick-veined and prominent, whitish yellow or greenish, with a thick, well-developed midrib. The head is small and usually absent. Cabbage with large fringed ribs. - A sub-variety of the previous one, but preferable for quality and production. Market gardeners near Paris grow a variety of green cabbage they call Bricoli; it appears to be descended from the cabbage with large fringed ribs, of which it is undoubtedly a bastard. It doesn't apple, but produces a large quantity of thick, extremely curly, lobed, petiolate leaves. It is very hardy and can withstand the coldest weather. GREEN CABBAGE. - FORAGE KOLS. - NON-POMMED CABBAGE Brassica virens. This class comes under the heading of agriculture, since the main use of its various varieties is as animal feed. However, once the leaves have been softened by frost, they can be used for a variety of culinary purposes. When the plant goes to seed, which happens every two years, the young, tender shoots are picked and eaten like asparagus or broccoli. Farmers in Charentes, Poitou, Haute-Vienne, Dordogne, Vendée, etc., take them to market. In general, all varieties of green cabbage have fleshy, fairly broad, almost smooth leaves, except for a few blisters (and those that are laciniated), the midrib is very well developed, the petiole is

long and very large, especially at the base. CULTIVATION. - Sow from March and April to July in good, freshly-turned soil; spring sowings are generally preferred. These cabbages are so hardy that the seedlings, though large, can be transplanted: we have replanted some very strong ones, which have recovered perfectly and developed vigorously. VARIETIES. - The same varieties to grow are : Cavalier or cow cabbage. - The most widely grown, very productive, develops very quickly, very hardy. Chou vert branchu du Poitou. - Very productive, hardy variety, highly esteemed in Poitou and Limousin. Chou moellier blanc. - Excellent variety, very productive and fast-growing; the stalk becomes very thick and strongly swollen in the middle; cut into thin slices, it is very tender and, like beet, is used as animal feed in winter. It can be used cooked or raw. Less hardy than beets, it fears the cold. In northern France, it is harvested before the frosts. - It differs from white moellier by the purplish tint of its stem; it is hardier and seems to withstand the cold better. Animals prefer white cabbage; its color, which probably has a particular odor and flavor, seems to repel them. Flanders cabbage. - Red, very hardy and productive. Chou mille têtes. - Native to the Vendée region, where it is widely grown, very productive, but sensitive to cold. 40. Chou de Lannilis blond. - Highly esteemed variety in Brittany, fairly productive. Daubenton's perennial cabbage. - Particular variety with long, hanging branches, fairly productive. Mowing cabbage. - Stems are almost nonexistent, giving rise to a number of lobed, green leaves with grayish-white petioles, forming a strong clump that can be mowed several times. This variety gives good results only if planted in good, cool soil. i ne" Sarthe forage cabbage. - Very good variety, ample foliage, widely blistered, fleshy petiole, very hardy. Also worth mentioning: Palmetto. - Very attractive, but not very productive. Naples kale. - More ornamental than vegetable, it produces swellings like kohlrabi, which suggests that it is intermediate between cauliflower and kohlrabi. 0192 Large green kale. - More ornamental than vegetable, this variety is very hardy. Short-stalked green kale. - Short-stalked red kale. - Red variegated kale. - White variegated kale. - Variegated laciniated kale. - Variegated poliferous kale. - Large red kale. All these varieties are particularly ornamental. CABBAGE Brassica oleraeca gongiloides L. ORIGIN. - According to De Candolle, it originated in the ancient world. Here's what he says: 1° Brassicas with fleshy roots are native to temperate Europe. 2° Their cultivation spread to Europe and India before and after the invasion of the Aryas. Cultivation of the various brassica species has spread to south-west Asia since the time of the ancient Hebrews, bringing the origin of its cultivation to over 4,000 years ago. - Kohlrabi is sown from March to June, in sufficiently loosened, fresh soil, in beds, in nurseries. Water and mulch according to the time of sowing. When the young plants begin to grow a little strong, they are FIG. 18 - Kohlrabi. thinned out to prevent the root from shrivelling, which would then become an obstacle to the growth of the fleshy part, which needs very rapid development to be succulent and tender. They are planted when they are a little strong, in the same way as other cabbage varieties. They are generally planted in rows of six, 35 cm apart. Water as you plant, and keep watering throughout the growing season. For, if they lack water, they become woody and cordate, and therefore unsuitable for consumption; nor should we neglect hoeing and weeding, which must be repeated often. Remove the yellow leaves from the base. These cabbages survive the winter fairly well without suffering. VARIETY. - Early white Vienne kohlrabi. - Very early variety, small size, short leaves, few in number; can be eaten three months after sowing. White kohlrabi (fig. 18). - A very good hardy variety, reaching a high volume. Early violet Vienne kohlrabi. - An early variety with more or less the same characteristics as early white kohlrabi, but with the difference that the kohlrabi is surrounded by a purple skin that extends to the leaf petioles and veins. Ordinary purple kohlrabi. - A variety derived from white kohlrabi, but distinguished by its violet color; it is hardy and grows very large. Goliath violet kohlrabi. - A new variety, remarkable for its size; we've had balls weighing 7 and 8 kilograms; it's likely that in very good, moist soil, you can get better; tender and tasty. CHOU-NAVET. - RUTABAGA. - TURNEPS Cabbage Brassica oleracea Napus. Another breed quite different from kohlrabi in that the tuber it produces is almost entirely in the ground, forming an ovoid mass almost as long as it is wide (fig. 19). This tuber becomes quite voluminous, especially in good, fresh, well-smoked soils. In dry, chalky soils, it has given poor results. CULTIVATION. - Like kohlrabi, it is sown from March to June, either in the field or in nurseries. We tried both types of cultivation to compare results. Plants raised in the nursery and transplanted developed as vigorously as those sown in the field; we therefore conclude that it is better to sow in the nursery and then transplant in the field. Cultivation care is the same as for kohlrabi. However, in England, Ireland and much of Brittany, this vegetable is grown on a large scale, just as beet is in the North. In these regions, cattle are very fond of this turnip, which is an excellent winter feed and seems to fatten them up quickly. In general, this plant keeps very well and can spend the winter outdoors without suffering too much from the cold. However, they should be eaten during the winter, as they quickly go to seed and become tough in spring. VARIETY. - White turnip cabbage - FIG. 19. Turnip cabbage. An excellent variety that keeps well, forming a very large, tender turnip with good flavor. Highly esteemed in the Angoumois region, where it is grown in large quantities, it is one of the best varieties. White turnip with red collar. - It differs from the previous variety by the reddish tint of the collar; the flesh is very white and tender. White smooth cabbage with short leaves. - Flattened variety, similar in appearance or shape to the flat Egyptian beet, very early, medium size, very firm white flesh, excellent as a vegetable variety. Yellow turnip or green-necked rutabaga. - Rutabagas are all yellow-fleshed, and therefore form a breed of their own. The above-mentioned variety produces a large, spherical, slightly rounded turnip with a greenish-purple collar. Red-necked rutabaga. - A variety with more or less the same characteristics as the previous one, except for the purplish-red collar; the flesh is very good. Yellow, flat, early Rutabaga. - Very early variety, medium size; highly recommended for the vegetable garden. Champion rutabaga. - Excellent variety, very productive, good for large-scale cultivation, highly esteemed in northern and eastern France. Drummond rutabaga, red-necked. - Skirving rutabaga (red collar). - West-Nor folk rutabaga (red collar). - Laing rutabaga (whole leaf). - Oval rutabaga. - These last varieties, like almost all others, are of English origin. Cauliflower Brassica oleracea Botrytis. Cauliflower is a breed apart and quite distinct from other cabbage varieties. Leaves are generally upright, whole, elongated, smooth, with smooth edges, rarely divided, bluish-green, more or less dark. Petioles are very large and prominent, both above and below, and yellowish-white with strong veins at the base. The apple, which is in a way nothing more than an assembly of the flower's supports, should be very tightly grained and white, with the middle more developed than the edges; these are the main characteristics of a good variety. CULTIVATION. - The best production centers in France are near Paris, Châlons, Saint-Brieuc, Arras, and a large part of Brittany; these products are shipped in complete vagons to the Paris market. Many growers specialize in this area, and are sure to make a good living from it. Many of our products are shipped to London. Cauliflower is also grown in Germany, England and Switzerland. As you can see, cauliflower is mainly grown in northern countries; it does well there, because there is always some humidity, it rains often, and cabbage only grows vigorously when the atmosphere is damp. This explains why cabbages planted in dry soils do not develop in summer, and when autumn comes, with its frequent rains, they grow vigorously. When you have land with a moist bottom, you're always sure of the result. Growing cauliflower is not difficult, but it does require a certain amount of know-how if it is to be a success; without this, a lot of trouble is taken, only to end in cruel disappointment, as we have seen time and again. Cauliflower needs rapid development. Otherwise, the apples don't form well, they are small, not very tight, they look like fruit that has suffered, and are therefore little appreciated. To obtain this rapid growth, especially in summer, when cauliflowers are most widely planted, they need to be placed in moist soil, sufficiently fertilized with good, energetic fertilizers, and given abundant watering or irrigation. However, there are certain dry soils where summer cultivation, despite copious watering, always gives poor results. To prove the point, let's take a look at our comparative experiments. At the same time, we planted demidur Paris cauliflowers in three squares of different soil types: the first square was marsh soil, a little peaty, and consequently very wet. The second square was alluvial, with a high clay content. The third square was a silico-calcareous soil, very dry in summer. The plantations in the first two squares were not watered, while those in the third square were watered copiously throughout the hot season. At harvest, the apples in square number 1 were magnificent, with very tight, white berries; those in number 2 were quite pretty, but much smaller; those in number 3 were small, poorly formed and hard. It is therefore easy to conclude that cauliflower thrives best in strong, deep or moist soils, or in soils that are difficult to dry out. Cauliflower is sown at three different times: 1° in winter, for spring and summer; 2° in summer, for autumn; 3° finally, in autumn, for spring. The first seedlings are usually sown in a greenhouse or under a cloche, in January or February, on a warm bed. When the plant is strong enough to withstand transplanting, it is placed on another warm layer. During cold spells, they are covered with straw mats and gradually given air to get them used to the outside temperature. Towards the end of March or the beginning of April, they are transplanted into place, and produce in June-July. From the end of March to May 15th, we sow in the open ground, with good exposure; we transplant in place, as and when the plants are good, and we have products which follow one another from July to September. For these kinds of seedlings, it's best to choose tender or Salomon cauliflower, or even better, semi-hard Paris. The latter variety always does well, sown at any time of year, and is used almost exclusively by Parisian market gardeners. Summer seedlings can be sown from June 1st to 15th; they are planted in July or August, on a dark or rainy day. Properly treated, these plants bear fruit in October and November. This cultivation, which seems simple, becomes difficult if you don't have a lot of water. The last seedlings are sown in the ground in August-September, in well-exposed beds. The seedlings are planted out in good exposure and, when the cold arrives, covered with windbreak mulch. In the south of France, they overwinter fairly well without suffering, or keep very well under the slightest shelter. They are planted in March, and produce in May or June. Hard cauliflower is well suited to this season. When planted out, they are arranged as described for head cabbage. Lettuce or chicory can be counter-planted. Tender cauliflower can be planted much more tightly than other varieties. When the young plants are taken up, if possible, they should be watered well with liquid manure, and this operation should be repeated often while they are young. Hoe several times, and deeply. Often, as frost approaches, the apples on many plants are barely formed and too small to be eaten straight away. So, although they grow little, E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 11 during cold spells, we recommend the following method, which has worked well for us. Bring all the leaves over the apple in the shape of a cap, tie them loosely with a raffia or a rush; then leave them in this state for a few days; it takes cold temperatures of 8 or 10 degrees to reach the apples thus swaddled. When you want to keep some apples in winter, you can still try this method. When you realize that the temperature is going to drop, you choose a fine day, when the cabbages will be well wiped. Tear them off and put them in a cellar or storeroom. After removing the outer leaves, plant them upright in sand, which should be dry rather than damp. They should be visited frequently to remove any rotting leaves. Aerate them whenever the temperature permits. They can also be stored for some time, by hanging them upside down and keeping some of the leaves. This method requires a great deal of care to succeed, as the leaves quickly become moldy, so they need to be visited often. They can also be stored as follows: Start by forming a circular layer with good horse manure, which should be well compacted; when it is 15 centimetres high, place the first row of cauliflowers, previously stripped of most of their leaves and with their roots; arrange them in a circle, side by side, without squeezing them too much, with the apple outside the manure. Once the first row has been planted, the roots are covered with manure, which is carefully placed between each plant. Place a second bed of manure high enough so that the second row of cauliflowers doesn't touch the first, and continue in this way until the wheel is completely finished, which can be raised as high as you like. It's essential that the middle is always below the edges, to form a conical wheel. If they are placed in a healthy, warm environment, they will keep for a long time without deteriorating. In general, heads of cauliflower, placed in the various conditions we have just described, almost always wilt. The best way to restore them to their original freshness is to leave them with a fairly long stalk, which can be lightly pressed into damp sand, or even better, into wet moss; in this way, cauliflowers are as fresh as when they were growing. VARIETIES. - The cauliflower genus, thought to derive from green cabbage, has been classified into three main varieties: tender, semi-hard and hard. There is no particular characteristic that distinguishes them at first sight: the difference exists only in earliness, in the more or less tight grain, and in quality. Tender cauliflower - Generally small, very early, producing a small, tight head and dividing quickly. Semi-hard cauliflower. - It produces a fairly strong, upright plant, with a much larger, tighter-grained, whiter head than soft cauliflower. Hard cauliflower. - This is a strong plant, producing a large globular head, excessively tightly packed, very fine-grained and of excellent quality. Tender Paris or Salomon cauliflower. - Very early, forming a medium-sized, loosely packed head that does not keep long, suitable for winter and summer sowing. Early Erfurth dwarf cauliflower. - Very early variety, very short stalk, good head, grain a little tighter than Salomon, very white, does not keep well pure; its seeds must be obtained from Germany if you want to have very good products. Semi-hard Paris cauliflower. - Excellent variety, can be grown in any season, and produces fine products; large, firm, tight-grained apples; stores well; very hardy, highly esteemed on the markets. Chou-fieur Lenormand. - A very hardy, productive variety, which gave birth to the following variety and, it is claimed, has been advantageously replaced by it. Lenormand short-stalked cauliflower. - Very valuable variety in all respects, rustic; very short stem, leaves a little spread out, short, very thick, large, very firm head; long-lasting, extremely productive. Chou-fleur demi-dur de Saint-Brieuc. - A variety widely grown in Brittany, very hardy, producing globular apples with an excessively tight, very firm texture and a slightly musky, bland taste; ideal for export. Paris hard cauliflower. - A very common variety around Paris, where it is grown in large quantities, very late, hardy, forming large, regular apples with very tight, firm seeds. Dutch hard cabbage. - A semi-late variety grown on a large scale in Germany, where it is highly esteemed; we have tried growing it several times, but find that in slightly warm climates, the apples are relatively small compared with the development of the plant, which is excessively vigorous. Stadthold cauliflower. - A late variety, with a good, tight head and about the same growth habit as the hard Dutch variety, not widely grown in France. Walheren hard cauliflower. - A very late variety, to be sown in late March, first half of April, for autumn production. Sown in May or June, like broccoli, it spends the winter very well and apples very early in the spring. Excellent, tight-grained and firm. Giant Naples cauliflower. - About as hardy as Walkeren's, very large, very tall, very large head, firm, tight grain. English hard cauliflower. - Very late and resistant to cold. Alger cauliflower. - Very vigorous, with a large, high stem, a little late. Sicilian black cauliflower. - Vigorous and hardy; sown in autumn, it overwinters and apples in spring. New or little-known varieties include: Fournier's improved all-season hard cauliflower. - Similar to the old hard cauliflower, but a few days earlier; large, globular head with tight, white seeds; suitable for autumn cultivation. Châlons improved cauliflower. - Magnificent species with very large head, fine, tight grain, very firm, does well in autumn, its main season. The Dupanloup house in Paris, from which we obtained these two varieties, won first prize for this variety in 1885. Early dwarf Alleaume cauliflower. - Short stalk, large head with tight, white seeds; suitable for early crops. Lemaître short-stalked cauliflower. - Very good variety, early white, fairly large. Giant Naples cauliflower, half-early. - High-yielding variety, should be planted quite far apart. SEEDS. - Cauliflower seed is extremely difficult to obtain pure, which explains why it is always so expensive, costing from 70 to 120 francs per kilogram. When a variety is localized, it reproduces freely; the same variety sown elsewhere will produce bastardized plants. To harvest good cauliflower seeds, sow in September. In February or March, or even April, depending on the region, when they are ready for harvest, you choose the best-formed, stocky, vigorous plants, with a firm, tight head, free from small leaves mixed with grain. We cover the formed apple with a leaf to prevent it from hardening off; when it starts to open, it will rise, so it must be uncovered; we often water around the stem with the neck of the watering can. When the seedlings start to bloom, pinch off the tips of the most vigorous ones to feed the branches below. Watch out for aphids, and destroy them by spraying with salt water or nicotine, or sprinkling with powdered quicklime. As the siliques ripen, cut them off and lay them out on a cloth to dry in the sun; all that's left to do is winnow and clean them; it's said that the seed that's ripe first is the best. Seeds are good for five years; 30 grams contain 9880 seeds. BROCOLI Brassica Botrytis cymosa Broccoli (fig. 20) is an excessively late variety of cauliflower. It is assumed that all our early varieties are descended from this race, which itself comes from kale. The leaves are longer, narrower, more numerous, less darkly colored, with stronger, whiter veins than in cauliflower. The stalk becomes much FIG. 20. - Broccoli cauliflower. more voluminous, the apples in the whites are generally very large, fairly firm and very fine-grained. CULTIVATION. - Cultivation differs little from that of cauliflower, but is much easier. Sow from April to June in nurseries. Transplant if possible, and plant out when strong enough, at least 60 cm apart in all directions for large varieties such as Mammoth, and 50 cm apart for others. Hoe often and water copiously during the summer. Between each cabbage and in the false row, lettuces, romaine, chicory, etc. are counter-planted, or purslane, radishes, etc. are sown. In the south, south-west and part of western France, broccoli winters quite well without any shelter, but in the north it must be protected from frost. The most practical way to do this is to lay it down, face north, and cover as much of the stalk as possible with soil up to the first leaves. It must therefore be planted deep, and the best way is to adopt furrow cultivation; plant at the bottom, and when winter comes all you have to do is lay the plant on one side and bring the soil over it. VARIETY. - Early white broccoli. - Very early, easy to grow, very hardy, apples well and tightly, fine-grained, firm, keeps for quite a long time. Easter broccoli. - Small, very early variety, many grayish leaves, very regular, medium-sized head, firm, tight-grained; very good quality. Ordinary white broccoli. - Very hardy, semi-late variety, reaching a high volume; fairly large, very white, tight head; keeps for a long time without spreading. Roscoff white broccoli. - Fairly early variety, resembling early white broccoli, of which it is probably only a sub-variety; fairly large, regular, tight, rounded, hard-grained head; keeps well, widely grown in Brittany, mainly around Quimper, highly esteemed in England. Mammoth white broccoli. - Very late variety, very hardy; large, white head, tight, fine-grained, rounded and firm; highly recommended. Angers early white broccoli. - Very good, hardy variety with good production. Late white broccoli from Angers. - Good hardy variety with very large, very white heads. Purple broccoli. - Breed improved by t4 FIG. 21 - Broccoli violet branchu. market gardeners from Bordeaux, very hardy; fairly large, purplish apple, highly esteemed in the South-West. Branchy purple broccoli (fig. 21). - Widely cultivated throughout the Midi. Leaves are very dark purple, broad and somewhat wavy or curled. The shoots are also purple, long and form small apples at the ends. These broccolis are much sought-after11. by the working class, who eat them like green asparagus; they are tender and very tasty. Extra-early white broccoli. - New variety, highly praised. Very white, fine-grained, medium-sized head; fairly hardy. SEEDS. - Broccoli seed is easier to harvest than cauliflower seed, and less expensive. It requires the same care in cultivation and the same precautions in harvesting. When the stalks are strong and loaded with a quantity of sometimes very heavy capsules, to prevent them from being broken by the wind, they are tied to stakes or slats. When the fruit begins to ripen, care must be taken to keep out the birds, which are quite fond of it and must be chased away with scarecrows. It keeps well for five years. COMMON CIBBAGE Allium fistulosum L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant from Siberia (from the Kirghiz country to Baïcal); according to Donn, it was introduced in 1629. GROWING. - Chives are propagated from seed, sown in March or April and again in August, either in the field or in a nursery in good, well-smoked, recently tilled soil; the seed is lightly covered and lightly treaded. If sown in a nursery, when the young plants are strong enough, they are transplanted in place, either in beds or in borders, after trimming the roots, as is done for onions. Water lightly to facilitate recovery; other care consists of weeding and hoeing, and good watering in summer. This plant can also be propagated by dividing the stems, which are kept over winter under a leaf shelter, to be planted in spring. However, as it goes to seed every year, and provides many seeds, it is preferable to sow it, as it is more vigorous. In harsh winters, it should be covered with dry leaves or earth, and if you don't want to run out, place the clumps under a frame. VAriérés. - Ciboule commune. - Most commonly grown, hardy, elongated bulges, copper-red, very productive, fast-growing. Ciboule blanche hâtive. - Smaller than the previous variety in all its parts, with shorter, pinkish-white bulges; stiff, short, dark green, glaucous leaves. Less strong, with a finer flavor than common chives; more sensitive to cold. SEEDS. - The plant goes to seed in the second year, and is harvested in August; it is collected and processed like onions. It keeps well for three years; 30 grams contain around 2000 seeds. MAlaDIES. - When it develops rapidly, chives are sometimes subject to scald like shallots; this disease is due to the development of an underground fungus (Rhizoctonia) which attacks the roots and causes the plants to die. There are few remedies for this disease; just pull out the diseased clumps as soon as you notice them. USES. - Chives are frequently used in cooking, and the leaves are used as condiments in salads and omelettes. In the south of France, country people eat them raw with bread. CIBOULE VIVACE or CIBOULE DE SAINT-JACQUES Allium lusitanicum Lam. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to the same regions as the above. CULTIVATION. - This breed is distinguished from the others by its numerous, elongated, reddish-brown bulbs and large, stiff, glaucous-green leaves. It does not produce fertile seeds. A few flower stalks develop in spring, but the ovaries remain sterile. It can be propagated by dividing clumps and separating them in March or April. Same cultivation care and uses as for the previous species. It is sometimes confused with the chive: however, it is easy to avoid confusion, as the chive is much less voluminous in all its parts. CIBOULET Allium Schœnoprasum L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, found growing wild in southern France, temperate and northern Europe, Siberia, Kamchatka, northern America (Lake Huron). Cultivated for over two thousand years. CULTIVATION. - This plant, generally known as civet, reproduces by bursting its clumps in March or April; it is planted in borders at 20 cm in all directions, or in flowerbeds. Hoe often, water during the summer and spread a good mulch over the beds or around the plants. As these plants only need to be renewed every three or four years, every winter they are "terreauteed", i.e., after a good hoeing, about 4 or 5 centimetres of compost is spread over the bed or borders. Chives can withstand the coldest weather, but even so, it's a good idea, especially in northern climates, to cover them with leaves or manure. As they don't grow at this time of year, if you want leaves, pull up a few clumps and put them under a frame, on a warm layer as close as possible to the glass. You can even put them in pots and place them on the benches of a greenhouse. SEEDS. - Although chives produce a number of flower stalks topped by a bunch of purplish-red flowers, they do not produce fertile seeds. We have tried several times to get them to produce seeds by artificial fertilization, but have never succeeded. DISEASES. - According to our observations, this plant appears to be disease-free. USES. - More or less the same as the common spring onion; however, it is more frequently used in salads, herb omelettes and ham; it also goes well with snails. CUCUMBER Cucumis sativus L. Cucurbitaceae. ORIGIN. - An annual plant native to India, where it has been cultivated for over three thousand years; introduced into China two centuries before the Christian era. The cucumber is a creeping plant, with angular stems, rough to the touch and flexible; the alternate leaves are right-angled or shallowly cut; the flowers are yellow, single-petaled, cut into five roughly equal divisions, solitary, or more often in bunches of seven, eight or more, depending on the variety; it is mainly the male flowers that are grouped in this way and in greater numbers; but there is always a female flower, which can be recognized by the ovary already formed before the flower blooms. The flower is composed of a style with three stigmas, and three or four stamens without anthers. Female flowers are usually solitary, sometimes geminate. The fruit is cylindrical, oblong, smooth or lined with protuberances ending in a thorn, sometimes quite prickly. The rind is yellow, green or yellowish; the flesh is firm, transparent and watery. Cucumber cultivation is much the same as that of the melon, which it resembles in its botanical characteristics. It thrives in a variety of well-prepared, fertilizer-rich soils, but seems to do best in soft, slightly damp soils. The first cucumbers are sown in well-prepared soil from late March to June. This soil can be prepared in several ways. Some growers make holes 30 to 40 centimetres square, as deep, and 1m 20 apart. These holes are filled with good warm manure, which is firmly tamped down, covered with a few centimetres of soil and then sown. This is not a new process, but it gives fairly good results, especially in strong soils, as only a little soil is disturbed at a time. The most commonly used method consists in opening trenches, 25 to 40 centimetres wide and 20 centimetres deep. These are filled with fresh or half-consumed manure, compacted well and then covered with the looser soil removed from the trench; this is then stirred and crushed again with the spade. Rake over the whole to form a kind of ridge, and sow on the top of this ridge, after having formed a small trough where the seeds are to be placed; better still, trace a small radius with the hoe, sow along the whole line and cover with sifted loam mixed with half the soil; this precaution is essential for strong soils if you want good results. You can also sow in 10-centimeter pots placed on warm beds and without a frame; when the plants have developed their first two leaves, place them in well-prepared soil. This method works well: plants raised in this way produce earlier than seedlings planted in situ, but produce less: we have carried out comparative trials. When you have good soil at your disposal, i.e. sufficiently fertilized and fresh, you can operate in a different way. Once the soil has been well ploughed and fertilized, hoe it into rays 8 to 10 centimetres deep and 1.5 metres apart, and bring the soil from the edge of the rays to the middle, where the path should be, so that it is high; 50 centimetres from each side of the main ray, draw a small line for planting Romaines, which will be removed before harming the cucumbers; sow the seeds 10 centimetres apart on the line, cover with the mixture indicated above, or soil if fine enough, and water. Towards the end of April, this method can be tried with success. Once the young shoots have developed their fourth leaf, we recommend pinching them back, as this first pinch will soon give rise to four new branches, which will lengthen appreciably with their own particular vigor. The buds themselves will be pinched off at their third leaf, giving rise to a new series of branches which will be left to their own devices, but the tips of any that overgrow will be trimmed. Other care consists of watering during the hot season, hoeing, weeding and spreading a good mulch over the surface of the beds or at least around the plants. When cucumbers are grown for canning, they should be picked small and green. Picking should be done every two days, and the fruit should never be allowed to swell if you intend to pick small ones, as the crop would soon be exhausted; it's better to mark a few plants and let all the fruit swell. With a little care and time, con- combers can be rowed in the same way as peas, but with numerous branches. The first ascent of the stems is facilitated by tying them with rushes, after which they cling to themselves with their tendrils. They can also be trained in espaliers, as practiced in England and Russia, and directed as desired, as they lend themselves well to all sorts of shapes, provided you have the time to visit them often. Indeed, all these methods are better suited to an amateur than to a market gardener, who at this time of year is overwhelmed with work. In England, where cucumbers are grown extensively, special greenhouses are devoted to them, with the stalks attached to trellises a few centimetres from the glass, and when the whole greenhouse is furnished, the effect produced by the fruit hanging from all sides is quite original. VARIETIES. - Green cucumber, Petit de Paris. - A vigorous, high-yielding plant, much appreciated by confectioners, with small, attractive green fruits. Excellent variety for green harvesting. Generally grown everywhere. Large yellow cucumber. - Hardy, productive variety, producing beautiful fruit, good for green harvesting, generally grown everywhere. Very long green cucumber from China. - Little-known, new variety, fairly productive. Picked young, the fruit is long, green, with a few spines. Particularly suitable for candying. Ordinary long green cucumber. - A very good variety for outdoor use, giving fairly good yields, with long, green, good-quality fruit. Very large white Bonneuil cucumber. - An old variety with very large fruit, very productive, perhaps the hardiest, grown extensively around Paris for the perfume industry. Unsuitable for green harvesting, due to the yellow color of the fruit. Russian embroidered cucumber. - An early variety, producing small, dull yellow fruits dotted with small grayish lines, giving them a dirty appearance; can be grown for green harvesting. Long green Athens cucumber. - Vigorous, stocky, long, large fruit, keeps well, very hardy and fairly productive. Rollison's telegraph cucumber. - Variety bred by an English gardener, Mr. Walkelm, head gardener at the Manorhouse in Tooting, highly esteemed in England, where it is most commonly grown; long, early, crisp fruit; suitable for forced cultivation. Fournier cucumber, long green, early finish. - A new variety, very vigorous and fairly early; long, not very thorny, green fruit, very good for cultivation in the garden and for pickling. Fine Meaux gherkin cucumber. - New variety, not yet widespread, very productive, early, very good for preserving. Bourbonne improved gherkin cucumber - New variety, very productive, long, firm, green fruit, very good for preserving. Large, green, early gherkin. - New variety, high production, green, oblong, slightly elongated fruit, very good for confectioners. The following varieties are also grown: Early yellow cucumber from Holland. - Early white cucumber. - Parisian long white cucumber (fig. 22). Among the best English varieties are: Long green cucumber (Gladiateur). - Munroë long green cucumber. - Green half-long Bed-Fordshire cucumber. - Pike's defiance cucumber. - Man-of-Kant long green cucumber. - Duhe of Edinburgh green cucumber. - Green lony cucumber Doctor Livingstone. - Marquis of Lorne long green cucumber. - Tender and True green cucumber. We will mention a few other varieties of various origins. Quedlembourg giant long green cucumber. - This variety has not given us very good results in the garden. Full green Tuscan cucumber. - Very good variety for the Midi. Green Goliath cucumber. - Boston pick- ling cucumber. - Long green Cardiff cucumber. - A new, little-known variety, producing fruit 30 to 40 cm long. Almost all these varieties do not perform well in northern France when grown outdoors. SEEDS. - To obtain good seeds, choose FIG. 22 - Parisian long white cucumber. a few plants. When the fruit is characteristic of the variety, we mark it with stakes and add labels. We leave the fruit on the stalks until it begins to rot, then take out the seed, put it in a cloth and wash it with plenty of water. Dry in the shade. It keeps well for eight years. 30 grams contain around 1,500 seeds, and a liter weighs 500 grams. slugs and pests. - In addition to the slugs and snails that eat cucumbers, especially when they start to grow, cucumbers are also affected by a parasitic disease known as sucker disease. This is a small microscopic fungus that develops on the underside of the leaves, covering them with white or yellowish dust. Plants affected by this disease should be watered for a few days with a solution of lime and copper sulfate; if the disease persists, the sickest leaves should be removed and burned. This disease prefers etiolated, sickly plants, especially those lacking water. USES. - Cucumbers have long been used as food: their flesh is white, unpalatable, not very nutritious, and only suitable for strong stomachs. It is widely consumed in hot countries, due to its refreshing but somewhat laxative properties. It is eaten raw, in salads, with a strong seasoning, or cooked with lean or fat, and drizzled with the juices of roasted meats: raw, it is indigestible; cooked, it refreshes. The first fruits, picked before they are ripe and preserved in vinegar with various herbs, are known as gherkins, and frequently served on tables to stimulate the appetite. Cucumber pulp is used to make cucumber ointment, a cosmetic that is said to soften the skin. They are combined with sweet almonds to make calming and refreshing emulsions. SNAKE CUCUMBER Cucumis fiexuosus. Cucurbitaceae. ORIGIN. - India. CULTIVATION. - This plant is cultivated like melons, of which it is only a form; so it is wrong to call it a cucumber: it is called a snake because of the peculiarity of its fruits, which resemble a large contoured snake. We've had some plants whose fruits have become quite large and were not contoured: they were club-shaped; they ripened; they tasted more like melons than cucumbers. This plant is easy to grow. It is very hardy and more resistant to heat than gherkin cucumbers. SEEDS. - When the fruit is ripe, the seed is removed and cared for in the same way as for melons. MAlabiEs. - Virtually disease-free. USES. - When young, the fruit is used in the same way as gherkins, i.e. pickled in vinegar. This is the only use we know of. Cucumis Anguria L. Cucumis echinatus Mœnch. ORIGIN. - Annual plant from Jamaica; highly prized variety in Brazil, in Montevideo and Rio-Janeiro. GROWING. - Fruits are small, green and lined with fleshy, pointed, slightly curved protuberances, some of which form spines. The plant should be sown a little late, in May, to ensure sufficient warmth for rapid development. Same cultivation care as gherkin cucumber. USES. - Fruits can be preserved in vinegar or boiled and cooked in butter. GROSSED CUCUMBER Cucumis myriocarpus. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant of doubtful origin. Fruits are very small, quite good to eat, but bristling with long greenish hairs. Same cultivation and uses as above. ANE CUCUMBER Ecbalium agreste. ORIGIN. - Plant with perennial root, which becomes very large; small fruits, supported by a long, straight stalk, have the property of throwing their seeds forcefully and quite far, which is a great surprise to people unfamiliar with them. USES. - The fruits have been advocated as edible, but their pungent, bitter taste has caused them to be rejected. Cucurbits Cucurbita L. Cucurbitaceae. The squash genus includes all the varieties of Cucurbitaceae included under the names pumpkin, giraumon, gourd. In spite of the large number of varieties that exist, there are only three main races that cannot cross-fertilize each other; it is on these principles that M. Naudin's classification is based, to which we conform. CULTIVATION. - The cultivation of these different races is more or less the same. Squash are sown in the open ground, at the same time as cucumbers, in the second half of April, or in the first days of May. Pits are dug 45 to 60 centimetres wide and 15 or 20 centimetres deep. Fill them with good horse manure, mixed with other fermentable detritus. The manure is heavily compacted by walking on it. Cover the manure with loose soil, or potting compost mixed with vegetable garden soil. Sow three or four seeds per hole, cover with 4 cm and water. Instead of sowing in the field, you can sow in pots on warm layers and, when the cotyledons have developed, place them in the holes. Make a small basin around each plant and water. The distance between each hole varies according to the vigour of the variety you intend to grow. Generally speaking, 2 meters are required for small varieties, and 3 meters for others. When the squash start to grow, select the first two stems, then cut immediately above them; these stems soon grow vigorously. When they have reached 1.50 m, they are "marcotte", i.e. placed in a small gutter. They are held in this state by a small hook, and covered with soil. The buried part soon takes root, resulting in better vegetation. This operation can be performed two or three times on the same stem. On each of these stems, only one fruit is left; then the end of the branch two leaves above is pinched off; this must wait until the fruit is well knotted. A few days later, pinch back any fruit that has grown back into the leaf axils. Because of their very rapid growth and their herbaceous, watery constitution, these plants need copious watering in summer, especially if you want huge fruits, such as we have sometimes obtained weighing 80 to 100 kilograms, which is not uncommon, especially with large yellow pumpkins. Light hoeing is done at the base, and good, fat manure is used to mulch the crop. In general, farmers sow squash on piles of compost, or decomposing rubbish, and leave them to their own devices. The fruit develops quite well, but never becomes large, and this is just a fancy crop; these plants give better results grown in open fields. A few years ago, we planted them among our tomatoes, and as they were tied fairly high, the squash developed perfectly without harming the tomatoes. We encourage you to try this system, as you won't lose any ground. BREEDS AND VARIETIES. - Here is the list of cultivated races and varieties, according to M. Naudin's classification. Potir ons. Cucurbita maxima W. et A. ORIGIN. - Native to Guinea and cultivated for less than 2000 years. VArieres. - Large white pumpkin. - Vigorous variety, very large fruit, smooth dirty-white or yellowish rind, fine flesh with little color. Large yellow pumpkin. - The largest of all pumpkins; fruits weighing 80 to 100 kilograms or more are not uncommon. Very good, firm yellow flesh; prized in Paris markets and in the North. Green Spanish pumpkin. - Vigorous variety, medium-sized fruit, smooth, hard, green rind, succulent flesh, good keeping qualities, highly esteemed in the Midi region. Large green pumpkin. - Old variety, fairly vigorous, large fruit, cracked or embroidered rind, fairly hardy, good quality, little grown today. Bright red Étampes pumpkin. - Medium-sized fruit, with fairly wide, very pronounced ribs; bright orange-yellow rind; excellent firm, succulent flesh, keeps well, very hardy, highly recommended. Potiron gris de Boulogne. - Variety resembling the green Spanish pumpkin, very vigorous, bronze-green rind, fairly large fruit, fairly thick, bright yellow flesh, keeps well and is of excellent quality. FIG. 23: Turban squash. Ohio squash. - American variety, small fruit, very mealy yellow flesh. Valparaiso squash. - American variety, elongated lemon-shaped fruit, medium size, barely marked ribs, dirty gray, criss-crossed with small brodeE. BERGER, Vegetables. 12 very fine ries, fairly sweet pale yellow flesh, fruit does not keep well. Giraumon turban or Bonnet turc, Bonnet de prêtre (fig. 23). - Highly esteemed in the Midi, it is easily recognized by its turban or cap shape; very good, succulent, floury and sweet flesh. One of the best species to grow for small households. We also grow the following varieties: Brown squash. - Hubbard green squash. - Olive squash. - Valencia squash. - Small Chinese giraumon. - Eggplant squash. Pumpkin or Pepo squash. Cucurbita Pepo L. ORIGIN. - From southern Asia, according to Naudin; from America, according to some botanists; according to De Candolle, origin doubtful. VARIETIES. - Varieties belonging to this race develop less than those belonging to the pumpkin race. Leaves less large and with fairly pronounced lobes; branching stems very large, with strong, coarse and sometimes thorny hairs (fig. 24); medium-sized fruit generally of good quality and shelf life; stalk very hard at maturity and marked by five prominent angles. Seeds are smaller than in pumpkins, and almost always spotted or margined. Patagonian squash. - An old-growth variety, vigorous and hardy, very productive. Fruits 20 cm long, longitudinally marked with five regular, prominent ribs, good quality. Brazilian sugar gourd. - Small-sized variety, good production, very hardy, short, oblong, warty fruit with sweet yellow flesh, good keeping qualities. Marrow squash. - Runner variety with thin stem, deeply lobed foliage, small oblong fruit, FIO. 24 - Pumpkin, leaves and flowers. yellow or yellowish-white. It is a sub-variety of the Patagonian squash. The fruit is eaten young, when it is about half to a third of the size. When eaten at maturity, they lose much of their quality. They are highly prized in England, where they are widely consumed, and are known as Vegetable marrow. White non-runner squash. - Variety with short, thick stem, deeply lobed leaves, mottled with gray; elongated fruit, slightly swollen at the bottom. Flesh yellowish. Consumed like the previous variety. Touraine pumpkin. - Hardy variety, producing very good, huge fruit, but of mediocre quality. Italian squash or Coucourzelle. - Non-running, productive and hardy, with elongated fruits, spotted or mottled with yellow. In Italy, it is eaten in large quantities; it is used young and immediately formed. In this state, it makes an excellent and highly appreciated vegetable. Early cou-tors squash. - Courgeron de Genève, etc. Patisson. This genus, although classified in this race, is very different from the above-mentioned varieties; it is mainly grown as an ornamental plant, and is generally known by the synonyms Bonnet de prêtre, Bonnet d'électeur, Artichaut de Jérusalem, etc. Housewives usually place them on fireplaces as ornaments. These are non-running plants: pretty green leaves, with few lobes; very depressed, broad fruits, divided into obtuse lobes, which usually turn towards the umbilicus; very hard, orange-green yellow or flagellated green on a white background, depending on the variety; white, tender flesh, flabby and not very tight. VARIETIES. - Among the best varieties, let's mention : Yellow patisson. - Patisson panaché amélioré - Patisson galeux. - American white patisson (new). Coloquinte. CULTIVATION. - Very vigorous, but little cultivated; same cultivation as previous varieties. USES. - Can be used to hide a few ugly corners in the vegetable garden. They can even be used to decorate arbors, by climbing them up trellises. Their rapid growth makes them a first-rate plant for this purpose; what's more, the fruits, which are generally variegated or streaked, add a very pleasing decorative effect. The fruits are only picked when the first frost has passed over them, once dry, and when the inside is removed, they can be used to set seeds, which keep better than VARIETY. - These plants are very prone to interbreeding, which explains the indefinite number of varieties; the most commonly grown are: Striped pear coloquint. - Coloquinte galeuse. - Flat striped Coloquinte. - Pear-shaped coloquinte. - Oviform coloquinte. - Early apple coloquint. - Maliform coloquinte. - Coloquinte miniature. Bottle gourd, Calebasse, gourd. Cacurbita lagenaria L. ORIGIN. - From South America. CULTIVATION. - Same cultivation as previous races of coloquintes. These plants must be attached to a few stakes, trellises or oars, otherwise the fruit will develop poorly and not reach its normal shape. VARIETIES. - Some of the best to grow are: 12. Peach gourd. - Widespread everywhere, especially in Saintonge and Angoumois; after emptying the inside, peasants put them in the grape harvest when it is fermenting, then use them as a bottle, and put wine in them, which keeps very fresh. Siphon squash. - Another variety, widely cultivated in the Bordeaux region, and just about everywhere else, when the plant is made to climb, the fruit, which has a very pronounced terminal bulge, becomes very heavy; the neck then lengthens and thins, and the fruit in these conditions is better appreciated; it is not uncommon to find some containing 7 to 8 liters. In wineries, they are used as probes. Club gourd (fig. 25). - Like the previous one, but much larger and less regular. Courge plate de Corse. - A different variety from the above, with rounded, depressed fruits. Powdered pear squash. - A fairly large, pear-shaped variety, used for the same purposes as cucurbits. Butternut squash. Cucurbita moschata Duch. ORIGIN. - According to M. Coignaux, this genus originated in southern Asia. The name musk or moschata comes from the fact that these plants have somewhat musky flesh. GROWING. - Plants are generally very runny, with dark-green leaves that are sparsely lobed and speckled with white. They can also be recognized by the way the stalk spreads at the point of insertion on the fruit, dividing into five lobes and becoming very hard at maturity. VARIETIES. - Among the most esteemed varieties are: Naples solid squash. - Courge cou-tors du Canada. - Courge melonnée de Bordeaux. - VARIETY FIG. 25 - Club gourd. early coat rack. - Easter squash. - Provence nutmeg squash. - Yohohama squash, etc. Among the new varieties that we have not cultivated are: Mission squash. - Very early prolific squash. - Courge brodée de Thoumain, near Guitres (Gironde). - Portugal squash. - Corfu pumpkin. SEEDS. - To harvest the seeds of the different varieties of squash, giraumons, coloquintes, etc., we choose the best shaped, best characterized specimens, take out the seed, spread it on a cloth and leave it to dry in the shade; once it is dry, we enclose it in canvas or paper bags. It keeps well for five years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Squash is sometimes affected by a disease similar to celery uredo, a yellowish dust that clings to the underside of the leaves and acts on them like mildew on vines. Milk of lime and copper sulfate in the same proportions as for vines are excellent remedies. Sometimes, after vegetating for some time, the plant turns yellow and becomes chlorotic; the only remedy is to uproot it. In many cases, this chlorosis is only artificial, and is due to the ravages of the white worm that gnaws at the roots; before uprooting, it is therefore important to ascertain the cause of the disease. Sometimes subterranean fungi develop on the main roots, at the root collar, and in a short time the plant is lost. When young, plants are liable to be eaten by snails and loaches, which are fond of them. USES. - Gourds are frequently used, especially by the poorer classes. They are eaten in a variety of ways, usually cooked to make excellent lean soups or purées. Mixing them with milk, to which they go very well, makes excellent soups, but they are not suitable for weak stomachs. They are also used to make jams, marmalades and preserves similar to sauerkraut. Given to cows, they increase milk secretion. Fruits picked very young and small can be preserved in vinegar like gherkins. They are also used to make CRAMBÉ MARITIME orgeat. - CHOU MARIN Crambe maritima. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant, native to the coasts of Western Europe. Native to our coasts. CULTIVATION. - In France, it is grown very little, but in England, considerable crops are grown; at certain times of the year, London markets abound with this sea cale, which the English hold in high esteem. Crambé is propagated from seed, which must be sown as soon as it matures, in rows, leaving the desired distance between each plant, or better still, in nurseries. To do this, open small gullies 15 or 20 centimetres apart, 4 or 5 centimetres deep, and cover slightly with loose soil or compost. As soon as the seedlings have four or six leaves, place them in moist soil, as far as possible, well prepared and smoked to 60 centimetres in all directions. Seedlings are sown in the same way as cardoons, in bunches of three or four plants per clump. Put in more seeds than you need plants, thin out and water thoroughly. Instead of sowing the seeds as soon as they ripen, you can wait until spring, and sow early on warm beds, in small cups, at a rate of seven or eight seeds each. You can even sow on the bed very thickly, as some of the seeds have lost their ability to germinate and many do not germinate at all. Once the seedlings are strong enough, they can be planted. There is still a way of multiplying them, and it's the safest and the FIC. 26. - Crambe maritima, faster, but for this you need to have old plants at your disposal: we mean by sections or root cuttings. This is done in January and February. You divide old clumps of crambe, as you would an old sorrel plant. Put them in place, then water immediately. No matter how you plant it, you need to give it copious summer watering, frequent hoeing and weeding, and it's only after two or three years of good cultivation that you can begin to harvest; this harvest can last from eight to ten years. Before being released for consumption, crambé must be blanched (fig. 26) like cardoons; otherwise, the taste would be unbearably pungent and unpalatable; it is blanched from August to December. There are several ways to do this, either by imprisoning the stems in flowerpots or hollow tiles, placed side by side, covering the top and surrounding the base with earth so that no light can penetrate, or by using straw bedding, upturned wooden tubs, etc. Crambe can also be forced in the same way as for sorrel. As the roots always tend to emerge from the ground, cut the shoots fairly deeply, and as far as possible, don't let them go to seed, otherwise the plants will soon be exhausted. This plant grows spontaneously in seaside sands, i.e. in salty soils, and it is claimed that by spreading sea salt or ingredients containing brine, such as oyster shells, mussels and other shellfish, around the roots, we have a powerful fertilizer that contributes enormously to vegetation. SEEDS. - To harvest the seed, choose very vigorous clumps. They are left to grow, and when the seeds, which are very large, start to turn yellow, we pick up the stems where they have finished ripening in the shade. They keep for a year, rarely longer. DISEASES, PESTS. - Like all crucifers, crambe maritime is prone, especially when young, to flea beetle, which perforates the young cotyledons and first leaves. We recommend watering with salt water dissolved in limestone, as for cabbages. Slugs and snails are very fond of it, so it should be hunted down. USES. - Petioles are eaten blanched, like asparagus or cardoons, in a white sauce like celery root; cooked with sausage, they are delicious. They have a nutty flavor that's quite appealing. WALNUT CRESS Lepidium sativum. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - An annual plant of doubtful origin, according to De Candolle. I am inclined to believe that it originated in Persia, from where it may have spread to the gardens of India, Syria, Greece, Egypt and as far as Abyssinia: it was introduced into our crops in 1562. The plant is grown in Europe, northern Africa, western Asia and India. CULTIVATION. - Alen cress is an excellent plant, too little cultivated and too little known. Extremely hardy and easy to grow, it thrives almost carefree in any location and on any soil. Watercress is propagated from seed, which is sown almost all year round, except during the coldest months. Seedlings are sown in rays and very thickly. Cover little. During hot spells, sow in the shade, and since it is so easy to set seed, sow at least once a fortnight to avoid running out, and water abundantly if the soil is dry to ensure tender products. VARIETIES. - Cresson alénois frisé. - Deep-cut, almost laciniate, contoured leaves. Cresson alénois à larges feuilles. - Very good species, with broad, slightly serrated leaves, more abundant than in curly cress, less pungent. Cresson alénois doré. - A sub-variety of the previous one, differing in its pale green to yellow foliage, very vigorous and hardy, much appreciated. A few years ago, it was announced as a new variety; this was a mistake, as we found it mentioned in 1789. Dwarf, very curly watercress. - A new variety, producing a small tuft of attractive decorative effect. SEEDS. - Perhaps no other plant reproduces so easily. The seed germinates very quickly. At a temperature of 13 to 15 degrees, it sprouts in eighteen or twenty-two hours. When the stems begin to change color, they are collected and laid out on cloths in the sun. When they begin to dry, they are beaten; the seeds detach very easily. They keep for five years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Slugs and snails are not very fond of them and don't touch them. Only the flea beetle occasionally perforates the first leaves, but this does not cause much damage. We know of no disease that attacks it. USES. - The leaves are used as a salad garnish, or to garnish roasts. In winter, the seeds, because they germinate easily, are used to provide greenery indoors. It can be sown in planters or pots filled with moss or damp sand, in sponges, around a bottle or in clay, which can be given the most original shapes. EARTH CRESS Erysimum præcox. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Native biennial plant, found in a spontaneous state, growing mainly in damp, shady places and in demolition soil. CULTIVATION. - Sow from March to August, omE. BERGER, Vegetables. 13 bre as much as possible, especially during the summer, in rays, or better, broadcast in beds; use a fork or rake to cover. Emergence is easy. When the plant is strong enough, as it only goes to seed in the second year, it is eaten by picking the largest leaves in succession. In the second year, when the plant goes to seed, one or two more harvests of leaves can be made, then the whole plant is harvested. This is usually done even in the first year. This plant is very hardy and not very fussy about the choice of soil; it does well everywhere; a few weedings and waterings in summer are enough; seedlings must be renewed every year, so that there is always a harvest to be had. SEEDS. - To harvest seeds, let the previous year's seedlings rise. The stems are collected, as with Alen cress, and stored in the same way. It remains good for three years. USES. - The leaves are eaten in salads, mixed with romaine or chicory. They are also used with steak. The English are very fond of this plant. CRESSON DE FONTAINE Sisymbrium Nasturtium L. Cardamine Nasturtium Mœnch. Nasturtium officinale Scop. Cardamine fontana Lamk. Crucifers. ORIGIN. - Perennial, native and spontaneous throughout Europe and northern Asia. Called cailli, watercress, fountain cress, body health, etc. (fig. 27), this plant is known and appreciated just about everywhere. CULTIVATION. - Cultivation is not difficult, but a good source of clear water is required. Attempts have been made to grow it in tubs or basins filled with soil, with the water changed frequently, but this method is rarely used except by amateurs. Besides, plants grown in this way are never very beautiful. Once you have a suitable plot of land, you can try growing them, as they are very productive. FIG. 27: Watercress. Most often, we dig pits 40 to 45 centimetres deep, 2, 3 or even 4 metres wide, divided by beds at least 5 or 6 metres wide, which we use for cultivation. These pits should be laid out in such a way that they can be submerged if necessary, and have a slight slope to allow the water to drain off slowly. If, on the other hand, the land is on a slope, and the source is in the upper part, ditches are dug across it, and the water from one will pass into the other successively. But to do this, the water must be well regulated, so that all the pits have water at the same time and none dries out. The size of the pits has no influence whatsoever on good cultivation: we've seen magnificent results obtained with very small spaces. Once the pits have been dug, the bottom is smoked and ploughed, and levelled as best as possible. Once this operation has been completed, cress stems are transplanted 10 or 12 centimetres apart, chosen from among the most vigorous and beautiful. Water is added gradually, and harvesting doesn't begin until the stems are well developed and cover the bottom of the pits. This operation should be carried out in spring and during August. If, on the other hand, you want to propagate cress by sowing, in spring, once the pits have been prepared, you make shallow furrows 20 centimetres apart and sow the seed. Then rake over the whole area, and beat in with a beater. At first, only a thin trickle of water is left to lightly sprinkle the surface; too much will wash the seed away. When the seedling has grown sufficiently, submerge it completely and continue. Propagation by seed is much more time-consuming, and so is rarely practised by true cress growers; the first method we have indicated is the most expeditious and practical, but there are not always enough cress fields to supply the seedlings. Watercress is harvested using large planks or boards, which are placed across the pits. Cutting is done with a serpette, taking care not to disturb the roots, which would be detrimental to future production. After cutting, the roots are pushed up with a long-handled bat. You can even spread a light layer of well-consumed cow manure over the entire surface, which is then pressed down at the same time. Watercress likes shade, and too much heat is harmful. They should therefore be secured with lath or heather trays, placed on prepared frames with strong stakes driven into the soil of the watercress bed. These stakes must be high enough to allow you to work under them easily. In the heat of summer and spring, the same pit can be cut every twenty days. But in winter, reproduction is slower. When extreme cold is expected, watercress should be submerged to keep it from freezing, and the water drained off when the temperature eases. A well-maintained watercress bed can last for several years. When it needs renewing, proceed as described above. SEEDS. - When you want to harvest cress seed, you let some of it rise in spring and, when the stems start to turn yellow, you cut them and dry them by laying them out for a few hours a day in the sun, then you beat them and put them in bags. A liter of seeds weighs 600 grams; 30 grams contain 121,600 seeds. Germination lasts five years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Watercress has few enemies. Flea beetles sometimes pierce the leaves, but this only happens in watercress beds where there has been a lack of water and the plant has suffered. On the other hand, it is prone to being invaded and suffocated by other plants which, although not parasitic, take over the water so quickly that it becomes impossible to grow. These harmful plants are: duckweed, beccabunga speedwell and bergamot. The latter is less harmful than the former, which multiply very quickly. So, when renewing a watercress bed, make sure you don't add any. USES. - Watercress is eaten in salads or raw under roasted meats: the legendary watercress steak is far from out of fashion. Chopped with spinach or sorrel, it makes a delicate dish. JAPANESE CROSN Stachys affinis. Labiatae. ORIGIN. - Japan-China. - Introduced in 1882, propagated by M. Pailleux, who named them after Crosnes, the village in Seine-et-Oise where he lives. M. Naudin has proposed naming this vegetable Stachys tuberifera. GROWING. - Propagates by rhizomes and cuttings taken in spring. Rhizome propagation is preferable; they are planted in April, at a good exposure, in light soil, well amended to 40 or 50 centimetres. Towards the end of May, when the stems have reached 15 or 20 centimetres, they are weeded and ridged. After that, only light weeding is needed to avoid disturbing the rootlets; water during hot spells. Towards the end of October, you can start eating the rhizomes successively throughout the winter; they are all the better for having been freshly plucked. There's no need to fear frost, as they can withstand temperatures of 20 degrees below zero. VARIETY. - Choro-Gi. - New variety announced by M. de Vilmorin. USES. - In this plant, the food part is provided by the rhizome-shaped roots in a pearly white string, the size of an Oxalis crenata. They can be eaten like fresh flageolet beans, or as a garnish around roast meats, fried in boiling fat after having been previously cooked in fatty broth; au gratin with truffles or mushrooms, in simple or mixed salads, in macedoine, in butter with chopped parsley à la maître d'hôtel. They are preserved in vinegar like gherkins and capers, and taste like artichokes. They are a top-quality foodstuff, containing all the substances you need to eat. The yield is sufficiently high for this plant to occupy a place in every home garden. CUMIN OF MALTA Cuminum Cyminum L. Umbelliferae. Native to Upper Egypt. An annual, this plant is sown in the open ground in May. The seeds are used in pastries and liqueurs. COMMON ECHALOTE Allium ascalonicum L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Ancient authors have, to this day, given Palestine as the homeland of the shallot. It was brought back during the Crusades. It was found in the vicinity of the town of Ascalon; hence the scientific name Allium ascalonicum, corrupted into escaloigne, echalogne and finally échalote. But De Candolle destroys this assertion and says of this plant: "Despite the progress of botanical investigations in the East and India, this form of allium has not been found in the wild with any certainty. It therefore seems to me to be more than ever a modification of cepa, which occurred around the beginning of the Christian era, a modification less considerable than many of those observed in other cultivated plants. CULTIVATION. - The shallot is one of the easiest perennials to grow. It is very hardy and adapts well to any soil. It thrives in new demolition soils and platras, and seems to prefer soils with a certain amount of limestone. Most authors who have written about this plant claim that it does not adapt to recent fertilization; this is a mistake. By using only well-rotted manures, you can smoke the soil immediately before planting, and the shallot will succeed perfectly. Shallots are propagated by their bulbs, which are planted from November to February; autumn plantings are preferable in the south and south-west. Medium-sized bulbs are chosen, and buried so that the top part is level with the soil being worked. In some parts of the Midi and the West, bulbs are planted with a hoe, and the soil is raked as described above for garlic. But in general, and especially in the North, planting is done with a non-tapered planter, or simply with the thumb and forefinger, which is quicker; but the soil must be freshly worked. It is divided into plots of five or six rays, spaced 20 or 25 centimetres apart. The line is set at 20 centimetres. During the course of vegetation, we hoe and weed several times. When the tops begin to yellow, in June or the first two weeks of July, we pull them off and lay them flat on the ground. Leave them like this for three days, turn them over several times, then bring them into a well-aerated attic or shed, early in the morning when the tops are still moist and not brittle, to keep them intact. VARIETIES. - Common shallot. - This variety, which is the most commonly grown, is undoubtedly the best and most esteemed; very hardy, it produces abundantly. There are several other varieties, all derived from this one: Échalote hâtive de Niort. - Bulbs slightly larger than the common shallot; produces less. Échalote grosse de Noisy. - A variety highly esteemed in Paris; grows very large and keeps well. Echalote petite hâtive de Bagnolet. - Bulbs as small as a small spring onion, produces a lot and early. SEEDS. - The common shallot rarely flowers and produces few or no seeds. We have tried every possible means to obtain seeds, but have been unsuccessful. MAlAPiES, NUiSiBlE ANImALS. - In certain areas, shallots are subject to a disease that gardeners call scald, but which is produced by Rhizoctonia allii, a parasitic fungus with whitish or grayish filaments; this parasite attacks the base of bulbs and causes them to rot. We noticed that it always developed on buried clumps. To prevent this disease, which we have seen destroy whole fields of shallots, you need to dig up the diseased plants, and this operation will not only stop the disease, but also make the bulbs grow. 13. Uredo or rust sometimes invades the tops, but this disease only develops in the late season, i.e. when the shallot is ready to be pulled. USES. - Shallots are frequently used in cooking to enhance the flavour of dishes, such as the traditional shallot steak. It is also preserved in vinegar, like and in the company of gherkins. In some places, it's cooked under an open fire, and eaten with pepper or seasoned with oil. It's a delicacy that, it's said, was highly prized by the ancients. Picked young and still tender, the leaves are used as a salad ingredient, and can replace chives. Echalotte is an aperitif, but frequent use is harmful to health. JERSEV SHALLOT Quite a different breed from the common shallot, resembling an onion and tasting a little like one. Leaves are glaucous, long and quite bulbous in the middle. Bulbs are small, averaging six to ten per clump. CULTIVATION. - Same cultivation as the common shallot. SEEDS. - This breed goes to seed every year and produces fertile seed. If sown in July-August, a fairly good crop of bulbs can be obtained the following year. In some parts of the Charentes region, this breed is known as the onion of plenty, and is often confused with the potato onion. USES. - The Jersey shallot is highly esteemed in England and Ireland. It can be used for a host of culinary purposes, such as stews, sauces, or candied in vinegar; it keeps quite well, especially if care has been taken to let it ripen properly. OROINARY SPINACH Spinacia oleracea L. Miller. Chenopodea. ORIGIN. - The origin of this plant is doubtful. Olivier claims to have found it in the wild in Persia; according to FIG. 28 - Common spinach. Cassini, it comes from Asia Minor and was cultivated by the Arabs, to whom we owe its introduction into Europe. It does not appear to have been known to the Greeks or the Romans, although several scholars believe they recognized it in the Greek Crysalachanon. Pierre de Crescence claims that the spiny-fruited species was the first to be cultivated, which would explain the name spinach given to the genus. Spinach has been cultivated in Europe for around two centuries. De Candolle gives Persia as the home of the common spinach, where it has been cultivated for 2,000 years. It is also highly esteemed in China and Manchuria. CULTIVATION. - This is one of the easiest vegetables to grow. It is sown from February to November, in rows spaced 30 to 35 centimetres apart, or broadcast in beds. Cover the seed with the loosest soil possible. Spinach does well in a variety of growing soils, but preferably in low-moisture, well-smoked soil, with good, energetic fertilizers; feces and feather are suitable. When young, watering with liquid manure gives good results. Seedlings sown from February to July go to seed within the year; so, to ensure that you always have some to pick, it's essential to renew seedlings at least every fifteen or twenty days. Water copiously and often. On the other hand, seedlings sown from August to November only come up the following year, in April, after having given an abundant harvest throughout the winter, and round-seeded varieties are preferred. Viroflay spinach has always given us good results. For spring and summer, choose the prickly-seeded varieties, which give a better harvest and rise more slowly than the round-seeded variety; to hasten germination, market gardeners usually soak the seeds in water for twelve or fifteen hours. Once the seeds have sprouted, they need to be watered copiously and often, to prevent them from rising so quickly, and as soon as the plants are sufficiently developed, they can be pulled out for consumption. For winter plants, on the other hand, we start by picking the lower leaves, and only pull them out in spring, when the plant is rising. The most developed leaves are picked one by one, respecting the youngest. This apparently time-consuming operation can be carried out quickly, especially by people who are used to it; women are good at it. In general, market gardeners don't sow spinach on its own; it's always combined with lettuces, lamb's lettuce, chervil, etc. When it's ready to sell, we pull it out, always thinning it out; the roots are used to tie it up in small bunches. VARIETIES. - Spinach has been divided into two classes: prickly-seeded and round-seeded. Spinach with prickly seed (Spinacia spinosa Mœnch). - Common spinach. - A variety closely resembling the wild plant cultivated in some areas. Leaves are long, narrow, sharp, very sagittate and of mediocre quality. English or winter spinach. - A recommendable variety, producing very broad, long, thick leaves; it is very hardy and stands up fairly well to our winters; it is very good and well suited to spring and summer sowing. Epinard camus de Bordeaux. - Probably derived from the previous variety and improved by market gardeners in the Bordeaux area. Produces a rosette of rounded, well-developed, thick leaves; highly esteemed on markets in the South-West region. Round-seeded spinach (Spinacia glabra Miller). - This breed differs from the previous one in its seeds and vegetation. The leaves are broader and fuller, forming a compact, tight tuft; the leaves are fuller in all their parts than the other breed; they are said to taste better. Spinach monstrueux de Viroflay - A highly recommendable variety, producing very broad, sometimes 20 to 25 cm-long, thick leaves; very hardy, suitable for autumn sowing. Flanders spinach. - A widespread variety grown extensively in northern France; broad, hardy leaves; can be sown all year round. Lettuce leaf spinach. - Variety with broad, dark-green, very blistered leaves, spreading, compact clump, medium size, hardy, medium yield. Slow-rising spinach. - A little-known variety, which goes to seed less quickly than its predecessors. Green, blistered leaves; tight, compact clump; good yield, does well sown in autumn. Catillon's lazy spinach. - New variety, broad, thick, slightly blistered leaves, very heat-resistant. SEEDS. - Some authors recommend harvesting seed from spring-sown varieties, as this produces more and faster, but the seed is less nourished and not as good as that taken from autumn-sown plants. It's best to choose autumn-sown plants that have produced several leaf harvests. They start to come up in April, and the seed is ready for harvesting towards the end of June or July, depending on the soil. Generally, the seeds don't ripen all at once, and when you notice that a few plants are turning yellow and that the seeds clustered around the stem are changing color, pull them out and lay them on sheets or blankets in part-shade. From then on, you'll need to spend most of your time with them, until you've finished pulling them out. When they are completely dry, remove them from the stem by passing them between your fingers, clean them and bag them. They keep well for up to four years. 30 grams contain 2360 seeds, a liter weighs 530 grams. DISEASES, PESTS. - Spinach is sometimes affected by a fungus of the miller genus, which attacks the roots and kills the plant in a few days. This disease seems to thrive particularly in chalky, heavily smoked soils, as we have observed several times in our crops. There is no cure for this disease. Cutworms sometimes cause serious damage, cutting the root a few centimeters below soil level, causing the plant to turn yellow and die; the damage is only noticed when the roots are cut out; the best remedy is to destroy these worms by digging up the soil. Cutworms and white grubs are most common in light soils. If Botrytis tenella gives good results in destroying white grubs, as announced, let's hope it will be possible to wipe them out. USES. - Spinach is frequently used in cooking. Deprived of its primary water, it is a light, healthy food. It is used in lean soups. It can be prepared in a variety of ways, with butter or fat, or mixed with sorrel to soften its acidity. Alexandre Dumas was very fond of spinach, and prepared it in his own way: after boiling it, he seasoned it with goose fat. The leaves dye the wool prepared with bismuth nitrate a bright, solid, greenish lemon-yellow. Dried leaves produce a green alcoholic dye used to color table liqueurs. ESTRAGON Artemisia dracunculus L. Oligosporus condimentarius H. Cass. Composites.
