ORIGIN. - Herbaceous perennial, native to northern Asia, introduced into our cultures in 1596, known as serpentine, dragon herb, dragone, etc. FIG. 29: Tarragon (Artemisia dracunculus). "CULTURE. - Tarragon does not produce fertile seeds, so it is propagated by separating clumps in spring or July. It is generally planted in well-ploughed, well-smoked beds with good exposure. Lines are drawn and planted at 30 to 35 centimetres in all directions, watering as soon as planting is done and copiously during hot spells. In May, spread a good mulch over the beds, and loosen the soil surface with good hoeing. Although native to Siberian regions, tarragon fears the extreme cold of our winters, and to preserve it as frost approaches, cover the clumps with a few centimetres of compost, manure, straw or, better still, dry leaves; planting should be renewed every three years. To make sure you don't run out of tarragon in winter, place trunks on the boards; surround the trunks with thick stoves, rearranging and renewing them often. You can also prepare good layers, place the chests and panels on them, then pull out the clumps of tarragon and place them as tightly as possible, covering them with 6 centimetres of potting soil. When the stems start to appear, air them frequently. Cover with straw mats overnight to keep the stems fresh all winter long. Seeds of a plant called Russian tarragon (Artemisia Redwskii), which has the habit and appearance of tarragon but nowhere near the flavour, are commercially available; unscrupulous seed merchants sell the seeds of this plant as those of true tarragon, which produces none. The annual Tagetes lucida has also been recommended as a possible replacement for tarragon, its leaves having much the same odour and flavour. We doubt that cultivation of this plant will become widespread. SEEDS. - We have already said that tarragon does not produce seeds. The flower, however, is well formed. All the organs are perfect, so why this infertility? Could it be that too much heat at flowering time over-excites certain generative organs to the detriment of others? We can't say for sure, but there's something abnormal going on to keep this plant sterile. In the past, it must have produced a few seeds, because all the old authors who wrote about horticulture before 1800 mention it as multiplying from seed. Perhaps this is a mistake. DISEASES AND PESTS. - We know of no disease or insect that attacks this plant, probably because of its strong odor. USES. - Tarragon is widely used as an excellent condiment and salad dressing. It is also used to make omelettes and in pickles, which are preserved in vinegar. By macerating it for a few hours in vinegar, it flavors it. In Persia, people eat it mixed with bread. FLORENCE FENNEL Fœniculum dulce L. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant native to Italy. CULTIVATION. - Florence fennel is sown in March-April, in shallow rays 45 to 50 centimeters apart. The seed is lightly covered, and sprouts within a few days. When the plants are sufficiently developed, they are thinned out so that the remaining ones are 10 to 15 centimetres apart. During hot spells, water copiously and hoe several times. Young plants that have been thinned out can be transplanted into a bed prepared for this purpose; they recover quite well and produce a crop that succeeds those left in place. In July-August, a new seedling is sown for winter harvesting. When the base of the petioles is sufficiently developed and the whole forms a fairly voluminous whole, the soil is mounded and brought in around it. A fortnight later, you can start harvesting, continuing successively with the most advanced and the first ridged plants. SEEDS. - Fennel seeds are harvested from the first seedlings sown in spring. Choose the most beautiful plants that come up in summer; the seed is best harvested in August-September. It degenerates easily; it keeps well for four years. 30 grams contain 3450 seeds, a liter weighs 240 grams. DISEASES, PESTS. - Young fennel plants are sometimes eaten by large yellow slugs that gnaw at the base of the stalks; they can be destroyed by skewering them with a wire rod; or they can be kept away by putting unleached ash or quicklime around the stalks. USES. - The petiole base is eaten after blanching, like celery. The leaves are used to garnish cold meats. They can be boiled and eaten as a salad, or used to flavour certain dishes. A few leaves boiled with chestnuts give them an excellent taste. In France, fennel is little used. A knob of fennel is added to the court bouillon in which fish is cooked; but in Italy, it is the leading vegetable, and this is probably the origin of the popular Italian saying: Finocchio e pane mi bastan (I only need fennel and bread). In Italy and Provence, young shoots are eaten in soup or salad; on the Cape of Good Hope, they are cooked or roasted. Seeds are preserved with gherkins. Seeds are used to make liqueurs. The whole plant turns dark yellow. The essential oil is widely used in perfumery. BITTER FENNEL Fœniculum vulgare Gært. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - A perennial that grows naturally in France, preferably in rubble, roadsides and dry, arid places. Some authors give this plant as originating in Africa, where it grows abundantly in wasteland, to such an extent that it is considered a weed. CULTIVATION. - Same as Florence fennel. USES. - This variety is little cultivated; as a vegetable, it is preferred to the superior Florence fennel; in some regions, it is grown for its seeds, which are used to make liqueurs. SWEET FENNEL Fœniculum officinale. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Same origin as the previous variety. Perennial, biennial, distinguished from the preceding race by its less slender, thicker stems, more glaucous in color, by its sheathing petioles, swollen at the bottom, by its seed, which is much larger and twice as long, and finally by its less elevated, stockier habit. CULTIVATION. - Same care in cultivation. USES. - Same uses. Orobus faba Brot. Faba vulgaris Mill. Faba vicia L. Faba major Rivinus. Potamogeton bifolium Lapeyr. Leguminosae, Papilionaceae. ORIGIN. - According to De Candolle, the cultivation of fava beans dates back to prehistoric times in Europe, Egypt and Arabia. It was introduced into Europe, probably by the Western Aryans, during their first migrations (Pelasges, Celts, Slavs). It was later brought to China, a century before the Christian era, and even later to Japan, and more recently to India. As for the spontaneous habitat, it's possible that it was doubled a few thousand years ago, one center being south of the Caspian Sea, the other in northern Africa. The bean would therefore originate from the south of the Caspian Sea and has been cultivated for over 4,000 years. CULTIVATION. - Fava beans thrive in all types of well-smoked soils, but they do best in strong, free soils. The first beans are sown in October, and then fortnight by fortnight until March, so as not to run out. After all, they're only eaten green and half-formed, and successive sowings mean you can enjoy them for a long time, and make good use of them. Depending on the soil, they can be planted in rows of 40 or 50 centimetres, or in beds of four rows of 35 or 40 centimetres. Follow a 60-centimeter path. We sow at 50 centimetres, on the line, further apart if necessary when the soil is good, because with these kinds of plants we must avoid confusion,

which causes them to wither and delays early production. 237 In most cases, the lines are traced with a tracing tool, or simply stretched out with a string, then the beans are sown by pushing them in with the thumb at a distance of around 5 centimetres, or with a lightly eulogized planter. In either case, a little soil is brought in to cover them. In the south-west of France, farmers sow beans in furrows, at least 70 or 80 centimetres apart, putting four or five rows in each. This system is not bad in that the plants, although a little tightly packed, still receive air and don't wither. Production is generally very good. When the beans have reached a height of 7 to 8 centimetres, the first light hoeing is carried out when all traces of dew have disappeared. This operation is repeated every few weeks. Beans should never be watered, as the high humidity causes them to turn yellow. When the plant has reached a height of 35 to 40 centimetres, we pinch off the tip, which usually coincides with the tenth or twelfth leaf, and pinch off the top. The purpose of this operation is to stop the upward movement of the plant and to force the sap into the lower parts of the plant, which greatly advances fruit formation. In Angoumois, Saintonge and Quercy, beans are sown in small furrows with a forked or flat hoe; this is not a bad way of working, but it cannot be applied to all soils. VAriéTés. - Aguadulce bean with long pods (fig. 30). - One of the best varieties to grow in gardens, the pods are very long, from 35 to 40 cen- FIG. 30. - Aguadulce bean with long pods. 239 centimeters by 4 centimeters thick, they are particularly suitable for green sales. Seville bean. - An early variety with very long pods, highly recommended for green sales. Fêve de Windsor. - Hardy variety with numerous short pods, containing only two seeds. Fêve Julienne. - Variety with numerous pods containing only three beans, they are upright and grouped in threes and fours; very hardy, very good for green manure. Fève Julienne verte. - Distinguished from the previous variety by the green color of the bean, it is a later variety. Early dwarf broad bean. - A completely dwarf variety, it does not exceed 35 or 40 centimeters in height, with small, upright, very numerous pods, grouped in twos or threes, containing two or three square, thick, swollen beans. Suitable for early and forced cultivation. Fève de Mazagran. - A very good variety for large-scale cultivation, with numerous pods and small seeds. Fève naine verte de Beck. - Bean with white flowers. - Beans with long pods. - Violet bean, etc. SEEDS. - To harvest good seed, you must sow with this intention, i.e. treat the plants with the cultivation care we have indicated, but leave all the fruit, not picking any, as the first to form are the best and earliest. The plants are left to dry on the spot, then brought in, and when the weather is bad, they are shucked. The seed keeps well for five or six years. However, we recommend choosing seed from the previous year. 500 grams contain around 250 to 300 beans; a liter weighs 620 grams. MAlADIES, PEST ANIMALS. - Sometimes in poor soils, beans are affected by rust or uredo. It is difficult to destroy this disease, which rapidly invades plants, and the remedy we have indicated for chicory is the only one that has given us good results. The bean's cruelest enemy is the black aphid (Aphis nigra), which develops more especially on the upper part of the stem, but if you take the precaution of pinching early enough, its progress is impeded; several remedies have been tried, none of which produced any effect; nicotine alone seems to have given some results. USES. - Broad beans are an invaluable source of nourishment, and it is to be regretted that their use is not more widespread, as they could be of great help to the less well-off classes. Like beans and lentils, broad beans are very nourishing, and contain a considerable proportion of a nitrogenous substance, legumin, which is very similar to animal casein and contributes greatly to their nutritional qualities. According to Payen, 100 parts of beans contain: 24.40 legumin; 1.50 fat; 51.50 dextrin and sugar starch; 3 cellulose; 3.60 mineral salts; 16 water. Picked before they are fully ripe, when they are still green and dried, the product is less abundant but even more nourishing. Young shoots are eaten in salads and used in lean soups. E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 14 Fava beans are frequently used to fatten animals, and can be used as a good substitute for oats for horses. In some countries, it is mixed with wheat flour. The sweet scent of fava bean flowers has led to their use in perfumery in the form of distilled water; this water is used as a cosmetic, as is fava bean flour, which has been attributed with the property of removing freckles. In England, broad beans are cooked with honey and used as fish bait. Dry stalks yield a lot of potash when incinerated. GLACIAL FICOID Mesembryanthemum crystallinum L. Ficoidae. ORIGIN. - Native to the Cape of Good Hope. Perennial in greenhouses, but grown as an annual in our gardens. CULTIVATION. - Sow in the same way as spinach, in March, April or, better still, February on warm beds, transplant into small pots and plant out in May. Other care consists of weeding and hoeing, and above all good watering throughout the summer. This plant is resistant to the hottest weather, but the leaves are small and not very succulent. It's best to keep it well watered, as the yield will be quintupled. SEEDS. - To harvest the seed, select a few good plants and let them flower. When the capsules are ripe, they are collected, left to dry and the seeds extracted. One gram contains 5700 seeds, a liter weighs 760 grams and lasts five years. USES. - The leaves are chopped and cooked like spinach. Used in herb broths. It is said to be very healthy and nourishing. In Bourbon, the leaves are often eaten as a vegetable. In the Canaries, the seeds are pulverized and cooked; in the same islands, soda ash is extracted from this plant. FRAISIER COMMUN, FRAISIER DES BOIS Fragaria vesca L. Fragaria vulgaris Ehr. Rosaceae. ORIGIN. - The common strawberry is native to Europe, from the Shetland Islands and Lapland to the mountainous regions of the Midi, Madeira, Spain, Sicily and Greece. It is also found in Asia, from northern Syria and Armenia to Daourie. The strawberry plants of the Himalayas and Japan, which various authors have attributed to this species, are perhaps not, and this makes me doubt the habitat in China given by a missionary. It occurs spontaneously in Iceland, in the northeastern United States, around Fort Cumberland and on the northwest coast, perhaps even in the Sierra Nevada of California; the habitat therefore extends around the Arctic pole, with the exception of eastern Siberia and the Amur River region. The strawberry plant has been known since antiquity, but was little cultivated. Virgil and Pliny only mention it in passing. Cultivation was introduced to France in the 15th or 16th century. Champier, in the 16th century, spoke of it as a novelty in northern France, but it already existed in the Midi and in England (De Candolle). In France, the common strawberry is found in a spontaneous state just about everywhere, usually growing at the edges of hedges bordering forests, in high forest woods, where it seems to prefer damp clearings. Today, Fragaria vesca is rarely cultivated, preferring the Alpine strawberry, which has the advantage of producing fruit for part of the summer. CULTIVATION. - Same cultivation as the four-season strawberry. VARIETIES. - This breed has produced two distinct varieties in terms of fruit shape: Fraise des bois petite hâtive de Chatenay. - Early, small, round, dark-red fruit, very fragrant, does not come up. Strawberry from Montreuil. - Vigorous, very fertile plant with elongated, dark-red fruit, very late and productive, does not come up. USES. - Same uses as the four-season strawberry. ALPES OR FOUR SEASONS STRAWBERRY Fragaria alpina Pers. Fragaria semperftorens Hayne. ORIGIN. - This breed originates from the Alps, where it grows at fairly high altitudes. It was brought to France in 1754 by Fougeroux de Bondaroy, who had picked it on Mont Cenis. - Strawberry plants in general, and the small-fruited breeds in particular, are not very fussy about choice of soil; however, light, moist, healthy soil is preferable. In dry soils, watering is essential in summer if good production is to be achieved. Strawberry plants are propagated by runners, which are planted at two different times: in March or April, or at the end of summer, in August, September or even November. Planting in August and September is best, as the young plants have time to recover before the cold sets in, making them more resistant to the low temperatures of winter. In the early summer, they grow vigorously and, depending on the soil, form good clumps that produce abundantly. Those planted in April-May, on the other hand, don't bear fruit until the following year, apart from a few rare flowers here and there. Common and Alpine strawberries reproduce readily by sowing; sow as soon as the seeds are ripe, in June or July, in a slightly shaded bed with light, loose soil. Sow in small rays, lightly covered with good potting soil, and in ten or twenty days the seeds will sprout. As soon as the seedlings can withstand transplanting, they are thinned out and planted out in a nursery, and when it's time to set out in September, they are put in place as you would another runner. In spring, they produce as abundantly as those propagated in any other way. You can also sow in March-April, on a warm bed; the seedling is transplanted, on another cold bed, or in the open ground, and put in place towards the end of May or June. This procedure is somewhat safer, but also takes longer. Whichever propagation method you choose, the soil must first be ploughed deeply and fertilized with good, well-decomposed fertilizers. Horned cattle manure seems to be a good choice. Strawberries are planted in beds 1.20 m wide, with four 30-centimetre radii divided by 50-centimetre paths. Plant at 30 cm 14. along the line and in a checkerboard pattern. If the plant is small, put two together and, as soon as planting is complete, lightly water each plant to pack the soil around the roots. Before planting, trim off as much as possible of the root tips and leaves, and choose plants with white roots. Avoid planting FIO plants. 31. - Strawberry plants with black roots are a sure sign that they are too old. In the first days of March, as the new leaves begin to develop, hoe them deeply, taking care not to uproot them. We hoe again in April, then spread a good mulch over each bed. Other care consists of weeding, and above all, frequent watering, if you want to obtain good production. In seasonally dry soils, irrigation produces excellent results. In October, remove all runners and dead or yellowed leaves. The following year, the same cultivation care is required, as in the third year. After this period, most clumps die back and rot in winter, making planting irregular and requiring renewal. What's more, the fruit on vicious plants is smaller and less fragrant. FORCED CULTIVATION. - This breed of strawberry can hardly be forced. For this cultivation, see pineapple strawberry. VARIETIES. - La Meudonnaise, once highly esteemed around Paris; slightly blistered, blond-green leaves; large, dark-red, conical fruit, ripens late, very fragrant. Janus strawberry. - Good improved variety of the Alpine strawberry, very fertile, elongated fruit, bright red when ripe and remontant. Belle de Meaux (fig. 31). - New variety; large, conical, dark carmine-red fruit, very fragrant. It is said to be far superior to other varieties in terms of production and taste finesse. Fraisier des Alpes sans filets or Gaillon strawberry with red fruit. - This variety is very different from the others; it does not produce nets and multiplies by dividing clumps. Particularly suitable for borders, it is very hardy and produces abundantly throughout the summer. Netless Alpine strawberry with white fruit. - Same cultivation and hardiness as the previous variety, but the fruit is not very fragrant and not much appreciated on the markets. ANANAS STRAWBERRIES Fragaria grandiflora Ehrh. Fragaria ananassa Duch. ORIGIN. - Very obscure origin; it has never been known where it came from. It is from this breed, crossed with the Chilean strawberry, that the innumerable varieties of large hybrid strawberries, commonly known as English strawberries, originate. OUTDOOR CULTIVATION. - Cultivation is much the same as for the four-season strawberry. Preference should be given to runners that develop in the late season, and care should be taken to ensure that the plants from which they come are productive, as often the most beautiful runners are chosen from poor plants that are vigorous but not very productive, leading to degeneration. Multiplication by sowing is only done to obtain new varieties: in a sowing, there are always few subjects that characterize the species well. FORCED THERMOSIPHON CULTIVATION. - Forced cultivation is certainly not difficult, but it does require a certain amount of practice to succeed. There are several ways of doing this. In this type of cultivation, the most important point is how to prepare the plants for fruiting, since success depends on this and on the aeration of the plants at flowering time. In June or July, select the best runners and plant them 10 cm apart in a well-prepared bed, with plenty of smoke and, if possible, in part-shade. Water often. In October, pull out the runners and put them in six- to seven-inch pots filled with a mixture of 1/4 bedding mix, 1/2 garden mix and 1/4 leaf mix. Put three or four runners per pot, close to the sides rather than in the middle, and the plants develop much better. Once repotted, the pots are half-embedded in sand, tan, potting soil, etc., and shaded for a few days to facilitate growth. Shade them for a few days to help them recover, and leave them in this state until it's time to put them under tarpaulins or in greenhouses to force them out. Cover them if the cold is too severe. When the time comes to force them, i.e. around December 15, we clean them well, remove the yellow and dead leaves and put a certain quantity in place to be heated. We stagger the plants so as to have some to force until those in the open ground begin to produce. Push the pots about halfway into the tan or escarbill and space them 5 to 6 centimetres apart in all directions, then start heating. At first, a gentle heat of 12 to 15 degrees centigrade is maintained, day and night, until the fruit begins to knot, at which point the heat is increased to 20 to 25 degrees. From the moment we start heating, we take care to water the plants, which must be done regularly so that none dries out and wilts, which is their downfall. Water often and lightly, until the flowers appear; from then on, water no more. Once the fruit has set its knots, continue the operation until maturity. Whenever it's possible to give the fruit some air, this should be done, especially when sunburn occurs, rapidly raising the temperature under the windows. The fruit must not touch the ground. You can keep them elevated with rush ties supported by small sticks placed around the pots, or even better, you can make your own little wire rings that last for several years. Another very good way of preparing plants for forcing is to select the best runners in June and July and, without disturbing their attachment to the mother plant, repot them in small cups which you bury in the ground. They are held in place by wooden hooks. These young plants, under the influence of copious watering, develop rapidly and by the end of the summer are beautiful subjects perfectly prepared for forced cultivation. When the plants are weaned in October, they are potted into larger pots, of which there can be three in each 6-inch pot. Of course, this process is time-consuming and costly, but the plants grow vigorously and always produce excellent results. It's an excellent process, highly recommended for amateurs. The varieties that lend themselves best to forced cultivation are : Docteur Morère. - Docteur Nicaise. - Lucie Flament. - Gloire de Zuidwick. - Duc de Malakoff. - La Chalonnaise. - Marguerite. - Jacunda, etc.- Young plantations from the previous year, which have produced a first crop in the open ground, can also give good results if potted early and treated as described above. As for heating, the best systems are small, very low gabled greenhouses, 3 meters wide inside, with benches 1.10 meters wide on each side, and a passageway 80 centimeters wide in the middle. The benches are secured by T-irons resting on dice driven into the ground. The floors of these benches are made of terracotta tiles, also supported by T-irons and angle irons; one or two hot-water pipes run 25 or 30 centimeters along the underside of the bench. The tops of the benches are filled with tan, wood bran or sand to accommodate the pots, which are half-buried. Tarpaulins 1, 60 or 2 meters wide can also be used, with a tiled floor as described for the greenhouse, and a hot water pipe running underneath, 25 centimetres from the wall. As all the heat is concentrated underneath, you need to create openings that can be closed off at will, to let a little warm air into the part above where the plants are located. This tarpaulin system is very good, and gives very good results, but we prefer the small greenhouse where it's easier to keep an eye on the plants. HATÉE CULTIVATION UNDER CHASSIS. - In the early days of January, prepare good layers at least 50 or 60 centimetres thick, with 10 or 15 centimetres of charcoal or tannin residue, let the first fires start and place the strawberry pots in them, burying them about halfway. Cover with a frame. Other care consists of aeration as often as possible, to avoid mildew, as the damp heat of the layer often kills the strawberry plant. Water very sparingly, just to prevent the plant from wilting; you don't need to dunk the plants in water, the mist that comes off is enough; it's only when the fruit has set that you need to water seriously. Water is poured at the base of the plant, without wetting the leaves. Keep warm around the trunks and cover with mats at night to concentrate as much heat as possible. When the fruit has set, we give it plenty of air, and if the sun shines too hot, we shade it. When the heat of the layer diminishes, we prepare another and place them on top. Plants put into forcing in the first days of January won't bear fruit until the end of March, or the first fortnight of April. Another way of bringing forward the ripening of strawberries by a few days in spring is to place boxes and their panels on the beds in March; the paths are dug 20 centimetres deep, and filled with good warm manure up to the height of the boxes. From time to time, these stoves are reshuffled to maintain fermentation; air is given frequently, the plants are watered and cleaned often; in this way, ripe strawberries are produced a few days before those grown in the open ground. VARIETIES. - Here is a list of good varieties. Hybrid strawberries. - Carolina superba. - Vigorous, hairy plant, glossy green leaves, large, slightly flattened, short vermilion fruit; semi-late. Docteur Morère. - Very vigorous, hairy plant, large, broad, dark-green foliage, very large, slightly short, enlarged fruit; very productive. This variety is grown almost everywhere; it is one of the most recommended. Docteur Nicaise. - Plant of average vigor, relative fertility, very large fruit, weighing 40 to 50 grams; semi-hasty. Duc de Malahoff. - Very vigorous plant with large, dark-green leaves; large, enlarged, short fruit; very juicy yellow flesh; excellent, highly recommended variety. Marguerite. - Very vigorous, dark green foliage, very large, elongated, vermilion-red fruit, very sweet, fragrant white flesh; ripens late. Gloire de Zuidvick. - Excessively vigorous, attractive grayish-green leaves, large, conical, semi-early fruit, bright scarlet color, melting flesh. Strawberry Lucas. - Vigorous plant, bright green leaves, oblong, fairly large, dark red fruit, pale pink flesh, juicy, fragrant. Deserving varieties include: Général Chanzy. - Admiral Dundas. - Duke of Edinburgh. - Elton improved. - Goliath. - May Queen. - Cressent seedling. - Princess Royal. - Victoria. - Louis Vilmorin. - La Reine. - Won- derfull Myaths prolific. - Valentin Lebœuf. - Vicomtesse Héricart de Thury. - Sir Charles Napier. - Sabreur. - Jucunda. - Comte de Paris. - Doctor Hoog. - Bayard. - Belle Cauchoise. - La Chalonnaise. - Lucie. - Souvenir de Kief. - Belle de Paris. - Edouard Lefort. - Reine des hâtives. - Souvenir of Bossuet. - Captain. - Anna de Rothschild. - Sir Harry. - Muscadin de Liège. - La Constante. - Premier. - Sir Joseph Paxton. - Monseigneur. Fournier. FRAISIER CAPRON Fragaria elatior Ehrh. Fragaria moschata Duch. ORIGIN. - Perennial, indigenous plant. In the past, this breed was honored and cultivated everywhere; today, it is grown only in botanical gardens, where hybrid strawberries are preferred. CULTIVATION. - This type of strawberry is very hardy, multiplying easily through its numerous runners, but as there are both male and female individuals, it is essential to plant both for fertilization to take place, and when you intend to plant, you will need to label both at the time of production. Like the wild strawberry, this breed produces only one crop in spring, and does not come up again. VARIETIES. - Raspberry Capron. - The best-known and most widely cultivated in the past. The plant is very tall, with blond-green foliage and numerous, spherical, purplish or wine-colored fruits. The flesh is white, slightly yellowish, with a raspberry flavor. Belle Bordelaise. - Plant stockier than above, blond-green foliage, slightly grayish, large fruit, E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 15 254 FRAISIER ÉCARLATE DE VIRGINIE - FRAISIER DU CHILI elongated sometimes, very fragrant, raspberry-like, sometimes rises. It is still grown near Bordeaux. VIRGINIA STRAWBERRY Fragaria virginiana Ehrh. ORIGIN. - Perennial, from South America. CULTIVATION. - A little-known breed that is little cultivated today, due to its fruits, which are small and few in number, and do not come up. It is very hardy, and can remain in the same soil for longer than other breeds. An attempt has been made to fertilize this breed with the pineapple strawberry, and the resulting plants are very beautiful and hardy; same cultivation as the "four seasons" variety. CHILE STRAWBERRY Fragaria chilensis Ehrb. ORIGIN. - Perennial plant from Chile, brought back in 1714 by Frézier. Very common in the Andes, it is found everywhere. It is said to be so invasive in some parts of Chile that it has to be destroyed. CULTIVATION. - Plants belonging to this race are vigorous, stocky, with dark green foliage and very large, irregularly-shaped, bright orange fruits. It is from this breed, fertilized with the pineapple strawberry, that we have obtained the so-called hybrid or English varieties, some of whose fruits weigh up to 70 grams, and we certainly won't stop there: sowings and fertilizations continue, and there is no doubt that before long we'll be able to achieve something better. The Chilean strawberry is a little delicate; it fears the cold and especially winter humidity. In Brittany, it is grown extensively, but in healthy, sandy soils. Same cultivation as hybrid varieties. SEEDS. - To extract the strawberry seed, select the ripest, prettiest fruit; place on a shaded board; when decomposition begins, crush in a container full of water; the seeds will sink to the bottom and be scooped up for immediate sowing or drying. There's no need to wait until the fruit has decomposed. Once picked, if the fruit is ripe enough, the seeds can be removed. They keep well for up to three years. One gram contains between 800 and 2,500 seeds, depending on the species. pests, pests, pests. - The only pest that attacks this plant is white mould, which seems to develop mainly in plantations planted on top soil and where strawberries have been grown for a long time; the best remedy is to water the affected plants with a very clear milk of lime. We have tried iron sulfate, dissolved in plenty of water, but the results have not been satisfactory. Although few diseases attack strawberry plants, pests are very numerous. The white grub or chafer larva is very fond of its roots; in soils where they swarm, it is not uncommon to see entire beds destroyed in a short space of time; until now, the only means of destruction has been to kill it. Cutworms are still a terrible enemy, and there are some soils where you can't grow them because of this worm. If Botrytis tenella can destroy white and cutworms, growers will be delighted. Ants sometimes attack strawberries, but more often than not, they take up residence at the base of strawberry plants and dig galleries, or almost cover the plant with earth; to destroy them, place plates coated with honey on the ground; they go in and can't get out; then dip the plate in boiling water. Slugs are very fond of strawberries, which they gnaw off completely. Only those that touch the ground are damaged. Destroying these beasts is difficult, in the sense that they are always to be found. You hunt them morning and night, skewer them with a sharp wire, and throw them in a bit of slaked lime. You can also place cabbage leaves or pieces of carrot or turnip at some distance from the snail's body; the snail will take refuge under them to devour them, and then they're easy to catch. Snails rarely attack strawberries. The green and grey caterpillar sometimes devours the leaves in spring, so we look for it and crush it, which is the only way to get rid of it. The mole causes considerable damage to plantations, knocking them over and uprooting them by forming underground galleries. You must try to destroy them, either by setting traps or by lying in wait to kill them. We use wooden traps, which are better and safer than iron ones. The millipede is a very long, extraordinarily flexible little worm, which walks both forwards and backwards. It gets into strawberries that touch the ground, in very large numbers, and the inside of the fruit is completely devoured. There's no known way of destroying it. The molewort wreaks havoc in young plantations; it is especially in sandy soils, where they are abundant, that care should be taken. USES. - Strawberries can be served in a variety of ways, with wine, cognac, kirsch, rum, Madeira or even better, champagne. They can be eaten on their own, with sugar or mixed with raspberries. It is said that strawberries lose their fragrance when washed, but this is wrong. If you take the time to wash them half an hour before eating them, they recover their smell, and are cleaner and more appetizing. Strawberries are very digestive. Dissolved in water, they make a pleasant, soothing drink. They are preserved in jams and jellies. They even make an excellent liqueur when mixed with raspberries. Strawberries are used to make a syrup that is used to flavor creams and jellies. Strawberries quickly undergo vinous, alcoholic and acetic fermentation, and can be used to make wine, brandy and vinegar. Strawberries are balsamic and refreshing, and can only be criticized for their slightly laxative properties, which can be corrected with sugar and a little wine. However, as they are generally quite cold, it might be unwise to overindulge in them, especially after a big dinner; but on an empty stomach in the morning, or when the work of digestion has been completed, they are a most salutary dish. Perfumers use distillation to obtain strawberry scents that are very pleasant, and to which they attribute the property of making freckles disappear. In some countries, strawberry leaves are used instead of tea. Today, strawberries are the subject of a sizeable trade. We've managed to package them and ship them over fairly long distances, without them suffering too much. They still fetch a high price on the market, which proves that cultivation is lucrative. CULTIVATED RASPBERRY - -- Rubus idœus L. Rubus frambœsianus Lamk. Rosaceae dryadae. ORIGIN. - A shrub native to Mount Ida; at least, that's where it was first observed, but it is found spontaneously in mountainous regions throughout most of Europe, especially in woods and stony places. Rarer in wooded plains. GROWING. - The raspberry is a perennial shrub that is extremely easy to grow. It thrives in all soils, provided they are not too dry or chalky. It does best, however, in moist soils and semi-shaded exposures. We have seen it planted in northern exposures with very good results. It is propagated by rooting offshoots, which spring up in large numbers around old plants. Preferably choose those with the best roots and medium size; prune them a little if they are too long, refresh the roots a little and plant. The ground on which you intend to plant, whether in a bed or in a square, must first be ploughed well and deeply, and thoroughly fertilized with good, energetic fertilizers. Raspberries are usually planted from November to March. They are planted in rows 90 cm to 1 m apart, sometimes further apart, depending on the fertility of the soil. Raspberries are widely grown in the Charente region, particularly around Angoulême. Market gardeners counter-plant garlic or shallots, as these are radiated with the forked or flat hoe, so they only put in raspberry plants every three or four rays, which gives about the distance indicated; it's an excellent planting system that we recommend. In general, once the land has been well prepared and laid out, market gardeners dig small square holes 15 or 20 centimetres apart, and plant, putting loose soil over the roots. Once the raspberry planting is complete, onions, romaine, lettuce, Bacalan cabbage, etc. are intercropped. Other cultivation care for this first year consists of frequent spading or raking of the soil. In autumn, after pruning, we give the plants a good drying, taking care not to uproot the plants that are to bear fruit. Smoke again, and every year again, as the raspberry bush is an exhausting plant: we remove the new shoots, which would thrive at the expense of the fruit-bearing stems. Watering is useless, because if you have to water with a watering can, it's better not to plant raspberries (unless you can irrigate abundantly). Raspberries will only perform well if the soil is naturally moist. Raspberries should not be left to grow freely, as some growers have found. It is preferable to train or trellis the plant, which is more regular and more tasteful, and the growing costs are never higher. The best method is counter-trellising, which consists of planting a few 1.50 m high stakes at each end of the row and in the middle, which are driven in 30 cm. Three rows of wire are stretched, the first 40 centimetres from the ground, the second 40 centimetres from the first, and the third at the top of the stake. The fruit-bearing branches are attached by bending them at 45 degrees, and the new suckers that develop and are well placed as fruit-bearing branches for the following year are attached vertically to the wire as they grow. A well-tended raspberry plantation can last ten years or more. The Dutch plant in 25-centimeter-deep troughs, with a little ridging each year so that the roots, which always tend to rise to the surface of the soil, are at a suitable depth. PRUNING. - In certain favorable soils, the raspberry bush acquires great vigor; the stems become very strong. These stems are biennial, meaning they grow the first year, bear fruit the second and then die. Pruning should be carried out during winter, from November to February. Choose four of the most beautiful and best-placed stems, fewer if the plant is not very vigorous, and cut them off above one eye, at about a third of their total length, sometimes more, depending on their vigor. All old wood from the previous year is removed, cut flush with the ground, and all unnecessary suckers are removed. VARIETIES. - Some sixty years ago, we only had a few varieties of little merit and completely primitive. Today, we have some excellent ones. Among the best are A fruit rouge. - Merveille des quatre saisons red - with large conical red fruit, reaching up to the frosts. Perpetual red. - Remontant, very fructiferous and fragrant. Belle de Fontenay. - Large, dark-red fruit. Catawissa d'Amérique. - Very remontant, with red fruit and good taste, very fragrant. Falstaff. - Excessively fertile, very good quality, medium red fruit. Superb from England. - Fertile, large conical red fruit; highly recommended variety. Double bearing. - Very beautiful red fruit, remontant, one of the best varieties to grow for the market. Belle de Palluau. - Very large, conical fruit; fairly productive. Hornet. - Very large red fruit, fragrant; productive, good quality. Framboise de Brabant. - Red conical fruit; fairly productive. Yellow or orange fruit. - Merveille des quatre saisons yellow. - Very large, very fragrant fruit; productive. Chile yellow. - Very fertile variety, large yellow fruit, very good quality. Jaune d'Anvers. - Large, conical, yellow fruit; very productive. Brinhles'Orange. - Vigorous, fertile plant with large, bright orange fruit. SEEDS. - To harvest seeds, do as for strawberries: wash the fruit and the seeds will preci15. pent at the bottom of the water, collect them and dry them in the shade. Seeds should only be sown with the aim of obtaining new varieties: it is rare for a seed to faithfully reproduce its species; as far as possible, only fresh seeds should be sown, as they only keep for a year or two at most. USES. - Raspberries are widely used as a healthy, refreshing and soothing food. It is eaten in the same way as strawberries, with which it is often associated. Raspberries, sugar and water are used to make a pleasant drink. It can be used to make wine, alcohol or mead. To make wine, blend 15 kilograms of red raspberries and 3 kilograms of freshly picked redcurrants. The stems and bunches are removed and crushed in the same mortar. After resting for twenty-four hours, the juice is squeezed and the following is added per liter: half a pound of sugar; half a liter of brandy. Mix well and leave to stand until blackberry season. Then take 5 pounds of these fruits, crush them with a liter of water, throw in a broth and let stand thirty-six hours; press and add: Half a pound of sugar; 3 decanters of brandy per liter of liqueur obtained, and mix the two liqueurs which are shaken vigorously. This wine is good to drink only after one year. Raspberry ratafia is obtained by mixing : 600 grams raspberry juice; 1 kilogram sugar; 2 liters brandy. The liqueur is left to settle and bottled. The syrup is made with two-thirds raspberries and one-third redcurrants. Infused in wine, raspberries give it a delicious taste and aroma; with white vinegar, they give raspberry vinegar, which is used to make syrup. In industry, raspberries are frequently used to make carbonated lemonades. It is also used to make jams, jellies, preserves, compotes, etc. Confectioners and pharmacists use it daily, making cakes, tarts, ice creams and more. In some French vineyards, raspberries are added to the vat to give the wine its aroma. Lathyrus sativus L. Cicercula alata Mœnch. Leguminous plants. ORIGIN. - Annual plant of doubtful origin. I consider it probable," says De Candolle, "that the species existed before being cultivated, from the southern Caucasus or the Caspian Sea to northern India, and that it spread to Europe as a result of ancient cultivation, perhaps mixed with cereals. It is therefore a mistake to believe that the plant is indigenous. The Greeks called it Lathyros and the Latins Cicercula. It has several synonyms: Dent de brebis - Jarra. - Domestic chickweed. - Swiss lentil. - Spanish lentil. - Breton pea. - Lentillin. - Square pea. - Pois gras, etc. 263 CULTURE. - Chickling is not widely cultivated in gardens; it is mainly used in field crops. It is sown in March-April, in rows 50 centimeters apart, preferably in sandy soil, with plenty of smoke, so that the grain is cooked, since there is perhaps no other plant on which the nature of the soil has as much influence as on this one. Indeed, chaff harvested in strong soils succeeds perfectly and even produces a lot, but the grain is not cooked, and therefore becomes inedible; at most, it is good for animals. Other cultivation requirements are the same as for peas. - Chickweed is virtually disease-free; only snails and loaches are very keen on it when the plant starts to grow. Mice and voles devour it as soon as it is sown, but fortunately this is rare. USES. - The seeds are eaten like beans; they are very nutritious and have highly appreciated lactiferous properties. Flour is made from them to fatten poultry and pigs; properly cooked, the seeds make an excellent and very healthy food, and the opposite occurs when they are not perfectly cooked. GOMEO Hibiscus esculentus L. Hibiscus ficifolius Mill. A belmoschus esculentus Mœnch. Malvaceae ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to South America, little cultivated and little appreciated in France. GROWING. - Okra is propagated from seed, which is sown on warm beds from February to April; when the young plants have grown their second leaf, they are transplanted to another warm bed 10 or 15 centimetres apart. In May, when frosts are no longer a threat, they are planted out at 65 centimetres. It's best to choose FIG. 32 - Long-fruited okra. a good exposure and sufficiently amended soil, as this plant needs a lot of heat to ripen its seeds. When first transplanted, they can also be placed in pots and cared for in the same way as other plants. This way, they won't have to suffer from transplanting. Other cultivation care consists of frequent hoeing around the plants and copious watering in summer. VARIETY. - Long-fruited okra (fig. 32). - Low stem not exceeding 55 centimeters, leaf cut like that of mallow, upright fruit 15 to 20 centimeters long, pointed, thin, 2 to 3 centimeters in diameter. A sub-variety with larger, hanging fruit is grown. Round-fruited okra. - Stem 49 to 50 centimeters long, fruit 16 to 17 centimeters long by 40 centimeters wide, obtuse; earlier than the previous variety. SEEDS. - In Paris and northern France, seeds rarely ripen in the open ground, they need the greenhouse; from the Charentes, and moving up to the southwest and south of France, they ripen perfectly in good exposure. When the fruits containing the seeds have changed color, they are ripe: they are picked and laid out to dry in the sun. The seed then detaches quite well; it keeps well for five years, 30 grams contain 450 grams, a liter weighs 620 grams. USES. - When not yet fully developed, sliced and boiled in soups, sauces or even flavored water, okra fruits make these liquids thick and viscous, giving them a tangy flavor that the Creoles of Martinique and Guadeloupe find very pleasant, as they eat a lot of them. The seeds have been advocated as a substitute for coffee, and we have tried them; we doubt their use will spread. In Brazil, the fruit is eaten cooked with poultry. ORDINARY CURRANT Ribes rubrum L. ORIGIN. - The red currant is native to northern and temperate Europe, as well as to Siberia as far as Kamtschatka, and in America from Canada and Vermont to the mouth of the Mackensie River. Unknown to the Greeks and Romans, cultivation of this plant was only introduced in the Middle Ages. The cultivated plant differs little from the wild plant". (De Candolle). CULTIVATION. - The currant generally adapts well to all kinds of soil, and even exposure, as it can be found at many different altitudes. We know of some that have been planted under large trees, and mixed with other shrubs. Despite this, they grow well and produce a lot. However, this plant prefers cool, deep soil and a well exposed position. The currant can be propagated from seed, but this is a time-consuming and expensive method. It is rarely sown except to obtain larger-fruited varieties. The best way to propagate is by cuttings, which take very well. We choose young shoots of the year for this purpose, and put them in place or in a nursery, but they take so well that it's preferable to put them directly in place; this avoids transplanting, which is always detrimental to the plant. Cuttings are taken from October to February. Cuttings taken in autumn will always give better results than those taken later. Redcurrants can also be propagated by splintering rooted plants, which can be found in fairly large quantities in old clumps. Other cultivation techniques include removing dead wood and cutting back large stems to rejuvenate them. We spade around the plants at least twice a year, and give them several rakes or hoeings to prevent weeds from getting into the clump. PRUNING. - You can give the currant any shape you like. By pruning, you can form counter-trees on wire, in a ball, in a cone, in cords, etc. Pruning consists in removing dead wood and clearing the interior to avoid confusion, and all the current year's shoots are cut at five or six eyes above the previous year's size, i.e. above the impasto. VARIETIES. - Red currants. - Dutch red currant. - Long, tight bunches, very large, light-red fruit; late and very vigorous. Queen Victoria currant. - Loose, very long clusters; fairly productive. Versailles redcurrant. - Numerous, very large, light-red fruits in long, tightly-packed bunches. Hátive de Bertin. - Tight clusters, dark red fruit; very early. Cherry currant, large fruit. - Short, sparsely-packed bunches; large, light-red fruit; vigorous, stocky plant. Chenonceau currant. - Beautiful, loosely packed clusters. Glory currant from Sablons. - Fertile de Palluau - Fertile d'Angers, etc. White-fruited currants. - White currant from Holland. - Quite large fruit of very good quality, firm, transparent flesh. Transparent currant. - Very long clusters with large berries. Boulogne currant. - Imperial cherry. - Versaillaise. - Whites grapes - Verrières blanches, etc. GROSEILLER A FRUIT NOIR, CASSIS Ribes nigrum L. Ribes olidum Mœnch. ORIGIN. - The origin of the word blackcurrant is not well known. It is thought to derive from the highly esteemed liqueurs once made in Cassis, Provence, exclusively from the fruit of this plant. According to De Candolle, "blackcurrants exist in a spontaneous state in northern Europe, from Scotland and Lapland to northern France and Italy, Bosnia, Armenia, all Siberia and the region of the Amur River and the western Himalayas, often naturalizing, for example, in central France." CULTIVATION. - The blackcurrant differs from the redcurrant only in its slightly larger size, its lighter green leaves and the scent of its wood. The fruit, which is a beautiful jet-black color, must be picked as soon as it ripens, otherwise it will fall off. Cultivation is the same as for the redcurrant. As blackcurrants regrow less than redcurrants, you should not cut off too much old wood, which would reduce production. Same pruning. THORNY CURRANT. MACKEREL CURRANT Ribes Uva crispa L. Ribes spinosum Lamk. ORIGIN. - De Candolle says: "This currant grows spontaneously throughout temperate Europe, from southern Sweden to the mountainous parts of central Spain, Italy and Greece. It is also found in northern Africa, in the Aures mountains. Mr. Ball has found a fairly distinct variety in the Atlas mountains of Morocco, it exists in the Caucasus, and in more or less different forms, in the western Himalayas". This shrub grows in arid, stony areas. CULTIVATION. - The thorny currant is propagated from seed, marcottes, cuttings taken from two-year-old wood and rooted splinters. Cuttings take root with greater difficulty than other varieties. Because of its numerous, tight thorns, it is very suitable for forming impenetrable hedges, and is so hardy that it can be planted in all exposures and soils, VARIETY. - There are several varieties. Some come from England or America, where they are highly prized; green, red, purple or yellow fruit. Among the best are: Britannica, downy yellow. - Justicia, fluffy white. - Creeping Jane, smooth yellow - Favorite rouge, smooth red. - Whinhams Industry, large, dark red-brown fruit. - Washington, spiky pink. - Peace-Maher, smooth red - Roaring Lion, smooth red. - White-Smith, smooth green. French varieties include Ovoid green, transparent spiky, dull red, yellow, etc. SEEDS. - To harvest seeds, we select the most beautiful fruits and spread them out on planks. When they are fully ripe, they are crushed in water; the seeds sink to the bottom, are collected and dried in the shade; they keep well for a year. For best success when sowing in spring, they should be stratified in sand immediately after harvesting. DISEASES, PESTS. - The currant is sometimes affected by a disease that rapidly destroys it. This is a species of white fungus that develops on the roots and causes them to rot. It is frequently found in soils that are too damp. It has been suggested that diseased plants be watered with a very clear milk of lime. Small green caterpillars that devour leaves are also sometimes found, depending on the year, especially on red currants. We have seen young plantations completely stripped of leaves. The remedy is very difficult: you have to look for the eggs, which are laid in the shape of rings on two- or three-year-old wood, and destroy them. USES. - The currant is widely used. The fruit is eaten, and jellies are made from it. The fruit is used to make soothing and refreshing drinks, syrup and a pleasant wine that is drunk in the North, and even brandy. In medicine, redcurrants are refreshing and astringent, and stimulate the appetite. The acidity of redcurrants, more intense in the red varieties, results from the abundance of malic and citric acids. The currant is a medicated food that nourishes and refreshes. The leaves are used to dye yellow, the branches to dye black. The gooseberry breed is so named because the fruit is used green in a sauce for mackerel. In France, the fruits are used for little other than eating them raw, but in England they are used to spice up certain sauces before they are ripe. They can be eaten on their own, or as an accessory to meats. They are also used to make delicious cakes. When ripe, they are used to make a highly prized wine. Blackcurrants produce an excellent tonic liqueur, good for the stomach. It also makes a very good ratafia. As well as the seeds, unscrupulous confectioners use the wood and leaves to give perfume to a syrup they call blackcurrant. The fruit juice is dyed blue by alkali, purple by tin salt. A lacquer is obtained. The leaves give a yellow dye used to color brandy. COMMON BEANS Phaseolus vulgaris. L. Leguminosae, Papilionaceae. ORIGIN. - An annual plant of uncertain origin. It is said to have originated in South America, and to have been imported in the 14th century; it has not been found growing spontaneously anywhere, which proves that it has been cultivated for a very long time. The bean has several synonyms: Faverolle, faviole, fayau, faséole, fayon, fève à visage, fève de mer, mongette, banette, etc. OUTDOOR CULTIVATION. - In general, beans thrive in all sufficiently fertilized soils; however, they do better in light, moist soils. The first beans are sown in the open ground in the first half of April, and successively until June. In the south of France, you can start sowing in March. Early varieties are preferred. There are several ways to arrange bean sowing, with each region following its own traditions. In the Charentes, Dordogne, Vienne, Vendée and Deux-Sèvres regions, for example, beans are combed with a forked or flat hoe, as is done for garlic. This is an excellent method. In the South-West and part of the Midi, it is sown in beds, and here's how it's done for dwarf breeds. Once the soil has been well prepared and smoked, use the narrow end of the hoe to make small gullies Om, 10 and 50 centimetres deep. Sow in clumps at 25 or 30 centimetres along the line, or grain by grain at 8 centimetres. Cover lightly by about 3 or 4 centimetres, depending on the size of the seed. If the soil is too compact, cover with fine earth or compost. Rowing varieties destined to succeed dwarf breeds are laid out in rows of three rays at 40 centimetres. A fourth line is traced with the tracer to plant romaine, which is removed before it harms the beans. These are divided by a 50-centimetre path, which, once the lettuces have been removed, gives a 90-centimetre passage between each bed. This distance is necessary, firstly to form a bead of earth on each side, to retain water for watering and for the worker to pass through with his watering cans. You can also sow in clumps, using a hoe or a thin planter to make holes 40 centimetres apart in a chessboard pattern, in which you place five or six beans and cover. As beans in general, and row beans in particular, need a lot of air to grow well and produce a lot, this last method is the best and can be adopted everywhere. In some regions, notably the Charente, market gardeners have laid out their rowing bean beds in five rays at 30 centimeters. This distance is much too close, because once the beans are a little high, the middle three rows receive very little air and light, and consequently produce little. We've made this observation to a few growers, who have continued as before anyway - it's a routine that's hard to change. Whatever the sowing method used and whatever the variety, when the beans have reached a height of a dozen centimetres, they are hoeed, and then repeated at intervals of a few days. Chestnut, oak, plane and hazelnut are suitable for this purpose; in the Landes and part of the south-west, where there are many pine woods, they are used almost exclusively. A lath is placed on each bean, or tuft, and the appearance of these branches, generally very straight, produces a pretty eye-catching effect. The two rows around the edges are planted with the tallest branches slightly inclined towards each other, while the shorter middle branches are planted vertically. When the bush beans have reached a certain level of development, they are ridged; this operation is necessary in summer to maintain constant humidity and prevent the roots from being affected by heat, which would be detrimental to the plants. During hot spells, if the soil in which you have sown is dry, you must not spare watering, which must be very copious, and we even recommend irrigation if possible, as production will depend on watering if the soil is not naturally damp; without water, plants dry out, flower badly and often die before they have produced a quarter of their yield. Sometimes, in strong soils, as soon as the beans are sown, a heavy rainstorm settles and hardens the soil surface. In this case, the seeds would not stand up well if left as they are, so this crust needs to be broken by light hoeing. EARLY CROPS. - Beans in their green state are generally preferred by consumers, and when they can be harvested early, they represent a good source of income. Early beans can be grown in a variety of ways, using thermosiphon-heated tarpaulins, hot-layer frames or cloches. Thermosiphon cultivation is quick and successful. Sowing can start after December 15, and harvesting can begin in the second half of February. Thermosiphon will always give the best results, as the heat is even and the beans develop very well and produce a lot, but its use becomes expensive. As growing beans in hot frames is more difficult, and is the most widely used method, we will describe it below: In the second half of January, prepare a good layer 60 centimetres thick. Load it with 15 centimeters of good potting soil mixed with equal parts of vegetable garden soil. After this, the beans are sown in small clumps of three together, 35 or 40 centimetres apart. Four rows are planted per 1.33 m. After sowing, cover and pack well, water very lightly, and put up the panels. You can also sow one by one at 15 centimetres along the row. Under the influence of the background heat, the beans are quick to sprout; from this point onwards, you need to watch out for mildew, which is the main enemy of this crop; you need to go over it often to remove the affected parts, and give it air as much as the temperature allows, and gradually. Cover with straw mats during the night, surround the trunks with good stoves and reshape them often, adding manure each time. When the beans have reached a height of 25 centimetres, they are tilted towards the top of the trunk, secured with small wooden hooks. The ends of the stalks soon rise after forming a circuit, but the lower part remains flat. Despite this operation, which is only intended to prevent the beans from touching the glass, the trunks can be raised if necessary. When the flowers start to appear, give them plenty of air and frequent watering. Instead of sowing beans in place on the bed, as we have just indicated, some growers transplant them, i.e. they sow on a very small area, and when the plants have grown their first leaves, they transplant them on a bed prepared in advance. This method gives just as good results as sowing in place. So you can choose either system. The first beans are usually harvested six weeks after sowing. Cultivation under cloches starts in the second half of March, and is carried out in much the same way. Once the bed has been prepared, it is edged, the soil is put in, the cloches are placed in a chessboard pattern, and three or four beans are sown under each one, or transplanted, which is preferable. As soon as the crop has recovered, air is added. Keep the bells above them for as long as possible, raising them on three stakes or flowerpots, and remove them when all danger has passed. When it's cold, especially at night, cover with bedding or straw mats. The varieties that lend themselves best to these various growing systems are: 1° Belgian dwarf black bean; 2° White and yellow flageolet bean; 3° Early Dutch dwarf bean; 4° White bean with waffle leaves; 5° Russian Emperor bean; 6° Early Lyon glory bean; 7° Dwarf flageolet bean, the triumph of the châs- sis; an excellent novelty for this crop. SEEDS. - To harvest bean seeds, keep a piece of planking without picking them, and leave them to ripen on the ground. They are then picked and laid out in the shade in a well-ventilated, rodent-proof place. They are husked and put into small bags until the time comes to sow them. For varieties with oars, we recommend preventing them from climbing by pinching them. This will produce a very good crop of beans. Although beans retain their germinative powers for a long time, three years and sometimes more depending on the variety, it is preferable to choose seed from the previous year if you want to be successful. Red Soissons bean. - Harlequin bean, etc. MALaDlEs, NUISIBLE ANIMALS. - Sometimes, especially in silica-poor soils, beans are affected by chlorosis, turn yellow and stop growing; the best remedy is to water them from time to time with liquid manure in which a little iron sulfate has been dissolved, about 100 grams per 10 liters of water, and water at the foot as much as possible. Sometimes, as a result of sudden changes in temperature and too much prolonged humidity, the beans are affected by rot and rust or uredo; the pods are strewn with circular red spots which perforate the parchment and reach the beans, which are completely deteriorated. There's nothing to be done about it; fortunately, these diseases are fairly rare, and only show up in autumn, when the bean harvest is well advanced. White grubs, slugs, snails and crickets, when they swarm in a field, cause real damage to young bean seedlings. So we have to wage a fierce war against them. To protect the crop, surround the plant with powdered quicklime, soot and unleached ash, mixed in equal parts. USES. - Beans are widely used in the diet: along with potatoes, they are the staple of the poor man's diet. On the rich man's table, they are almost always served with a variety of meats, mainly mutton; everyone knows the ancient and traditional gigot aux flageolets, the delight of Parisians, or to put it better, of the majority of French people. Beans are popular simply because they're cheap. The ancients knew beans; Galen says that the Romans began their meals with beans preserved in vinegar and garum, to whet their appetites. Green beans are eaten half-formed. In Auvergne and Poitou, and even in Limousin, it is widely cultivated to be eaten dry, and in these regions is the main food of the peasants. It is very nourishing, but should not be eaten by weak stomachs or those with poor digestion. The pods can be pickled in vinegar like gherkins. Green beans can be preserved in several ways. Usually, they are blanched by throwing them into boiling, slightly salted water and leaving them for a few minutes after removing them from the heat; they are then drained, dried on cloths and placed several times in an oven, like prunes, on racks. Before using them, soak them first thing in the morning in lukewarm water. Alternatively, to preserve the beans, pour boiling water over them and leave them in for a few minutes, then cook them in boiling salted water, so that they are a little crunchy, drain them, sauté them in butter with parsley, and bottle them with their sauce when they are cold; in the bottles, they are given a five-minute boil. Flageolet beans can be preserved in the same way, but need to boil for three hours. They make excellent flour for fattening domestic animals. Burnt stalks yield a lot of potash. SPANISH BEANS Phaseolus multiflorus Wild. Phaseolus coccineus I.. ORIGIN. - South American plant. Perennial in its native country, but annual in our cultivations, called Haricot bouquet, Haricot à fleurs, etc. CULTIVATION. - Runner beans are excessively vigorous and hardy plants, at least in the Midi; they become more delicate in the North, due to the lack of heat. The varied colors of their flowers make them highly appreciated as ornamental plants. Runner beans are grown in the same way as Phascolus vulgaris, but as they are very vigorous, they need to be rowed with high branches. VARIETIES. - Runner bean. - Large, vigorous, productive variety; broad, short pods, light wine-red beans with black spots, red flowers. White runner bean. - Very vigorous variety, the best to grow for eating, reaching 3 meters or more in height; white flowers in numerous clusters; broad, flattened pods; very large, thick, white beans. Bicolor runner bean. - A vigorous variety, highly original for the oddity of its flowers, which are half red and half white; highly esteemed in the North, where it is grown as an ornamental. Black runner bean. - Very vigorous, fairly hardy; short, broad pods; completely black bean. Hybrid runner bean. - Very vigorous variety; flowers with the same variegation as the bi-colored bean; broad pods; grayish-yellow grain, spotted with brown. DISEASES, PESTS. - This breed is much hardier than Phaseolus vulgaris, resists disease and is rarely attacked by insects: slugs and snails will devour a few young plants. USES. - The runner bean is a very popular plant in the South of France. It can be eaten as a half-formed bean or green, when it is edible; but when dry, it is not as good as an ordinary bean, is not very floury and has an unpleasant pungent taste. In southern Spain and part of Portugal, this breed is grown to the exclusion of all others. The liter weighs between 730 and 735 grams, and 100 grams contain around 75 beans. LIMA BEANS, Phaseolus lunatus L. Phaseolus inamœnus L. Phaseolus lunatus macrocarpus Bentham. ORIGIN. - Perennial in its native country, annual in our cultivations. This breed is mainly cultivated by E. BERGER, Vegetables. 17 in the Argentine Republic, the United States, the West Indies, Havana, almost all the French colonies in America and throughout Africa. CULTIVATION. - Cultivation of this breed is much the same as that of the above-mentioned varieties, with the difference that in the North and in Paris the pods almost never, or rarely, all reach maturity. In cold years with rainy autumns, they don't ripen at all. Throughout the Midi, and particularly in Provence, it does fairly well; it's only from Toulouse onwards that we can begin to grow it successfully. The Lima bean should be sown early, towards the end of April or first fortnight of May, at the best possible exposure. In temperate climates, we recommend sowing in pots, under a frame, and then planting in the open ground, when the soil is sufficiently warm. It requires the same cultivation care as row varieties. As it grows very tall, it should be given sufficiently high stalks. VARIETIES. - Lima bean. - Very late; broad, short pods, joined by several together; white, yellowish, flattened bean, very elongated and broad, a little kidney-shaped. 90 seeds weigh 100 grams. American growers' catalogs mention several other sub-varieties of this bean, with green, yellow and white seeds. Marbled Cape bean. - Very characteristic variety; dark red beans around the umbilicus and at one end; the rest are white with red dots. Each bean weighs 1 gram. Stéva bean. - A variety of Phaseolus lunatus, known in Havana as the fève plate or fève créole, this bean is highly prized by rich and poor alike. Very early: the first pods ripen quite well in the northern climate and in Paris. In the Midi, it is a perfect success. Yellowish-white grain, similar to Lima, but smaller. COWPEA BEAN. DOLIQUE MONGETTE. Dolichos unguiculatus L. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to South America, highly esteemed in tropical countries, and in Europe. in warm countries such as Portugal, Spain, Italy, etc., and in France in the southern zone. CULTURE. - This breed, which grows to no more than 60 centimeters in height, does quite well in Paris and the North, provided it is given very good exposure. Cultivation is the same as for dwarf common beans; straight or curved pods, almost cylindrical, 20 to 25 centimeters long; dirty white grain, with a small black spot around the umbilicus. Grain merchants in Italy and Portugal mention several varieties, differing from this one only in grain size. USES. - This breed is eaten green and half-formed; dry, the quality is far inferior to the others. Dolichos sesquipedalis L. ORIGIN. - Annual. From South America. Highly valued in Buenos-Ayres, Montevideo, Chile. CULTIVATION. - In some parts of Provence, this species is widely cultivated and does very well. We have grown it in Bordeaux and have been very satisfied. Cultivation is the same as for Soissons string beans. As this plant is very heat-loving, it needs a good, sunny exposure and lots of watering in summer. This is the only way to ensure success. VARIETIES. - Cuban cowpea bean. - An extremely curious variety of the asparagus cowpea bean, introduced from Havana some ten years ago: it is far superior to the previous variety. We have grown it for a very long time and have always been satisfied with the results it has given us. This variety is vigorous and relatively hardy: it grows very tall, up to 3.50 m, or more, depending on the soil. Very numerous pods, grouped in threes and fours, 50 to 60 centimetres long. SEEDS. - Pale, wine-yellow color, white umbilicus encircled by a small black line; small seeds. 100 grams contain 630 seeds. USES. - The pods are eaten green, very tender and tasty, especially when they are about half as long. Dried, the asparagus bean has no flavor and is rather bad than good. DOLIC BEAN LABLAB Lablab vulgaris Savi. Dolichos Lablab L. ORIGIN. - This species is widely cultivated in India and tropical Africa, says De Candolle. Cultivation is at least three thousand years old: "The lablab is unquestionably spontaneous in India, and even, it is said, in Java. It has become naturalized in the Seychelles Islands as a result of cultivation. The authors' indications do not allow us to say that it is spontaneous in Africa. CULTURE. - The lablab bean is grown very little in France, a lot in Egypt, and a little in Spain and Italy. This breed is very vigorous: it can reach 4 to 5 meters in height; very productive in hot countries. Same cultivation as paddle varieties. Should be considered more as an ornamental plant than as a vegetable variety. Not very productive in the North. The seeds, three or four per pod, are white or black, as there are two varieties, one with white flowers, the other with purple. USES. - In America, the lablab is cultivated for its grain, which is eaten like soissons in France. HVSOPE Hyssopus officinalis L. Hyssopus decussatus Persoon. Hyssopus myrtifolius Desf. Hyssopus ruber Mich. Labiatae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, evergreen plant, or rather sub-shrub, native to Spain, according to some authors, and Italy, according to others. GROWING. - Hyssop is easy to grow. It is usually planted in borders and pruned, like thyme, twice a year, in March and September. It is propagated by sowing seeds in rows in spring. Cover sparingly, as the seed is very fine. When the young plants have reached 7 or 8 centimetres in height, they are transplanted to the edges of driveways. Weed and hoe frequently to rid them of weeds. It can also be propagated with rooted cuttings and cold cuttings. It thrives best in strong, chalky soils, and is hardy enough to withstand the ordinary winters of northern France. Plantations should be renewed at least every four years. SEEDS. - In spring, we keep a few plants, which we don't prune as they rise, and in July, when the ears are ripe and before they are completely dry, we pick them with care, as the seed detaches very easily. They are spread out on a cloth and exposed to the sun for one or two days. The seeds are collected and winnowed. They keep well for three years. According to Vilmorin, 1 gram contains 850 seeds. DISEASES, PESTS. - In very rich, heavily fertilized soils, every year many plants succumb to the attacks of powdery mildew, which attacks the roots and especially the crown. Affected plants first wilt, then turn yellow. We know of no effective remedy to destroy this parasite. As soon as it is noticed, the plants must be pulled up without mercy and thrown away or burned. The site should be spaded as closely as possible to other plants, in order to destroy all the germs of the disease. As for insects, they are not very fond of this plant, and hardly touch it, probably because of the strongly aromatic smell of all its parts. Sometimes, however, flea beetles pierce the leaves, but we have never observed any significant damage. USES. - Hyssop is used in cooking to enhance the flavor of dishes, especially sauces. It is frequently used, especially in northern France, with thyme in green pea stews. The buds of the young, still-tender branches are used in particular. It is also used in Russian salads. The Jews used hyssop to make purification brushes. CHINESE IGNAMUS Dioscorea Batatas Decaisne. Dioscorea Japonica Hort. 295 ORIGIN. - The Chinese call it Sain-in. According to Dr. Bretschneider, this plant is indigenous to China, as it is mentioned in the oldest work on medical matters, that of Emperor Schen nung. According to M. de Vilmorin, the yam was introduced to France in 1848 by M. de Montigny, French consul in Shanghai. There are several races of yam, all cultivated and highly esteemed in warm countries, particularly India, Japan and China. CULTIVATION. - Chinese yam is not widely cultivated in France, at least not as a standard crop, due to the great difficulty involved in digging up the tubers, which are usually found between 60 cm and 1 m deep. This plant can be propagated from bulblets born in the leaf axils, but this method does not give as good results as propagation from medium-sized tubers about the size of an index finger, which are planted whole. These tubers can be put into vegetation as early as March, in 10-centimeter pots placed on beds. In May, the tubers are removed from the pot and planted in the ground; this will produce earlier and more abundant crops. Otherwise, the best time to plant tubers is the first half of May in temperate regions, and April in the south. The soil in which you intend to plant must be well-developed and of good natural composition: you can't expect to get good results from poor-quality soil. Water, too, must not be lacking, and if possible, irrigation should not be neglected, as the plant stops growing when it lacks moisture; however, this moisture must not be stagnant. Once the soil has been prepared, plant with a chalk line or draw lines spaced Om, 40 apart. Plant at 25 centimetres on the line, tighter if you want smaller tubers. Twenty days after planting, we ream them up with branches at least 1 metre long, which is enough for the stems to wrap around them, and at the same time, hoeing is much easier than if the plants were lying on the ground. During the summer, the plants are hoeed several times, and then pulled out in November. The tubers, which are sometimes very long and large, weighing up to 1 kilogram, keep very well, much like potatoes. VARIETIES. - There are several varieties of yam, all imported from Japan or China. SEEDS. - In our regions, the bulblets that sprout from the leaf axils take the place of seeds. They are harvested in autumn, before the plant is grubbed up. DISEASES, PESTS. - The yam's natural enemies are snails and slugs, which devour the young stalks as they begin to grow. Cutworms can also eat tubers. The plant sometimes turns yellow towards midsummer, but this is due to excessive humidity or the soil's lack of siliceous matter. USES. - The rhizomes are eaten like potatoes; the flesh is white, light, floury and very pleasant to the taste. CULTIVATED LETTUCE Lactuca sativa L. Composites. ORIGIN. - An annual plant of ancient origin. It is said that the first seeds were sent from Rome to France in 1540 by Rabelais, who sent them to Cardinal d'Estrée. The typical species grows in southern Europe, northern Africa and western Asia. The Lactuca genus has been divided by botanists into two distinct races: head lettuces and romaine lettuces; the habit of each of these genera is so different that it is easy to recognize them. HATÉE AND EARLY LETTUCES. - The cultivation of early lettuces is extremely easy: all you need is the right tools, and sufficient equipment consisting of bells and panels. Twenty years ago, Parisian market gardeners used to grow this type of lettuce extensively, and found it a profitable winter crop. Today, early lettuce is still grown, but less so than in the past. The capital's markets are invaded by fresh produce from the Midi, Spain, Algeria, etc., which contributes to a decline in all similar products. What's more, for some years now, lettuces grown under glass have been subject to a terrible disease, Perono- spora gangliformis. The first early lettuces are sown towards the end of September and October, under frames or cloches on the ground, with good exposure. However, if sowing without panels, make sure the seedlings are as close as possible 17. to the glass. If sowing under cloches, first prepare the soil with a spade, then spread a small layer of potting soil over the surface. The cloches are placed before sowing, to fix the exact location, then the seed is sown, treaded and covered with potting soil; under a frame, the same procedure is followed, with watering. Don't let the air in; once the plant has risen, if it's too thick, thin it out a little, and if it's sunburned, shade it. When the plant is strong enough to be transplanted permanently, prepare an ados, place three or four rows of bells in a chessboard pattern, lift the plant as much as possible with the root ball, and plant five to eight lettuces under each one, depending on the species, water and press lightly on the top of each bell to fix them firmly to the ground, so that no air penetrates. Shade or smear the inside with loosened soil. Panel planting is preferable, as there is no wasted ground; generally, six or seven rows are planted in 1.33 panels.
