When the sun's rays are hot, if the glass is not smeared, it must be shaded; similarly, when frosts occur, it must be covered with straw mats. If the weather is too cold, the cloches should be lined with bedding or dry manure, with the straw mats on top. In this case, it's preferable to use cone-shaped straw mats, which fit over the bell, thus saving a lot of labor. The plantations are visited from time to time, dead or rotten leaves removed, weeded and lightly watered if necessary. About a month or forty-five days after sowing, the lettuces should be ready to harvest. Sowing should be staggered from fortnight to fortnight until February, so as not to run out. From November onwards, sow on warm beds. The cultivation methods described above apply to the north of France, as well as to temperate regions. In the south of France, a good sheltered exposure is sufficient in winter. All the varieties known as spring lettuces lend themselves very well to early cultivation. These are the only varieties that can give good results. CULTIVATION IN THE OPEN GROUND. - Head lettuces generally thrive in all soils that are sufficiently fertilized, and preferably in those that have been under cultivation for a long time. Lettuces should be planted in recently tilled soil: they are planted in rows of four at 25 centimetres apart, 20 or 25 centimetres apart on the row, sometimes more, depending on the species. Smaller varieties, such as gotte or crepe lettuces, can be planted closer together. Each plant is watered with the spout as soon as it is transplanted. A few weeks later, when the lettuces have started to grow, we give them a first hoeing, which we repeat a few days later. The surface of the soil must be constantly loose to encourage growth. VARIETIES. - Gardeners divide lettuces into four groups: 1° Spring lettuces; 2° Summer and autumn lettuces; 3° Winter lettuces; 4° Cutting lettuces. 1° Spring lettuces. - These varieties are all small or medium-sized. They are sown from February to the end of April. The first sowings should be made on warm beds, or in a well-exposed bed, or at least sheltered by windbreakers. In northern France, the first plantings in March are made on costières or ados. But in parts of the West, South-West and South of France, this precaution is not necessary. Laitue gotte (white seed). - A very old species, small in size and well suited to forced cultivation. Laitue gotte (black seed). - Little different from the previous one, very early. Slow-growing gotte lettuce. - Excellent variety, almost all apple.

Pancake lettuce (with white and black seeds). - Two small varieties, very good for forced cultivation. Tennisball lettuce (black seed). - A very old variety in cultivation, a little bulkier than crepe, apples well. Red-edged or red cord lettuce. - Excellent variety, larger than gotte, very good and highly regarded on the markets. Tom-Pouce lettuce. - A new, highly recommendable species, small in size and good for forced cultivation. 2° Summer and autumn lettuces. - There are a great many lettuces in this class, and some are very large. They are sown in nurseries from March to July. Transplant when the plant is strong enough, and water copiously during hot spells. A good mulch is necessary in dry soils. Blonde summer lettuce or non pareille lettuce, royal lettuce. - This variety is universally known. It is grown almost everywhere; medium size; round, tight head; white seed. Laitue blonde de Versailles. - A very fine variety with a large, elongated, firm head; white seed. Berlin blond lettuce. - Black-seeded variety, fairly tight head, tender and tasty. Chavigné blond lettuce. - Quite large head, very full and tight, forming quickly, very slow to set, highly recommended. Lettuce Bossin. - Large, not very tight, very wide, up to 40 cm in diameter. The midrib, very accentuated at the bottom, makes it depreciate, very tender, black seed. Maltese lettuce. - Quite large, not very tight, very tender, resists heat well; white seed; suitable for the Midi. Brown Batavia lettuce. - Tall, elongated head, not very tight, tender, rises quickly, very good for hot climates. Naples cabbage lettuce. - Very large variety, like apple, which is tight and firm; keeps well; white seed. Batavia blond lettuce. - Tender, loose-headed variety; white seed. Big brown lazy lettuce. - Excellent variety, known as meterelle lettuce in the Southwest; very hardy; black seed. Big lazy blond lettuce. - A very good summer variety, good head and tight; white seed. Lettuce merveille des quatre saisons or red Besson lettuce. - Very good hardy variety, can be sown in any season; black seed. Palatine or red lettuce. - Very good variety for summer, good head, black seed. Other varieties include Simpson's early lettuce. - Small, white seed. Red Dutch lettuce. - Black seed. Blood lettuce, improved. - White seed. Greasy green lettuce. - Small, peeling well, black seed. Lorthois or Trocadero lettuce. - Small, white seed. Big Normandy lettuce. - Medium, yellow-seeded. New or recent varieties include: Giant blond lettuce. - Very fine variety, apple FIG. 35 - Giant blond lettuce. large, firm, hardy, good heat resistance, white seed (fig. 35). Large, stubborn brown lettuce. - Firm and tight. Ohio lettuce. - Firm head, good size, very good for summer, white seed. Triumph lettuce. - Rounded head, very large, very hardy, difficult to set seed. 3° Winter lettuces. - These varieties are very hardy, and survive our ordinary winters quite well with some shelter. They are sown from August to October. Young plants, when strong enough, should be transplanted to a good exposure, to speed up production. The first seedlings produce lettuces that are good for harvesting before or during the winter, but the last plantings in October, or the first two weeks of November, overwinter, and vegetation starts up again in February. They bolt towards the end of April and are followed by spring seedlings. Passion or babiana lettuce. - Excellent variety, good head, medium size, very hardy, successful only at this time of year, white seed. Red winter lettuce. - Very fine variety, hardy, good head, good size, very good for the South-West, white seed. Brown winter lettuce. - Medium-sized variety, small, tight head, very hardy; white seed. Large blond winter lettuce. - Very fine variety, hardy and of good quality; white seed. New varieties include Trémont winter lettuce. - Excellent variety, hardy, fairly strong head, tight; white seed. White Passion lettuce. - Hardy and of good quality; black seed. 4° Cutting lettuces. - This breed includes varieties that do not head, that are sown very thickly, in all seasons on beds, on ados or in the open ground; in general, they are always mixed with other crops. Some of these varieties form a small apple, but not very tightly packed. Among the best are Beauregard leaf lettuce. - A very fine, tender variety, much appreciated in the markets. American leaf lettuce. - Leaves tinted bronze-red, very tender. California leaf lettuce. - Very vigorous variety, high yielding, very tender and tasty. Ordinary cutting lettuce - Blonde cutting lettuce. Generally speaking, all varieties of head lettuce can be grown as cutting lettuces; they just need to be picked young, although certain breeds are always hardy. In Bordeaux, these are called baby lettuces. SEEDS. - Seeds are easy to harvest. Spring and summer lettuces bolt the first year, only winter lettuces bolt the following year. Varieties sown after June do not ripen their seeds in the North. When the height of the plants is sufficiently high, and the seeds begin to form, you need to keep an eye on the birds, which are very fond of them. They keep well for four years. DISEASES, PESTS - Early lettuces, grown under cloches or panels where they need as little air as possible to succeed, have for some years been invaded by a cryptogam that rapidly destroys them, the Peronospora gangliformis, called miller by Parisian market gardeners. This parasite develops with extraordinary rapidity under the influence of concentrated humid heat, and generally begins with small, barely visible yellowish or white spots on the underside of the leaves, usually near the veins, especially the midrib. When the spots come together, a multitude of small, vertical, mold-like fibers become visible, each fiber or arbuscule surmounted by a reproductive sporule. After a few hours of rejoining, the oldest affected part becomes mortified and decomposes, leaving the leaf as a rotten mass. On the advice of Mr. Millardet, Professor of Botany at the Bordeaux Faculty of Science, we have carried out several trials to curb this disease. Bordeaux mixture, as used today for vines, would give good results, but the toxic principles of copper are an obstacle to its use; however, by washing the lettuces in clear water with a little sulfuric acid, there is nothing to fear. A very simple and above all harmless treatment, which has given us the best results, is as follows. Dissolve one kilogram of white plaster in 10 liters of water, add a little well-sieved soot, mix everything together, and use it to drench lettuces affected by the disease; ensure that the liquid is distributed over the leaves in the form of rain, a syringe for drenching greenhouses works well; to remove the plaster adhering to the leaves, drench them with fresh water a few hours before cutting them, and then wash them with plenty of water. Nicotine, reduced to a tenth and mixed with fat lime, has not given satisfactory results. Brown lettuces seem to be more resistant to this disease than blondes. This disease seems to develop especially on plants grown on beds. Let's hope it doesn't become more hardy, and that our outdoor plantings don't suffer from it! Slugs and snails are very fond of young lettuce plants, as are white grubs and cutworms, which cut the roots. Destroy them by any means necessary. USES. - Lettuce is an excellent vegetable, much appreciated on all tables. It is used either cooked in fat or raw in salads. ROMAINE LETTUCE CULTIVATION. - Romaine lettuces, which have a different shape to ordinary lettuces, can be grown in the same way. VARIETIES. - They are classified into winter, spring and summer varieties. 1° Winter romaine lettuces. - Sow not too thickly, in August-September, in a well-smoked bed. When strong enough, they are planted in good, recently ploughed, smoked soil and watered immediately. The last plantings should be done in the first fortnight of November, no later. Green winter romaine lettuce. - Very old variety, good head, not very sensitive to cold, black seed. Winter green Romaine. - Fairly hardy variety; medium-sized head, fairly tight, black seed. Winter red Romaine. - Very hardy, high, fairly large, tight head, very slow to bolt, black seed. 2° Spring romaine lettuces. - Balloon romaine lettuce. - Very bulky variety, strong, tight, white head, black seed. Blonde romaine lettuce. - A very old variety, grown just about everywhere, hardy, adaptable to all temperate climates, as long as it has plenty of water in summer. Early, it can be grown under frames or cloches, white seed. Lettuce verte maraîchère. - A very old variety, excellent, not very tight and white, hardy, white seed. Romaine grise maraichère. - Highly esteemed by growers around Paris for early vegetables, medium size, apples well. Slow-growing blond lettuce. - A new, little-known variety, it bolts very late, with black seeds. Trianon early blond romaine lettuce. - New variety, large, very full head, white seed. Early flat romaine lettuce. - New, very good for cultivation under cloches, white seed. Potato lettuce. - Very small, black seed. 3° Summer romaine lettuces. - These varieties are more resistant to summer heat, but can be sown in spring, just as spring varieties can be sown at this time. They can be sown from the second half of May to July. Among the best varieties are Alphange romaine lettuce. - Black and white seeds, two varieties with little distinction. English brown romaine lettuce. - White and black seeds. Perfected variegated or blood lettuce. - Flagellated with reddish spots; very tender. SEEDS. - Seeds are harvested in the same way as head lettuces. USES. - Same uses. LAVENDER Lavandula Spica D. C. Labiatae. Indigenous, perennial, this plant reproduces by separating clumps and taking cuttings in autumn and spring; can be propagated by sowing. Used as a condiment in sauces. LENTILLE Ervum Lens L. Lens esculenta Mœnch. Leguminosae. ORIGIN. - Native annual. COOKING. - The lentil (fig. 36) is a plant of great FIO. 86. - Lentil. cultivation, it is rarely grown in gardens. It prefers a soft, light soil, which is where it produces the most and at the same time where the grains are the hottest. This legume is very easy to grow. It is sown in March, in rows or bunches. Weed when necessary. It requires no further care until harvesting, which takes place towards the end of August, beginning of September. SEEDS. - To preserve lentils well for sowing, it's best to leave them in their pods until it's time to sow them. They keep well for four years. VARIETIES. - Lentille large blonde or lentille de Lorraine. - Green Puy lentil. - March lentil. - Winter lentil. Common lentil, a variety grown extensively in Eastern Europe and Germany. lentils, pests. - Lentils are sometimes affected by bruchids, a small beetle whose larvae develop in the grain, partially gnawing it, and then dying. Lentils, ravaged by this insect, are unpalatable and harmful to health. Many remedies have been tried to prevent the bruchid from depositing its eggs, but none has worked. As a result, lentil cultivation has been abandoned in many parts of northern France, where the insect is rampant. USES. - Same uses as beans. Valerianella olitoria Mœnch. Valeriana Locusta L. Valerianae. ORIGIN. - Indigenous annual herbaceous plant, known locally as Boursette, Blanquette, Clairette, Poule grasse, Barbe de chanoine, Coquille, Pommette, Laitue de brebis, Orillette, etc. CULTIVATION. - Lamb's lettuce is not very difficult to grow in any type of soil. However, it seems to do best in light, well-smoked soils. They are sown from August to October, broadcast, in beds, alone or among other crops such as turnips or radishes. Sow fairly thickly, covering sparingly; the most important thing is to tread the soil sufficiently after sowing. Other cultivation care consists of weeding, in order to remove the weeds that invade. FIG. 37. - Round-leaf lamb's lettuce. VAbieTes. - Round-leaf lamb's lettuce (fig. 37). - A very good, hardy, tender variety, highly esteemed on the markets, especially in Paris, where market gardeners grow it almost exclusively. Mâche verte d'Etampes. - Very good variety, forming a rosette of tight leaves, tender, withstands cold well. Mâche verte à cœur plein. - Tight, firm rosette, short, intense green leaves, very good variety, less hardy than the preceding ones. Large-seeded Dutch lamb's lettuce. - Hardy, productive variety, comes very large and very good for northern countries. Italian or Regency Lamb's lettuce (Valerianella eriocarpa Desv.). - Distinct from common lamb's lettuce in that it is blonder in all its parts, and in the length of its foliage. Rosette, fairly full, tender and tasty, it is especially suited to the Midi. Italian lamb's lettuce. - A variety derived from the previous one, with broader, rosette-shaped leaves, very strong and full. Mâche de Lyon. - A good new variety, highly regarded at Lyon markets, with broad, spoon-shaped leaves. Mâche à feuilles panachées. - Variety with variegated or marbled yellow or white leaves; not very productive, it seems destined to disappear from cultivation. SEEDS. - Seeds are harvested from seedlings sown in October. They generally ripen in June. As soon as they are ripe, the seeds detach easily; it is therefore necessary to pick the uprights before they are fully ripe, and lay them out on cloths in the shade, where they will complete their drying, then simply beat them with a stick to detach the seeds. Some seeds always fall to the ground. In this case, once the seed carriers have been removed, we recommend sweeping the surface of the soil, removing the largest clumps of earth and immersing the whole lot in a tub of water. The seeds float to the surface, where they are collected and dried in the shade. They keep well for four years. For seedlings, it's best to choose seeds that are two or three years old; they germinate better and faster than those from the previous year. DISEASES, PESTS. - In naturally damp winter soils, lamb's lettuce sometimes suffers from uredo or rust; excess humidity can also cause yellowing, but there's no need to worry too much about these diseases, which are rare. Slugs are very keen on young seedlings, which they would devour completely if we weren't careful. Ashes, soot, quicklime or hydraulic lime thrown on the seedlings will keep them away or kill them. USES. - Lamb's lettuce is used especially in salads. SWEET CORN Zea Mays L. Grasses. ORIGIN. - Corn was imported from America to Europe around the middle of the sixteenth century. An annual plant, it is known locally as Turkish wheat, Spanish wheat, Barbary wheat, Garouille (in the Charentes region), Indian wheat, etc. CULTIVATION. - Corn is sown in place, in April-May, in well-smoked soil. It is laid out in rows 50 centimeters apart, and at the same distance along the row; during the summer, it is hoeed and weeded several times, and it is customary to bring back the soil a little around each plant, in the form of a mound, to prevent the roots from being affected by excessive drought. Many growers, in order not to lose ground and at the same time to guarantee it, sow a tuft of dwarf beans between each corn plant. This is an excellent method. VARIETIES. - Early dwarf sweet corn. - Very good dwarf species, not exceeding 70 centimeters, white grain, very sweet. Sweet corn, wrinkled, evergreen. - Late and fairly tall. Vigorous, very tender ears. There are still a dozen varieties of sweet corn, highly esteemed in the United States, where this vegetable is in honor. In France, they are rarely grown. Varieties cultivated in large-scale farming, although less sweet than the above-mentioned breed, can nevertheless be used for the same purposes. SEEDS. - To obtain good sweet corn seeds, select a few vigorous plants, and keep their first fruits. When they are ripe, in September, or even earlier depending on the region, they are harvested, then their husks are removed by turning them towards the base of the cob. In this state, they are exposed to the air and sun, where they ripen completely. The seed keeps for two years. DISEASES. - The only disease we know of is ergot, which develops on the ears of corn. There is no remedy, but affected ears must be removed, as it is a poison. USES. - Sweet corn is widely used in the United States, where it is served on every table. The young cob is used, when the kernels are still in their milky state, just as beans are used in France. The whole ear is also used when it is just formed. In France, the main use is to pick the ears very young and pickle them in vinegar like gherkins. In this state, they make an excellent condiment. MARJOLAINE VIVACE Origanum vulgare L. Labiatae. A native perennial that can be propagated in the same way as thyme. It has the same uses. WHITE MARRUB Marrubium vulgare L. Labiatae. Indigenous, perennial, sown in the spring. It can also be propagated by dividing clumps. The leaves are used as a condiment. MARTYNIA Martynia lutea Lindl. Sesame. From Brazil, annual, this plant requires a lot of E. BERGER, Vegetable plants. 18 heat to develop. Sow on a bed in March; plant in the open ground along a wall facing south in May. The oddly shaped fruit can be pickled in vinegar like gherkins. CURWOOD Malva crispa L. Malvaceae. Annual, from the East. Sow in a nursery in April, preferably on a bed; transplant when plants are about 8 cm high. The leaves are used to garnish desserts. Melissa officinalis L. Labiatae Perennial plant of southern Europe. Reproduces by dividing clumps in spring. During winter, it must be covered with litter or long manure, especially in northern regions. The leaves are used as a condiment and in the preparation of liqueurs. MELON Cucumis Melo L. Cucurbitaceae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to tropical Asia, cultivated since time immemorial. The cantaloupe melon (cantalupo) was imported from Italy to France by Charles VIII in 1405. EARLY CROPS. - Growing early melons is much easier than you might think. Here's the one that has always worked for us, and which is the most practical. In January, prepare a good thick layer of horse manure and oak leaves; place the trunk and fill with 12 or 15 centimeters of soil mixed with free-range earth. The fires are allowed to burn through the layer, which takes three or four days, then the seed is sown in rows 95 centimetres apart, the seed is covered, and the straw mats are spread over the panels and maintained until emergence, which takes thirty-six or forty hours. During the day, we remove the mats, weather permitting, wipe off any mist, give the plants some air if the sun appears, and shade them. When the young plants are strong enough, i.e. when the first two leaves begin to appear, they are transplanted into 10-centimeter pots, planted up to the cotyledons, and at a rate of two plants per pot, face to face close to the sides; once this is done, the pots are watered and buried in a new layer, where good warmers have been set up to the height of the trunk. When the young melons have grown sufficiently, they are topped above the first two leaves. This pinching, which constitutes the first pruning, encourages the two branches to emerge from the axils of each leaf. Once they have reached this stage of development, the melons must be placed in their final position. In a layer prepared as described above, and this time filled with 14 or 15 centimetres of potting soil, two or four plants are planted in the middle of the trunk, depending on the species. Water lightly, then cover with panels, keeping a close eye on the mould that is bound to grow inside the trunks. Also, when the sun comes out, air is gradually supplied and warmers are set up, often needing to be reworked to maintain their warmth; pruning must not be neglected either, and must be carried out in good time. Towards the end of April, give the plants plenty of air and lightly baste them. Cover with mats every evening until this time, to prevent chilling. In May, the melons begin to ripen, and successively until mid-June or even later, if sown a few days apart. The best varieties for this type of cultivation, and at the same time those whose fruit is best appreciated, are: the cantaloup Prescott petit hâtif; the cantaloup noir des Carmes; the cantaloup de Bellegarde, etc. There is no need to grow other varieties. HATÉE OR SEASONAL CULTIVATION. - Parisian market gardeners, who excel in melon cultivation, have adopted a system they call culture de saison (seasonal cultivation), but which in reality is nothing more than hasty cultivation. Towards the end of March, beginning of April, a good bed is prepared for sowing, in the same way as indicated above. Once the seedlings are sufficiently developed, they are transplanted into 9-centimetre pots, which are then buried on the bed, or transplanted directly onto the bed at a distance of 10 centimetres in all directions. In May, prepare a series of layers, one in front of the other, in a vegetable patch where the previous year's fertilization had been imperfect; place the trunks in them and fill them with 15 centimetres of the best soil in the patch, levelling it as best you can. Three days after this operation, the melons are planted, either in clods, if they have been transplanted onto the bed, or in pots. When the plants are well established, and even when they have grown sufficiently, temperature permitting, the panels are removed and cultivation takes place in the open air. CULTIVATION UNDER CLOCHES. - Melons are sown at the same time as above. Around May 15, in the North, or earlier if the region is temperate, trenches 50 or 60 centimetres wide and 35 centimetres deep are prepared, filled with warm manure at least 50 centimetres thick and then loaded with the best loose soil, producing a sufficiently rounded bed. Rake well to even out the top and plant 1 metre apart along the line. Immediately after planting, cover the melons with cloches and wrap them in litter or heather for a few days to help the plant take root. As soon as the melons have picked up and are beginning to vegetate, the cloches are lifted to give them a little air, gradually increasing until they are removed, i.e. when the branches can no longer fit under the cloches. In many cases, growers keep the cloches up for a few days, supporting them with three racks placed in a triangle. In this way, it is said, the plants benefit from a greater amount of heat than in the open air. However, around June 10, they can be removed. Other cultivation care is the same as for plants grown without panels. Water sufficiently, but not excessively. These cultivation methods apply to temperate regions and the north of France. In the Midi, Centre and South-West, they are not applied in the same way. GROWING ON TEENS. - Growing on teens is the most common method, and the best for full soil. Once the soil has been ploughed with a spade, lines are drawn with a chalk line, 1.40 m apart. The ends are marked with stakes; once this is done, a 40-centimeter trench is opened with the spade, i.e. 20 centimeters are taken from each side of the line, to a depth of 35 to 40 centimeters. The soil from the trenches is discarded to the right and left. The trenches are then filled with good horse manure, well moistened with urine, 18. tamped down by trampling on it several times, then covered with soil, preferably the best. A rake is passed over the whole, which should form a bulging part in the middle, the axis of which should be the line originally traced, which is re-established by placing the string in the holes of the stakes left for this purpose at the start of the operation. Once the melons have been sown or transplanted into pots, towards the end of April or May, they are planted in pairs, 1 or 1.20 m apart on the line. When planting, form a good basin around them for watering, and cover with cloches or small glazed wooden squares specially designed for this purpose, much used in the South-West. Other care consists of watering, pinching and light hoeing around the plants. CULTIVATION IN THE GROUND. - Open-ground cultivation is very easy, and is the practice in the South of France: Toulouse, Avignon, Arles, Mont- pellier, Perpignan, Narbonne, etc. In Tours, Angers and Nantes, this type of cultivation is practiced, but only on limited areas. Farmers in Gironde and Lot-et-Garonne grow it among vines and in open fields. In the first few days of May, the ground where the melons are to be planted is thoroughly hoeed, then round holes 40 to 50 centimetres in diameter are dug, 40 centimetres deep at 1.50 or 2 metres apart. Fill them with still-warm horse manure, tamp it down and mound the soil, forming a kind of funnel at the top, then sow. Place four or five seeds per hole and water lightly with an apple. When the melons have sprouted, select three of the most beautiful and uproot the others; then spread a light mulch around them; all that remains is to prune them. In general, growers only topped the plants and then left them to grow wild; despite this, we have seen some really beautiful products, but they need the sun of the Midi. The best varieties for this type of cultivation are the maraicher melon, the Cavaillon melon, the sucrin de Tours melon and the green melon, etc. PRUNING MELONS. - There are two main pruning systems advocated by melon growers, and they apply to all varieties and all crops. First system. First pruning. - When the stem is sufficiently developed, it is pinched above the first two leaves, giving rise to two lateral branches in the axils of each leaf. While performing this first pinch, remove the two buds that begin to appear under the cotyledons. Second pruning. - As the two lateral branches mentioned above have sufficiently developed their third leaf, they are pruned above the second, giving rise to four new branches. Third pruning. - The four new branches, once they have grown their third leaf, are also pruned above the second. This is almost always the last pruning. It encourages the emergence of new branches, which generally bear fruit. However, there are certain varieties where a fourth pruning becomes necessary. In this case, we always pinch above the second leaf. When a fruit is well set, pinch off the branch bearing it, one or two leaves above. Some of the other branches are then removed and become useless. As a general rule, if you want to obtain beautiful fruit, leave only two per plant, one on each side. Second system. - This pruning procedure is much simpler than the previous one, and generally gives just as good results. It consists in pinching the main stem above the second leaf, cancelling the cotyledon buds, and, when the lateral branches have developed their sixth leaf, pruning above them and then waiting for the fruit to appear; when it is set, operate in the same way as indicated for the first system. VARIETIES. - The best and simplest classification for melons is that adopted by M. de Vilmorin. They are divided into two main series: embroidered melons and cantaloupes. FIG. 33 - Embroidered melon, lon sucrin de Tours, etc.; 1°Embroidered melons (fig. 38). - We will mention only the best species: Melon de Cavaillon with red and green flesh. - American pineapple melons, with red and green flesh, or pocket melons. - Hon-flower melon. - Market melon. - Me- 2° Cantaloupe melons (fig. 39). - Alger cantaloupe melon. - Carmes black cantaloupe melon. - Black cantaloupe melon from Portugal. - Cantaloupe melon. Prescott. - Prescott cantaloupe melon, silver-white background. - Sucrin cantaloupe melon, etc. SEEDS. - To obtain good melon seeds, you need to choose beautiful fruits that are characteristic of the species. When you eat them, you remove the seed, wash it and dry it in the shade. The best to sow is the three-year-old variety. The germination period is six years. DISEASES AND PESTS. - The melon is affected by several diseases, miller on the roots and uredo on the leaves, and sometimes by another parasitic disease, which resembles mildew on the vine; the best remedy is Bordeaux mixture. Grey and green aphids also wreak havoc in certain regions, where the undersides of the leaves are eaten away in a multitude of places. It has been suggested to dip poultry feathers in oil, and to prick them among the affected melons, or to place small boards coated with coaltar: the flying insects stick to them and succumb. Nicotine easily destroys aphids. Snails are very fond of young seedlings, especially when they start to emerge. A sprinkling of ash or soot will keep them away. USES. - When perfectly ripe, slices of melon are eaten at the beginning of a meal to stimulate the appetite. It is said that melon is very indigestible, so it should only be eaten in moderation. The young fruit, as large as walnuts, can be preserved in vinegar like gherkins. WATERMELON Cucurbita Citrullus L. Cucurbitaceae. ORIGIN. - African annual. CULTIVATION. - Watermelons need a lot of warmth to develop, so they are rarely grown in France, except in the South, on the Mediterranean coast, in Antibes, Grasse, Gap, etc., where they do well. In the north, the fruit almost never reaches its normal volume, which means it's never good. In the Midi, melons are sown in the ground, as is the case for melons grown outdoors. In the North, they need to be grown in a hurry. If you want beautiful fruit, don't prune them. VARIETY. - There are two species: the red-seeded watermelon and the black-seeded watermelon. From these come a multitude of varieties, prized in America but little grown in France. The beautiful melons we see on our markets are shipped in part from Spain. USES. - The pulp is eaten like a melon; the main use is to make jam, which is excellent. PEPPERMINT Mentha piperita L. Same cultivation and uses as the following. GREEN MINT Mentha viridis L. Labiatae. Indigenous, perennial, multiplies by dividing creeping stems in spring; plant in well-smoked, moist soil, recut stems in autumn and cover with compost. The entire plant is used as a condiment, as well as to make flavored liqueurs or baths. NAVET Brassica Napus L. Cruciferae. ORIGIN. - Biennial plant from temperate Europe. CULTIVATION. - Turnips are not very difficult to grow in the right soil, and thrive almost anywhere. However, they seem to prefer limestone soils; depending on the environment, they are either mild or strong. Turnips can be sown from March to September; the first seedlings, from March to May, set seed immediately, while those sown after this time do not set seed until the following year. This plant should therefore be considered an autumn vegetable. Sow broadcast, in patches, in recently ploughed and smoked soil. Cover lightly by harrowing with a fork. Straw. When the young plants have grown their third leaves, thin out, weed and water. Watering must be copious during hot spells, as turnips, to be good and tender, must not suffer any break in their vegetation; they must be eaten at about three-quarters of their size. To get them very early, sow them in January and February, under cold frames or on muted beds. In the past, Parisian market gardeners used to grow this type of crop a lot, which provided them with good profits, but which they have now more or less abandoned. The best varieties for this type of cultivation, as well as for first-season seedlings in the open ground, are: the Munich turnip or rouge plat de mai, the turnip des vertus Marteau, the rond de Croissy, the blanc plat hátif, and so on. In the south of France, turnips make it through the winter without suffering too much, but in the north they suffer a lot and become hollow. That's why market gardeners are in the habit, when cold weather approaches, of digging them up, burying them in the sand outside and covering them with litter when the temperature is too low. Others put up windbreaks, glass frames or wooden panels. FIO. 40. - Turnip var. Hammer. FIG. 41 - Red turnip, flat, early, whole leaf. VARIETIES. - There are a considerable number of these, of which we shall mention the best known: Navet demi-long des vertus, var. Marteau (fig. 40). - Croissy turnip. - Navet rouge plat de mai ou de Munich. - Round green turnip. - Very early, full-leaf, flat red turnip (fig. 41). - Half-long, white turnip. - Limousin turnip. - Norfolh, white. - Montmagny yellow turnip. - Globe white turnip. - Navet cougoulou du Kahsmir, a new and very deserving variety. - Golden ball yellow turnip, etc. SEEDS. - Roots from the previous year, selected in autumn, are planted in March: by June or July, the seeds are ready for harvesting. They are treated in the same way as cabbage seeds. They keep well for five years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Turnips are virtually free of fungal diseases. However, there is no shortage of insects; the most dreaded is the flea beetle (Phyllotreta nemorum), which attacks young seedlings and destroys them in a matter of days, especially in dry weather. To combat them, plants can be sprinkled with unleached ash, pyrethrum powder, black soap solution, nicotine, etc. Some growers have recommended soaking seeds in brine, rolling them in flower of sulfur, plaster, lime, etc., before sowing them. We have not tried any of these procedures. The white grub is also an enemy of the turnip: in soils where this insect abounds, most of the roots are eaten away. USES. - The root is an essential ingredient in stews. The leaves, blanched in the dark, are excellent for a variety of dishes. Before flowering, the young shoots are used in the same way as broccoli. AROMATIC NIGEL Nigella sativa L. Ranunculaceae. Oriental annual. Sow in spring in E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 19 place, with good exposure. The ripe seed is used as a seasoning in various culinary preparations. ONION Allium Cepa L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - According to De Candolle, the onion, which is an annual and sometimes perennial plant, was found growing wild in Persia, Afghanistan, Beluchistan and Palestine. Its cultivation dates back over four thousand years. CULTIVATION. - Cultivation is extremely easy, and can be done in several ways, depending on the region. In the south and south-west, sowing takes place in August-September, in beds or in the field; transplanting takes place in October-November and in January, February and March. In the North and Centre, sow in February and March, in rows or wide beds. When the plant is strong enough, thin it out and transplant in areas where the seed has failed. Weed and wait for maturity. This method is easy and gives good results: however, the produce is less attractive than with the Midi method, where the plants are transplanted. Onions thrive best in light, humus-rich soils; liquid fertilizers are well suited to them, and by renewing them often, you can obtain magnificent products. Young plants intended for transplanting must undergo a preliminary operation, which consists in cutting off the roots by about half their total length, without touching the leaves. They should not be buried too deeply, and should be watered as soon as they are planted. Other care consists of light hoeing and weeding. When you want very small onions, which are used whole and are always highly prized on the market, sow very thickly in February or March, water if necessary to encourage emergence, then water only rarely; this way you obtain very small, perfectly healthy bulbs which, the following year, replanted as seedlings, give quite beautiful products at the end of the summer. Young seedlings are also well-suited to being covered with tan or grape marc: this method has been adopted by growers in the south-west of France, and has proved very successful. Similarly, to force and activate the vegetation of early white onions in spring, a certain quantity of poultry feathers should be added to the fertilizer. Onions are harvested when the leaves are completely yellow and dry. Ripening can be accelerated by laying the stalks on the ground, either with a rake or simply with your foot. It is even claimed that this operation promotes bulb enlargement. VARIETY. - There are many varieties of onion. Early varieties include: White onion, small, extra-fine 42. - White onion, small, extra-early Barletta. early Barletta (fig. 42). - Queen's early white onion. - Very early Nocéra onion. - Early Paris white onion. - Early white onion from Valencia, etc. Good-keeping varieties include: Oignon jaune de Danvers. - Oignon jaune paille des vertus, - Lescure yellow onion. - Oignon géant de Zittau, - Oignon jaune soufre d'Espagne. - Trébons yellow onion. - Early yellow piriform onion. - Ordinary pale red onion. - Niort pale red onion - Aigre onion. - Giant Rocca onion (fig. 43). - Round, flat Madeira onion. - Dark red onion. - Flat red Italian onion, etc. SEEDS. - In autumn, or in February and March, select the best onions and plant them in rows 40 to 45 centimetres apart, 20 centimetres apart. When the flower stalks are sufficiently developed, as the head is very FiG. 43. - Without this precaution, the wind will quickly destroy them by detaching them from the base. Once ripe, they are gathered into bundles and hung in a dry, well-ventilated place. Once dry, we extract the seeds by rubbing them between our hands or on a sieve, and, to clean them quickly, we plunge them into a vase filled with water: the best seeds rush to the bottom; we collect them and spread them out in the shade on cloths or planks to dry. They keep well for two years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Onions can suffer from a number of parasitic diseases. The most terrible is rhizoctonia, which starts in the lower, inner part of the bulb; affected parts give off an infectious odor. Rust sometimes attacks the leaves, which then dry out rapidly. USES. - Onions are used daily in the kitchen, and it would be difficult to do without them; they are pickled in vinegar like gherkins. In the south of France, country folk eat it raw, with pepper. It's said to be quite healthy. EGYPTIAN ONION or ROCAMBOLE A type of onion that differs greatly from other races in its habit and type of vegetation. The stem, which rises each year, produces small bulbils at the top, which take the place of seeds. CULTIVATION. - These small bulblets are planted in spring, in rows 25 cm apart, in dry rather than damp soil, as they rot easily. It is only the following year that they come up, and in turn produce new bulblets; as for the bulbs, they are average, but difficult to preserve, the bulblets keep much better. USES. - Same as onion. VAriety. - The Catawissa onion (fig. 44), introduced from America as a perennial, is only a sub-variety of the Egyptian onion; its stems are taller and less swollen in the middle. The bulblets are elongated, smaller and very prolific, for as soon as they reach full size, they themselves develop a fistulous stem, which produces new bulblets, so that on one plant there are sometimes two or three tiers of green shoots and bulblets. Same cultivation as the Egyptian onion. PATATE ONION The patate onion is a special breed, which produces a vine like Jersey shallots. CULTIVATION. - It is planted in February or March, in loose, well-smoked soil from the previous year. In July-August, the bulbs are harvested successively from the center, as it is quite rare for them to reach maturity all at once, which is why many growers have given up cultivating them; preservation is all the more difficult the larger the bulbs. Does not produce seeds. USES. - The flesh is very sweet and of excellent quality. COMMON GARLIC Rumex Acetosa L. Polygonaceae ORIGIN. - Native perennial, improved by cultivation; typical species found in low, damp meadows. GROWN OUTDOORS. - Common sorrel is propagated from seed and clump fragments; sow from February to August in well-smoked, recently tilled soil; divide clumps at the end of winter, before they sprout. Sorrel is well-suited to borders and flowerbeds, but should be renewed every four or five years. Market gardeners plant sorrel in beds among spinach or other vegetables; most pull up young plants for sale, and therefore sow them successively throughout the summer. The seed, being very fine, needs almost no covering. Transplanted, this plant succeeds better than sown in place; very voracious, it can take all powerful fertilizers, especially liquid manure: hoe, weed and water copiously during the summer. FORCED CULTIVATION. - Sorrel is insensitive to the cold, and frosts only slow down its growth. To get sorrel through the winter, especially in the North, it must be forced; this operation can be done in place or on a bed. In situ, the boxes and panels are placed on boards, the paths are dug out a little and filled with good stoves of fresh manure; at night, they are covered with straw mats or litter, and if the day is sunny, they are given a little air: the stoves are often reshuffled and stirred, fresh manure is added each time, and they are lightly basked from time to time. After preparing the bed, which is at least 40 centimetres thick, we fill it with 15 centimetres of potting soil, then plant the sorrel very tightly. Old clumps that you want to destroy are good for this, but two- and three-year-old clumps are preferable. During cold spells, cover with straw mats and aerate, depending on the temperature; this way, you'll have sorrel to pick all winter long. VARIETIES. - Several varieties have emerged from the common sorrel, with leaves of varying widths. We quoteFIG. 45. - Belleville sorrel. rons: Belleville broad sorrel (fig. 45), the best and most widely cultivated. - Lettuce leaf sorrel. - Giant blond sorrel. - Virieu sorrel - virgin sorrel, etc. SEEDS. - Seeds are harvested from two- or three-year-old seedlings from the previous year; they will be all the better for it; as soon as they are ripe, they fall off easily, so you need to keep an eye on the seed holders; as they don't all ripen at once, you successively collect the good ones, put them on cloths and expose them to the sun. They retain their germination capacity for two years. DISEASES AND PESTS. - Sorrel is attacked by a white mildew that develops on the roots, especially on old clumps, and destroys them fairly quickly; to get rid of it, the affected plants must be pulled up. Leaves are sometimes eaten by a number of small insects, notably a leaf beetle (Gastrophysa raphani), which, in May and June, can be found in innumerable numbers on the leaves, gnawing them right down to the roots. The aphid (A phis rumicis) also wreaks havoc, especially on seedlings. All these insects are difficult to destroy, even to keep at bay, and the remedies recommended to date are of dubious effectiveness. USES. - Sorrel is used daily in the kitchen, in lean soups and purées, and in combination with spinach to make excellent lean dishes. GARLIC AND SPINACH Rumex Patientia L. Polygonaceae. ORIGIN. - Native, perennial. CULTIVATION. - This species, found almost everywhere in the wild, is little cultivated. It produces abundantly very long leaves, which appear at the end of winter, a few days before the other races. Same cultivation, same uses as common sorrel. Little damage from insects. VIRGIN SUGAR Rumex montanus Desf. Rumex arifolius All. Polygonaceae. ORIGIN. - Native perennial plant, the wild type of which is found spontaneously in the fir forests of central France and in the Alps. CULTIVATION. - This species does not produce seeds, so it can only multiply by bursting its clumps in February or March, or in October. The leaves are broader than those of common sorrel; it is less acidic. Same cultivation care, same uses. 19. OXALIS CRENELÉE Oxalis crenata Jacq. Oxalidees. ORIGIN. - From Peru, perennial in its native country, but annual in our cultivations. CULTIVATION. - Oxalis is a fancy vegetable, so it is rarely grown in France. It is propagated by its tubers, which are planted in May in good, well-smoked soil with good exposure. In northern France, tubers should be planted from April onwards, in pots placed on warm beds. Plant them in rows 80 cm apart in all directions. When the stems have grown sufficiently, we bury them or cover them with potting soil to encourage bulb formation. To harvest the bulbs, wait until the first frosts have destroyed the upper part of the stems. USES. - The young, tender shoots are eaten in salads or as sorrel. The tubers, highly prized in South America, are eaten like potatoes, after being dried in the sun or in an oven to remove their acidity. PAK-CHOI. CHINESE CAU Brassica Sinensis L. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Annual plant from China. CULTIVATION. - The pak-choi is a breed of cabbage completely different from the European varieties; it looks more like a pear than a cabbage; the petioles are large, elongated, fairly pure white, the leaves a shiny dark green. Sowing begins in February and lasts until September; the best sowings are those made in July and August, as the plants don't bolt until the following year. Sow preferably in rows 40 centimeters apart, thin out several times, water copiously while young, weed and hoe, pulling the soil back a little around the plants to help them overwinter; only in the Midi do they resist frosts well; in the North, they suffer greatly and are sometimes lost altogether. USES. - The leaves are eaten like cabbage, and the petioles are used in the same way as chard. After being boiled in salted water, they are eaten like asparagus, and make excellent lean soups. PANAIS Pastinaca sativa L. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Native plant, biennial. CULTIVATION. - Parsnips are easy to grow, and thrive in any soil that is sufficiently amended and fresh; slightly heavier soils seem to suit them better. Sow in spring, either broadcast or in rows 40 cm apart, covering the seed, which germinates capriciously. When the plants are strong, thin them out and transplant in places where the seed has failed, after trimming the tips of the leaves. Hoe and weed several times during the summer, water and mulch if the soil is too dry. Parsnips are very hardy, resisting even the most severe frosts, so they can be harvested as needed. This plant is mainly grown in the North, Normandy, Brittany and around Paris, but is rarely cultivated in the South of France. VAriety. - Round parsnip, the best. - Long parsnip with hollow crown (fig. 46). - Guernsey half-long parsnip. - Improved parsnip (Le Bian). SEEDS. - Parsnips go to seed in the second year of sowing, in July-August; when the umbels change color, they are cut and spread out on cloths which are exposed to the sun to complete ripening. They detach very easily from their receptacle. Germination lasts only one year. DISEASES, PESTS. - Slugs and snails devour young seedlings; white grubs sometimes gnaw the roots. USES. - Parsnips are an essential ingredient in a good pot-au-feu. It is also eaten with fat or in white sauce; it is said to be a FIO. 46. - Long parsnip. excellent food for horses. Convolvulus Batatas L. Convolvulaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, but annual in our crops; from South America, cultivated in all warm countries. CULTIVATION. - Without being difficult to grow, the potato requires meticulous care, especially when it comes to preserving the tubers, which is the most delicate point. The best method, which has always worked for us, is to bury them in pine bran, avoiding them touching, and to place them in a warm, healthy spot. Potatoes can be propagated from seed, but this process is not widely used in France. It can be propagated by tubers, and by cuttings, which can be taken in warm layers under a bell, or in tan. To do this, tubers are planted early, in December or January, also on layers. Once the young cuttings have recovered, they are placed in small 7-centimeter pots on warm beds, and given larger pots as they develop. In the Midi region, they can be planted in the open ground from April, on muted beds or ados. In the North, they have to be planted on layers, under panels in March-April, as you would do with early melons. When frosts are no longer a threat, i.e. in May, the boxes and panels are removed, and the plant is grown in the open air. This plant needs a lot of water in summer, so that it can develop easily. It's only after four to five months that the tubers are ready for harvesting; for this, wait as late as possible, and when the first frost has destroyed the tops, as it's mainly in the late season that they grow. Keep a close eye on them, as they do not keep well. VARIETIES. - There are a large number of potato varieties, most of which are of little value in our climates; the best for our regions are : Malaga pink potato. - Ile-de-France potato. - White Tahitian potato. Patate grosse jaune. - White Sageret ovoid potato. - Patate violette et rouge, etc. DISEASES, PESTS. - In our climate, the potato is virtually disease-free. Yellow snails are quite fond of tender young shoots. White grubs dig tubers. USES. - The flesh of the potato is sweet and very tender, a little watery; it is used like potato. Cooked in ashes, it is excellent. PERCE-PIERRE Crithmum maritimum L. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - Indigenous, perennial plant found growing in old walls or on cliffs along the seashore. CULTIVATION. - Sow as soon as the seed is ripe, i.e. in autumn; the following spring, transplant the plants, preferably at the foot of the walls; comes without any care. USES. - The leaves can be pickled in vinegar, like gherkins. PARSLEY Petroselinum sativum Hoffm. Apium Petroselinum L. Umbelliferae. ORIGIN. - This biennial umbellifer grows wild in southern Europe, from Spain to Macedonia; it has also been found in Tlemcen in Algeria and in Lebanon (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - Parsley is very hardy, adapting well to all types of soil. It is sown from February to September, either in the field or in nurseries; in general, it is sown in the field; this is a mistake, because to obtain beautiful products, they must be transplanted; this operation is carried out when the plants are still young; the pivot is trimmed before planting; parsley is arranged in beds, rows or, better still, bor- dures; all exposures are suitable. The seed is slow to germinate, taking between twenty-five and thirty days for regular emergence. In a hot greenhouse, we germinated it in twelve days. To grow well, this plant needs to be kept very clean, so no effort should be spared in weeding and hoeing, and to avoid running out in summer, it should be watered copiously. In winter, parsley doesn't grow much: so to avoid running out as the cold approaches, place boxes and their panels on the beds, and surround them with good stoves when production diminishes. You can also pull up old plants, with their roots, and place them side by side, on a layer previously prepared and loaded with 25 centimetres of potting soil mixed with free earth; you bury them up to the collar. Water, then cover with panels; all that's left to do is provide air when the weather permits. This way, you'll have parsley to pick all winter long. VARIETIES. - Large Naples parsley. - Very vigorous, rather like celery. Fern-leaved parsley. - Very curly dwarf parsley - Two very good varieties, with very finely cut foliage. Dark green curly parsley. - A highly recommendable novelty, contrasting with its blond-green predecessors. Large-rooted parsley, sweet early. - Same crop as parsnip. The roots, which reach a certain volume, are eaten like parsnips. The leaves have the same uses as common parsley. Parsley with large, long roots. - Very long roots; less productive and less popular than the preceding variety; same cultivation. SEEDS. - Parsley seeds are harvested from the previous year's seedlings. They usually ripen in August-September. Umbels should be cut preferably in the morning, at dew, so that ripe seeds cannot fall out. They are then laid out on cloths in part-shade; once dry, all you have to do is strike them with a stick to detach them from their receptacle. They retain their germinative capacity for three or four years. Practitioners consider the two-year-old seed to be the best. DISEASES, PESTS. - The root aphid is the most dreaded of these enemies, living in innumerable numbers above the crown and sucking the sap. Fortunately, they are not found in all soils. Slugs and snails are not very fond of parsley. USES. - Parsley is very useful in the kitchen, where it is used every day. Cooked or raw, it is used in sauces, stews and lean soups. PE TSAI Brassica Sinensis L. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Annual plant from China. CULTIVATION. - This vegetable is little cultivated in Europe; it is a race of cabbage in the genus of Pak-choi, but with a much duller overall color. Same cultivation and uses as Pak-choi. COMMON PEPPER Capsicum annuum B. Solanaceae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant from Brazil. CULTIVATION. - Peppers are grown in much the same way as eggplants. Sow in warm beds, transplant in January or February, very close to the glass, and give air FIG. 47. - Piment cardinal. whenever the temperature permits. In April, for the Midi, and in May, for the Nord, they are planted in well-worked and sufficiently smoked soil; they are planted in the same way as eggplants; many growers dig 25-centimeter-square holes which they fill with well-consumed manure or potting soil, and then plant. This is a very good method for unimproved land. During the summer, water copiously. Weed and hoe frequently, and in very dry soils, spread a good mulch around the plants. VARIETY. - Long red pepper. - Widespread variety, good yield, very strong flavor. Long cayenne pepper. - Very widespread, very strong flavor. Long yellow pepper. - Good variety, grown in the Midi, very strong flavor. Piment Cerise. - Small, round fruits, excessively hot flavor. Chili pepper. - Erect, productive fruit, very hot flavor. Purple pepper. - Vigorous, hot flavor. Sweet chili pepper. - Good variety with large late fruits. Spanish sweet pepper. - The largest of its kind, very mild, much appreciated in the South of France and in Spain, where it is grown almost everywhere. Monstrous pepper. - Mild, irregularly shaped fruits. Tomato pepper. - Fruits are flattened and grooved like tomatoes, not very productive, with a sweet rather than hot flavor. New, good-quality varieties include: Piment cardinal (fig. 47). - Red fruit. Chinese or celestial pepper (fig. 48). - Numerous conical fruits, changing color from green to creamy yellow or purplish, to coral red and golden yellow, very original. Ruby-King. - American variety, very large. Golden-yellow mammoth. - Very large, sweet. Piment carré doux d'Ampuis. - Giant Procopp pepper. - Cranberry pepper, three new, highly recommendable varieties, etc. FIG. 48. - Chinese pepper. SEEDS. - Chilli seeds are easy to harvest. When the fruits are ripe, they are picked, laid out in the sun for a few hours, and the seed is removed and dried by laying them out on cloths in the shade. It keeps well for four years. In the Paris climate, it does not always ripen well. MALADIAS, PEST ANIMALS. - In certain soils, probably poor in silica, chillies, despite the best care and fertilization, become chlorotic and do not grow; they are stunted and die. We've tried several remedies, none of which have worked. Slugs and snails devour it when it's young. The former attack the stem, which they perforate, the latter the leaves. Care should therefore be taken with young plantations. USES. - Young fruit is pickled in vinegar and combined with gherkins. In the south and southwest of France, they are eaten raw in salads. In Spain, when the fruit is ripe, it is dried in the sun and then used in the same way as pepper. PIMPRENELLE PETITE Poterium Sanguisorba L. Rosaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, native. CULTIVATION. - This plant practically grows itself. It requires almost no cultivation care. It is sown in the spring, in March or April, or at the end of summer, in August-September, in rays 30 centimetres apart; once emerged, thin out if necessary. Weed and hoe several times during the year, removing dead leaves as they occur; when they have set seed, shave them off completely. It is best grown as a border plant. USES. - Eat young leaves in salads. DANDELION Leontodon Taraxacum L. Taraxacum officinale Wigg. Taraxacum Dens leonis Desf. Compositae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, native. CULTIVATION. - Dandelions have only been cultivated in gardens for about twenty years. In the past, we were content to collect those that grew spontaneously in meadows. Today, through successive selections in seed carriers, we have succeeded in improving this plant; the varieties we have created are fixed, reproduce well and have an unquestionable superiority over wild plants. FIG. 49: A very early improved dandelion. Dandelion is sown in spring, in March and April, in the field or in a nursery for transplanting, in rows 40 centimetres apart and 5 centimetres deep. Cover the seed very lightly and, if the weather is warm, water, otherwise emergence will be poor. When the plants are strong enough, they are hoeed for the first time, and then weeded again during the summer. The young plants to be transplanted are selected when they have grown their first three leaves. They are planted at the same distance as above, but 5 centimetres apart. Water immediately after planting. From November onwards, we start to bleach them, doing this in the same way as we do capuchin beard. In spring, when they start to grow, we bleach them in place, either by covering them with sand, litter or soil, or by placing them on a bed, etc. VARIETY. - Improved full-hearted dandelion. - Very early improved dandelion (p. 49). - Mossy dandelion. - Chicory dandelion. A very remarkable novelty forming a compact clump of long, upright leaves; blanched, it is delicious. SEEDS. - Dandelion goes to seed in the second year after sowing, in spring; the seed is ripe towards the end of April and May; it keeps for two years. USES. - Green or blanched, eaten as a salad. PEAR Altium Porrum L. Liliaceae. ORIGIN. - Leeks are biennial, originating in Mediterranean regions, according to De Candolle. CULTIVATION. - The leek is an excessively voracious plant, and only succeeds well if the soil in which it is planted is thoroughly fertilized with very powerful fertilizers. Liquid fertilizers, especially fecal matter, produce the best results. Leeks are sown from December to July. The first sowings, from December to February, should be done in a warm layer under a frame, or in a flowerbed with a good southern exposure. The layer should be filled with 10 centimeters of potting soil, mixed with a third of vegetable garden soil, all well levelled, then sown, and with a small board, the seed is placed in the soil and covered with 8 millimeters of the same mixture; water and cover with panels; give air, when the temperature permits. When the young plants are sufficiently developed, they are transplanted to beds and planted in the ground. These leeks are ready for harvesting in June, succeeding those of the previous year. From February to July, leeks are sown in the open ground, broadcast in beds in soil that has been smoked and prepared for a few days. If the soil is loose enough, it is covered with potting soil, grape marc or even tannin. Then spread a good mulch. Spring sowings are generally the most popular. When the plants are as big as a goose feather, they are transplanted into the ground in beds of six rows, 20 or 25 centimetres apart, depending on the variety. Plants are planted 15 centimetres apart on the line; the beds are divided by a 50-centimetre path, where early cabbages or other vegetables are planted. The young plant, which has had the tips of its leaves and about half its roots trimmed, should be planted fairly deeply, so that when it is uprooted it has as much white as possible. In the South-West and part of the South, they are planted inclined and very deep, and when they are strong enough to be sold, they are unshovelled on one side, and laid down by burying them up to the birth of the leaves. This produces a lot of white, but the plants never get too big. Long leeks are preferred for this purpose. In the North, some market gardeners pull them out and bury them side by side in a gauge. Other cultivation care consists of weeding, frequent hoeing and copious watering in summer. VARIETIES. - Monstrous Carentan leek. - Excellent variety, very productive and voluminous. Poireau gros de Nîmes. - Very large and hardy; particularly suitable for the Midi. Poireau long d'hiver de Paris. - Very good variety, highly esteemed in the North; especially suitable for late-season planting; very hardy. Poireau gros court. - A very good variety, highly recommended for the Centre and Midi regions. Poireau jaune du Poitou (fig.50). - A very good variety; highly esteemed in the West, where it is grown extensively. FIG. 50. - Yellow leek from Poitou. Very large Rouen leek. - Very hardy; does well throughout the North. Long leek from Tarentaise. - A vigorous breed, highly esteemed in the Lyonnais region; very hardy; withstands the harshest winters without the tips of the leaves suffering. There are other varieties of leek grown in England and America: those we have tried have not given us good results. In certain parts of the Charentes region and in Lyon, a breed called poireau à gousses or poireau perpétuel is cultivated, which in our view must be the wild leek found spontaneously throughout the Midi. Cultivation seems to have improved it somewhat. SEEDS. - Leek seeds are harvested in the same way as onion seeds. They keep for two to three years. DISEASES, PESTS. - In dry soils, leeks are attacked by a small white worm, acro sporium, which gets into the heart of the plant and wreaks havoc by gnawing the young leaves. The best way to destroy it is to cut the stem below the affected part, but once is rarely enough: if necessary, you'll have to go back two or even three times. Slugs and snails sometimes devour young seedlings. USES. - Leeks are used every day. It is used for pot-au-feu. Excellent cooked in its own juice or in white sauce. Cooked on the grill and seasoned with oil and vinegar, it is a very popular dish on the Ocean coast. A refreshing and healthy vegetable. PEAR or BEITE Beta vulgaris L. Chenopodea. ORIGIN. - Native biennial plant found in sandy seaside soils throughout the Mediterranean region as far as the Caspian Sea, Persia, Babylonia and the Canary Islands. Cultivated for over two thousand years. GROWING. - Pear trees are very hardy. They can be grown in much the same way as beet. They are sown from March to May, 40 to 50 centimetres apart. If the plant is too thick, it is thinned out to leave a 30 to 40-centimeter gap between the rows. Plants removed during thinning can be transplanted after trimming the pivot and root tips. Water immediately after planting, choosing a rainy or dark day for the job. Weed, hoe and water during hot spells. E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 20 About seven or eight weeks after sowing, you can start harvesting the pear, choosing the best-developed leaves. In Lyon, where this plant is widely grown, it is mounded like artichokes. It survives winter very well in the Centre and Midi, without suffering too much, but in the North, even in Paris, heavy frosts destroy it. In these regions, when cold weather approaches, it's a good idea to FIG. 51. - White pear. cover them with bedding or straw mats, or to bring them into the vegetable greenhouse. White pear can also be planted as a border plant, just as it can be sown in August and September for spring consumption. VARIETY. - Common blond pear. - Highly esteemed in the East and part of the West, where it is the staple of country folk's lean soups. Poirée blonde à cardes blanches de Lyon. - A beautiful and honest variety, with very large leaves and broad ribs, highly esteemed in Lyon, where it is grown almost exclusively, and is the most recommended: less hardy than the previous variety. Poirée à carde blanche (fig. 51). - Very good hardy variety, highly esteemed in northern France and Belgium. Poirée à carde blanche frisée. - Very blond, curly and blistered leaves; fairly hardy. Chilean yellow-ribbed pear. Chilean red-ribbed pear. - Two very tall varieties, reaching 50 to 60 cm, more ornamental than vegetable. Very broad-ribbed white pear with green leaves from Lyon. - An excellent variety, producing large, white, tender, fleshy and long petals. Better winter hardiness than golden-leaf pear. Cutting pear or spinach chard. - Very common variety, of mediocre quality; very hardy. SEEDS. - Pear does not set seed until the second year after sowing; seeds are harvested like beet. Germination lasts six years. DISEASES, PESTS. - It is attacked by the same diseases and insects as beet. USES. - Like cabbage, pear leaves can be used in lean soups. Mix with sorrel to correct its acidity. The petioles, or chards, can be prepared in a variety of ways: in white sauce, fried in butter, boiled in salted water; they can be eaten, like asparagus, in vinaigrette, etc. An excellent vegetable, but unfortunately not widely grown. peas Pisum sativum L. Legumes. ORIGIN. - Cultivated for over two thousand years; native to the southern Caucasus, Persia and northern India. Annual (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - Peas (fig. 52) are not difficult to select - - FIG. 5°. - Pea. of soil, it succeeds almost everywhere, provided it has sufficient fertilizer. However, it seems to do best in sandy soils, sufficiently mixed with clay. Peas are grown almost everywhere in France, varying from one locality to another, but they are most widely cultivated in the Midi, Sud- Quest and part of Brittany, as there is perhaps no other vegetable that gives the grower such remunerative results. Peas are sown very thickly, from the end of October to April, in rows spaced according to the vigor of the species: from 80 centimeters to 1 meter, for large row varieties; from 40 to 60 centimeters, for dwarf varieties; from 70 to 80 centimeters, for half-dwarf varieties. In production centers such as Bordeaux, Agen, Toulouse, etc., seedlings are sown on their sides, preferably facing south, so that when they germinate they are sheltered from the north. In vegetable growing, to make the best possible use of the land, seedlings are planted in two rows spaced at Om, 35 apart, with a pathway of Om, 60 for dwarf varieties; dwarf varieties are spaced as we have indicated, but without observing a pathway. When the peas have reached 6 or 8 centimetres in height, they are lightly hoeed; when they have doubled in height, the operation is repeated and they are lightly ridged, with the soil on either side. They are then trained with branches that have numerous ramifications right from the base. Chestnut, oak and poplar are excellent for this. Once the branches are in place, simply leave them to grow and weed them from time to time. Cultivation care is the same for dwarf varieties, and we recommend above all not neglecting to butter them properly: this is an excellent operation designed to prevent the first heat waves from reaching the roots; without this, the base turns yellow, and the harvest is compromised. 20. 30 years ago, market gardeners in the Paris area used to cultivate early peas; today, they have virtually abandoned them, since fresh peas from Algeria, Spain, the Midi, etc. are available almost all year round at the market halls. However, peas are still grown by amateurs. EARLY CROPS. - In the first few days of November, choose a good southern location, such as a hillside sheltered from the north. A warm layer of annealed manure, mixed with other detritus, is laid down. Place the trunks, then fill with 15 centimetres of vegetable garden soil, mixed with a little potting compost. Pack and sow in rows, 30 centimetres apart. Four rows are planted per 1.33-metre box; seeds are arranged in groups of three or four together, or one by one on the line at 5 centimetres. Cover with 2 cm, and water lightly if the mixture is too dry. Put up the panels, and give them some air during the day when the sun comes out, to chase away the humidity, which is very harmful. Cover with straw mats or bedding during the coldest weather. When they are sufficiently developed, as they always overextend, they are laid down towards the top of the trunk: to do this, they are held in place by a lath placed on the base of the stems, and this wood is removed when the heads are raised. Once the fourth leaf has developed, they are pinched off at the top. The best varieties for this type of cultivation are the following: Very early dwarf pea with frame. - Pois nain très hâtif d'Annonay. - Couturier dwarf pea. - Dutch dwarf pea. - Paris or Gonthier dwarf pea. - Blue-Peters pea. - Pois Merveille d'Amérique, etc. We can also operate in another way: we sow in a nursery under a very thick box, and once they have emerged, we spread a light layer of well-sieved soil over the entire sown surface to encourage the young roots to emerge. When the peas have reached 7 or 8 centimetres in height, they are transplanted three or four at a time into trays prepared as described above, or as follows: the trays are placed in a well-exposed position, directly on the ground, then a good iron or so of soil is removed from the inside and thrown back into the paths; once this has been done, the soil is ploughed and smoked; the peas are raked out and transplanted as described above. Water immediately after planting. Other cultivation care is the same as for the bedding method. VARIETIES. - There are a great many pea varieties, classified as follows: 1° round-grained shelled peas, both oar-shaped and dwarfed; 2° wrinkled-grained shelled peas, both oar-shaped and dwarfed; 3° peas without parchment or mangetout, both oar-shaped and dwarfed. We will mention the best varieties of each genus: Shelling peas, round-grained, rowed. - Early William pea. - Étampes marvel pea. - Dutch Michaux peas. - Michaux peas from Ruelle. - Clamart pea. - Leopold II pea. - Green pea. - Pois d'Auvergne. - Daniel O'Rourke pea. - Noyon green pea, etc. Dwarf round-grain shelled peas. - Very early dwarf peas. - Dwarf Couturier peas. - Dwarf Paris or Gonthier peas. - Full basket peas or Filbashet. - Blue-Peters pea. - Dwarf bishop pea. - Dwarf pea sunrise. - Pois nain l'évêque, long pods. - Dwarf pea of Touraine. - Pois orgueil du marché. - Pea princesse royale. - Very early dwarf pea from Annonay, etc. Peas for shelling, wrinkled grain, with oars. - Pea shah of Persia. - Telephone pea. - Green wrinkled pea with oars. - Duke of Albany pea. - Large white sweet wrinkled pea. - Sweet Knigth wrinkled pea. - Alpha pea. - Large green wrinkled peas (new), etc. Dwarf wrinkled peas for shelling. - American marvel peas (fig. 53). - Stratagem peas. - Pea profusion - FIG. 53 - Shucking peas, wrinkled grain, dwarf: American marvel. Dwarf snow pea (new). - Wilson pea. - Early white dwarf wrinkled pea. - The best of all peas. - Veitch wrinkled pea, etc. Peas without parchment or mangetout, with oars. - In this breed of pea, the pods are generally very large, fleshy and thick, with almost no parchment membranes, so that they are eaten like green beans, pods and all, and can be shelled; but the flavor of the beans is always a little pungent; highly esteemed in eastern France, Spain and Switzerland. Peas without forty-day parchment. - Peas without butter parchment. - Peas without H 2 D melting parchment FIG. 54. - Peas without melting parchment, from Saint-Désirat. 357 Saint-Désirat (fig. 54). - Giant parchmentless pea with very long pods. - Ram's horn parchmentless pea. - Green mangetout peas (new). Dwarf parchmentless or mangetout peas. - Early Breton dwarf parchmentless peas. - Very dwarf parchmentless peas, early to frame. - Dwarf white pea, etc. SEEDS. - To harvest good pea seeds, choose a piece of row and let it ripen on the ground. The first flowers, and consequently the first fruits formed, are recognized as the best for producing good early seeds. For this reason, we recommend pinching them off above the fifth or sixth flower stage "for row varieties". When they are almost fully ripe, they are plucked and laid out in the sun. They are then hand-picked. They retain their germinative power for four years; left in the pod, they keep for almost twice as long. peas, harmful animals. - Peas, especially the early varieties, are attacked by an insect of the beetle order called bruchus (Bruchus pisi), 5 millimeters long, which penetrates the grain and gnaws away the starchy part; their destruction becomes impossible. There are also two very small caterpillars (4 millimeters), which wreak havoc on young peas: Sitones lineatus and Sitones sulcifrons; they wreak havoc in full sun; when it rains, they hide at the foot of the plants. To destroy them, we recommend dusting the plants with insecticide powder, quicklime, soot, unleached ashes, etc. Snails and slugs also destroy young seedlings, especially spring ones. USES. - Peas are eaten when the seed is half-formed, when they are an excellent dish and considered very healthy and highly prized; they are served alone in a stew, or with game or other meat. Considerable quantities are canned and shipped all over the world. In some parts of the West, farmers eat them raw. Cicer arietinum L. Legumes. ORIGIN. - Southern Caucasus and Caspian Sea. CULTIVATION. - The chickpea is highly prized in Spain, where it is widely cultivated; in some regions, it is the staple food of the poor. In France, it is grown very little; only in the Pyrenees do a few farmers sow it. In the Midi region, chickpeas can be sown in February and March, but in the Nord and Centre regions, it's best to wait until April or May, when the soil is sufficiently warmed; they are planted in rows 40 or 50 centimetres apart, in pairs, 20 or 25 centimetres apart. Other cultivation care is the same as for dwarf beans, with the difference that watering need not be copious, as this plant does not fear drought. The chickpea, which grows to a height of 50 or 60 centimetres, doesn't need to be raked; the stem, being rough and consistent, holds itself up. VARIETIES. - Only the white chickpea is known in France, but there are two other varieties: one with a black grain, and the other with a red grain, which are highly esteemed, it is said, in the English Indies, where they are grown in large quantities. SEEDS. - Harvested like dwarf beans. USES. - Chickpeas are eaten in the same way as broad beans: they are used to make purées and soups. When young, the whole plant makes excellent fodder. POTATO Solanum tuberosum L. Solanaceae. ORIGIN. - Tuberous perennial. Native to Chile and Peru, cultivated there before the discovery of America, it was imported into Europe between 1580 and 1585, first by the Spaniards, then by the English, during the voyages of Raleigh, in Virginia, and propagated in France by the good care and disinterestedness of Parmentier, towards the end of the 18th century. OUTDOOR CULTIVATION. - The potato is not very fussy about the choice of soil, and thrives in any good soil, not too compact, well fertilized with energetic fertilizers. In the north, tubers are planted in early April, and the closer you get to the south, the earlier you plant. In the south and south-west, planting starts in January, but the best time is the second half of February. Plant in rows spaced 70 to 90 centimetres apart, depending on development, and 40 to 60 centimetres apart. Medium-sized, whole tubers are preferable. Cover with 10 or 12 centimetres of crumbly soil. Once the stems have emerged from the soil by about 15 centimetres, we hoe them for the first time, and a few weeks later, we hoe them a second time, ridging them fairly heavily. Although not essential, this hoeing operation is nevertheless useful, as it keeps the stalks cool and prevents the tubers from spreading too much, making them easier to pull out. Potatoes can also be propagated from seed, which is sown in layers. In January and February, the plant is transplanted to another layer, preferably in pots; planting is easier and recovery is assured. This method of propagation is not widely used, but is employed by sowers seeking to obtain new varieties. Potatoes can also be propagated by smothering cuttings in cloches or on layers; once rooted, the seedlings are placed in pots, and when the roots have lined the sides of the vase, they are planted permanently. This procedure is only applicable when you want to propagate rare varieties; we tried it with the Early rose variety, and the results were quite satisfactory. Potatoes are harvested when the tops are yellow and even dry. When they are uprooted, they are laid on the ground for a few hours to dry out, then brought in to protect them from bad weather, especially frost. In vegetable growing, you don't wait until the potatoes are ripe to dig them up; you take them to market as soon as they're big enough to sell. Only a few rows are kept, which are left to ripen on the ground to obtain good seed. CULTIVATION OF EARLY POTATOES. - Growing early potatoes is a great source of profit for the grower. It can be done in several ways. The best system is to prepare a layer 40 to 50 centimetres thick in January, even in December, and surround it with good stoves.
