Load it with 20 centimetres of good soil, then mark out four rows per box of 1.33 and plant the tubers at 35 centimetres on the E line. BERGER, Vegetable plants. 21 in a chessboard pattern. Cover with straw mats on cold nights, and give them air during the day, weather permitting; in this way, you can harvest in the first fortnight of March. If, instead of building up the layer with manure, a 50-centimeter layer of previously used tan is placed in the trunk, the tubers can be planted directly into it, with good results. Alternatively, if you have a flowerbed or square with good southern exposure, you can place boxes directly on the ground and surround them with stoves. Drain the interior well, and plant the tubers as described above. Put up the panels, water if necessary, and cover during cold spells. The result is good potatoes for harvesting in April. You can also put tubers in six-inch pots and bury them on a layer, among other crops; being transportable, these pots can be placed on other, warmer layers, when they become a nuisance or the layer no longer provides enough heat. This type of cultivation used to be widely practiced in the Paris area. Under the bench of a greenhouse, tubers can also be buried in sand or potting soil; they will produce in a short time and without much care. The varieties best suited to these different growing systems are: Marjolin, - Early rose, - Reine des hâtives, etc. The Marjolin variety, to give good results, must be sufficiently germinated before being planted; to do this, it is placed upside down in wicker baskets, which must be placed in a warm place. VARIETIES. - Potato varieties are extremely numerous. America, England and Germany supply us with many. M. Vilmorin has classified them into : 1° Round with yellow flesh. - 2° Long with yellow flesh. - 3° Long with variegated flesh. - 4° Bright red skin. - 5° Pink or flattened red. - 6° Red, long, smooth. - 7° Red, long, notched. - 8° Violet or variegated violet. We will mention a few good varieties belonging to each genus. Potato thistle. - White horn. - White elephant. - Snowflake. - Belle de Fontenay, - Early rose (fig. 55). - Imperator. - Institute of FIG. 55. - Early rose potato. Beauvais. - Joseph Rigault. - Magnum bonum. - Marjolin. - Marjolin Tétard. - Prince of Wales. - Wonder of America. - Pasteur. - Quarantaine de la Halle. - Royale (fig. 56). - Saint-Jean or Segonzac. - Van der Veer, Victor, very early. - Flourball blanc. - Gloire de Lyon. - Jaune du Poitou. - Reine des hâtives. - Souvenir du Congrès. - Junon. - Souvenir of the 1889 Exhibition, etc. SEEDS. - Potato seeds are fairly easy to harvest; not all varieties produce them. The fruit, which resembles an alkékenge, is harvested when the tops are yellow or dry. They are left to dry for a few days, then the seed is scooped out; they keep well for two years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Stems, as well as tubers, are affected by the disease that plagues solanaceous plants in general, and tomatoes in particular. The MIIIIR FIG. 56 - Royal potato. material Coulet et Chausse, which is used in the vineyards of Bordeaux and is a compound of sulfur, iron and copper sulfate powder, attenuates the disease without destroying it. Bordeaux mixture has been recommended by M. Prillieux. An insect of the Coleoptera order, the Colorado Doryphora (Doryphora decemlineata), about 1 centimeter long, gnaws leaves down to the core and prevents them from developing. The larva is as voracious as the perfect insect; multiplication of this insect is extraordinarily rapid; in the second generation, one hundred females can give birth to 50 million, and by the third, they can be counted in the billions. In 1877, Europe was threatened by an invasion of Doryphora; fortunately, it was stopped in time. Silpha opaca is another small beetle that causes much the same damage as Doryphora. According to M. Grosjean, the best remedy for destroying these two insects is copper arsenite (Scheele green or Paris green) or London purple (lime arsenate, colored with rosaniline, and mixed with powdered plaster, flour or wood ash). This treatment can be applied dry or as a spray. These various mixtures are violent poisons. White grubs and cutworms are very fond of potatoes, which they partially gnaw. In soils where they are abundant, they are a real scourge, as the most beautiful tubers are eaten away. Let's hope that Botrytis tenella will destroy these enemies of our crops. USES. - The potato is widely used in the diet, and is of great service to the working classes. It is a healthy food, but a little difficult to digest. It is used to make starch and alcohol. Fed to certain animals, it rapidly fattens them. PERPET Portulaca oleracea L. Portulaceae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to the entire region stretching from the western Himalayas to southern Russia and Greece (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - Purslane is very sensitive to frost, so it should not be sown until all danger of cold has passed. Sow from May to August, in broadcast beds, without covering, lightly with a fork or rake, and tread, spread a light mulch, then water lightly and often, until the seeds are well established. Weed. For early purslane, sow in layers from January to March. Cover with panels and, after emergence, give it plenty of air. VARIETIES. - Broad-leaved golden purslane. - The best and most appreciated. Golden purslane. - Green purslane. - Very common in gardens, where it reseeds itself. SEEDS. - The first full-ground seedlings, sown in May, should be chosen as seedbeds. The seed matures during the summer. The first capsules are collected as they ripen; don't wait until they are yellow, as they will then fall off very easily. Once dry, they are cleaned and placed in bags. They keep for seven or eight years. DISEASES AND PESTS. - In dry soils, flea beetles can puncture leaves. USES. - Can be used in salads, either cooked or raw. Mix with sorrel and spinach for lean soups. EVERY MONTH RADISH Raphanus sativus L. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Annual plant of temperate western Asia (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - This breed can be sown all year round in the open ground, among other crops; in winter, a good exposure is chosen, and it is sheltered during cold spells. However, it is from February to October that the most seedlings are sown. Sow broadcast, in beds or rows. In summer, choose a cool exposure and, to make sure you don't run out, repeat sowing every twelve or fifteen days. Copious watering is necessary to keep the plants tender and firm. In the North, during the coldest months of the year, we sow under frames or cloches, or on beds made outside and simply covered with 10 cm of terracotta, which we shelter with windbreakers or wooden panels during the night, or on upstands. Given the short time it takes for this plant to reach maturity, it should be combined with other crops; the main thing is not to sow too thickly and to pull up the plants when they are ready to harvest. VARIETIES. - Round scarlet forcing radish, extra-early. - Round, pink, white-tipped forcing radish. - Round, pink, há̂tif forcing radish. - Three new varieties of great merit, extreme earliness. Round, pink or salmon-colored radish. - Round, pink, FIO-tipped radish. 57. - Round radish, pink, early. early. (fig. 57). - Round radish, oxblood red. - Radish, half-long, scarlet. - Radish, half-long, pink. - Radish, half-long, pink with white tip, the most widely grown of all varieties. - Radish, half-long, scarlet, early. - Radish, half-long, white, to be forced, early. - Radish, round, purple with white tip. - Round radish, yellow, extra early. - Radish, round, white. - Long, pink radish - Long or salmon radish, etc. SEEDS. - Radishes intended for seed production should be sown in autumn and stored over winter for spring planting. Or better still, sow in February, in warm beds, giving as much air as possible, to avoid wilting. Transplant in 12 to 15-centimeter beds in all directions. Seeds are good for harvesting in July-August; they keep well for four or five years. USES. - Small radishes of any month are served as hors-d'oeuvres; in some regions, they are eaten in salads with their leaves. SUMMER, AUTUMN AND WINTER RADISHES This series includes all the larger varieties that grow more slowly than all-month radishes. They are sown from March to August, in rows or beds, in good, well-tilled soil.

smoked. Water during the summer, weed, hoe and thin out to leave 10 to 15 centimetres or more between them, depending on the species. We had Gournay winter violet radishes, weighing 3 kilograms. In the Midi, most varieties survive the winter fairly well without suffering. But in the North, they need to be covered or placed in the vegetable greenhouse, buried up to the collar in river sand; they keep fresh for part of the winter. Outside, they are covered with litter or, better still, earth, like carrots. VARIETIES. - White, round, summer radish. - Yellow or red summer radish. - Giant white Stuttgart radish. - Half-long white Strasbourg radish. - Summer long black radish (fig. 58). - Black radish, large round, winter. - Large white radish, Augsburg. - Grey winter radish FIG. 58. - Long black radish, horseradish. de Laon. - Purple winter radish, Gournay. - Radish, early pink. - Vienne radish. - Radish, white-tipped. - Radish, white with green collar. - Pink winter radish, Chinese. This last breed is excellent for winter cultivation, but unfortunately is not widespread enough. 21. SEEDS. - The seeds of winter radishes are harvested in much the same way as those of all-month radishes, with the difference that they must be sown in autumn. Seedlings, stored in the cellar or vegetable greenhouse, should be replanted in March. DISEASES AND PESTS. - We are not aware of any disease affecting this plant. Flea beetles gnaw holes in the leaves, but this does not prevent them from growing. To keep them away, sprinkle the seedlings with plaster or powdered quicklime. Slugs are also keen on young seedlings, which they devour whole. In dry or chalky soils, especially if there is a lack of arro- sements, they are affected by small white worms that hold onto one of the sides. These products are very inferior and should not be eaten. USES. - Black radishes have the same uses as those of all months. RAIPONCE Campanula Rapunculus L. Campanulaceae. ORIGIN. - Native biennial. GROWING. - Rapunculus is sown from June to August, either in rays or broadcast in beds. The seed, which is extremely fine, is mixed with sand or sifted earth: without this precaution, sowing would be too thick. We don't cover; we just tread the ground with the beater, then spread a light mulch over the entire sown surface, and lightly baste until emergence is achieved; once this has taken place, we can water with an apple. As the young plants are very susceptible, to protect them a little from the sun's rays, sow radishes or spinach beforehand and fork them in. Harvesting begins in January and February. Harvesting continues right up to seed set. SEEDS. - Seeds are harvested from the previous year's seedlings. They are ripe towards the end of June and July. They keep well for five years. One gram contains 25,000 seeds, the smallest of all vegetable seeds. DISEASES, PESTS. - In rainy years, raiponce is sometimes affected by rust, which renders the plant unusable. Slugs and snails are fond of it, especially when it is just emerging. USES. - Rapunzel is eaten in salads, like lamb's lettuce; the root, which is shaped like a long radish, is eaten like the leaves. RHUBARBE HVBRIDE Rheum hybridum Ait. Polygonaceae. ORIGIN. - Mongolian perennial. CULTIVATION. - Vigorous plant (fig. 59), requiring deep, rich soil that is compact rather than light. It is propagated by splitting stumps, selecting the plants with the longest, thickest petioles. Plant out at the end of winter, towards the end of March or the first fortnight of April, in rows 80 cm or 1 m apart in all directions. Water immediately after planting, and hoe from time to time to keep the soil surface very clean. Harvesting does not begin until the second year. A plantation can last seven or eight years. Every year, at the end of winter, before sprouting, we give the plant a good ploughing and smoke it copiously, and when the flower stalks start to grow, we cut them as soon as they appear to give the leaves strength. Rhubarb can be propagated from seed, which is sown FIG. 59. - Hybrid rhubarb. as soon as mature, in July-August, in the open ground, or in February-March on warm beds, to be planted in the course of the summer. This type of propagation by sowing is seldom used, as the plants that come from it are never fully grown. In England, where rhubarb is widely grown, it is strengthened during the winter. To do this, the best clumps are pulled out whole, with their rootballs, and placed in greenhouses, buried in tan or under thermosiphon-heated tarpaulins. This practice is not widely used in France, as it would be very difficult for the grower, since confectioners generally obtain their supplies from foreign markets. It has always seemed to us that by cultivating a large area of this plant, in the open ground, we could make a fairly good profit, by interspersing it with other crops, which would not harm it. VARIETY. - There are several breeds. The best varieties are derived from Rheum hybridum: FIG. 60: Young rhubarb leaves. Tobolsk early red rhubarb. - Rhubarb Victoria (Myatts). - Rhubarb Mitchells Royal Albert. - Monarch rhubarb, etc. Gooseberry rhubarb (Rheum ribes) and Nepaul rhubarb (Rheum australe). - Very good for vegetable gardening. SEEDS. - During the summer, select a few of the best plants, at least three years old. Preference is given to those with the longest, widest petioles. By July-August, the seed is ripe and carefully harvested. Keeps for three years. USES - The very tender young leaves (fig. 60) can be used like spinach; the panicles, before the flowers have blossomed, can be used like cauliflower; the main use is for the petioles and ribs, which make excellent jams and cakes. They are also used daily in pastry-making. ROSEMARY Rosmarinus officinalis L. Labiatae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, indigenous, very common in limestone hillsides bordering the Ocean and the Mediterranean, especially in Spain. GROWING. - Rosemary is sown in April-May, in rows, in recently ploughed and smoked soil. Water to encourage germination. Once the seedling is strong enough, transplant it to a secluded spot at the foot of a wall or on an embankment: this is where the plant does best. Prune and remove dead wood. Rosemary treated in this way can live for a very long time. SEEDS. - Rosemary doesn't produce seeds until the second year after sowing. They keep well for four years. USES. - The leaves are used as a condiment and seasoning in sauces. ROQUETTE CULTIVATION Brassica Eruca E. Eruca sativa Lamk. Cruciferous. ORIGIN. - Native annual. CULTIVATION. - Sow from March or April to August, in rows or florets, in beds like salsify. Water abundantly during hot spells. Seedlings sown from March to June go to seed quickly, so they need to be renewed often. Other care consists of light hoeing and weeding. SEEDS. - Seeds harvested from overwintered plants are preferable, as they are better nourished. For this reason, you should choose plants sown at the end of summer, in August. The germination period is four years. USES. - The tender young leaves are eaten in salads, with a flavor similar to that of wild horseradish. SALSIFIS Tragopogon porrifolius L. Composites. ORIGIN. - Native biennial plant, found in the wild almost everywhere in ancient meadows. It is also spontaneous in Greece, Dalmatia, Italy and even Algeria (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - Salsify (fig. 61) thrives best in deep, light, well-fertilized soil. Sow in spring, from February to June, in rows spaced 25 to 30 cm apart; cover with the loosest soil. If the soil is firm, the seeds should be covered with other light, sifted earth or potting soil, otherwise, as they are very capricious, they will fail. If the plant is too thick, thin it out. FIO. 61. - White salsify. Weed and hoe several times during the summer, and give copious watering. Harvesting begins in late September, and continues throughout the winter and into spring. The first seedlings planted in February-March can be harvested from June onwards. SEEDS. - Seeds are harvested from the previous year's seedlings. It is important to cut off the downy part of the seed before it opens, otherwise it will be difficult to harvest and many seeds may be lost. They keep for a year. DISEASES, PESTS. - Rust sometimes appears on the leaves, especially in wet years; plaster and Bordeaux mixture seem to mitigate the effects of this disease, but it must be treated right from the start. In some oily soils, seedlings sown early in the spring are affected by two myriapods, the long-antenned geophilus and the flattened polydesmia, which enter the seed as the seedling begins to emerge and completely gnaw the interior of the seed, resulting in complete failure to emerge. We've had our first seedlings completely destroyed many times, and we know of no remedy for this. There's only one thing to do, and that's to sow again, but you have to wait until it's warm enough. USES. - Young salsify shoots are eaten in salads, either green or blanched; the root is frequently used in sauces and stews of all kinds. The juice is used to mark linen, producing a color similar to that of rust. Satureia hortensis. Labiate. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to our mountains. CULTIVATION. - Savory (fig. 62) is a very hardy plant, adaptable to all soils, but prefers a sunny exposure. It is broadcast in April among other crops. It can also be sown alone, in shallow rows, and transplanted if too thick, preferably in borders. When some plants have produced seeds, some always fall and sprout the following year. SEEDS. - Seeds are easy to harvest; spring seedlings come up during the summer, are pulled up and spread out on cloths exposed to the sun. They can be detached from their husks by tapping them with a stick. Their ability to germinate lasts three years. USES. - Savory, which is very fragrant, has the same uses as thyme. SARRIET VIVACE OR MOUNTAIN SARRIET Satureia montana L. Labiate. ORIGIN. - Perennial, native. CULTIVATION. - This plant is grown like hyssop. It is sown in spring or August, preferably in hard borFIG. 62. - Common savory. It tolerates transplanting very well and is not affected by cold. Hoe from time to time. Each year, in spring, cut back the old stems fairly close to the ground. A well-tended plantation can last a very long time. USES. - Same uses as annual savory. Salvia officinalis L. Labiatae. Native, perennial. Propagates by seed, sown in spring or autumn, preferably in rows, in borders, or by rooting fragments at the end of winter. Same cultivation and uses as thyme. Clary sage (Salvia sclarea) can be grown in the same way and is used for the same purposes. SPANISH SCOLYMUS Scolymus hispanicus L. Compositae. ORIGIN. - Native plant, biennial, well known in the South of France, in Provence where the inhabitants call it Cardouille. CULTIVATION. - Sow in rows in spring, in May; do not sow before this time, as the plant sets seed in the same year, which prevents the roots from growing. Preferably choose good, well-smoked, deeply-tilled soil. Growing care is the same as for salsify or scorzonera. Roots can be pulled up in October and successively throughout the winter. SEEDS. - Seeds should be harvested from the previous year's seedlings. They are difficult to collect because of the thorns. They keep for three years. USES. - The root, once the tough axis has been removed, can be used like salsify. It is excellent and has a very fine taste; it is regrettable that it is not more widely grown, as it is a delicious vegetable. SCORSONERA D'ESPAGNE or BLACK SALSIFIS Scorzonera Hispanica. Composites. ORIGIN. - Spontaneous perennial in Europe, from Spain where it is common, southern France and Germany, to the Caucasus region and perhaps as far as Siberia (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - A very hardy plant, very easy to grow. Sow in spring from February to June, as for salsify. Cultivation care is the same, with the appreciable difference that the scorsonera root can remain in the ground for several years without becoming tough; on the contrary, it only grows. They can be harvested in the first year, but it's better to wait until the second, when the produce is more beautiful. Once the seeds have been harvested, cut the uprights flush with the ground and hoe the beds. SEEDS. - Seeds should be harvested from three- or four-year-old seedlings, as they are better nourished and should be collected as they mature. They are larger and less elongated than salsify seeds. Germination lasts two to three years. USES. - Used in the same way as salsify: it is said to have a finer taste; it is an excellent vegetable, which is not sufficiently appreciated. SOJA D'ÉTAMPES Dolichos Soja L. Soja hispida Mœnch. Legumes ORIGIN. - Annual plant, found spontaneously in China, Cochinchina, Japan and Java, cultivated for over 4,000 years. CULTIVATION. - Soybeans are sown in spring, in March, April and May, depending on the region, in rows like bush beans, which are grown in the same way. VARIETIES. - In China, and especially in Japan, soya varieties are perhaps more numerous than bean varieties in Europe; there are climbing, half-dwarf and dwarf varieties. Some climbing varieties have been tried several times in France, with unsatisfactory results. Étampes soybean. - This is the best variety for our regions. Ordinary soybean. - Yellow grain, earlier than the previous variety, but less productive. SEEDS. - Harvested like bean seeds. USES. - Fresh beans are eaten in the same way as shelled beans; dried, they can be used in the same way, but must first be soaked in water for a certain time. COMESTIBLE SOW Cyperus esculentus L. Cyperaceae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, southern Europe. CULTIVATION. - Yellow nutsedge multiplies by its tubers, and by dividing clumps; in April and May, it is best placed in a cool spot, as this plant needs a lot of moisture to develop. Tubers are planted in threes 30 centimetres apart in all directions; the stump fragments should be 35 to 40 centimetres further apart. Once planted, water copiously and irrigate when the weather is hot. Then the clumps will spread enormously. The tubers are harvested in November, and keep well over winter in a closed, dry place. Tuber vegetation can be stimulated by placing them in pots and layers in February. If you want to keep old plants in place, mound them with earth and surround them with manure over winter, like artichokes. USES. - The tubers, which are as large as a hazelnut, can be eaten raw or roasted; they taste a little like almonds, and contain a certain amount of oil; orgeat is made from them. TETRAGONIA CORNUE (New Zealand spinach). Tetragonia expansa Ait. Mesembryanthemata. ORIGIN. - An annual plant from New Zealand, discovered by Cook and introduced to France in 1772 by John Banks. Since then, it has been found wild in Tasmania, southern and western Australia, Japan and southern America. It remains to be seen whether it has been naturalized in these places (D. C.). CULTIVATION. - Tetragonia is an excessively vigorous plant, which develops all the better when the heat is strong. It does well on old beds or in slightly chalky soil. Sow from the end of April and throughout May, in bunches 80 cm apart. Three or four seeds are placed in each hole, leaving only the best. It can also be sown in pots and placed in layers in March, to be planted out in May; it can be transplanted, but must be kept in the shade for a few days to ensure a good start. Other cultivation care is almost non-existent, just a little weeding and watering. It is customary to soak the seeds twenty-four hours before sowing them, to activate germination, and they should always be placed in the best possible exposure. SEEDS. - Seeds are easy to harvest in our regions. Choose a seed-bearing plant and leave it to develop, without cutting off any leaves. The seeds are harvested as they ripen, and dried in the shade; they keep well for five years. DISEASES, PESTS. - Snails are very fond of young seedlings, which they quickly devour. USES. - Used for the same purposes as spinach; gourmets agree that it is superior in taste and more nutritious. It's a pity that tetragonia isn't appreciated more than it is, as it can be a great summer spinach. The hardiness and vigor of this plant are such that a well-timed plant can grow to 1.20 m or more in diameter and provide for an entire family. ORDINARY THYME Thymus vulgaris L. Labiatae. ORIGIN. - Perennial, native to the arid mountains of southern France. CULTIVATION. - Thyme is generally planted in borders, to hold back soil and prevent it from mixing with the sand in driveways. Thyme is propagated by sowing in spring, either in the field or in a nursery. The fine seed is lightly covered. When the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted or thinned out, if sown in situ. It can also be propagated by separating clumps, from October to April. Before planting, shorten the roots and stem tips; once well established, a border can last four or five years, but it must be pruned twice a year, in March and September, to keep the borders low and narrow. We hoe and weed from time to time, to keep it clean and weed-free. VARIETIES. - Ordinary thyme or French thyme. - Small, grayish leaves, very aromatic; most widespread in the Midi region. Winter thyme or German thyme. - Taller plant than the above, with broader leaves, larger seeds and less aromatic flavor. SEEDS. - Thyme seed is best harvested from plants planted two or three years earlier; to do this, set aside a piece of bed in spring, pruned in autumn. The seed is ripe in June-July. It keeps well for five years. The winter variety produces far more than the small-leaf variety, which is why it is much more widespread than the other. THYME, NUISSIBLE ANIMALS. - Thyme is sometimes invaded by a white mould that develops on the roots and rapidly destroys them. In 1885, we had 500 meters of borders destroyed by this parasite. The remedies we tried were inconclusive. Slugs and snails seem to avoid it, probably because of its strong odor. USES. - Thyme is used as a condiment in sauces and stews of all kinds. It is also used as a fumigant to sanitize apartments contaminated by contagious or epidemic diseases. TOMATO Solanum Lycopersicum L. Lycopersicum esculentum Dun. Solanaceae. ORIGIN. - Annual plant, native to Peru. GROWN OUTDOORS. - The tomato is a plant that does well in all sufficiently fertilized soils; it needs good, energetic fertilizers. Sow in warm beds from January to the end of March. Once the young plants are strong enough, they are also transplanted onto warm beds. Water immediately after transplanting, shade the panels for a few days to help the plants take root, then give them air whenever possible to prevent them from shrivelling. Transplanting is a necessary operation for tomatoes; it produces well-branched, well-sprouted plants. In the second half of April in the south of France, and in May in the north, you plant them in recently tilled soil. They are planted in rows 60 to 80 centimetres apart, depending on the variety, and in two-row beds. The widest path should be between 80 centimetres and 1 metre, which is necessary for watering in summer. In certain regions of the south-west, plants are planted in 25-centimetre-wide holes filled with consumed manure or potting soil. When planting, reserve as many rootballs as possible, to ensure a better take-up; water copiously after planting, and again throughout the summer. Spread mulch around the plants, and about a month after planting, put a stake on each plant. Chestnut or pine stakes, 1.10 to 1.50 m high, are chosen for this purpose and inserted at the base of each tomato, where the shoots are attached as they develop. In some regions, trellises are made up of slats and stakes, laid horizontally and vertically, or with two wires; the first 30 centimetres above the ground, and the second 35 or 40 centimetres from the first; all this is complicated, and not worth a single stile placed at each foot. Tomato pruning is a necessity, and easy; you choose the first two branches that branch off, and E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 22 remove all the others. These are pinched off above the third bunch of flowers, for early varieties, and above the fourth or fifth, for late varieties. In September, to facilitate the ripening of the last fruits, we remove the leaves; the branches must be carefully tied back as they grow. If, in the course of the summer, it is possible to water several times with liquid manure or faeces in sufficient quantities, this should not be neglected, as magnificent results can be obtained with this fertilizer. GROWING EARLY TOMATOES. - Early tomato cultivation is quite lucrative, despite competition from products from Spain, Portugal and Algeria, which appear on our markets towards the end of March-April. Sow in December or in the first days of January, on warm beds, to ensure that the seeds germinate as quickly as possible. Once the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted into small 8-centimeter pots, which are then buried again in a layer. For a few days, they are deprived of air, to help them take root quickly, under the influence of background heat, and then given a little air when the weather permits. About three weeks after transplanting into pots, prepare a good layer, 60 centimetres thick, and fill it with 20 centimetres of potting soil mixed with vegetable garden soil. Five days later, when the layer has begun to burn, plant the tomatoes at a rate of five or seven plants per trunk. When the tomatoes have grown sufficiently, the two most beautiful branches are selected and all the others, present or to come, are removed. These two branches are attached to small stakes driven into the bed at some distance from the plant. When these branches are sufficiently full of flowers, we pinch off the ends; when the sun comes out, we give them air, baste them often, and water each plant carefully, without excess moisture. When the fruit is about to ripen, remove the surrounding leaves to bring them forward. You need to maintain good stoves, which often need to be reshaped; you raise the trunks when the plants come to touch the glass. Plants can be grown in pots, potted successively in larger vases, up to a maximum of 20 cm. As they are transportable, when one layer no longer warms up, they can be placed on another. Care is the same as above. Here's another excellent procedure, which we recommend and which almost always gives excellent results. Sow in September on an old bed, when the plants are strong enough, prick them out, then take cuttings from the heads, which grow very well under cover and on beds that are not too hot. Once the cuttings are rooted, they are placed in pots, which are then placed on layers and kept that way until they are ready to be planted. In this way, you obtain plants that produce a lot in spring, are very dwarfed and very early. The best varieties for these different types of cultivation are : The very dwarf, very early tomato with large fruit. - Early dwarf red tomatoes with crisp leaves, etc. VARIETIES. - There are a large number of varieties, especially in recent years. America has sent us a certain number, not all of which are of good quality. Among the most popular varieties, we would like to mention: Very early outdoor tomato. - Excellent, very early, good disease resistance; suitable for first-season planting. Large red tomato. - Very old variety, very good quality. Large, smooth red tomato (Trophy). - Beautiful smooth fruit, full flesh, very good. Early large red tomato. - Very good, old variety. Early dwarf red tomato. - Good for early cultivation. FIG. 63. - Scarlet Mikado tomato. Improved red apple tomato. - Very good and productive. Early red Chemin tomato. - New variety, very popular at Paris markets. President Garfield tomato. - Variety whose fruits become enormous, of good quality; we have exhibited some weighing 700 grams. Tomate roi Humbert. - Elongated, small fruit of fairly good quality. FIO. 64. - Champion tomato. Scarlet Mikado tomato (fig. 63). - New American variety, very large fruits. Purple Mihado tomato. - New, with huge, smooth fruits; the previous variety is preferable for quality. 22. New American tomato no. 400 - This little-known variety, marketed by Messrs Beney, Lamand and Musset, Lyon, is highly commendable from all points of view, with very regular, dark crimson fruits and fine, tasty flesh containing few seeds. Tomato perfection. - Very productive. Livingstone tomato. - Large, round, smooth fruit, early, bright scarlet (new). Champion tomato (fig. 64). - American origin; numerous, smooth, purplish-green fruits. Laye red tomato with stiff stem. - Straight stem, very consistent, supports itself without any support, not very productive, not very recommendable. Pear tomato. - Cherry tomato. - Purple apple tomato. - Round yellow tomato. - Yellow tomato, large, smooth, etc., all second-rate. SEEDS. - To harvest excellent tomato seeds, choose the most beautiful fruits, the first to form as much as possible. Let them ripen well on the vine, then pick them and place them in a warm spot, under a panel for example; arrange them side by side on boards. A few days later, the seed is removed with the blade of a knife or a wooden spatula. This seed is then placed in a sturdy cloth, or a light-colored canvas bag, with the upper part firmly attached, and, holding the whole thing in a container filled with water, they are squeezed several times in all directions, to remove the viscous part, and then laid out on boards or cloths in the shade to dry. They retain their ability to germinate for four or five years. DISEASES, PESTS. - In recent years, tomato plantations have been affected by a disease similar to that affecting potatoes, peronospora infestans. This disease generally starts towards the end of June, attacks first the leaves and then the fruits, which are stained with black spots, preventing them from growing and causing them to rot. Several remedies have been tried or advocated; so far, none has produced serious results, as far as we know. M. Prillieux recommends treating several times with Bordeaux mixture, to which a little iron sulfate should be added. The Coulet et Chausse product, a compound of copper sulfate, iron sulfate and sulfur used to combat mildew in the Bordeaux region, appears to have some effect. Soot, ash and raw plaster, mixed in equal parts and spread on leaves in a liquid or dry state, have given us the best results. USES. - Tomatoes are widely used and consumed all over the world, mainly in Europe and the Americas. They are used to make preserves, which are very popular in winter, as well as jams. In the South-West and South of France, tomatoes are eaten raw in salads, either on their own or mixed with hot peppers. Young green fruit can be preserved like gherkins. VALÉRIANE D'ALGER Fedia cornucopiæ Gærtn. Valerian. Annual plant, native to Algeria. Sow in the open ground, in rays 40 cm apart, from April to August. Thin out and water copiously during hot spells. When tender, the leaves are picked one by one and eaten as a salad. Worms Astragalus hamosus. L. Leguminosae. ORIGIN. - Native annual, growing spontaneously in deep, compact soils. CULTIVATION. - Sow in place in April, covering sufficiently. Thin the plant if it is too thick. Hoe to keep the soil permeable to water, which must be copious during hot spells. USES. - The fruit, shaped like small worms, can be placed in salads, as an innocent surprise. To be used during the twelve months of the year. January. FULL GROUND. - Break up, smoke and spade the soil, prepare the mixes to load the layers, prepare the asparagus pits, put the trunks on those you want to force into place, watch over the plantations, on ados or costiere. In warmer regions, the South-West and the Centre, sow the whole series of early cabbages and cauliflowers, spring lettuces, Romaines, leeks, turnips, pink radishes, early carrots, chervil, spinach, peas, broad beans, white onions; plant the first early potatoes in good exposure. Permanently plant Bacalan, Bonneuil, Brunswick or Tabouret cabbages, etc., various strawberry plants, virgin sorrel; prune old raspberry and redcurrant plants, plant young ones. Hoe, weed and rake peas sown in October. In the North, outdoor sowing is not very important. You can only prick out under frames. Visit vegetables stored indoors often. UNDER SIEVES. - On warm beds, sow tender and semi-hard cauliflowers, Brussels sprouts, early Savoy, York, St Denis, various lettuces and Romaine, turnips, bush beans, short carrots, leeks, full celery and celery turnips, tomatoes, peppers, melons, cucumbers, eggplants, etc. Force Strawberries, make Capuchin Beard, put Sweet Potatoes in vegetation. Plant Marjolin or Royal Potatoes, sow very dwarf Peas, renew stoves and layers, cover with mats, litter or leaves at night, give air and shade when the sun appears. February. In well-exposed areas, sow short and medium-length carrots, radishes, spinach, common and tuberous chervil, spring lettuce, Romaine, onions, leeks, early turnips, parsley, peas, broad beans, sugarloaf cabbage, Brussels sprouts, Joanet, Brunswick and the whole Milan curly series; Sow the first early beets, salsify, parsnips, salad burnet, sorrel, scorsonera, watercress, etc. Plant garlic, shallots, onions, split clumps of chives, redo sorrel and thyme borders, plant autumn-sown chives, Bacalan and Bonneuil cabbages, potatoes and asparagus, prepare mushroom wheels, etc. COUCHES-CHASSIS - Reshape stoves by adding new manure. Provide air during the hot hours. Force Asparagus and Strawberry plants into place and layers, resow Aubergines, Tomatoes, Peppers, Melons, Cucumbers, Basil, Full and White Celery, Kohlrabi, Tender and semi-hard Kale, Italian Curly Chicory, Short Leek, Dwarf Beans, Lettuce, Purslane, etc. Transplant strong plants. Carefully monitor bedding crops, on which success will depend. Cover with straw mats or bedding as soon as the weather turns cold. March. FULL GROUND. - Hurry to finish the big jobs, spade the squares, bury the fertilizers, visit the watering utensils, gutters, pipes, watering cans, pumps, merry-go-rounds. Sow peas, the last of the broad beans, spring lettuce, leaf lettuce, Romaine, radishes, leeks, wild chicory, early onions, parsley, chervil, watercress, sorrel, Turnip, Salsify, Scorsonera, Spinach, Beetroot, Poirée, Arroche, Savoy cabbage, Cauliflower, Kohlrabi, Kohlrabi, Chives, Parsnips, Savory, Burnet, Asparagus, etc. Celeriac and celeriac can be sown in good sunlight. Plant the last shallots and garlic, Bacalan cabbage and other varieties, Romaine, lettuces, potatoes, sorrel, thyme. Replace missing strawberry plants, replenish asparagus plantings, plant young shoots, replant watercress beds, plant and sow again. Weeding and hoeing. Start artichokes, smoke and spade. Plant seed-bearing roots, beets, parsnips, kohlrabi, etc. Lightly cover young seedlings or susceptible plantings. FOLDING BEDS. - Layers are very useful for transplanting peppers, eggplants, tomatoes, etc. Stoves are still used during the first fortnight, at least in the North. Sow Peppers, Tomatoes, Melons, Cucumbers, Tetragona, Beans, Celery, Basil, Curly Chicory, etc. Strawberries and the last Asparagus continue to be forced. Monitor the pruning of melons, and continue cutting potatoes and yams. Lettuce and curly chicory are being transplanted on warm soil after sowing chervil and radishes. Beware of sunburn, which is very hot at this time of year. April. IN THE GROUND. - Work in the open ground is the most important part of the year. Sow all types of vegetables in large quantities: Celery, Savoy cabbage, Head cabbage, Cauliflower, Brussels sprouts, Kohlrabi, Turnip cabbage, Carrots, all varieties, Salsify, Scoro- nera, Scolyma, Wild chicory, Dandelion, Chervis, Beet, Burnet, Artichoke, Pear, Cardoon, Baselle, Leeks, Watercress, Watercress, Landcress, Thyme, Savory, Romaine and Lettuce, Pink and Chinese Radish, Chervil, Parsley, Spinach, Asparagus, Peas, Beans, etc. Plant potatoes, apple cabbage, cauliflower, curly endive (sown in January in layers), early beet, onions, Romaine, lettuces, leeks sown in layers. If frosts are no longer a threat, plant peppers, tomatoes, eggplants, melons, cucumbers, squash and celery, depending on the climate. Silkworm artichokes, make new plantings. Weed, hoe and thin out seedlings. CHASSIS LAYERS. - Sow Chicory, Scarole, Baselle, Tetragone, Melons, Aubergines, Peppers, Tomatoes, Celery, etc. on warm beds. These same plants can be transplanted in nurseries on warm beds. Harvest Beans, Strawberries, Tomatoes or other forced vegetables, keep a close eye on these crops, baste, shade, give air and prevent Melons from getting sunburned. Multiply sweet potatoes and yams. May. FULL GROUND. - With soil sufficiently warm, sow and plant all vegetables listed in April. Also sow Florence fennel and other varieties, rocket, tuberous chervil, Spanish mallow without danger of it rising, all varieties of bush and pole beans, turnips, curly endive and Scarole. Plant the various varieties sown in March and April. Water, hoe and weed all crops, and thin out seedlings. CHASSIS LAYERS. - Only muted layers are now made for planting melons, squash, cucumbers, etc. Start harvesting melons, which should not be left to ripen too much. Remove all the layers and monitor the related crops of Beans, Tomatoes, Peas, etc. Cut sweet potatoes. June. FULL GROUND. - Copious and frequent watering. Sow all plants mentioned in April and May, adding: Black Radish, Rapunzel, Choux-Brocolis, branchu and pommé, Brocoli-Mamouth, Golden Savoy and late winter Pontoise cabbages, Scarole en cornet, Chou de Vaugirard, etc. Continue transplanting plants sown the previous month. Prune melons and tomatoes; set up open-air mushroom wheels. Harvest towards the end of the month, Garlic, Shallots, as well as seeds of Lamb's lettuce, Turnips, Cabbage, Sorrel, Chervil, etc. Remove runners from strawberry plants every month, mulch and water copiously. Hoe and weed all crops. LADIES. - Layers are no longer needed; simply harvest produce as it becomes available. Put away panels and trunks. July. FULL GROUND. - Copious watering. Hoe and weed all crops frequently, mulch delicate crops. Sow Radishes of all varieties, Scarole, Chicory, Lettuce, Carrots, Lamb's lettuce, Spinach, Chervil, Watercress, Kidney beans, to harvest green, and :. Plant Savoy cabbage, Broccoli cabbage, Vaugirard cabbage, Schweinfurth cabbage, hard cauliflower; plant strawberry plants sown in May, transplant varieties to be forced; butter full celery, thin out leaves on celery root; stuff a few cardoons, blanch chard pear, escarole and chicory. Pinch tomatoes and melons. Harvest seeds as they ripen. CHASSIS LAYERS. - Layers are no longer of any use. Remove the manure and soil used to make them. Store panels and trunks under cover. August. FULL EARTH. - We agree with some authors that August is the beginning of the horticultural year, as a whole series of new cultivation operations begin and continue without interruption until July. With the heat still high, watering takes up a good part of the time. Ploughing, smoking empty areas, replanting immediately. Mulch seedlings, hoe and weed. Sow carrots, onions, cabbage, Bacalan, Coeur-debœuf, York cabbage, etc., turnip, Chinese radish, black radish, pink radish, lettuces, winter Romaine, Scarole, bastard and round Scarole, Scorsonera, round-seeded spinach, lamb's lettuce, chervil. Plant Leeks, Scarole en cornet et ronde, Laitue merveille des quatre saisons, Céleri rave, Céleri plein, Choux de Bonncuil, Choux Brocolis, Cauliflower, etc. Multiply Watercress by cuttings, make Mushroom wheels, re-border Thyme, Hyssop. Butter Celery, Cardoon; blanch Chicory, Scarole, E. BERGER, Plantes potagères. 23 Poirée à cardes. Cut back artichoke stalks that have stopped giving; pull up onions. Harvest ripe seeds. Visit cucumber, melon, tomato and eggplant plantations frequently. LAYERS. - Layers are useless; continue to remove used manure and potting soil and pile them up. September. FULL GROUND. - As the heat is less intense, reduce watering. Thin out seedlings, hoe and weed often. You can still sow all the plants listed in the previous month. Plant long winter leeks, Scarole en cornet et bâtarde, late Savoy, Norwegian and Pontoise cabbages, winter lettuce, winter Romaine, artichokes, the last Celery-Raves, etc. Bastardise Celery and Cardoons, Poirées à cardes, blanch Chicory and Scarole. Continue harvesting Potatoes, Onions, Melons, Cucumbers, Potatoes, Cabbage, Tomatoes, Aubergines, etc. Carefully collect all ripe seeds; remove runners from large-fruited Strawberry plants, cut off all leaves and hoe deeply; pot Strawberry plants for forcing. BEDDING. - On teens, plant Lettuces, Gottes, sow Radishes towards the sin of the month. In the North, start forcing Asparagus, sow Green Beans. Stock up on manure and litter for the next crops. Mushroom stacks built indoors. October. FULL GROUND. - The days are getting shorter, the rains are abundant, so watering is not very important. Continue to hoe, weed and clean the various crops. Sow Lamb's lettuce, Radish, Spinach, Chervil, Parsley, Watercress, Winter Lettuce, Romaine, Peas, Broad Beans, etc. Plant Winter lettuce, Romaine, Scarole, Leeks, Strawberry plants Virgin sorrel, Thyme, Hyssop, Perennial savory, Lavender, Onions, Bacalan cabbage, or other cabbages. It's important to be aware of the climatic conditions of the region you're in, so as to sow and plant only those vegetables that can survive the winter without suffering too much. In soils likely to be too wet in winter, start breaking up the soil, buttering up celery and pear, and stuffing cardoons. At the end of the month, butter artichokes to protect them from frost. Cut Asparagus stems, harvest seed. LYING. - Towards the end of the month, the beds start to be used for sowing or planting green beans, short carrots, lettuces, chervil, radishes, asparagus, sorrel, peas, potatoes and so on. Start growing Capuchin Beard and Withloof. Under cloches, on ados, or on warm layers, sow or transplant Lettuces, Gottes, Romaine verte, sow Pink Radishes in the intervals. Place the boxes and their panels on the strawberry beds every month, to prolong production. Give crops under glass plenty of air and water. Pile up manure, leaves and any other fermentable matter used in couchos. November. FULL GROUND. - As cold weather begins to set in, cover susceptible vegetables to prevent frost damage. Butter artichokes or surround them with straw, litter or manure, as well as cardoons; sow peas, broad beans, lettuces, lamb's lettuce, spinach, radishes, chervil, etc. Plant Peas, Broad Beans, Lettuce, Lamb's lettuce, Spinach, Radish, Chervil, etc. Butter Celery in place, or pull it out, and place it in sand or bedding soil. Plant winter lettuces, Bacalan cabbage, sugar loafs, beef hearts, York hearts, garlic, shallots, onions, etc. Plant cabbage varieties whose seeds you want to harvest. Continue ripping. When heavy frosts are forecast, bring plants likely to suffer into the cellar or vegetable greenhouse, or cover them with litter, leaves or windbreaks, which can be removed once the temperature has risen. BEDDING. - Sow dwarf peas for transplanting, beans, lettuces for cutting and carrots, short carrots, radishes, peas, potatoes, etc. Continue forcing Asparagus either in layers or in place, as well as Parsley and Sorrel, make Capuchin Beard and Withloof; Mushroom wheels. Layers should be the object of constant care, as the temperature is highly variable. Provide air, to prevent moisture from concentrating inside; cover with mats; at night maintain good stoves or accots. December. FULL GROUND. - There are few seedlings to be sown, but at the right exposure, along a wall, you can sow Peas, Broad beans, Spinach, Early onions, Radishes, Lettuces, Cerseil. & Plant Garlic, Shallots, Onions, Lettuces, Romaine, Head cabbage, etc. Put large onions in the ground, to eat green or for seed. Rake, drain, transport soil, spade, bury fertilizers, make mats, clean seeds, watch over vegetables that have been brought in. LADIES. - Continue forcing out old asparagus claws and forcing in younger ones. Plant early potatoes, sow green beans, short carrots, melons. Cucumbers, Cauliflowers, Short leeks, Radishes, Romaines, Peas, Tomatoes, etc. Maintain and reshape stoves. These sowings or plantings must be done with care, as carelessness can result in the destruction of an entire harvest. * III END PREFACE. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LES ENGRAIS ET LEUR APPLICATION A LA FERTILISATION DU SOL By Albert LARBALETRIER Professor of Chemistry at the Pas-de-Calais School of Agriculture 1 vol. in-18 jesus, 360 pages, with 68 figs, hardback (Bibl des conn. utiles). 4 fr. The judicious application of fertilizers is one of the most important factors in ensuring high yields and remunerating agricultural labor. To obtain good results, it is necessary to take into account the nature of the soil, the time of application, the doses to be applied and the distribution in the soil. Mr. LARBALETRIER explained these concepts with remarkable clarity. He successively reviews plant nutrition, the elements of soil fertility, amendments, marning and plastering, plant fertilizers and cakes, animal fertilizers and guano, mixed fertilizers and manure, and chemical fertilizers containing nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium. The book concludes with advice on the purchase and use of fertilizers. DUJARDIN 1 vol. in-18 jésus, 368 pages, with 66fig. cart. (Bibl. des conn. uttiles). 4 fr. - LES FLEURS A PARIS CULTURE ET COMMERCE By Ph.-L. de VILMORIN 1 vol. in-16, 324 pages, with 203 figs (Bibl. scient. contemp.)... 3 fr. 50. The prodigious development in the taste and use of flowers has brought about a veritable revolution in their cultivation and trade. Where do all these flowers come from? who grows them, ships them, receives them, distributes them? what's the best way to use them? which ones are best suited to which uses? M. de Vilmorin explores all these topical and practical questions. The author first takes the reader on a comparative tour of various countries. Then, focusing on the city of Paris, he describes the procedures and organization of sales at IIalles, flower markets, resellers and florists' stores. He then indicates the origins of the main flowers sold in Paris, reviewing both glasshouse and Midi crops. The author then lists the main plants that are the object of the grower's care and, pointing out the merits of the various species as well as their cultivation, he deals with annuals, biennials, perennials, bulbs and outdoor plants, then talks about orchids and greenhouse plants, flowering trees and shrubs, roses in particular, and finally plants special to the cultivation of the Midi, and bouquet accessories, various greens, mosses and ferns. LES ORCHIDÉES MANUEL DE L'AMATEUR Par D. ROIS Assistant au Muséum d'histoire naturelle de Paris 1 vol. in-18 jésus. de 350 p., avec 120 fig. cart. (Bibl. des conn. utiles). 4 fr LES PLANTES D'APPARTEMENT ET LES PLANTES DE FENÊTRE By D. BOIS 1 vol. in-18 jesus, 388 p., 169 figs, hardback (Bibl. des conn. utiles.). 4 fr. The modest price of this little book, the elegant form in which it is presented, and the numerous figures that accompany the text make this volume a practical guide within the reach of all those who like to grow plants in their living rooms or on their windows, but nevertheless presenting the scientific ex actitude that is too often sacrificed in works of this kind. First of all, the author introduces us to the phenomena of plant life, as they normally occur, in order to show how detrimental to their health is the environment in which they find themselves placed, when we associate them with our lives, in apartments, where light is lacking, air is scanty and vitiated, where dust impedes respiratory functions, etc. Knowing what is needed to maintain life, the reader can then strive to create the best conditions for prolonging it. Watering is an issue on which the health of plants largely depends. There are a host of considerations on this subject which show how much care needs to be taken in the practice of this operation. Potting, sowing, marcotting, cuttings, transplanting, pinching and pruning are also covered in several chapters. The second part lists, in alphabetical order, the plants that can be grown on windows and balconies. The most widespread are made recognizable by the figures that accompany their descriptions, followed by notes on use, cultivation and propagation. Each species is described by its scientific and common names. Finally, there are lists to help you choose between climbing species, those suitable for hanging vases and those that can live in the shade. The third part is devoted to apartment plants: description, uses, cultivation, arrangements in window boxes and cachepots, in aquariums; conservation of cut flowers and their arrangement in bouquets. In a word, M. Bois has sought to bring together in this little book everything of interest to the many horticultural enthusiasts whose garden is a window or terrace, and whose greenhouse is a living room. Remedies on hand; first aid before the arrival of the doctor and surgeon; art of caring for the sick and convalescents. This book is the result of fifteen years of practice in the country and the city. In writing it, the author's aim was to put it into the hands of the kind-hearted people who devote themselves to the relief of our physical miseries. who often live far from a doctor or pharmacist, and who are called upon not only to give consolation, but also advice, a completely elementary and practical work, a sure guide to the care to be given to the sick and convalescents In town as in the country, we don't always have the doctor near us, or at least as soon as we would like; often we even neglect to seek his care for a simple indisposition, in the first days of an illness. To obviate these inconveniences, the author has given a description of common illnesses; he has explained their symptoms and followed them with the appropriate treatment, carefully avoiding the complicated formulas that only doctors know how to apply. The Dictionnaire d'Electricité et de Magnétisme is a veritable. . . 25 fr. electrical encyclopedia, in which the reader will find a complete exposition of the principles accepted today, as well as descriptions of all applications. Particular attention has been paid to the many applications of electricity and magnetism to industry and railroads. The Dictionnaire d'Electricité et de Magnetisme, composed and printed in its entirety in less than eighteen months, and written immediately after the 1829 Universal Exhibition, is the only work of its kind to keep abreast of the most recent discoveries, and to describe the devices and applications that have recently been produced, both in France and abroad. NOUVEAU DICTIONNAIRE DE CHIMIE COMPRENANT Les anplications aux Sciences, aux Aris, à l'Agriculture et à l'Industrie A L'USAGE DES INDUSTRIELS, DES FABRICANTS DE PRODCITS CHIMIQUES DES AGRICULTEURS, DES MEDECINS. DES PHARMACIENS, DES LABORATOIRES MUNICIPAUX DE L'ÉCOLE CENTRALE, DE L'ÉCOLE DES MINES, DES ÉCOLES DE CHIMIE, ETC. ar Émile BOUANT Agrégé des sclences physiques, professeur au Iycée Charlemagne Avec une introduction par M. TROOST (de l'Institut) 1889, 1 volume in-8 de 116) pages, avec 650 figures. The author has confined himself to the field of practical chemistry... ... 25 fr. The preparations, properties and analysis of common compounds are described with the necessary developments. Industrial processes are described in such a way as to give a precise idea of methods and apparatus. It was necessary, while remaining scientific, to free the facts from over-specialized terms and hypothetical theories. The author has overcome both these difficulties. The style is elegantly precise and the developments are proportional to the practical importance of the subject. On every page, you'll find information on the applications of various bodies that you'd have to look for in a hundred special treatises, which are rarely to hand. This work therefore has the advantage of presenting a complete picture of the current state of science. ENVOI FRANCO CONTRE UN MANDAT SUR LA POSTE COURS DE TRAVAUX PRATIQUES Par E. JUNGFLEISCH Professeur au Conservatoire des arts et métiers à l'École supérieure de pharmacie de Paris 1 vol. in-8 de 1240 pages avec 372 figures, cart. ... ... . 25 fr. M. Jungfleisch's treatise will serve as a guide for all schools wishing to organize chemistry experiments. Here is an outline of the subjects covered: Book I. - General instruments and procedures. Book II. - Studies of chemical elements and compounds. This part is made up of the operations taught during the first year of study; these operations are preparations of mineral or organic substances and experiments designed to reveal the general properties of simple or compound bodies. They are based on facts chosen in such a way as to provide the student with the greatest possible amount of theoretical and practical knowledge. The first chapter is devoted to metalloids and the compounds they form. The second deals with metals and their combinations with metalloids and other metals. The third chapter deals with organic substances. Book III. - Analysis. The author explains in detail the practical methods usually adopted, and enables the student who has followed the series of exercises indicated to carry out regularly and accurately any of the procedures recommended in the special works. In the same spirit, second-year manipulations have been introduced. After the necessary indications for qualitative analysis, both inorganic and organic, Book III describes the procedures for quantitative analysis, either by weighing or by volumetric methods. As the examples have been chosen from among the most interesting bodies, the main subjects of analytical chemistry are examined. DALLET - LE MONDE VU PAR LES SAVANTS DU XIXe SIÈCLE Illustrated with 800 figures A splendid volume of 1100 pages gr. in-8 with two columns Paperback, in art cover.... . . . . . 18 fr. Hardback, gilt edges ... . . . . . . 22 fr. At a time when France has just celebrated the marvelous progress of its industry, it would be unfair not to associate with this triumph of engineers and industrialists the illustrious scientists to whose work we owe the countless conquests that science has made during the century that is drawing to a close, and which is truly the century of science. The world we live in offers us a marvellous spectacle; day by day better studied and better known, it presents itself to us with its varied tableaux which provoke our admiration and whose hitherto impenetrable secrets modern scientists have uncovered, thanks to the admirable working instruments which have unleashed their power of investigation. We thought it best to give the floor to the masters themselves, and let them explain their discoveries in their own magnificent language, which bears the stamp of their powerful individuality as well as their luminous and persuasive conviction. Le Monde vu par les sarants (The World Seen by Sarants) is aimed at all those, young and old, who are curious about things natural, who seek sweet joys and true emotions in serious reading, and at those who have no positive notion of the history of our globe; it will bring profit and pleasure, amusing instruction and instructive amusement; it will exercise the active curiosity of childhood; it will be a subject of meditation for mature age; placed within everyone's reach, it will spread the salutary lessons of science throughout the family home. The figures, sown in profusion and, so to speak, on every page, are the work of our best artists; they are the living commentary of the paintings that unfold before the reader. This encyclopaedia, in which true luxury of execution is combined with unusual affordability, constitutes both a rich album and an interesting book, which speaks to both the mind and the eyes, serious enough to instruct, original enough to charm. - SENT FRANCO AGAINST A MONEY ORDER ON THE POST Feb. i820 Lyon. - Imo PHIRAT AINÉ, A.
